Chokin’ on the Smoke (From the Fire You Started)

by thetalee

Summary

After the bridge collapse, with everyone except Ravi and Albert out with injuries, Buck finds himself once again in the captain’s seat. But someone in the city has started playing with matches…

Notes

Title is from "The Arsonist" by Puscifer

See the end of the work for more notes

An Old Face and New

Buck woke up earlier than he normally would have, the morning of his first shift after the bridge collapse. He moved quickly to shut off the alarm, but probably needn’t have worried. Eddie barely stirred where he lay against a mound of pillows, except to let out an unhappy grumble when Buck slipped out from under his arm.

Healing with broken ribs was exhausting, Buck knew, and it did not help that the position that Eddie needed to sleep in was not the one his husband naturally fell into. More than once in the night, they were both woken up by Eddie rolling over in his sleep to curl up with him and letting out a pained yelp for it. So he was tired and the prescription given to him by the doctors made him drowsy (and also a bit loopy, to everyone’s amusement) on top of that, so it was no surprise that the shrill noise of the alarm clock didn’t wake him.

Sliding out of bed, Buck went into the bathroom to get his morning started. The shower was quick and perfunctory, and afterwards he dressed in his uniform. And after that, he tried to tame his curls in front of the mirror. “Tried” being the keyword, because he’d barely gotten the comb wet before Gavin was letting out a shrill cry of his own.

From that point, the morning got chaotic. There was one less pair of hands to help with everything than there usually was, and more of an urgent need to get it done before he had to go. Buck got Gavin changed and settled in his high chair, went to go make sure Chris was awake enough to say good morning to, and then had to go through another check to ensure that his work bag had everything he needed and that he was also completely packed for Texas.

All that to say that, by the time Chris was shuffling out of his room and Carla was being let into the house by Abuela, Buck was running behind.

He sat down on the edge of the bed and shook Eddie. Gently, and then not so gently.

“Eds, babe, wake up,” he said, pushing at his shoulder. “Eds. Eds.”

“Wha’?” his husband mumbled, blinking his eyes open with a yawn.

“I’m taking off.”

“Okay,” Eddie said, eyes already sliding closed. “Have fun, be safe.”

Buck rolled his eyes, bent down to kiss him, and left him to go back to sleep. Carla was helping get breakfast on the table when he entered the dining room and he gave the woman a grateful hug for it.

“Okay, kiddos.” He slapped loud and noisy kisses on top of both Gavin and Chris’ head; the former giggled, the latter complained about him messing up his yet-to-be-combed hair. “Be good, don’t set the house on fire. I’ll be home this afternoon just long enough to pick up our bags and your dad, and then you’re not seeing me until Sunday.”

They had a flight to Austin, Texas that evening, and then another early on Sunday morning to get back in time for Buck’s shift that afternoon. Eddie had insisted he was up to it, when Buck asked, even though TK and Carlos would totally understand if they couldn’t make it. They’d never let them live it down, but they’d understand. But Eddie was a Diaz and the Diaz men were a stubborn breed, so he was going to soldier through a three hour flight in uncomfortable seats with broken ribs. Twice.

(Admittedly, he’d probably be drugged to the gills the entire time. The fun part would be getting him off the plane, out of the airport, and to the hotel.)

“I know, Pops,” Chris said, sounding all put upon. He still reached out and snagged his arms around Buck’s waist for a hug.

“I’m good for taking him to his equine therapy tomorrow,” Carla assured him. “And I’ll be here on Sunday morning.”

She’d be there Sunday morning because that was when Abuela and Pepa both went to church, and no one wanted to try and make a one year old sit through a Catholic service. And Christopher had zero interest in doing it, either. Speaking of religion, Abuela stopped Buck as he tried to rush through the door, tutting at him.

“You know better, Buckito,” she chided, even as her hand moved in front of him. Every morning, she crossed him and Eddie before they went to work, and it was silly of him to forget about it in his hurry. She then tapped at her cheek until he bent down to kiss it. “That’s better, now get going. Darse prisa.”

Buck only just managed to keep from rolling his eyes and being smacked for it.

The drive to work was lonelier than it normally was, and he blasted the radio to drown out the silence. When he got to the station, he stowed his bag in his locker and then stood in the bay for a moment, just trying to figure out what to do. C-shift was just starting to wind down in preparation for shift change, and he’d need to talk to Captain Mayfair for anything important he needed to know. But, perhaps most pressing, he had to find space for the two temporary 118ers coming in to keep their things.

The lockers were easy. They kept a spreadsheet listing which ones were in use and which weren’t. So, Buck picked two that were empty and slapped Post-It notes with their names on the metal doors. It was the cages for their turnout gear that proved tricky. Or, maybe “tricky” wasn’t the right word for it.

Part of what led to the entire mess of poor decisions that was his lawsuit against the department was that day he found his name taped over. It was physical proof that insecurities weren’t unfounded, that his friends and family really were moving on from him, that they had replaced him just as he always feared they would. And perhaps it was the memory of his own feelings on that day that had him putting the temporary name tags on the last two empty cages with vicious glee.

(It felt good, like loosening a knotted muscle that he’d entirely forgotten about.)

Jones’ name plate was still there. The cage was empty and his gear was missing, Buck had no idea where it currently was, but his name was still there. His name was still on the cage, and his stuff was still in his locker, but Jones wasn’t and wouldn’t be.

They’d have to remove it and let someone else use the cage, Buck knew. Eventually. Not yet, though.

“Diaz!” Captain Mayfair’s voice had him jumping a little. She was a short lady, carrying a bubbly aura of Mom-ness wherever she went, and was always smiling. Though today that smile was a mere ghost of itself. “You’re here early today. We saw you scuttling back and forth.”

“Yeah. Getting things set up for…” He trailed off with a gesture at the taped on names.

“Sucky work.” She held out her hand, a small loop of black cloth hanging from it. “There’s a box of them upstairs, for when everyone gets here.”

“Oh, thanks.” He slipped the loop over his shield badge, much as she had hers.

“How is everyone?” she asked him. “Tim didn’t really know, when I asked.”

“Oh, uh. They’re all back home and complaining. Pops- Er, Bobby apparently tried to roll himself to the elevator when no one was looking, before he was discharged.”

“Things must be hectic at home,” Mayfair remarked, staring at his hair. “I don’t think I’ve ever seen your curls.”

“Ah, yeah.” He ran his hand through them, feeling self conscious about it. There was gel in his locker, or should be, so he could do it when he had time. If he had time. “Was in a hurry this morning.”

“It’ll get easier, once things aren’t so hectic,” she assured him. “Now. There’s not really anything important for you to know before you take over. Not anything that isn’t in the logbook, at least…”

They were missing tools that had been on the engine, apparently, a couple halligans and the heavy duty suction cups he’d used on the windshield of the camper van. They hadn’t been put back in their compartments before the collapse, and they hadn’t been recovered since. The lack of the suction cups could affect how they handled extracting a victim from a car crash, at least until they were replaced.

They were also short an ambulance until the one got fixed up. Besides the damaged windshield from Buck landing on it, there were various other things that needed addressing, and Mayfair hadn’t heard the ETA on those repairs getting done. Which explained why they were only getting a single paramedic, from B shift, filling in for both Hen and Chimney. But hey, it hadn’t been blown up by an antique grenade, so it was doing better than previous ambulances.

Those on A shift started trickling in as he got breakfast going, mostly for himself since he hadn’t had time while rushing out the door, and with them came the ones that were filling in the gaps. Jordan Crawford, the paramedic from B shift, was one of the first to arrive. And after them, came…

“They said that you were in charge today, Buckley,” was how Lena Bosko announced herself, stomping up the stairs. “Who decided that was a good idea?”

“Bobby and Chief Williams,” Buck told her, turning down the burners. “And it’s Diaz now.”

“Ah, yeah, I heard ‘bout that. When did that start?”

“Uh, the Christmas after…” He waved the spatula he was holding, vaguely gesturing at nothing. “Y’know, after.”

“That explains so much.”

Buck couldn’t help the huff of amusement he let out, because Bosko wasn’t wrong.

“Okay, so, most everyone is the same as when you were last here,” he said, transferring the food from skillet to platter; he made enough on the off chance that one of the others would want any. They tended to eat breakfast before coming in, but one never knew. “Over there is Ravi and Albert, he’s Chimney’s little brother. That’s Crawford, they’re usually on B shift. Our probie should be around here somewhere. Try not to punch him.”

That had Bosko scowling at him.

“Is that because of the whole thing?” she asked, crossing her arms defensively. “Because I didn’t know your boy would-”

“What? No.” Buck shook his head. “I mean, screw you for that, but no. It’s because he will say something offensive, either to or in front of you, and you’ll probably be tempted to punch him for it. And since I’m wearing the captain’s hat, I’ll have to deal with the paperwork.”

“If he’s so bad, why hasn’t he been kicked to the curb, yet?”

“He’s Chief Torres’ nephew.” 

The answer got a silent oh from her.

Captains didn’t have the power to fire anyone, instead sending the termination request to brass, who would then sign off on it. It was usually just a formality, especially if they were being written up due to something like, say, stealing the ladder truck to get laid. But since Deputy Chief Torres was the one who signed those requests…

“Um, Captain Diaz?” The speaker was standing at the top of the stairs, looking around. He was short but broad in the shoulders, with bright red hair and absolutely covered in freckles. There was a tupperware container in his hands.

“Substitute captain,” Buck corrected, getting the newcomer’s attention. “You’re Tobias Klein?”

“Toby,” the man said, coming over to shake his hand.

“Toby,” Buck repeated. “I’m pretty sure there’s paperwork you need to fill out, I’ll get it from Cap’s office after I eat.” He refused to think of it as his office, he was simply keeping the seat warm. Gesturing between Toby and Bosko, he asked, “Do I need to find spare turnouts for either of you?”

They both shook their heads, and he clapped his hands together.

“Excellent. Ravi, Al?” he called, getting the younger men’s attention from where they were bickering about something on the other side of the loft. “Can you show Toby around?”

“Will do, Cap!” they chorused.

“I made sausage links, if either of you want any,” Buck offered. Bosko grabbed one straight off the platter, but Toby shook his head. 

“Already ate,” he said, heading for the fridge. Holding the tupperware up, he asked, “Okay if I put this in here?”

“S’long as your name’s on it,” Buck told him, heading for the table with his plate. “And you don’t forget it in there.”

They made a point of cleaning out the fridge once a month, but that was still plenty of time for something to turn into a petri dish, if it was pushed into the back. Buck may or may not have been responsible for that happening once. Or twice. Okay, several times.

They ended up having enough downtime that morning for Toby to get a tour of the station and then fill out the paperwork that was involved in starting at a new station, temporarily or not. It wasn’t much, since he was transferring from one of the other stations and wasn’t a brand new hire. Bosko didn’t have to fill out anything extra, since she was returning to the 136 once the others were cleared to come back.

Unfortunately, they didn’t have enough time for Buck to get his curls under control. The alarms went off just as he got the gel and comb from his locker, and he grumpily threw them back in before heading for the engine.

It was just as awkward to hop into the captain’s seat and be the one directing them as it had been those two shifts after the fire at dispatch. More awkward, even, and a lot more painful, after Buck absentmindedly told Jones to take a turn. The silence was thick and heavy following that, until he cleared his throat and continued giving them the details of what they were headed into.

That first call was a minor kitchen fire. Then there was another call, this time someone had a fall, so that only really required the paramedics. After that was a fender bender, with accompanying road rage that required police aid. Not Athena, disappointingly; she’d taken a few days off to make sure her husband didn’t get his stubborn self put back in the hospital too soon.

Her words.

They managed to get enough of a stretch of time without calls, or at least without calls that required everyone to go out, for Buck to make everyone lunch. He settled on spaghetti, since they had pasta and a thing of Bobby’s homemade sauce. No one complained about the choice, and Toby didn’t even get his tupperware out of the fridge.

Of course, throughout the meal- Actually, throughout the entire day, he’d caught Wolfe staring at Crawford with a constipated expression. Like the androgynous paramedic was a puzzle and the probie was having the damndest time trying to solve it. That was a mess that he would inevitably have to clean up, but Crawford hadn’t said anything - if they’d even noticed - so Buck left it alone for the moment.

He did, however, privately remind the probie that staring could be considered harassment. And that it was also rude. All the while trying to ignore the irony that was him saying it.

“We don’t have to go,” Buck said for what was, perhaps, the fiftieth time since he got home. And he hadn’t been home for that long.

“No,” Eddie insisted, trying to bend down so he could put on his socks and shoes. “I can go to our friend’s wedding. I’ll be- Mierda!”

The other man grimaced in pain, aborting the motion to wrap an arm around his torso. Buck sighed and knelt in front of him, picking up the socks he dropped and started tugging them up over first one food and then the other.

“They won’t mind,” he told his husband. “They’ll understand.”

“We are going to our friend’s wedding,” Eddie said, scowling down at him, even as he obediently lifted his feet when asked. He never did like being the one who needed help, even after all the therapy. “We couldn’t be there for the funeral.”

“Fine, you stubborn ass,” Buck said, making no effort to keep the fondness from his voice. He didn’t bother tying Eddie’s shoes, he’d just have to slip them off at the airport, anyway. Getting to his feet, he pecked his husband on the lips. “Your bag’s in the jeep, along with mine. We just have to say goodbye to everyone and then hurry out the door.”

Saying goodbye was, as always, the most difficult thing. Gavin didn’t know enough to realize that them going on a trip was any different from them heading for work. Though he never did much appreciate that, either. It was a different matter altogether for Chris, who did know the difference, and who had a general idea of what had nearly happened not too long ago.

As much as he was barreling full speed ahead into teenagehood and everything that came with it, the boy loved his fathers. And he did not like the idea of them being gone for an entire day so soon after such a thing.

“We’ll be fine, mijo,” Eddie assured him, hugging his son as tightly as he could without irritating his ribs. His son, meanwhile, was practically handling him with kids’ gloves. “We’ll both be back on Sunday.”

“They’ll probably make us call you while we’re there,” Buck told him, bouncing Gavin in his arms. “Teek was upset that he missed the cooler Diaz, when he and Carlos were up here.”

“You had better be calling us when you land,” Pepa tutted. She was staying with her mother while they were gone. “And before you board the plane to come back.”

“I will,” Buck assured her, because there was no doubt that his husband would be too out of it to remember. He glanced at his watch, managed not to cuss, and handed the toddler to Pepa. “Babe, we need to go.”

The moment they got to the jeep, he handed Eddie a bottle of water and some of his pain meds. His husband glared at him, but swallowed them down without complaint. Hopefully, they’d kick in before they took off, because there was no way that economy seats were going to be comfortable for either of them.

A Wedding in Texas

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie go to a wedding.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

They were running late.

Buck really should have expected it, honestly. Austin was two hours ahead of Los Angeles, so a three hour flight still had them landing on the far side of midnight. Eddie, bless him, managed to fall into a restless sleep on the way, which he didn’t quite manage to shake off when it was time to disembark. He basically sleepwalked through the process of getting their bags and rental car, and then checking into their hotel room.

The next morning wasn’t much better. Both of them were slow to get moving, and the dial for Eddie’s stubbornness had been turned way up. He spent the entire time, while Buck got ready and then helped his husband do the same, arguing against taking anything harder than tylenol for his ribs. There was no point in going to the wedding, he argued, if he was just going to doze through the entire thing.

And Buck could understand, he did. He remembered gritting his teeth through the agony in his leg just so he could be present at Eddie’s shield ceremony and be present. Buck just also happened to be vehemently against the idea of the love of his life putting himself through pain if he didn’t need to be. They may “just” be broken ribs, but the set of Eddie’s jaw, the pinched look around his eyes, the stiff way he was holding himself, they all told Buck how much those broken ribs ached with every breath.

Eddie was stubborn, but so was Buck. The argument ended with a compromise that neither of them liked, and Eddie taking half the dose that he had been, with the promise of telling Buck if he needed more.

They arrived at the venue with scant minutes to spare, the pair of them sheepishly ducking into the back row of fold-out chairs. It was an outdoor venue, a beautiful spring day with the sun shining, and thankfully no one was staring at them. No one but Paul, at least, the man standing at the front and looking at them in amusement. The other members of the 126 that they knew were all seated closer to the front, so they could at least avoid the teasing about them being (nearly) late until after the ceremony.

A recording of a ukulele started playing while Owen escorted a woman that Buck recognized as Carlos’ mom down the aisle and to her seat in the front row, before going to his spot near Paul. There was a chair left conspicuously and obviously empty beside Mrs. Reyes.

“Have you talked to him?” Buck asked, keeping his voice hushed.

“I haven’t had the chance,” Eddie whispered back.

Carlos’ dad, Buck knew, had been shot and killed the night before the bridge collapse. TK had said that he was handling it as well as could be expected, that is: not well, but he hadn’t said much more than that. Buck had simply been too busy to press him for more details in the days before making the trip down.

The music changed then, everyone getting to their feet as TK and Carlos started their way down the aisle together. When they made it to the end, where Paul and Owen and a woman that must have been Carlos’ ex-wife, Iris, were standing, everyone sat back down.

“See that?” Eddie said under his breath. “That’s what it looks like when both grooms show up expecting a wedding.”

Buck stomped on his husband’s foot and smiled innocently at the woman in front of them, when she turned back in curiosity at the sound of Eddie’s quiet cursing.

“Friends, family,” Paul started. Buck settled in to listen and to watch, grabbing Eddie’s hand. “We gather together on this day to celebrate the union of Carlos Tomás Reyes and Tyler Kennedy Strand in marriage…”

“You didn’t have to come, you know,” TK said. With the ceremony over, with vows and rings exchanged, they’d moved to the wedding reception. There were tables set up and a nearby area of the floor was quickly filling with dancing couples. “I know things are a mess back in LA.”

“What kind of friends would we be, if we missed your wedding?” Buck asked, as innocent as he could.

TK glowered at him.

“Let it go, I am begging you.”

“No.”

TK rolled his eyes and turned slightly in his seat to face Eddie.

“How do you live with him?” he asked.

“He’s very cute,” Eddie replied, pecking a kiss on Buck’s cheek as his husband preened. “The cooking helps, too.”

“Oh, I see.” TK gave a sage-like nod. “You keep him around because he’s pretty and cooks for you. A proper little housewife- Ow!” He glared and rubbed at his shin, where Buck had kicked him under the table. “That hurt, Sunshine.”

“Good.”

“Excited for your honeymoon?” Eddie asked. He was holding himself looser than he had been before, Buck noted, the painkillers kicking in.

“Oh I am. We’ve got a resort booked,” TK said. “Sunny skies, a nice pool, and room service.” He waggled his eyebrows. “Where did you guys go?”

“Uh, we didn’t.” Buck shared a glance with Eddie and shrugged. “We spent a few days alone at home, and then went back to work.”

“Lame.”

“With his luck, anything else would have ended in a disaster,” Eddie pointed out. “Bobby and Athena tried for a cruise, and they had to cancel for her dad. If we tried it, the boat would probably hit an iceberg.”

“My luck?” Buck scoffed. “You’re the one who ended up under a fridge in a camper van.”

“And then you were my hero and saved me.”

“Damn right.”

“How are you doing?” TK interrupted to ask, frowning at Eddie. His eyes flickered up and down his torso, like he was trying to develop x-ray vision to see under the man’s suit. “Buck said your ribs…?”

“Just a couple broken ribs,” Eddie said. “But I’m feeling no pain, now.”

“He popped one of his pills when we got here,” Buck said, and then scowled at his husband. “Just the one.”

“I wanted to actually be awake!”

“Yeah, it would have been awkward for our I Do’s to be interrupted by your snoring,” TK joked.

“Nah, Buck’s the one that snores.”

“That’s true,” their friend agreed. “It’s amazing anyone gets any sleep at your place with how loud- Ow! Sunshine!”

“Everythin’ alright over here?” Carlos asked, stepping up behind TK and wrapping his arms around his shoulders.

“Buck keeps kicking me,” TK whined.

“Please don’t break my husband,” the officer begged, and then he paused as the smile on his face got even bigger.

“I told you!” Eddie crowed, pointing at him. “I told you that being able to say husband was better.”

“You did,” Carlos agreed. He held out his hand. “C’mon, husband, I wanna dance.”

“Then let’s dance,” TK said, taking his hand and standing. “Husband.”

Buck watched the pair get lost in each other, swaying slowly and holding one another close. His friends were happy, and he was so very happy for them.

“I wanna dance, too,” Eddie declared, pushing his chair back and getting to his feet. “C’mon, mi sol. We haven’t had a chance to properly dance since you finished your lessons with Myra.”

“We dance in the kitchen all the time,” Buck pointed out, even as he stood up, too.

“Kitchen dancing is not the same,” his husband protested, grabbing him by the hand and dragging him onto the floor with all the other couples. “We’re at a wedding and we’re all dressed up.”

“How’re your ribs?” he asked, before they did anything.

“They’re fine,” Eddie assured him. And it did seem like they weren’t bothering him at the moment. “I’ll tell you if they start bothering me, promise.”

“Yeah, I don’t think I’m gonna trust your judgment, tough guy,” Buck told him. He gripped Eddie’s hips. “Nothing too fancy, and I’ll try not to step on your toes.”

“Don’t make promises you can’t keep.”

“Hey!”

As the sun set, the music switched from slow to something more energetic and the dancing followed suit. Buck made the executive decision to vacate the dance floor, dragging Eddie with him. It was a task made difficult by how, as the pain meds really kicked it, his husband got clingier and clingier. At least it was easier to convince the man to take another dose this way, once Buck deemed it time.

“Why are you two sitting over here instead of dancing?” Grace asked, as she and Judd came over to join them. Judd pulled out a chair for her, and she rewarded the action with a quick kiss before sitting down.

“Didn’t want to risk irritating his ribs by bouncing around too much,” Buck explained. He held out his hand to her. “I know we kinda know each other, but I don’t think we’ve actually met. I’m Buck, this is Eddie.”

“Grace,” she said, smiling sweetly as she took his hand and shook it. “You know Judd.”

“He and Marjan stole my husband during the wildfires,” Buck joked.

“How’re ya doin’, Hollywood?” Judd asked.

“I’m fine,” Eddie said, slightly muffled by the way he was nuzzling into Buck’s neck.

“He’s a little high, right now,” Buck explained, patting his husband on the head as Grace laughed. Maybe that second dose had been a bit too much.

“Well, this is Tommy,” she said, as the woman in question pulled out a seat beside her, along with a man Buck had seen her dancing with earlier. “And that’s Trevor.”

“Captain Vega,” Buck greeted her, shaking her hand as well.

“Captain Diaz,” she said in return, because clearly TK had been talking, and he was quick to correct her.

“Substitute captain. Just until Pops is back.” At her curious frown, and the way she glanced over at where Owen was lurking on the edges with his half-brother before her eyes went to where TK was dancing with his new husband’s ex-wife, he said, “Not my actual dad.”

“Fuck your dad,” was Eddie’s helpful addition to the conversation.

“Thank you, babe.”

“Your baby,” Eddie said. “Your bebé, y tú eres mi sol.”

It took everything Buck had to not laugh at that. The others were not so kind, Judd bursting out in raucous laughter while Grace and Tommy both cooed at him.

“I’m thinking you would’ve regretted sleeping through everything less,” Buck said. “Anyway, we’re uh, at a table of captains.”

At that, Judd shifted awkwardly in his seat, Grace reaching over to lay a comforting hand on his.

“Not me anymore,” he said. “The captain’s back, and I’m takin’ a leave of absence, maybe early retirement. To take care of my kid.”

“Oh.” Buck had heard about Wyatt’s accident from TK. “How, uh, how’s he doing?”

“He’s… Doin’,” Judd said. “It’s tough, y’know? This is tough, but he’s tougher.”

The big man gave a firm nod, like there was no possibility of his son not being tougher than his injuries.

“I can’t imagine.,” Buck said. “I- I mean, I thought my life was over after the ladder truck, and I was a real pain in the ass to everyone with my PT.”

“Ladder truck?” Tommy asked, head cocked a bit.

“Oh, uh. A kid with a grudge against my captain stuck a bomb on our ladder truck,” he explained. “It flipped and threw me out the cab, and then landed on my leg.”

“That was you!?” Trevor blurted out. His eyes darted down, like he was trying to see Buck’s leg through the table, so Buck stretched it out.

“Healed right up,” he said, giving his knee a light thump. “It was everything else that came after that really tried to do me in.”

“God on high,” the man said, shaking his head.

“He and TK have the same dumb luck,” Judd said. “We’re lucky a meteor hasn’t hit us just from the two of them being together.”

“Pretty sure we gave Pops a few new gray hairs last year,” Buck told them. “You should have seen his face when we mentioned coming down here for this. Like he was seeing another tsunami coming for the city.”

“Oho, yes, TK told us all about your little adventure together,” Tommy said, sounding far too much like Athena at that moment. “Came in for his next shift swearing he’d never get into a car with you again, if you were the one driving.”

“Also said your folks grounded you,” Judd teased.

“Teek talks too much,” Buck said, glowering over at the man. “It wasn’t that bad. Neither of us even died that time.”

“That time, he says.” Grace shook her head.

“Who here hasn’t technically died once or twice?” Buck asked.

The other four stared at him, before slowly raising their hands. Eddie did as well.

“You horrify me,” Grace told him, and then leaned closer. “Tell us more.”

“I don’t think I want to hear more,” Trevor said, shaking his head.

“Hold on, wait, TK told me about something a while ago, and I know he had to be making it up,” Buck said, leaning forward as well. “Did his dad really break open a frozen guy’s chest trying to do compressions?”

“Yup.” Tommy nodded. “I was there. Cracked it right open.”

“How!?” Buck demanded. “There’s like, so much stuff in the chest cavity.”

“Stuff,” Eddie mocked, though the effect was lost with how it was mumbled.

“No idea, but it happened,” the paramedic captain said. “That wasn’t what killed him, though.”

“No shit,” Buck blurted out, and then went wide eyed before saying to Grace, “Sorry.”

She waved the apology off.

“Okay, okay, besides that,” he said. “Weirdest calls?”

Tommy shared a look with Judd, and then smiled at Trevor.

“Did TK tell you about the frogs?”

Buck felt very much like Cinderella, having to escape the ball before the clock struck twelve. They stayed long enough to hear Tommy sing, and then hunted everyone down to say their goodbyes. Eddie was more with it at the moment, how he was holding himself giving away that the painkillers were wearing off.

“Already?” TK said when he saw them approaching. He was snuggled up to Carlos, almost in the other man’s lap.

“Already,” Buck confirmed. “Early flight back, I’ve got a shift in the afternoon.”

“I’m serious,” his friend said, getting up to hug him. “You didn’t have to come.”

“Yeah I did.”

“We’re grateful that you did,” Carlos said, having gotten up as well. He hugged Eddie, being careful with his ribs, and then got bearhugged by Buck. “Okay, okay, feelin’ the love.”

“Enjoy your honeymoon,” Eddie said, patting TK on the back. “Try not to die.”

“Only if you two promise the same,” the paramedic said. “Hey, if we can swing it, we’ll try visiting Los Angeles again. I wanna see the munchkins in person.”

“I can already see Pops’ look of despair.” Buck hugged his friend again, and then reluctantly stepped away. “Okay, we really gotta go before we turn into pumpkins.”

“It was Cinderella’s carriage that turned into a pumpkin,” Carlos pointed out.

“Whatever.”

They managed to drag themselves away, stopping every few moments to say goodbye to someone else, and eventually got to the parking lot and their rental car. They got in, Eddie flopping heavily into his seat and then letting out a hiss of regret. Buck rolled his eyes at him.

“We’ll dose you up before bed,” he said. And then, because it was nagging at him, he asked, “Do you regret it?”

“Breaking my ribs? Yeah.”

“Our wedding. Not getting married,” he hurriedly clarified, because his husband looked like he was about to go on a rant. “But how we did it?”

Eddie thought for a moment, and then shook his head.

“No.”

“...No? Just, no?”

“Yup,” he said. “I didn’t need fancy. We could’ve eloped.”

“Like I said, Ma got scary about that.”

“No one likes a scary ‘Thena.”

“They do not,” Buck agreed. He started the car. “Alright, let’s get to the hotel and out of these suits.”

Eddie’s mouth curved into a smile.

“I know that we can’t do anything too athletic with my ribs,” he said. “But I think we can figure something out while we have a hotel room to ourselves.”

“Y’know,” Buck said, pulling out of his parking spot a bit too quickly. “I think you’re right.”

Chapter End Notes

You wanna know the worst part, in my opinion, of how there'll probably not be another OG/LS crossover until Fox gives LS the same treatment? That we'll never get a scene of Athena and Tommy together.

My kingdom, to see Angela Bassett and Gina Torres on screen together.

Pride

Chapter Summary

In which Buck asks a question and then meets someone's mother.

The good thing about an early flight from Austin to Los Angeles is that, thanks to timezone changes, they got home while it was still early in the day, with Abuela and Pepa still at Sunday Mass. They were greeted at the door by the excited duo of Hoover and Gavin, and Buck had to maneuver around them with Carla’s help to deposit a mostly-asleep Eddie onto the couch. When he got back from fetching their bags from the jeep, he found their youngest curled into his husband’s side while the dog had his head resting on the man’s knee, tail wagging so fast it blurred.

The man himself was already on his way to falling asleep again.

“They missed you.” Carla sounded very amused as she said it. Her phone was out, no doubt taking pictures.

“Oh yeah, they missed one of us, alright,” Buck said, rolling his eyes with a smile and heading for the bedroom with the bags. “Chris still asleep?”

“Like a hibernating bear,” the aide confirmed, following behind him. “Pretty sure I heard him growl when Isabel poked her head in there before leaving.”

“He used to be a morning person.” He dumped the bags onto the bed and started unpacking them. Their suits were first, folded neatly on top, and he laid them out to be dry cleaned later. “Can you believe it?”

“I don’t think I’ve met a teenager yet that likes getting up in the morning.”

“But he’s not a teenager yet,” he pointed out with a pout. She laughed at him.

They’d only packed for the single day, so unpacking was easy and mostly consisted of tossing everything into the laundry hamper to be a problem in the future. Eddie’s phone charger was plugged into its spot by his side of the bed, while Buck’s got transferred to his work bag. His fingers brushed against something plastic when he did a final once over of their travel bags, and his eyes went wide when he pulled out his prescription bottle.

“Ah, shoot.” In the chaos of hurrying for the plane that morning, it had been completely forgotten. He checked the time on his watch and winced. “If I take it now, is it technically early or late?”

“What time do you normally take it?” Carla asked.

“At breakfast, so about seven,” Buck answered, shaking out a pill. “And I took it at that time yesterday, but Austin’s a couple hours ahead, so…”

“Pretty sure you’ll be okay if you take it now,” she said, popping into their en suite and coming back with a small paper cup of water. He took it from her with a grateful smile and threw back the bupropion before washing it down. “Has it helped any?

Buck paused to think about it before he answered.

“I think?” he hedged. Last week, he’d woken up to an empty space in the bed where Eddie was supposed to be and hadn’t panicked, like he would have those mornings after his adventure in coma land. And his thoughts were less of a rambling and disjointed mess, with no sudden and disconnected jumps between topics. “We’ll see how much it helps when I’m back at the Leadership Academy.”

Which, now that he brought it up, Buck wasn’t sure how that would work with him covering for Bobby. Earlier in the year, before the lightning strike, he had to miss an entire week of work because of the classes. And maybe it wouldn’t be a problem later, after the others were cleared to come back, but until then…

“Here’s to hopin’ it’ll make a difference,” Carla said, patting him on the arm. “I know you struggled, the last time you tried.”

“Understatement,” he said. He still had the recorded lectures on his phone, maybe he should try and see if he could sit through them without his attention wandering off like Hoover chasing after a butterfly. Or like Gavin chasing after a butterfly.

Or like Buck himself chasing after a butterfly.

(Butterflies were pretty, damn it, and he had an app on his phone that told him the species if he could take a clear enough picture.)

“Have you eaten anything yet?”

“Uh, airline food,” Buck said. “Didn’t have time for an actual meal before getting to the airport.”

There hadn’t been time for much of anything before getting to the airport. His curls were, once again, loose from their regular styling. They were starting to get on his nerves, to be perfectly honest.

“Come on then,” Carla said, gently yet insistently pushing him out of the bedroom and into the hall. “I’ll fix something up for you boys. Maybe the smell will entice the bear out, too.”

“Carla, you don’t have to-” was how the protest started, but he got interrupted by a sudden yelp of a curse from Eddie, followed by Gavin’s startled crying and barking from Hoover. “I should…”

They split up at the dining room, Carla going left into the kitchen and Buck heading right for the living room. Eddie was still on the couch, slightly hunched over and clutching at his abdomen with one arm, the other patting Gavin as he attempted to calm him down. The dog was pacing back and forth while he whined, clearly aware that something was wrong but having no idea how to fix it.

“Aw, little guy,” Buck said, sweeping the toddler up into his arms. “Did Daddy scare you?”

“Oh sure, take his side,” his husband complained, wincing and grimacing as he uncurled. 

“How badly did he hurt you?” Buck asked, adjusting his hold on Gavin as he knelt down. With a freshly freed hand, he tugged Eddie’s shirt up enough to get a look at his side, as if that would help him see his ribs. All he really got was a better view of the still quite colorful bruises that mottled his skin.

“Not too bad,” Eddie said. He absentmindedly scratched at Hoover’s ears when the pooch pressed his head into his thigh, on the opposite side from Buck. “Surprised me, more than anything. He just pressed his little hand at the right spot. Wrong spot.”

“One of us should have been in here with you,” Buck said, letting the shirt fall back down and stretching up to kiss him. “To keep him from climbing all over you.”

“Not your fault, mi sol.”

“Still, babe, I’m sorry,” he insisted, bouncing Gavin a bit as he stood back up. “You gotta be careful, okay, kiddo? Daddy’s a bit delicate right now.”

Gavin, being just a couple months past his first birthday, didn’t pay the words any mind. He stretched out his little hands for Eddie and gave a demanding little,

“Daddy!”

“No, little guy, Daddy’s hurt.”

“No, no, it’s fine,” Eddie said. He looked about and then grabbed one of the throw pillows, placing it against his stomach. “Give him here.”

Buck was dubious, but he helped situate Gavin in Eddie’s lap all the same. He figured the kid would get bored soon, anyway, and wander off in search of trouble. No doubt with Hoover right on his tail, because the dog was as attached to the youngest Diaz just as much as he was to the oldest.

“Carla’s fixing us breakfast,” he said, once he was certain the danger to Eddie’s ribs were properly minimized. “Do you want me to bring your plate out here?”

Eddie gave him what might have been the bitchiest look he could manage at the time.

“I can sit at the dining table.”

“Okay, okay, forgive me for thinking about your comfort.”

“Fu-” Eddie paused, glanced down at Gavin who was grinning up at the both of them. “Fudge you.”

“Nice catch.”

“Gracias.” The man frowned and then craned his neck to look over both the pillow and child. “Am I still wearing my shoes?”

“You are.”

Eddie scowled at the confirmation, an expression which then morphed into a pout that he directed at his husband.

“Oh, you are pathetic,” Buck grumbled, even as he knelt back down. “Can’t even take off your own shoes…”

“I’m hurt,” the other man whined. “And I’m holding our kid.”

“I was not this bad after the lightning,” he said, throwing Eddie’s shoes in the general vicinity of his own, over by the door. He’ll tidy that up later, before he went off to work. “And I died.”

“Your ribs were just cracked, you could bend over without puncturing a lung,” Eddie retorted. There was a tightness around his eyes, and Buck wasn’t sure if it came from the lingering pain of his ribs or the reminder of Buck’s brush with mortality. “Don’t say it, mi sol.”

“Don’t need to, you already thought of it,” he said, and then tapped Gavin on the nose. “Try not to break your dad anymore, okay?”

“We’ll be fine,” Eddie insisted, fingers digging into Gavin’s sides to tickle him. The kid squealed in laughter.

Buck left them to it and headed for the kitchen, Hoover padding along right behind him. The smell of bacon was filling the air, and it was evidently attracting more than just hungry dogs. Buck froze in the middle of the dining room and gave the most dramatic gasp he could.

“He’s awake!” he crowed.

The look Christopher gave him, even as he rubbed the sleep from his eyes, was just as venomous as his father’s earlier one

The shift so far had been calm and easy, much like the one before he left for Texas. A traffic accident, an embarrassed couple who misplaced the key, and a tree set on fire in Silver Lake. Buck really hoped they caught whoever was doing that, it was getting annoying and those fires were going to spread to a house, one day soon. But the shift was very Q-word, he’d even had time to get his curls under control and eat supper before leaving home.

So he was very aware that he was taking the risk of upending that peace, when he spotted the probie looking bothered about something in the locker room after their last call and, instead of moving on with life, decided to put on his big boy breeches and see what was up.

“Is everything okay?” he asked, sitting down on the bench next to Wolfe.

“What?” The probie looked startled. “Yeah- Yes, Bu- Cap- Sir.”

“You sure?” Buck pressed. “Because you’re looking very…” He gestured at him. “Down, considering that the only thing lost on our last call was a cypress.”

“We’re firefighters,” Wolfe said, almost spitting the word out. “But that was the biggest fire we’ve been to since I started. A burning tree.”

“Hey, man,” Buck said. “Any day we don’t have to rescue someone from a burning building is a good day.”

“I guess.” He didn’ sound like he believed it, himself. “It’s just… Uncle Randolf used to tell me all these stories, and I’d watched them actually fight fires, and…”

Ah. Buck was pretty sure he knew what this was about, now.

“Why did you become a firefighter?” he asked him.

“What?”

“Why did you become a firefighter?” Buck repeated. “I mean, I did it at first because of Backdraft.”

“Backdraft?”

“It’s about firefighters in Chicago. There’s an arsonist-”

“I’ve seen the movie,” Wolfe interrupted. “Uncle Randolf makes fun of it.”

“Well.” Buck had to take a moment to keep from snapping at the kid. It wasn’t his fault that his deputy chief uncle had crappy tastes in film. “That movie was why I thought of becoming a firefighter at all, but I stayed with it because I got to be the tough guy and I got to help people.”

“Okay…”

“So, why did you become a firefighter?” he repeated, once again. “Were you just trying to be like your uncle?”

“N- No,” the probie said, though he didn’t sound too sure. Neither was Buck, to be honest. 

“Then-”

“Captain Nash?” a woman’s voice interrupted. Buck turned in his seat and spotted her standing by the locker room door. She was older, a good few years older than Bobby. And she looked tired, Buck thought. Exhausted.

“Bobby - Captain Nash - is out at the moment,” he told her, getting up and heading over to greet her. “Can I help you, Ms…?”

“Tabitha,” she said, glancing around. “Austin’s mother.”

Oh. Oh. This was-

“Mrs. Jones.” Behind him, Wolfe let out a barely audible “Oh” of his own. “It’s…” It wasn’t nice to meet her, not in these circumstances. “I’m Buck, that’s Dalton.”

He gestured at Wolfe, who nodded and then scuttled out of the locker room, fleeing from the awkwardness.

“My son went to your wedding,” she said, barely paying the probie any mind.

“He did.” Buck rubbed at the back of his neck, wishing that he could make his escape, too. “I guess you’re here for…?”

“His things, yes. I have a box in my car.”

“His work bag’s still in there,” he told her. Jones’ locker was at the far end and had been, as far as Buck was aware, untouched since… Since.

It was the first time he actually looked into the man’s locker. The inside of the door was plastered with pictures, most of them showing people that Buck recognized, along with a magnet striped in black, gray, white and purple. There was a black duffel bag, and he carefully pulled that out and sat it on the bench. As he watched, Tabitha reached down with a shaking hand that she simply placed on top of it.

“Austin loved this job,” she whispered. “ I remember the day he got his posting, and when he finished his first year. How proud he was. He was always so proud of this job. We knew there were risks, but I never really thought…”

Tabitha set her shoulders and unzipped the bag. His civilian clothes, the ones he’d changed out of that last morning and the ones he’d fully intended on changing back into at the end of the day, were neatly folded inside. She reached in, pulled out the shirt and stared at it for a moment, before dropping it back inside. Then she stepped around the bench to Buck’s side.

“I never understood,” Tabitha said, fingers brushing against the magnet. “He explained it to me and Alan time and again, but I never understood why he couldn’t find a nice girl to settle down with. Or a nice young man, I would have been fine with that. I just didn’t want him to be alone and unhappy…”

“He was happy, I think,” Buck felt compelled to say. “He didn’t… He didn’t need to be with someone to be happy.”

Her face scrunched up at his words and he was certain she was about to start crying. She thankfully didn’t, because he had no clue what he would do then.

“I was- I am so proud of him.” Tabitha peeled the magnet off the locker door, handling it with all the care of an ancient relic as she tucked it into her son’s duffle bag. “I don’t think I told him that enough. Do you have any children?”

“I do,” Buck told her.

“Tell them you’re proud of them,” she ordered. “I don’t care if they’re showing you stick figures they drew or a test they just barely passed. Tell them you’re proud.”

“I will,” he promised, and it might have been the easiest promise he had ever made.

“And your parents?” she asked. “Do they say they’re proud of you?”

He thought of Athena, at the hospital with her hand cupping his face as she told him those exact words. He thought of Bobby, the man’s arm thrown over his shoulders as he pulled him close, like he was showing him off, and the hugs that always managed to make him feel sheltered, and the way the captain trusted him to train Ravi and then Wolfe.

“They do,” he said.

“Good,” she said with a sharp nod, like she would have taken it as a personal insult if the answer had been any different.

There was a moment of awkwardness, where he watched her carefully remove a picture from the locker door like she was terrified it might collapse into ash and dust just from being touched, before he cleared his throat.

“I’ll- I’ll let you have your privacy,” Buck said, heading for the exit. “I’ll tell the others to stay out, until you're done.”

“Thank you,” she said, not looking at him. He was halfway out when her voice stopped him with a question so sudden he thought that even she looked surprised to be asking it. “Was it fast?”

“Was…?” Oh, God. “He- He fell. Uh, on impact, he… Yes, it was fast.”

“So he didn’t suffer.” Her voice was dull and her eyes focused on the pictures.

“No, ma’am.”

“Thank you,” Tabitha said again. 

Buck wasn’t sure this was something anyone should be thanked for. He just nodded and made his escape, heading for the loft long enough to tell the others to steer clear of the locker room until Jones’ mother was gone. After that task was done, he hid himself away in Bobby’s office.

With the door closed, he took out his phone and scrolled through his contacts. There was a moment where his thumb simply hovered over the screen, before he swiped the name and waited for the call to go through.

“Buckaroo,” Athena greeted him cheerfully.

“Hey, Ma.”

“Everything okay? I thought you were working.”

“I am, everything’s fine. I just had a moment and wanted to check in…”

Limit, Reached

Chapter Summary

In which Buck has to be bad cop.

Buck frowned as he looked into the station’s fridge. He’d just finished making breakfast for the others and realized, much to his dismay, that they were running low on ingredients for actual meals. So now he had a clipboard in one hand, so he could mark down the shopping list for a store run. It probably wouldn’t happen until the next time they were on shift: Reynolds didn’t do “family meals” like Bobby did, and Mayfair absolutely refused to do store runs for any shift but her own anymore, after she and Bobby got into one too many squabbles regarding what she had bought.

Bobby could get very, very particular regarding his ingredients.

“Is there anything any of you want added to the list?” Buck asked, grabbing a piece of bacon off his plate to bite into.

“We’re out of pretzels!” Albert shouted over from where he was sitting at the table with the others, all of them busy with their own plates of food. Bosko was telling some story that had most of their attention, though Buck himself wasn’t listening to it.

“Besides junk food.” He rolled his eyes, but added them to the list all the same.

“Can we keep bananas off the list?” Crawford requested, half-turned in their seat to face him. “I’m allergic.”

“No bananas,” Buck said, writing it down in bold letters and underlining it. “Since we’re talking about it, any other foods to avoid if I don’t want to kill anyone?”

“It won’t kill me,” they said. “It’ll just make me break out into awful hives.”

“So you’ll just wish you were dead.”

“Exactly.”

“What about you, Toby?” Buck called over to their new driver-engineer. The only thing on the man’s plate was some of the scramble eggs he’d made to go with the bacon. “Any allergies?”

“Oh, uh.” The man looked caught out for a moment, and maybe a little nervous. “No pork. Or shellfish.”

Buck wrote that down, and then frowned at the words on the page. Those two things together…

“Would you prefer it if I didn’t mix dairy and meat for meals?” he asked, going out on a limb. Toby blinked at him, wide-eyed, before nodding like a bobblehead.

“Yeah. Please.”

Buck closed the fridge door and carried his plate and the clipboard to the table, sitting them both down in front of the empty seat that had been left for him.

“I might need to ask you what-” he started to say, only to cut himself off mid sentence when he heard Bosko say,

“And then he grabs a box of cat laxatives off the shelf.”

“Why are we talking about cat laxatives?” he asked.

“We’re not,” Ravi answered, smiling far too brightly. “We’re talking about you.”

“Talking about- Oh God.” There was only one story that Lena Bosko could be telling that involved him and cat laxatives. “No.”

“Oh, yes,” Bosko said, grinning unrepentantly at him.

“I hate you,” Buck told her, dropping into the chair.

“I’ll live,” she said with a shrug. “So anyway, he gives up trying to convince us it’s a coincidence that he’s there at the same time we are. No, no, he’s there to apologize.”

“I was,” he groaned, mostly into his hands.

“That’s when Diaz starts into him,” she continued, like he never said anything. “The rest of us are standing off to the side, watching like it’s a damn trainwreck that we can’t tear our eyes away from. And it just gets worse when he brings up his kid-”

“Our kid,” was the almost instinctive correction.

“And Buck there looks like a kicked puppy.”

“He brought Chris into it?” Albert sent a wide-eyed stare at Buck, who stuffed a rasher of bacon into his mouth instead of answering. Because he was not actually enjoying this story as much as the others clearly were.

“The exact words he used,” Bosko said, “were, ‘do you know how much Chris misses you?’ Like the poor kid was caught in the middle of their divorce.”

“I’m pretty sure I heard Chimney calling it a lover’s spat behind their backs,” Crawford commented. “And they weren’t even together then. He and Wilson had a field day with the gossip, afterwards.”

“I have literally no idea who any of those people are,” Toby commented, his eggs forgotten. “I thought Cap was Diaz.”

“That’s still weird,” Buck mumbled.

“You’ll meet them when they get back,” Ravi said.

“When are they all scheduled to come back?” Albert asked, leaning back in his chair so the front two legs were off the floor. He was going to fall back and knock himself out, and then Buck would be stuck handling the paperwork for an on-the-job injury. “I know Howie- Chimney,” he corrected, for the others’ sake, “is going to be out for a while.”

“Eddie’s like, mid-June at the earliest. But the others…” Buck knew there’d been an email in his poor overflowing inbox about this very topic. “I think Hen’s due back in a couple weeks, once she gets the all clear from her doctor.”

“Oh, thank God,” Crawford said.

“We’re due to get the ambulance back at the same time,” he said, taking joy in popping their bubble. The fact that the station was currently down an ambulance was the reason they only had one paramedic to cover for both Chimney and Hen. “You’re not escaping that easily.”

“Rain on my parade, why don’t ya.”

“There’s still time for you,” Ravi said, jostling Toby with his shoulder. “You can still run away.”

“I advise running,” Crawford told him. “This shift is a madhouse. I bet if you ask real nicely, you can-”

Buck didn’t get to find out what Crawford thought Toby would be able to do by asking nicely. All throughout breakfast, Wolfe had been sitting quietly, and so Buck hadn’t been paying him much attention. Which was evidently a mistake, because the probie suddenly burst out with,

“What are you!?”

“Excuse me?” Crawford asked, because it was apparent that the question was directed at them.

“What are you?” Wolfe repeated. “A guy or a girl? Because I can’t figure it out.”

“Wolfe!” Buck snapped, while Crawford merely answered with a simple, “Yes.”

“But that doesn’t-”

“Dalton Wolfe!” Buck snapped again, louder this time, as he stood up. That finally got the probie’s attention, who glanced up at him and then cowered at the unpleasant expression Buck knew he was wearing. “Office, now.”

“But-”

“Now.”

The probie shoved himself away from the table and got up, stomping towards the captain’s office. He looked very much, Buck couldn’t help but notice, like Chris did on those rare occasions he was in trouble enough to be sent to his room. God, he was barely more than a kid.

The others all watched him go in awkward silence, before turning their heads to look at Buck. They were synchronized as they did it. It was creepy.

“You were right,” Bosko spoke up. “I am tempted to punch him.”

“If it helps,” Crawford said, “I’m not actually all that offended by the question.”

Buck was almost certain that was only because Crawford didn’t care enough about Wolfe and anything that came out of his mouth to be offended. He wished he had just an ounce of their detached apathy, his life would be so much less stressful.

“He offered to show me how to check the equipment,” Clarke chimed in from farther down the table. “On his first day here.”

“He kept trying to tell Hen what to do with patients,” Albert added. It hadn’t been an issue since Crawford started filling in, simply because Buck hadn’t been letting the probie anywhere near them while they worked on a patient. It was barely tolerable when it was entirely Bobby’s problem to deal with. But now that it was Buck’s…

He grabbed the clipboard off the table and headed to the office that was, for the moment, his. He was not looking forward to this, and would like nothing more than to be able to dump this onto someone else’s lap, just like Mehta had done. Especially when he got there and found the probie standing in the middle of the room, arms crossed over his chest as he glared at the wall.

“Sit down,” Buck said, closing the door behind him. Wolfe did as he was told, petulantly throwing himself into one of the chairs in front of the desk. Buck crossed the room and tossed the clipboard down on top of some paperwork that Bobby hadn’t gotten through before the bridge collapse, and which Buck probably needed to.

“It was just a question,” Wolfe said, already defending himself.

“It was rude and disrespectful,” Buck said, certain that Bobby was at home laughing. He still had no clue what the probie had said, just before the bridge collapse, that resulted in Jones losing his temper. Part of him wanted to know, to ask Ortiz and get the story there. Part of him was half-convinced he was better off not knowing. “Do you respect the people you work with?”

The probie seemed thrown by the question.

“Of course I-”

“It doesn’t seem like it,” he interrupted. “Not when you’re asking questions like that. Not when you keep making comments and jokes that have your coworkers trying their best to avoid being stuck with you.”

“Just because everyone else is rude and can’t take a joke,” Wolfe said. “That doesn’t make it my fault.”

“If everyone you run into has a problem,” Buck countered, “then the problem is probably you.”

The hurt expression on the kid’s face almost made him feel guilty for saying it. But he’d tried and tried to rein him in, to get him to tone down on everything. He’d thought they were getting somewhere, that they were making progress, and maybe they were. But now Buck had to run the entire shift, and so he couldn’t focus on babysitting one single firefighter like he had been.

“Do you have an answer?” Buck asked him. “My question from yesterday, why you want to be a firefighter?”

Wolfe shrugged, sulking in his seat now that his feelings were bruised.

“You’ll have time to think about it,” he said. “Because you’re suspended for your next shift.”

That got a reaction.

“You can’t do that!” the probie protested.

“And if that alarm rings before Reynolds takes over,” Buck continued, not bothering to argue about what he could or could not do, “you’ll be staying behind.”

Wolfe glared at him, jaw tense.

“Am I dismissed?” the kid asked, with a tacked on, “Sir.”

“You can go.”

Buck watched Wolfe leap to his feet and storm out of the office, slamming the door closed behind him. He sighed and sat down at the desk, burying his face in his hands and feeling so very tired.

This was why he always made Eddie be the bad cop at home.

“I’m home!” Buck called out as he entered the house, narrowly avoiding being knocked over by Hoover’s excited greeting. He had to use one arm to hold him back long enough to actually get through the door and close it behind him. That done, he set down his work bag and knelt to give the pooch his well-deserved scratches. “Why can’t Eds greet me like this? Huh, boy?”

“Because Eddito still sleeps,” Abuela answered the hypothetical question. She was holding Gavin, but when the little tot started squirming she put him down. “The little one had nightmares last night. I offered to stay up with him, but…”

“Aw, you had nightmares?” Buck wrapped an arm around the kid when he toddled over, and he squeezed him tight. “Lemme guess, Eddie was all, ‘I’m a man, I can take care of my kid’?” He dropped his voice a few octaves as he said it.

“Si, si,” she said, laughing. “Not in those words.”

It was probably closer to, “No, ‘Buela, I’ve got this. Go back to bed.” But that didn’t let Buck make fun of his husband.

“Chris get to school okay?”

“He did. Carla sends her love.”

“Good.” Buck stood up, lifting Gavin up as he did. “Okay kiddo, what shall we do today while Dad catches up on his beauty sleep? We could go to the park, but I’m half terrified I’ll blink and you’ll have sprinted off somewhere.”

Their kid was fast and slippery; it really wasn’t much of an exaggeration to say that Gavin could disappear at the drop of a hat. He managed to do it often enough in their backyard, and it was an enclosed space. Trips to the park were much less stressful with two or more adults to keep an eye out.

“Is your sister working today?” Abuela asked.

“She is not.” Or at least, Buck was pretty sure she wasn’t. “What about it, little guy? Wanna go see Jee-Yun?”

“Jee! Jee!” Gavin shouted excitedly, bouncing in his arms.

“I think that’s a yes.”

He set Gavin down so he could take his work bag to the bedroom, using the opportunity to check on Eddie. The man was sleeping soundly, not even stirring when Buck dropped a kiss on his forehead. Then Buck stopped by Gavin’s room, to grab the diaper bag once he made sure it was stocked up.

To be perfectly honest, Buck hated how far away Maddie and Chimney lived now. On a day with good traffic, sure, it wasn’t that far away. But how often did Los Angeles have “good” traffic? Depending on when they left the house, the trip to theirs could take twenty minutes or it could take an hour. Luckily, today was closer to the former than the latter and they were pulling up in front of the Not-a-Murder House half an hour after hitting the road.

Maddie opened the door not quite immediately after he knocked.

“Oh thank God,” was what she said when she saw him. “I won’t be the only adult anymore.”

“What, did your house get overrun with children?” Buck asked her, entering the house when she stepped aside.

“Might as well have,” his sister complained, leading him out of the entryway. The living room was occupied, not by kids, but by Chimney and Hen lounging pathetically on the furniture.

“Hi, Buck,” the pair of them greeted him, barely raising their heads.

“Hi,” he said, setting Gavin down. The little guy immediately ran off to where Jee-Yun was playing, the pair of them falling into a babble-filled conversation. “Are these the children?”

“They are,” Maddie confirmed, coming to stand beside him. “I’ve been running around all morning fetching juice pouches. Wanna guess how many were for Jee?”

“I’m gonna guess… None?”

“Correct!”

“In sickness and in health,” Chimney moaned from his comfy chair. Maddie rolled her eyes.

“I haven’t made any vows yet.”

“Yesterday, Eddie pouted at me until I took his shoes off for him,” Buck told his sister. He dropped the diaper bag by the couch and sat down beside Hen. “How’s your head feelin’?”

“Like I’m perfectly fine and can go back to work today,” she said.

“Uh-huh.” Judging from the email he’d seen, her doctor and the department did not agree with her assessment. “And what does Karen think?”

“...she hid my phone.”

“Karen dropped her off this morning,” Maddie said. “Because Toni’s off with Clive somewhere.”

“You’re babysitting,” Buck said.

“I’m babysitting.”

“And what about you, Chim?”

“The days grow long and cold. The darkness, it comes for me, it-”

“He’s fine,” Maddie said. 

“I’m dying!”

“I said I’d get you your chips, calm down, drama queen.”

“Are you feeling nostalgic?” Buck asked his sister, getting back to his feet so he could help her fetch the children their snacks. Chimney was sounding very much like he used to, back when they were kids and he did something to get himself hurt.

“I’m feeling something, alright…”

Do(n't) Jump

Chapter Summary

In which people are living on the edge. (But not of glory.)

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The first thing that Ravi did, upon seeing Buck at the start of their shift on Wednesday morning, was shout in alarm. This was enough to catch the attention of his boyfriend, who’d been right behind him on the stairs to the loft.

“What did you do to your hair?” Albert demanded, crossing the distance between them so he could gawk better.

“Buzzed it,” Buck answered, running a hand over the short - very, very short - strands.

He had done it the day before, after yet another rushed morning to get Chris out the door while Gavin cried about being hungry and uncomfortable in a soiled diaper. Eddie, damn him, had slept right through it on his mound of pillows. Even with Abuela’s help, Buck still had to skip gelling his curls down, which meant he had to go through school drop off - and then the store afterwards, because Abuela needed him to buy something - looking like the overgrown child that people liked to joke he was.

When he’d gotten home, he went to the bathroom to get his hair in order. But then he realized, with maybe a bit too much tiredness fueling it, that the morning rush wouldn’t be getting better until Eddie got better. So, he’d decided - again, maybe not as clear headed as he should have been - to remove one of those steps for the foreseeable future. He’d dug out the clippers from where they’d been tucked away, and got to work.

Abuela had tsked with disappointment when she saw it. Eddie had out and out mourned, stopping just short of rending his clothes, the overdramatic goof.

“It’s a look,” Lena commented, having joined them in the middle of the two men’s minor freakout.

“It’s easier,” Buck said with a shrug. He saw Albert take out his phone and point it at him. “What are you doing?”

“Sending everyone a picture,” Albert answered, tapping at the screen for a moment before tucking it away. 

Buck rolled his eyes, even as he felt his phone repeatedly buzz in his pocket. He rolled his eyes again when he realized that, as everyone else was migrating up the stairs as well, they each paused to stare at him. Or rather, at his (temporary lack of) hair.

“Alright!” he called out, getting everyone’s attention once his watch said it was time for their shift to start. “First off: Yes, I buzzed my hair. No, you may not touch it.”

There was a sound of mock disappointment from Crawford.

“Sorry,” he said, not even attempting to sound like it. “But only my husband gets to molest my head. Secondly, store trip’s today. Who’s coming with? And remember, you get first pick on the snacks if you do.”

They just took the engine to the store, so not everyone went with them. Normally, there were plenty of hands being raised, each person there wanting to be able to grab their particular brand of chips or flavor of Pop Tarts or whatever. But today, as Buck watched, the only person who raised their hand was Toby.

“No one else?” Buck looked around at everyone, noticing how they were all, in turn, looking awkwardly at each other. “Seriously?”

“Are you going to have a clipboard?” Ravi asked, dread coloring his words.

Buck glared at him.

“Thank you for volunteering, Firefighter Panikkar.” He smirked at the man’s groan of despair. 

“We’ll remember your sacrifice,” Albert told his boyfriend, patting him on the shoulder.

“And just for that, you’re coming too, Han.”

“Damn it.”

“Where’s Wolfe?” Clarke asked. “This sounds like a job for the probie.”

“Wolfe isn’t working today,” Buck said. Everyone looked relieved at the news, including the ones who’d only dealt with the kid for two half-shifts, which actually made Buck feel kinda bad for him. “And to answer your question, Ravi, I am going to have a clipboard.”

How else would he keep track of the list and be able to mark off the items as they got them? He’d learned the hard way that going into a store without it was just flirting with disaster, going up and down every aisle while he tried to remember what he needed, constantly backtracking as he remembered to get butter, too, only after leaving the dairy aisle with the eggs and he was halfway to the checkout lane.

“What’s so bad about a clipboard?” he heard Toby ask, after he’d dismissed everyone and headed for the captain’s office.

“Everything,” was Ravi’s cryptic and entirely unfair answer, followed by a chorus of agreements.

There was absolutely no respect around here.

“I just want to make sure that everything’s… Okay.”

Buck paused in the paperwork he was bullshitting his way through to stare at his phone. His sister had called him - evidently for the third time, judging from the missed calls - shortly after they’d returned from the store trip. He’d left the others with the task of putting the food up before the alarm rang and was in the process of dealing with the annoying parts of the job that he’d been avoiding when his phone went off.

Maddie, much as he could have predicted, was concerned.

“Everything’s fine,” he told her. “This is about my hair, isn’t it?”

“It just isn’t like you!” she exclaimed. “I know how much time you spend in front of the mirror, so excuse me for being worried when Albert suddenly sends us all a picture of you bald.”

“I’m not bald,” he said. “And that was why I did it. It saves time in the mornings.”

“You’re sure you didn’t have a… A Britney Spears, circa Oh-Seven moment?”

“Pretty sure. It’s just one less thing to deal with in the mornings.” That, and he didn’t think his curls invoked the image of a mature adult that he needed as a (substitute) captain. How was he supposed to lead if everyone looked at him and saw an overgrown child? “What are you doing right now, anyway? Are you back at work? It sounds like you’re at dispatch.”

“I’m on break,” was her flippant answer.

“Were you on break the last two times you tried calling me?” he asked. “Wait, did you leave an injured Chimney to try and take care of Jee on his own?”

“She’s at daycare. Howie’s set up in front of the TV with plenty of snacks and juice pouches within reach. He’ll be fine until I get home,” Maddie said. “Now tell me, how did Eddie react to your new look?”

Buck let out a hum.

“You know that really old movie Chimney made us watch? The one with the monkey people?”

“Planet of the Apes?”

“That one, yeah. That scene at the end, where they find the Statue of Liberty sticking out of the ground and the guy freaks out. That was Eddie.”

“Adorable.”

“You’d have thought he walked in on me butchering Hoover in the tub, the way he acted.” The alarm went off and he let out a sigh, throwing down the pen he’d just been playing with at that point. “Okay, one of us has to actually work now.”

“Have fun, be safe.”

“Seriously?” Buck rolled his eyes and hung up on the sound of her laughing before he ran out to the trucks.

There were calls that came in that firefighters dreaded. Anything to do with children, for example. Or calls to certain areas of town that everyone knew was going to be a pain in the ass, just for the types of people that were bound to be involved. And then there were the calls that were, unfortunate to say, as routine as they were tedious, thanks to Los Angeles’ very nature as a city.

When they arrived at the scene of this particular one, a tall apartment building in the middle of downtown, Buck jumped out to find Athena waiting for them on the curb. Other officers were herding the small crowd of gawkers out of the way. There was the faint sound of shouting from up above them, but wind and distance kept the words from being clear.

“Sergeant,” Buck greeted her.

“Captain,” she responded, smirking as she said it. Her eyes flickered to his hair, eyebrows raising.

“Substitute captain,” he stressed, almost automatically. “What is it today?”

“They’re too busy shouting at each other to actually talk to us,” Athena said, all professional now. She gestured up as she spoke, Buck’s gaze following the line of her finger to the jumper several stories up. “Nathan Turner’s the one half-out the window, it’s their bedroom. The door’s locked and he refused to open it when we knocked.”

“And the one sticking their head out?”

“The husband, Donnie,” she told him. “From the sounds of things, one cheated on the other with their agent. The other’s agent.”

“Ouch,” Lena said.

“Do we know who did the cheating?” Albert asked, craning his neck to look up at the arguing couple. “Allegedly.”

“I’m willing to bet it’s the one not threatening to throw himself out a window,” Crawford said.

“That’s not really much of a guarantee,” Buck commented. He remembered how much he’d wanted to throw himself out a window, and that was only from how awful he felt that he’d somehow made Eddie convinced it was even a possibility.

“So what’s the plan, Cap?” Ravi asked.

Buck knew what the plan would normally be. This was a sadly routine call, because this city was filled with people who were constantly on the verge of throwing themselves out a window. But the normal plan was gonna need some adjustments, in light of Bobby’s absence. So he needed to figure out who would be filling in, and where.

He glanced at Ravi, the younger man staring at him expectantly with the others, and came to a quick decision.

“Here’s what we’re gonna do…”

Athena escorted Buck and Albert to the Turners’ apartment, once Buck told them all the game plan and they had their marching orders. The shouting was audible through the closed door, and there was an officer in the hallway to keep the curious neighbors out of the way.

“Just get back inside and we can talk about this!” Donnie, presumably, was shouting as Buck let himself and Albert in.

“There’s nothing to talk about!” Nathan’s response was audible through the open window. “I’ll see you in hell!”

“Fine, you want to jump? Go ahead and jump!” Donne looked behind him and let out an exasperated sigh when he saw the pair of them. “I have no idea what to say to him.”

“Anything but that,” Buck said, joining him at the window. He peeked out, spotting the airbag being blown up just below. Crawford was standing beside it, bag at the ready, in case things went sideways.

“He just isn’t listening to me,” Donnie complained. “He thinks I cheated on him and we’re just going in circles.”

“Have you tried apologizing?”

“I’m not gonna apologize for something I didn’t do,” the man said, following it up with a shout at his husband to, “Put that foot back inside, or I swear to God!”

“Listen, sometimes in a relationship, you just have to say that they’re right, even when they’re wrong,” Buck told him. “Sometimes especially then, because it’s just easier than dealing with their stubborn ass.”

“I’m telling Eddie you said that,” Albert chimed in. Buck glared at him.

“Fine,” Donnie huffed, and then leaned far enough out the window that Buck instinctively grabbed his shirt. “Nate? Honey? I’m sorry, I don’t know what I was thinking. You’re the only one for me.”

There was a brief silence, during which Buck and Albert stared at him incredulously. And then,

“You’re such a lying bitch!” Nathan shouted back.

“Fine! I hope you land on your ass!” Donnie ducked back in and crossed his arms. “See!?”

“Please tell me the agent wasn’t offering an acting gig,” Buck said, shaking his head. “Because that was just sad.”

“Modeling,” Donnie said, quickly following it with, “I didn’t sleep with her!”

Buck rolled his eyes as he leaned out the window again. Nathan was straddling the window sill, leg swinging where it was hanging outside. A glance above showed that the others were doing their job, he just needed to keep the man’s attention.

“Nathan, buddy,” he called out. The man looked up from where he’d been glaring at the airbag below. He didn’t look like he’d been crying, but Buck wasn’t sure if that was a good sign or not. “It sucks to be cheated on-”

“I didn’t cheat on him!” Donnie immediately argued from inside, but Buck ignored him, too busy trying to remember the story Bobby always spun for these occasions. He’d heard it so many times before, he really should have it memorized by now.

“But we’ve all been there, man. I had a girlfriend cheat on me, and…” His eyes flickered up to check on their progress. “And- And I thought she was the one. Marriage, kids, white picket fence, everything. So I went to the store and bought her a ring, a really nice ring.” Another glance up. “Then I took her to the Eiffel Tower, all romantic, you know? And I got down on a knee, and I popped the question, and everyone was staring at us. And do you know what she said?”

Another quick check. He gestured at Albert, who spoke into his radio.

“She said to me-”

And then Ravi was swinging down from above, his boots colliding with Nathan’s chest to send him falling back inside. He perched on the window sill for a moment to give Buck a nod before clambering through.

“I actually have no idea what she said,” Buck confessed to a slightly shell shocked looking Donnie, before ducking back inside. “Pops never got that far.”

One of the good things about being (substitute) captain, he found, was that he didn’t have to deal with the clean up. After they walked Nathan out and got him loaded up on the ambulance - because jumpers all got a reservation for a psych hold, regardless - Buck got to stand around looking pretty while everyone else ran around packing the airbag and rappelling equipment back up.

“So,” was how Athena announced her presence right beside him. He startled a bit, because he had not heard her coming. “What brought on the…?”

She gestured at his head, a twirling motion with her wrist that managed to get the message across.

“Got frustrated,” Buck told her, running his hand over the buzzcut again. “It’s one less thing to have to be worried about in the mornings, y’know?”

The sergeant let out a little hum.

“I don’t think I like it.”

“It’ll grow back out,” he said with a shrug. “How’s Pops?”

“He is the worst patient,” Athena complained, frustration lacing her words. “If I hadn’t gone back to work when I did, I might have smothered him with his pillow. Lovingly, of course.”

“Of course,” Buck agreed. “Is he going to be up for Sunday?”

She stared at him for a moment and then, very deliberately, reached over and smacked him on the head.

“Ow!” He rubbed at the spot she hit and pouted at her. “Ma, physically assaulting a fir- Hey!”

“Boy, if you don’t want to be smacked again, you should stop being stupid,” she told him. “Not that you can help it, sometimes.”

“That’s mean.”

“Who’s that?” she asked with a jut of her chin, ignoring his whining.

“That’s Toby,” Buck said. “The new driver-engineer.”

“Ah.” The good humor seemed to drain out of them both at that. “Do you know if they’ve set a date yet?”

“Later in the month, I think,” he said. It took a while to plan out a firefighter’s funeral, with all the pomp and circumstance and ceremony that went into it. “His mom came by the station, last shift.”

Athena grimaced at that.

“But for the grace of God…” She heaved out a sigh. “It looks like everything’s packed up, Captain. You should get going.”

“What are they gonna do, leave me behind?” he joked, even as he headed for the trucks.

“Don’t do anything Bobby wouldn’t do!” she yelled after him. And then, clearly remembering the things Bobby had done, she followed it with, “Don’t do anything Bobby wouldn’t want you to do!”

Lena and Ravi were both grinning at him when he climbed into the captain seat of the engine. Even Toby was smirking a little.

“Shut up,” Buck told them. “Drive, Toby.”

“Aye, Cap.”

“I don’t think Bobby would want you to-”
