“Ravi,” Buck interrupted without looking at him, “I make the chore charts and cook the dinners. Do you want to be stuck with bathroom duty after chili night?”

The silence that followed was answer enough.

Chapter End Notes

Yes, I did steal this call from season one. Mostly. No shame in my game.

So Deleterious

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie has a visitor, Buck tries not to stick his foot in his mouth, and there's a fire.

Eddie ached.

It hurt to sit up, and it hurt to stand, and it hurt to lie down, and it hurt to breathe. That was the problem with broken ribs, how there wasn’t really a comfortable way to exist with them. They weren’t the worst pain he’d ever experienced - he did have four scars from bullets pockmarking his skin, after all - but it was a constant discomfort. And at least when he was recovering from being shot, he could bend over and untie his own shoes.

He had to do it one-handed, but he could still do it.

The hospital had sent him home with a week or so worth of the heavy duty stuff, which Eddie both loved and hated. Loved, because they at least allowed him to breathe without wanting to cry. Hated, because it had him drowsy and loopy, filled his head with cotton and made the world around him go fuzzy at the edges. He wouldn’t have taken them at all, would have made do with the tylenol they had in the medicine cabinet, except he was watched like a hawk whenever it was time for the next dose. Even Christopher got in on it!

But now that prescription had run out, replaced with a prescription of high strength naproxen, which currently had “NOT FOR BUCK” written on the label in black sharpie. Not that Buck was in the habit of taking pain medication that was prescribed for someone else, but just in case. No one wanted to run the risk of an allergic reaction, after all.

Anyways, the opioid-derived prescription had run out, and now he actually was making due with what was basically a higher dose of over-the-counter stuff. And it helped, he definitely felt the difference when the meds kicked in versus when they hadn’t yet, but there was still a persistent ache. One he couldn’t do anything about, because there wasn’t really anything that a person could do for broken ribs.

A broken leg? Put it in a cast and stay off it. An arm? Again, cast it and don’t use it. But ribs? They used to wrap them, back in the day, before they realized that it harmed more than it helped. And it’s not like he could just not move his ribs. The doctor had actually instructed the opposite: he was supposed to spend some time every day breathing as deeply as he could, to prevent the risk of pneumonia. And breathing, as most people were aware, required his ribs to move.

That’s what Eddie was doing at the moment, actually. He sat at one of the dining room chairs, breathing deeply and slowly, holding it in before letting it out, and then repeating. Abuela was puttering about the kitchen, fixing up lunch, while Hoover followed on her heels and Carla chatted away from a stool at the breakfast bar. Gavin was running circles around the room, stuffed orange rabbit clutched in one hand and his gray blanket dragging along the floor from his other.

“How are you doing over there, Eddie?” Carla asked, and Eddie slowly let out his breath.

“Just peachy,” he said, not even making an effort to sound convincing. The home aide snorted and took the cup of water that Abuela handed her. She sat it in front of him, along with a pair of pills that she shook out of his prescription bottle, and then eyed him down until he swallowed them. “Thanks.”

He watched Carla’s eyebrows raise at the fact that he didn’t even make a show of being stubborn.

“Must be hurting, then.”

“They ache,” he confirmed, and then sniffed at the air. “What are you making, ‘Buela?”

“Quesadillas,” Abuela answered. “We had left over tortillas.”

“Smells good.” Eddie reached out and snagged Gavin as he zoomed past, tugging him into his side and then into his lap. It took a bit of maneuvering, to manage that without straining his ribs. “Doesn’t it smell good, little guy?”

“Good,” Gavin repeated with a nod, and then lifted up his rabbit. “Bun.”

“That is a bunny,” Eddie agreed. It was one of the toys they’d bought for Jee-Yun when she was over, in that month before Maddie returned from Boston. It was pretty much Gavin’s favorite toy. “Does it have a name?”

“Bun,” his kid repeated.

“Ah, okay, mijo. Bun the bunny.”

“Bun el conejito,” Abuela chimed in from the kitchen. “Necesitamos trabajar en su español, Eddito.”

“Si, si, Abuela,” Eddie said, nodding. “I know. We’ll have him throwing bilingual tantrums by the time he turns three.”

“Bueno.”

Knocking at the door interrupted any more of the conversation. Eddie started to get up before Carla’s hand on his shoulder stopped him. The home aide went to answer it, opening the door with a politely curious,

“Hello?”

“Oh. I think I went to the wrong house.”

Eddie recognized that voice.

“Stanley?” he called. Carla stepped back, letting the older veteran in. Stanley was wearing shorts again, the prosthetic leg on display. He was alone though, his usual canine companion absent. “Where’s Sage?”

“At home with Cora. I didn’t want to risk upsetting Hoover, bringing her into his home,” Stanley explained. “And you must be Carla, right?”

“I am,” she said, closing the door behind him.

“That’s mi abuela, Isabel, and this is Gavin,” Eddie said, and Abuela gave a greeting from the kitchen. Gavin pressed himself into Eddie’s shoulder, clearly feeling a bit shy. Understandably so: Stanley was a big man, with an equally big beard. “So what’s brought you over today?”

“Well,” Stanley said, walking over. He paused and gestured at one of the chairs at the dining table, only sitting down when Eddie nodded for him to do so. “I heard you were hurt, so I just wanted to check in on you.”

“I didn’t know Buck had your number.”

“He doesn’t, he told Nik and Gerty.”

“Ah.” Nik and Gerty were two others from the veterans’ group that he’d gotten close to. They hosted each other for dinner on occasion, and the pair had taken to texting Buck to check in on them whenever Eddie missed a Saturday meeting for whatever reason. If he said he didn’t appreciate it, he’d be lying. “It’s just some busted ribs. I’m fine.”

“He’s being real brave about it,” Carla said as she went by to retake her seat at the breakfast bar.

“Mainly I came by to ask if you figured out how you were getting to the meeting on Saturday,” Stanley said. “There’s no way you’re cleared to be driving at the moment.”

Eddie didn’t need to see Carla or his abuela to know what look they were giving him. If he got behind the wheel right now, there would be a line of people waiting for their turn to yell at him for it.

“I… Actually hadn’t thought that far ahead,” he admitted, absentmindedly running his fingers through Gavin’s hair. Buck was working on Saturday and Carla would be needed to take Chris to his horse therapy. He could ask Pepa, maybe, but then either he’d be stuck at the library afterwards while he waited for her to come back or she’d be stuck waiting for him to be done.

“It’s a good thing I came by, then,” Stanley announced, thumping the table. “I’ll just give you a ride.”

“Won’t that have you driving past the library?” Eddie was fairly certain the man lived on the other side of it from them.

“So?” Stanley asked. “It’ll be fine. We can’t have you not coming to meetings, Eddie. You’d be missed.”

More like they’d worry that he went the way of Mills and so many others, and then they’d probably have more than just Stanley showing up at their door. 

“We were about to have lunch,” Abuela said. “Would you like to stay, Stanley?”

“It does smell good,” the veteran admitted.  Then he heaved out a sigh and heaved himself up. “But if Cora knew I ate without her, she’d have my hide.”

“And we can’t have that.”

“No, ma’am. And I’d best be getting on home,” Stanley said. “Eddie, I’ll see you bright and early on Saturday. With muffins!”

“I like him,” Carla commented, after the man had departed. “He’s… Jovial.”

“He’s definitely something,” Eddie agreed.

At least they wouldn’t be stuck figuring things out on Saturday, now.

“Hey, Toby?”

Toby, who’d been in the middle of cleaning the engine’s side view mirrors, straightened up and turned his head at the sound of the captain’s voice. Well, substitute captain, as the man corrected just about every time. Diaz - Buck - was standing by the short hall that led to the station’s office space, awkwardly shuffling his feet.

“Yeah, Cap?”

“Gotta ask you something.”

Curious, Toby went over after making sure the cleaning supplies were out of the way. They could be called out to an emergency at any moment, and it would be embarrassing if they were held up because someone tripped over a bucket.

He hadn’t been entirely certain about transferring stations at first. Chief Williams had come to him personally, since it was such short notice, and he’d of course accepted. It wasn’t the job itself, even though he’d only passed everything he needed to be a driver-engineer just that winter, but the fact that he’d be filling a dead man’s shoes. There was a legitimate concern that, no matter what he did, Jones’ coworkers would not be very accepting in their mourning.

They were a bit hesitant with him, he could tell. A bit awkward in their interactions, especially on that first day when the (substitute) captain called him by the wrong name. But they’d been very welcoming over all, not a one of them giving him any trouble for replacing their late friend. His only true complaints laid with the probie, Wolfe, whom he learned managed to rub everyone the wrong way.

Though no one could tell him if the kid - and Wolfe was a kid, there was no way he was long out of high school - was doing it on purpose or if he was just like that. There wasn’t any malice fueling his comments, from what Toby could tell, just horrible ignorance. Willful ignorance, because there was no way Wolfe made it this far in life without someone, or multiple someones, getting on his case about the things that came out of his mouth.

“What did you need to talk about, Cap?” Toby asked when he was standing beside Buck. The 118’s (substitute) captain was definitely different from his previous one, but he didn’t have any problems with the man yet. Actually, no, he had one problem: that damned clipboard. He’d assumed everyone was just being overdramatic, that Ravi was simply giving the guy a hard time.

He wasn’t, and that day’s store trip was definitely going to feature in Toby’s nightmares.

“We’re working on Saturday,” Buck said. He sounded hesitant as he said it, the verbal equivalent of tiptoeing.

“I know.” Toby was well aware of what days he was scheduled, thanks.

“And I know Saturday’s the Sabbath-”

“Actually, the Shabbat starts at sunset on Friday,” Toby interrupted.  “And it ends at sunset on Saturday.”

“Oh, that’s- Okay, good to know,” Buck said, and then grumbled under his breath about someone named TK. It sounded a bit rude, from what Toby could make out. “Okay, so uh. So I looked, and there’s a bunch of things that you’re not supposed to do on that day? Like, working. And driving.” Then the man’s eyes went wide. “Wait! You are Jewish, right? I- I just assumed, but-”

Toby raised his hand to put a stop to the captain’s panicked rambling before it could really begin. He was oddly touched by this conversation. A bit weirded out, but touched all the same.

“Yes, I’m Jewish,” he said. “And yes, the Shabbat is supposed to be a day of rest.”

“Okay, so- so…” Buck’s hands made a frantic twisting motion in the air in front of him, somehow managing to get his meaning across quite well. “So!? I have no idea how this is going to work, since, y’know. You’re not supposed to.”

“Pikuach nefesh.”

Buck blinked at him.

“Pika…?”

“Pikuach nefesh,” Toby said again, pronouncing it slowly. “Saving a life trumps everything else. There’s a bunch of technicalities and what-have-yous, with thousands of years of arguing and debating, but it’s basically this: if it is necessary, in some way, to save a life, then it’s fine. And since I, a mere mortal man, cannot predict when any call we go on might be a life threatening emergency, then I must assume that each and every one is.”

“Okay.” Buck nodded, that frantic energy leaching away. “A mere mortal man?”

Toby shrugged.

“It’s the phrase my rabbi used when I asked him the same thing.”

“I feel less dumb now,” Buck said. He was tapping his fingers on his thigh and wasn’t quite looking at Toby, obviously thinking about something. “Okay, but chores. I can’t just assign you no chores for the day and give everyone else more to do.”

“You could,” Toby joked. “The chores keep the station running properly, which I have to assume is necessary to possibly save a life.”

“Even cleaning the bathrooms?” The question was asked rather dubiously, and he shrugged in response.

“It’s pushing it, but yes.”

“Alright.” Buck nodded to himself, a hand rubbing at his mouth. “On those days, I’ll try to make sure you’re assigned something that isn’t pushing it, I guess. Inventory, maintenance, that kind of thing. And you said it’s actually sunset to sunset, right?”

“Yup.”

“Then this Saturday, I give you permission to put off scrubbing the toilets until the evening.”

Buck grinned at him; Toby stared balefully back.

“Gee, thanks, Cap.”

“De nada.”

“We’re going to a house fire near USC, ” Buck informed everyone as they sped through the streets. He was clutching the tablet in his hand, frowning at the information that Dispatch was giving him. “Toby, turn here.”

“Aye, Cap.”

The alarm had gone off shortly after their conversation, during which Buck was certain he’d stuck his foot in his mouth about two or three times. At least. He was just thankful that the other man hadn’t seemed offended by any of it. It was the kind of thing that Buck would have panic-called TK about, except his friend was busy getting busy on his honeymoon and he wasn’t about to interrupt that for his own stupidity.

“There’s an off-duty firefighter on scene,” Buck continued. “They’re saying everyone’s accounted for, but we’ll see when we get there.”

The blaze was visible from down the street, quickly eating its way through a two-story house that was probably quite pretty before today. A group of young women were clustered on the sidewalk, a tall young man just beside them. Toby parked the engine, the ladder coming to a stop just behind and the ambulance pulling up a bit ahead, and they all hurried to get out.

“Toby, Ravi, get those hoses hooked up,” Buck ordered, not even looking at the pair but knowing all the same that they were hurrying to follow the command. “Ortiz, get that ladder extended so we can hit it from the top.”

He made his way to the group of civilians, Crawford and Albert hurrying from the ambulance. The women didn’t look hurt, just frightened, and very young. College aged, and he wondered if May knew any of them. The man had his back to Buck, talking to the girls.

“Sir? Are you the- Wolfe.”

“Hey, Cap,” the probie said, having turned around finally.

“That answers the question then,” Buck muttered, and then asked louder, “Is there anyone still inside?”

“No, no, this is all of us,” one of the women said. She was clutching to the housemate beside her. “He got us all out.”

“It was- It was nothing,” Wolfe said, coughing a bit. Buck frowned, stepping back to allow Albert and Crawford room to work.

“It happened so fast,” one of the others said. “We were watching a movie, and then suddenly there was smoke and heat. And then he was banging our door down.”

“Some smoke inhalation,” Albert declared, stethoscope pressed to Wolfe’s chest. “Some oxygen, he’ll be fine.”

“I’ll get him taken care of while you check over the rest,” Buck said. “C’mon, Dalton.”

The walk to the ambulance was short, though they stopped once or twice for Buck to relay orders. Since everyone was already safely out, they were free to simply focus on containing and putting out the blaze. Bosko was already at the top of the ladder with a hose, spraying the roof as the flames crept across it.

“It was wild,” Wolfe was saying, pausing to cough. “I was just driving by, y’know? And then I saw the fire, and just… Acted.”

“You probably saved their lives,” Buck told him, glancing back at the fire. It sounded like things had gone quickly, so there was no telling how things would have gone if Wolfe hadn’t intervened. Worse than some cough, that’s for sure.

He made Wolfe sit down and dragged over an oxygen tank.

“You did good,” he told him, clasping him on the shoulder, much like Bobby would do with him.

The probie beamed from behind the oxygen mask.

(It wouldn’t be until much later that Buck realized he didn’t see Wolfe’s bright yellow mini cooper anywhere near the fire.)

It's Totally Disneyland

Chapter Summary

In which little Gavin tries for freedom, and Chris has an appointment.

“Are you pulling another double?” Buck asked, pausing just outside the door to the locker room, changed into his civvies and ready to go. Their shift had just ended, and most of them were eager to head home. “Most” being the operant word, because Ravi was standing in the bay in full uniform still.

“Yup,” the younger man said, dashing forward to kiss Albert on the cheek before rushing up to the loft. “I’ll see you when I get off!”

“Okay, Rav,” Albert said, letting out a sigh when his boyfriend was gone.

“How many doubles has he pulled this month?” Buck asked as the pair of them exited the station for the parking lot.

“Too many,” was the answer he got. “He keeps saying it’s for a surprise, whenever I bug him about it. Though I have no idea what the surprise could be…” 

Buck at least knew the surprise wasn’t for a ring, based on how Ravi reacted when he’d asked at the beginning of the month. He was still slightly, and irrationally, insulted on Albert’s behalf.

“Maybe he’s taking you to Disneyland,” he offered as he got to the jeep and unlocked it. Albert’s car was parked just a couple spaces away, right next to Ravi’s. It probably made it easier, logistically speaking, than having one of them stranded at the station without a vehicle. “What are you doing today? Since you’re on your own and all.”

“I dunno,” Albert admitted with a shrug. “Watch TV. Maybe go swimming.”

The apartment building that Albert and Ravi lived in came with a pool, Buck knew. He’d wondered more than once on the morals of visiting the pair just to use it, since it was undoubtedly less crowded than the beaches.

“Deidra’s supposed to come over today for her monthly visit,” Buck said, tossing his work bag into the passenger seat. “But you’re more than welcome to come over and hang out. You actually have a chance at being Gavin’s favorite uncle, since it obviously can’t be me.”

Buck knew that if he hated how far away Maddie and Chimney lived, then Albert couldn’t be fond of it, either. The apartment he and Ravi lived at was past the old Casa de Diaz, meaning that before Chimney and Maddie had moved, the only one that lived farther from the station than them out of their core group had been the Wilsons.

Now, what used to be a ten minute drive for Albert to visit his brother and niece could take an hour or more. Buck wondered how often he actually got to see Jee-Yun these days. He and Ravi weren’t regular guests at the Sunday dinners, after all.

Albert glowered at him.

“Maybe I’ll bribe him, like you bribe Jee,” he said. Buck just shrugged at the accusation. “Yeah, sure, I’ll come over. Better than watching Murder She Wrote and napping on the couch.”

“Try not to get lost,” Buck quipped, before slipping into the jeep’s driver seat and closing the door.

It wasn’t a long drive home from the station, one of the reasons they’d gone with the house that they did. Within fifteen minutes, Buck was pulling into the driveway and Albert was parking along the front curb. He grabbed his work bag and bounded for the front door, getting it open just as the younger man was joining him. 

As always, he was greeted by an excited Hoover. Today, though, he was also greeted by a squealing, wet, and very naked toddler streaking towards him.

“Uh, babe?” Buck called out, dropping the bag and scooping Gavin up before the kid could make a break for freedom. “Did you lose something?”

“He is so fast,” Eddie said, leaning against the doorway between the dining room and the hallway. One arm was wrapped around his middle, and he was holding a towel that had clearly been meant for the wiggling toddler. “Hey Albert.”

“Hi, Eddie,” Albert said, kneeling down to give Hoover his tribute of pets.

“And slippery,” Buck said. There was now a giant wet spot on the front of his shirt, the size and shape of the kid in his arms. “Didn’t want to take a bath?”

“Oh, he didn’t mind the bath,” Eddie said. “He minded having to stay still to be dried, especially when he heard the door.”

“C’mon, little guy, let’s get you dried and dressed.” Buck shifted his hold on Gavin. “Be right back, Al.”

Albert waved him off, content with the dog.

“This one,” Eddie said, reaching out to brush his fingers along Gavin’s curls when he was close enough, “made a mess of himself this morning.”

“Fits right in, then,” Buck said, stepping around his husband and down the hall. Abuela was in Gavin’s room, setting out clothes for the kiddo. “Hola, ‘Buela.”

“Buenos dias, Buckito,” she replied. “How far did he make it?”

“Stopped him at the door,” he told her, setting Gavin down on the changing table and taking the towel from Eddie. “Okay, little guy, let’s get you dry and put some pants on you, and then you can play outside some.”

“He literally just got out of the bath, mi sol,” Eddie pointed out. “Let’s try to last an hour before he needs another one.”

“But playing in the dirt is healthy for him,” Buck retorted, wrapping Gavin up in the towel like a giggling burrito. “How does that look?”

“Perfect.” Eddie didn’t actually roll his eyes, but it was still audible. “Why did Al follow you home today?”

“Ravi’s pulling another double,” Buck explained, rubbing Gavin down. When he lifted the towel, Gavin’s hair was sticking up everywhere. “Now that’s perfect.”

“Has he had breakfast?” Abuela asked, taking the towel from him. Buck glowered at her even as he snatched the fresh diaper from her hand.

“I feed my people, thank you.”

“Mocoso,” was her reply, smacking him on the shoulder with a tiny pair of pants

“Do you hear your bisabuela calling me a brat?” Buck asked Gavin, getting the diaper fastened on. It wasn’t easy, because the kid squirmed about as much as Buck himself did. “Al probably won’t say no to actual bacon if you offered him some. I’m trying to keep the meals at the station kosher for the new guy.”

That seemed to be Abuela’s cue to head for the kitchen, handing Eddie the pants as she went. There was a guest in her house, after all, and she had an excuse to feed them.

“So I should expect a lot of turkey bacon in my future,” Eddie said, passing off the pants before helping keep Gavin distracted and still while Buck got him dressed the rest of the way. “Is this what you’re telling me?”

“It might be.”

Eddie’s whine was a bit overdramatic, in Buck’s opinion.

Deidra arrived for her monthly check-in not much later. Albert was still hanging around, which ended up being very helpful: in his efforts to endear himself as the Cool Uncle, he kept little Gavin out of the way so Buck could do some last minute picking up and even a load of laundry, which always got pushed off until the very last minute.

“What happened across the street?” was the first thing out of Deidra’s mouth when the door opened.

Buck grimaced. He didn’t have to ask what she was referring to: they hadn’t gotten around to taking down what was left of Edward and Shanna’s house, and the charred remains were quite visible through their front window.

“House fire,” he said, stepping aside to let her in.

“Did anyone…? Ah.” Clearly his face told her the answer, because she let the question go. “Hello, Eddie.”

“Hey, Deidra.”

“This is Albert,” Buck said, gesturing to the younger man, who awkwardly waved to the social worker with the hand that wasn’t keeping Gavin in his lap. “My sister’s fiance’s brother. And our coworker.”

“Nice to meet you,” she said. “Where’s your grandmother?”

“Her room, watching one of her shows,” Eddie answered. “Hoover was curled up with her, last I checked.”

“I’ll try to be quiet then. How has little Gavin been?”

“He tried to go streaking down the street this morning,” Buck informed her. “We have an appointment for him next month, to get his next round of shots.”

“Oh, that will be fun for you,” Deidra said, openly laughing at them as she took a small notebook out of her purse to write something down. “He’s gonna scream and cry and hate every moment of it.”

“Looking forward to it.”

“Sounded real convincing there,” Albert taunted from the couch. Buck scowled at him.

“No one asked you, Al,” he sniped, and then turned back to Deidra. “I can get you the info for his doctor, if you want it.

“Sure, I’ll take it for his file.” She tucked her notebook back into her purse and clapped her hands together. “Okay! Let’s do our usual tour of the place, so I can move on to the cases I actually have to worry about…”

“Sunshine! How goes your boring, domestic life this week?”

“Why are you calling me, TK? Aren’t you supposed to be busy enjoying your honeymoon?”

“And we have been. I’m checking in with everyone. Dad sounded kinda weird on the phone, dunno what that was about.”

“And Carlos’ mom?”

“About as well as you could expect. I think I’ve been doing a damn good job, keeping my husband’s mind off it.”

“I’ll bet. Have either of you even left the resort you’re staying at?”

“You know we have! I sent you and your hubby pictures.”

“Yeah, the only pictures you’ve sent me, I got during shift. And the first one I saw was definitely not work appropriate, so I didn’t look at the others.”

“What, why? What’s wrong with the pictures I- Oh. Ohhhh. I didn’t mean to send those.”

“Eds said he got them while he was with Abuela.”

“Oops.”

“How did you even get Carlos to agree to them?”

“He was a bit distracted. And I was really, really convincing… The lighting in our room is nice, isn’t it? We look good.”

“Oh my god, TK.”

When Eddie last got hurt on the job, when he’d been shot, it had been horrible for everyone. It had obviously been horrible for Eddie, being the victim of it all, but it hadn’t been limited to him. Trauma had a splash zone, and much like Eddie’s blood, it had splattered onto everyone around him. It was that thought which led Buck to bring up the idea of having Chris see someone again, a decision which was vindicated when it became obvious just how anxious the kid was becoming about their mortality.

Chris had only seen Dr. Beaumont for a few months, because unlike his fathers, he wasn’t a tangled mess of neuroses. Plus, he didn’t have a job that threw him into the middle of someone’s worst day, multiple times a day. The fear of his fathers never coming home wasn’t lingering over him like a deathshroud, and he had the skills needed to sort through the lingering anxieties when they came up, so the doctor himself made the decision that he wasn’t necessary any longer. At that moment, anyways.

One of the first things Buck did the morning after the bridge collapse was call Dr. Beaumont’s office and make an appointment for Chris. It was something that should have happened following the lightning strike, when Buck actually had died, but that had been a traumatic clusterfuck for all the adults involved. And then they’d been too busy dealing with Buck and Eddie’s mental health to really think about Chris’.

They dropped the ball without even realizing it, and Buck felt more than a little guilty about that.

He and Eddie picked Chris up early from school on Friday, before driving him to Beaumont’s office. Chris’ attitude was… Not reluctant, but like the act was a chore. So pretty much how he viewed any other doctor’s appointment, really.

“He’s doing fine,” Dr. Beaumont told them afterwards, while Chris was busy charming the receptionist. The best word that Buck could think of to describe the doctor had always been “grandfatherly.” “Some of the old anxieties are making their return, and he told me about a couple nightmares, but that’s to be expected after what you’ve gone through in the past, what? Three months?”

“He hasn’t mentioned nightmares to me,” Eddie commented, turning slightly to look at Buck. “Did he say anything to you?”

“Not a word,” Buck said.

“He probably didn’t think they were important enough to trouble you with,” Beaumont said with a shrug. “This isn’t his first rodeo with these fears, and he still has the coping skills from when you were shot, Mr. Diaz. You’ve made sure he has a strong support system, and he knows that he can talk to either of you about anything that’s bothering him.”

“So he’s actually okay?” Buck asked, just to make sure. “He’s not just bottling everything up until he explodes on us?”

It had been a bit horrifying to learn just how much Chris was bottling up, when they first brought him to Dr. Beaumont. It really did feel like they’d only just managed to avoid the kid going nuclear by just a hair’s breadth. Or perhaps screaming at people in a grocery store and then joining a fight club.

“He’s doing fine,” the doctor repeated. “If it would make you feel better, you’re more than welcome to make a follow-up appointment for him to come back. I don’t think it’s necessary, but your insurance will cover it either way. And I have a floor at home that needs refinishing.”

“Thanks, doc,” Eddie said. “We’ll think about it, but don’t write that check just yet.”

“Darn.”

They didn’t go home right away. Gavin was with Abuela and Pepa, no doubt being spoiled rotten, so they were free to go out and do something with Chris. They left it up to him, which meant they ended up getting ice cream. Buck didn’t complain, especially since it meant he didn’t have to worry about Eddie taking it easy with his ribs.

He wondered if that was part of why Chris didn’t choose something like the zoo.

“So, mijo,” Eddie said, digging into his bowl of vanilla ice cream with a spoon. “I hope you’re not missing anything important today.”

“I’m not,” Chris assured him. His own ice cream was so covered in sprinkles that it was hard to make out what flavor he’d gotten originally. “Just English and Spanish.”

“Aren’t you getting a B in Spanish, Mr. Diaz?” Buck asked, smirking at the way both of his boys rolled their eyes.

“It’s Spanish Spanish, Pops,” Chris complained. “It’s different.”

“Yeah Pops, totally different,” Eddie agreed. “And ‘sides, what did you get in it?”

“We’re not talking about me,” Buck said, sniffing haughtily. He hadn’t taken it in school; a foreign language wasn’t necessary to graduate. “Did you get your grade back on your mission model, yet?”

Bobby had helped him with it, along with Michael in a video call, just before the bridge collapse. Buck and Eddie had simply sat back and watched, content to leave it up to the “professionals.” They did, however, give unhelpful comments and bits of advice until the fire captain banished them to their own backyard with the dog.

(Gavin and Abuela, of course, had been allowed to stay.)

“I did,” Chris said, nodding.

“And?” Eddie prompted. He reached over and stole a scoop of Chris’ ice cream. He mostly got sprinkles.

“Dad!” the kid complained, pulling the bowl closer to himself. “I got an A.”

“Good on you, Superman.” Buck reached over and messed his hair. And then, remembering Mrs. Jones’ visit to the station just the other day, he added, “I’m proud of you.”

Christopher beamed at him.

Drowning

Chapter Summary

In which Buck rubs the probie's nose in it, and then needs cheering up.

Chapter Notes

Content warning for the death of a minor.

At the beginning of their shift on Saturday, after Buck was done with their morning briefing and assigning chores, he (not literally) grabbed the probie and (again, not literally) dragged him to Bobby’s office. Wolfe had practically bounced into work that morning, returned from his short suspension and was still riding the high from his bout of off-duty heroics. That bubble was about to be popped, and Buck didn’t feel much guilt about it.

“Is this about Wednesday?” was the first thing out of Wolfe’s mouth when Buck closed the door behind them. “The fire?”

“No,” he said, and watched the probie literally droop at the answer. Had he thought he was being brought in here to be praised? What would Bobby say… “Not that you didn’t do good on Wednesday, because you did. You possibly saved those girls’ lives. You definitely kept them from getting hurt.”

That had the kid perking up again, and he opened his mouth to say something that Buck ruthlessly cut off.

“No, we’re here to talk about what happened before you were suspended,” he said, moving around to stand behind the desk. He gestured to one of the chairs and Wolfe moodily threw himself into it, crossing his arms over his chest and letting out a petulant sounding,

“Oh.”

“This station has always been a home to me,” Buck said, trying to think of what to say to get his point across. “The people I work with, a family. I don’t expect you to feel the same, I know a lot of the others don’t. But we spend entire days stuck together. Sometimes our lives literally depend on one another. It is something that requires trust and respect. It definitely requires that we like or at least tolerate each other.”

“I like the people here,” Wolfe said. “Mostly.”

That last word was said under his breath, and Buck was almost certain he wasn’t supposed to hear it.

“Do you think your co-workers like you?” he asked. It might as well have been a literal slap to the face, with the way Wolfe reared his head back, eyes going wide. “I told you on Monday, if there’s a problem with everyone you run into, then the problem is probably you. Half the things that come out of your mouth are rude and disrespectful to those you work with. Ignoring the way that you still can’t be bothered to say Ravi’s last name right, no matter how many times we’ve corrected you, there’s all the times you’ve tried to tell Hen and Clarke and Bosko how to do their jobs. Jobs they’ve been doing for literal years, probie. You don’t do it with anyone else, so I’m thinking it’s because they’re women. 

“Then there are the times like- Like when you asked when I “turned gay.” Or a couple weeks ago when you asked Albert if he knew karate. And if you tried to tell me that it wasn’t because he’s Asian, I’ll call bullshit.”

“Well,” Wolfe started, and Buck already dreaded what was about to come out of his mouth. “Don’t they all-”

“No.” Buck rubbed at the spot right between his eyes, at the top of the bridge of his nose. He let out a sigh and dropped his hand. “I was an asshole when I started, a punk who thought he farted roses. It wasn’t until Pops- until Bobby rubbed my nose in it like a bad puppy and then nearly threw me out on my ass that I got my act together.”

“So, what?” the probie asked, shoulders hunched up around his ears. “This is you rubbing my nose in it?”

“It is, yeah. For what it’s worth, I don’t think you’re trying to be an asshole.” He slid a paper across the desk. “But you’re still being an asshole.”

Wolfe picked the paper up, jaw dropping as he looked back up at Buck.

“Sensitivity training?” He sounded almost offended.

“Yup,” Buck said, nodding. “Because eventually, you’re going to be dangling from a rope and your well-being will depend on the person at the other end. If they don’t like you, if you’ve spent all your time offending and pissing off everyone around you, then…” He trailed off with a shrug. “I think you can do better. I think what they’ll go over at that sensitivity training will help you do better.”

“I don’t have a choice about going, do I?” Wolfe had turned his face away and was now glaring at the wall, jaw jutting out a bit. It was terrifying how much of the kid’s behavior kept reminding Buck of Chris at his most moody. Any moment now, there'll be a phone call saying Wolfe was skipping Science Club and then the kid will be shouting that Buck wasn’t his dad.

“Nope,” he said, popping the p.

A knock on the office door stopped anything else that Wolfe might have wanted to say. When the door opened, it was not anyone that Buck was expecting.

“Chief!” he yelped, standing up straighter. A glare had Wolfe scrambling to his feet, as well.

“Am I interrupting anything?” Williams asked, stepping inside. Very rarely was there a pleasant reason for a battalion chief to be visiting the station, especially in full uniform.

“No, ma’am, we just finished,” Buck said. He looked at Wolfe and nodded to the door. “Dalton, go ahead and get started on your chores while we have time.”

“Yes, sir. Ma’am.” The probie hurried out of the office, leaving the (substitute) captain and the battalion chief alone.

“He looked familiar,” Williams commented, watching him go.

“He’s Deputy Chief Torres’ nephew,” Buck said, and got a silent “ah” in return. “So, uh. What’s brought you to us today, ma’am? Not- Not that we mind having you here. Visit whenever you want.”

The chief let out a sigh and took the seat that Wolfe had just vacated, so he sat down in Bobby’s chair.

“Austin Jones’ funeral,” she said.

“Oh.”

“Oh, indeed.” Williams nodded. “His parents have decided on a funeral with full honors. I know you helped arrange the funeral for Firefighter Delacroix when he passed, so I’m sure you’re at least somewhat aware of some of the details we’re ironing out.”

“I am,” Buck agreed, remembering Red, the retired firefighter he met shortly after he and Eddie got their shit together, but just before the pandemic really hit. “What is his family asking from us, specifically?”

“The use of apparatus,” the chief said. That almost definitely meant the engine. “Honor Guard and pallbearers. His brother and brother-in-law have both volunteered for the latter, but that’s only two people.”

“I’ll talk to everyone, see who all’s interested.” 

Buck imagined there would be more names than there were open spots, especially when he passed word on to Reynolds and Mayfair and the other shifts were added to the pool. There wasn’t much concern about shifts conflicting with the funeral date, either, because the station would doubtlessly be offline for it. Especially if the engine was being requisitioned.

A thought struck him, and he let out a groan that had Williams raising her eyebrow in a silent question.

“I’m gonna have to tell Eddie and them that they can’t volunteer,” he explained, dragging a hand down his face. “Not with their injuries.”

Eddie was barely allowed to lift Gavin, much less help carry out a coffin. Bobby and Chimney were no better.

“Good luck with that.” Williams was smirking at him. “When are they all scheduled to return? Do you know?”

“Hen is next Friday, the same day we get the other ambulance back,” Buck answered, chewing on his lip in thought. “Eddie’s a couple weeks after that, depending on what the doctor says. Chimney isn’t until the end of July or early August, at the earliest. Pops… I’m not sure, they haven’t really said. He wasn’t hurt much more than Eddie was, so I’d think it would be around the same time as him.”

He’d bother them about it at Sunday dinner.

“Those kinds of injuries are harder to walk away from, the older you get,” the chief said, an odd tone to her voice that Buck couldn’t quite place. “But it sounds like you’ll be warming that chair for at least another month.”

“Hopefully not much longer than that,” Buck said. “I’m supposed to go back and finish up at the Leadership Academy, make up for what I missed when I, y’know. Got smited by God.”

Williams snorted out a laugh, and then looked so very guilty about it.

“Diaz,” she said. “Buck. I’m sure that what you would learn there would be very useful to you. And it would look nice and shiny on your resume. But do you honestly think you need it?”

“Um.” Buck blinked at her. “Y- Yes?”

It was insulting, the way the battalion chief rolled her eyes at him.

“Two weeks ago, you were thrown into an impossible situation,” she said. “Nearly your entire team was struck down and trapped, along with civilians. You rallied and took charge and rescued everyone that could be rescued, long before any other teams were in position to even take stock of the situation.”

“It wasn’t just me,” Buck protested. “I couldn’t have done it at all without Ravi.”

“That’s often the case with good leaders.” She stood and rapped her knuckles on Bobby’s desk, before heading for the door. “I need that list of names within the week, Diaz.”

Battalion Chief Williams was gone before he could say anything.

There were calls that came in that firefighters dreaded. Calls to certain areas of town that they all knew were going to be a pain, just for the types of people that were bound to be involved. Or calls that were as sadly routine as they were tedious, like the jumper call from earlier in the week. But at the very top of the list were the calls that involved children.

They’d barely finished lunch, when the alarm rang, which was the only detail of the call to be thankful about. The house they rushed to was on the corner of 8th and Norton, in Wilshire Park. There was a woman flinging open the wrought iron gate that led into the yard when they pulled up.

“Please, you need to help her!” she sobbed the moment Buck and the others started jumping out. She grabbed him and started dragging him. “Please, over here!”

“Okay, ma’am,” Buck said, going along with the distraught woman. The others were hurrying behind him, Albert and Crawford carrying their medical bags while Bosko and Toby got the gurney out. “You’ve gotta tell us what happened.”

Dispatch hadn’t been able to give them much in the way of details. From the way the woman was acting, he figured he knew why. What they had been told was enough to have them practically sprinting through the yard: there was a child, and there was a pool.

And indeed, when they went around to the back of the house, they found a pool that took up pretty much the entirety of the backyard. Near the edge of it, a man was hunched over a small and unmoving form. He was soaked through and dripping, and was too busy with the motions for CPR to even notice their arrival.

“Ravi,” Buck said, jerking his head at the man. Ravi moved forward and gently peeled the father away, giving Albert and Crawford room to work. “What happened, ma’am?”

“We- We were cleaning,” she said, still gripping tight to his arm from where she dragged him along. “I th- thought she was in her room, napping. She just started to walk, she’s so fast, but she just started to walk…”

“I left the back door open,” the father said. “I didn’t- It was only for a moment. I looked away for only a moment. She was napping, so I didn’t- I didn’t think- I came back out, and I found…”

He didn’t finish the sentence, he didn’t have to. Buck could all too easily imagine the exact scene he came out to find.

Albert looked up at him; he shook his head.

The woman wailed.

Just got back from a bad call

You might want to check on Buck

Eddie stared at those two texts from Ravi, concern bubbling in his stomach. He was in the middle of washing up Gavin, who’d ended up wearing lunch more than eating it, when they came in and so he hadn’t read them immediately. The texts didn’t tell him much. They told him a lot, but they didn’t tell him enough, and it was moments like now that he really hated not going into work with his husband.

It rang for a moment when he called Buck, and then another, before he answered.

“Hey, babe.” His husband sounded subdued, which made Eddie even more concerned. “What’s up? Stanley get you to your group and back okay?”

“He did,” Eddie said. “I was stuck at the library for longer than I’d like, ‘cause he’s a talker. What… What are you doing? You’re not on a call. Doesn’t sound like you’re with the others.”

Normally, he’d be able to hear the sounds of activity in the background, the noise of their coworkers going about their work. Right now, all he could hear was the clicking of a computer mouse and the tapping of a keyboard.

“Took us offline for a bit,” Buck said, sounding distracted. “I’m in Bobby’s office, looking up swimming lessons for Gavin.”

“We have time. He just started walking.”

“We should have done it months ago.”

The words were spoken harshly, borderline angry. Eddie might not have gotten magical math powers from a bolt of lightning, but he was capable of elementary level arithmetic. Ravi said they’d gotten back from a bad call, and now Buck was upset and focused on Gavin learning how to swim. It was an easy picture to put together.

Too bad it wasn’t a pretty picture.

“Mi sol, what happened?”

Buck’s sigh was heavy.

“There was a kid, there was a pool, there were parents not paying attention.”

Not a pretty picture, indeed.

“I’m pretty sure they do lessons at the Y,” Eddie said, helping the only way he could at that moment. “We’d just have to work it around our schedules..”

“Oh, yeah, that… That could work.”

“I’ll look while you’re at work.”

“Speaking of work, there’s an incident report that I’m putting off doing.”

“You’d best get to it, then. Before you forget,” Eddie said. “Love you.”

“Love you, too, babe.”

Eddie stared at his phone for a moment after the call ended, not content with how it went. His husband was upset, and he wanted to do more than help look up swimming lessons. Gavin made a noise beside him, where he was happily playing with Bun the stuffed bunny in the middle of his floor.

“That could work,” he thought aloud as an idea came to him, and then crossed the hallway to Chris’ door. He knocked and waited for him to answer before popping his head in. “Hey, mijo.”

“Hi, Dad.”

“So your pops sounded pretty upset at work, when I talked to him just now,” he said. “You wanna help me cheer him up?”

Chris thought about it for no more than a second before he was nodding.

“Sure.”

“Good man,” Eddie said, before going off in search of Carla, since he wasn’t supposed to be driving yet.

There was a knock and Buck looked up to see Ravi standing in the open door to Bobby’s office.

“You have visitors, Cap.”

“Visitors?” Buck wondered aloud as he got up to follow him into the bay. “Is Chief Williams back?”

“Uh, no, it’s not her.”

The question of who it was got answered when he turned the corner on the engine and spotted them. He practically bounded forward, picking Chris up in a bear hug to swing him around. When he put him down, he gladly took Gavin out of Carla’s arms.

“What are you guys doing here?”

“You sounded upset on the phone,” Eddie explained, and Buck couldn’t resist leaning in to kiss him as deeply as he could, with a wiggling toddler squeezed between them. There was a pair of wolf whistles behind him, no doubt Albert and Ravi.

“Your man asked me to drive them all over, since Isabel’s out with Pepa,” Carla said, and then peered around him at the gathering crowd. “I see some new faces from the last time I was here.”

“Oh, right!” Buck stepped back, letting everyone get a better view of his family. “ Carla, this is Crawford, they’re filling in from B-shift. Dalton there’s the probie, and this is Toby. Toby, this is Eddie. The other Diaz.”

“Nice to meet you,” Toby said, shaking his hand.

“This is Chris, the cooler Diaz,” Buck said, pulling him into his side. “And this little guy is Gavin. Can you say hi to everyone, buddy?”

Gavin hid his face in Buck’s shoulder, instead.

“Hey, Diaz,” Bosko chimed in, when the introductions were done and everyone was done cooing over the suddenly shy toddler.

“Bosko,” Eddie said, nodding to her. He broke into a grin. “How’s your cat?”

Buck blinked at him.

“You have a cat?” he asked her.

“Nope,” Bosko said, smiling while she shook her head.

Buck looked at her, and then at Eddie, and decided he had better things to focus on than whatever that was about.

“C’mon, let’s head upstairs,” he said, leading the way. “Chris, I bet you can beat Ravi’s score on Mario Kart…”

It's the Implications

Chapter Summary

In which Buck catches some flack.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“Okay, so be honest,” Eddie said, leaning forward a bit in his seat, as far as he could without his ribs making their dislike of it known. He and Buck were curled on one of the couches, watching Chris and Ravi play an extremely bloodthirsty match of Mario Kart while everyone else gathered around to coo at Gavin. The tot had slowly thawed to all the strangers, and was more than willing to take his proper place at the center of everyone’s attention. “How has it been with him in charge?”

There was a moment of silence, broken only by the soft smack of Buck’s hand against his husband’s arm.

“It’s been awful, Eddie,” Ravi said, not even looking away from the screen. “He threatened me with bathroom duty after chili night.”

“So awful,” Crawford agreed, the others chiming in as well with barely concealed laughter in their voices.

“I don’t know who thought it was a good idea.” Albert looked Buck straight in the eye as he said it, smirking all the while. “I think Bobby should’ve been checked for a concussion.”

“It’s been okay,” Toby said, the only one among them that would be spared when the time came. “Mostly.”

Okay, nevermind. They were all dead to him, now.

“Mostly?” Buck repeated. “What do you mean, mostly?”

“Um, well, at the store…”

“He probably means the clipboard. We all think you’re a dictator with it,” Ravi said. He looked away from the screen, one of their avatars celebrating their victory, and visibly cowered before whatever expression Buck was making at him. “I- I mean, you’re just very- Very, uh…”

“Spirited,” Albert offered. It was probably meant to be whispered, but everyone was so quiet as they watched Ravi try to climb out of the hole he dug for himself that it was perfectly audible.

“Spirited! You’re very spirited when you have a clipboard!”

“No, no, I think you were right the first time,” Eddie said, turning his head to grin at Buck. “Give him a clipboard and it’s like we’re living through the Porfiriato. You should have seen him when we were getting things ready for Gavin’s birthday. Or Jee-Yun’s.”

“My niece,” Buck automatically provided for both Toby and Bosko, without breaking the glare he had set on Eddie. “You weren’t complaining at the time.”

“‘Course not, mi sol. I think it’s cute.”

The was the sound of mock vomiting from the double act of Albert and Ravi, which was rightfully ignored.

“I think you’re doing an awesome job, Cap,” Wolfe said, ignoring the incredulous looks that everyone gave him.

“I know you’re just kissing my-” Buck cut himself off with a glance at Chris. “You’re just kissing up to me-”

“Good save,” Carla slyly commented from where she was seated.

“-And it’s good instincts, probie,” he continued, ignoring her. “I approve.”

Ravi grumbled something that Buck didn’t quite hear, though he could make out something about a chainsaw. Whatever it was, it had Chris and Albert both laughing. Buck just rolled his eyes and ignored the hyenas.

Eventually, most of the others drifted off to either get started or finish their chores, get in their required exercise time, or just because they had better things to do than ogle their (temporary) boss’s kids. Not that Buck could begin to guess what could be more important than his kids, but to each their own. The only ones that lingered were Bosko and Toby, the former glancing between Buck and Eddie, and the latter having pulled out a book and was opening it up to read when Chris offered him the other controller.

“Sorry, can’t,” the driver-engineer said, and actually sounded rather apologetic about it when Chris pouted in disappointment. Though he cheered up when Carla reached out her hand to take the controller.

“Wait,” Buck said, settling Gavin in Eddie’s lap - the kid was absolutely not allowed to be free until he was back down stairs - to lean forward. “You can’t even play video games?”

“It counts as manipulating electricity,” Toby explained with a shrug.

“Huh. And that’s… Bad?”

Toby’s answer was to just shrug again, before settling in with his book.

“What did you do to the probie?” Lena asked, head turned as she watched the young man in question cross the bay. “He’s been less… Obnoxious today.”

“Oh, uh. I rubbed his nose in the fact that he’s been an as- Er.” He leaned down and smiled at Gavin. “That he’s not been a very pleasant person to work with, and that he wasn’t making any friends.”

“Rubbed his nose in it?” Eddie sounded amused as he asked.

“I might not have been very nice when I was laying it all out,” Buck admitted, sheepishly smiling up at him. “But I think I got through to him.”

“So you can be the bad cop if you want. Why can’t you do it at home?”

“I can’t be the bad cop at home, babe. I’m the cool dad.”

“Okay, so how did this happen, exactly?” Bosko broke in to ask, even as Eddie opened his mouth to shoot back at his husband. “When I transferred back to the One Thirty-Six, the two of you were, uh. Not in a friendly place.”

The question has Toby peeking up from his book as he looked at them in clear interest. He was clearly remembering the story of their grocery store spat that Bosko had so kindly shared with everyone.

“Therapy,” was Eddie’s short and simple answer.

“Therapy,” Bosko repeated dully.

“Yup, therapy,” he said. “Turns out, that touchy-feely place was actually what I needed.”

“That, plus some mistletoe and Christmas magic,” Buck added, feeling smug. Which he thought was entirely fair. “Oh! Wait, I think you helped, too.”

Bosko stared at him, before finally asking,

“How?”

“Well, it was your incredibly stupid idea-”

“I didn’t know that he would-”

“-that led to him being sent to Frank in the first place,” Buck said, talking over Bosko. Eddie just ducked his head, avoiding the too-knowing look that Carla shot him and the curious one that Toby was wearing. “I mean, clearly Frank and I were the real MVPs, but you helped.”

“Thanks,” Bosko drawled.

“You’re-” welcome was what Buck started to say, but the station’s alarm chose that moment to go off, joined almost immediately by Gavin’s upset cries. It seemed that their reprieve had ended without Buck realizing, and now Dispatch was once again sending calls their way.

Everyone started scrambling for the trucks while the details of the emergency were rattled off overhead. Buck jumped to his feet on instinct, though he hesitated before he took a single step. His kid was there crying, after all, and everything in him screamed to make it better.

“Go,” Eddie urged, smacking him on the rump even as his other hand rubbed the toddler’s back. “Do your job, capitán. ”

Buck sighed and nodded.

“I’ll see you guys at home!” he yelled over his shoulder, and joined the others at the trucks.

“It was very loud, I know,” Eddie whispered to the hiccuping toddler in his lap. He was half-turned, so he could watch the trucks peeling out the bay doors. “We’ll go home after the coast is clear, and you can play with Hoover.”

“Need me to take him?” Carla asked. The television was turned off, and Chris was gathering up his crutches from where he had them leaning against the coffee table. 

“I’ve got him,” Eddie insisted, his stubbornness in full force as he got himself to his feet while he held Gavin. He did his best to hide the wince of pain that the maneuvering caused, and he hoped Carla didn’t notice.

It was a hope that was dashed to pieces when, shortly after setting Gavin down to sprint off after the dog, Carla shoved a glass of water into his hands along with some of his prescribed painkillers and glared until he took them.

Sunday dinner was a tradition that someone would have to take out of Buck’s cold, dead hands before he ever gave it up. Sure, it didn’t happen every Sunday, since they didn’t exactly have a Monday to Friday, nine to five job. But it was something they tried to do every Sunday they could, and they almost always managed.

This wasn’t the first Sunday since the bridge collapse, it was actually the second, but it was the first Sunday that Buck wasn’t working. Technically speaking, there had been nothing stopping everyone else from getting together on the Sunday before. Other than the fact that Buck probably would’ve cried about it, at least.

And the fact that it would’ve been a physical hardship for some of the others to actually show up. Just like it almost was this time.

“Oh, Pops.” The words fell out of his lips unbidden when he saw Bobby coming up the drive. Shuffling up the drive was perhaps more apt, with both Athena and May trailing close behind him, the two women looking as if they half-expected to need to catch him at a moment’s notice.

“I’m fine, Buck,” the fire captain insisted, even as he paused at the porch steps. His expression turned determined and he grabbed the railing to heave himself up one painful looking step at a time. A glare had Buck refraining from rushing forward to help him. “I’m up and walking after part of a bridge fell on me, I’m fine.”

“It’s his back,” Athena explained, sticking close to her husband.

A long time ago, back when Bobby was still a firefighter in St. Paul, Minnesota, he hurt his back on the job. It was the start of all his troubles, honestly, the fallen domino that led to his family’s deaths. A lot of addictions, Buck knew, started as attempts to self-medicate. Bobby’s started as a way to cope with the chronic pain that the injury caused him.

Buck stepped out of the way, holding the front door open to allow Bobby and Athena to pass. May paused just inside the doorway and heaved out a sigh.

“He’s been like this the entire time,” she said, voice pitched low. “That man is so stubborn, I swear.”

“Did the doctors give him anything?” Buck asked her, closing the door.

“Oh, sure, but you should really be asking if he’s taking any of it.” May rolled her eyes. “We made sure it’s nothing like he was on before-” Before being, of course, when he first hurt his back. “-but he still throws a fuss whenever it’s time for a dose.”

“Sit your ass down, Robert Nash,” Athena snapped. The pair of them peered through the dining room, spotting her in the kitchen. She’d dragged one of the bar stools around and was pointing at it as she glared at her husband. Her husband who, foolishly, opened his mouth to argue. “I said sit!”

Bobby sat down with a scowl, dragging the stool forward a little so he was closer to the counter that Abuela was laying ingredients on.

“Ma did say she was tempted to smother him with a pillow,” Buck said, straightening up from where he’d instinctively started sitting down at the sergeant’s words.

“It’s been bad,” May said. “Nice hair, by the way.”

“I know you’re being sarcastic, but thank you. Have you got your final grades, yet?”

“Passed everything. Maybe not with flying colors, but I passed.”

“And already, you’re doing better than me.”

Buck went to the backyard, crossing through the dining room and the kitchen and the utility room, to where everyone else was gathered. Albert - because Ravi was working another double - was being chased around by Gavin, Chris, and the dog while Eddie and Pepa watched from the comfort of their patio chairs.

“Ma and Pops are here with May,” he informed the adults.

“Oh, we heard,” Pepa said, sounding amused. “Your captain being stubborn?”

“Like father, like son,” Eddie commented, making his aunt laugh. He finally looked up, the smile he was wearing slipping away. “Hey, what’s wrong?”

“Nothing, just… Pops is really hurting.”

“He had part of a bridge fall on him,” his husband pointed out. “Of course he’s hurting.”

“I know,” Buck said. “It’s just that last time- Christ. When he got caught in the ceiling collapse at the dispatch fire last year, he was fine. Sore, but fine. This time, he’s…”

Eddie reached up and gripped his wrist, giving it a comforting squeeze.

“He had part of a bridge fall on him,” he reiterated. “After he fell off it. Of course he’s hurting, mi sol, but he’s fine. Fine enough to be driving Athena spare, at least.”

“And he’s getting older,” Pepa said. “It’s harder to walk away from things like that, when you get to our age.”

“I guess.” It was the same thing Chief Williams had said, more or less. It wasn’t very comforting.

“Go, cook us food,” Eddie said, giving him a light push. “I’ll bring the kids in after Gavin’s run off a bit more energy.”

“Alright.” Buck leaned down, pressed a kiss to Eddie’s forehead, and went back inside.

Bobby and Abuela were well in the middle of preparing dinner, while May and Athena watched them from the breakfast bar. Abuela moved about the kitchen, handing Bobby what he needed and then taking away what he was done with. Buck grabbed an apron off its hook and joined them after he tied it on.

“So, Buck,” Bobby said, pausing in the middle of chopping up vegetables. “How’s it been at the station?”

“Weird,” he admitted. “It’s been weird. I had to tell the Eiffel Tower story while Ravi did the Maneuver. And people actually do what I tell them.”

“You’ll get used to it,” Bobby said, laughing. “How’s, uh. How’s the new guy?”

“Toby’s okay. No one’s given him a hard time, which is good,” Buck said. “Oh, we’ll have to change some of the things we cook at the station for him, though.”

“That’s fine.”

“And I think I’ve actually got the probie to behave.”

Bobby tilted his head back to give Buck a quizzical stare.

“How so?” he asked.

“Well, I suspended him for a shift, and then I signed him up for sensitivity training,” Buck explained. “And uh. I may have also, possibly, implied that if he didn’t stop being an asshole that his coworkers might just let him die?”

The kitchen was filled with a heavy, dumbfounded kitchen, broken only by the sound of water slowly coming to a boil. And then there was a chorus of,

“Buck!”

“¿Qué demonios te pasa?” Abuela demanded, smacking him with the spoon she was holding.

“I don’t need that translated,” Athena said, shaking her head at him. “Boy, what were you thinking?”

“Hey, it worked!” Buck defended himself. “He didn’t make one offensive comment yesterday!”

“What, exactly, did you tell him?” Bobby said, the familiar tone of disappointment to his words.

“Just that, y’know, he’ll end up dangling from a rope and if he’s offended and pissed off everyone, then…”

“Then, what?” May sounded far too amused. “You told him that they’d just let him drop?”

“Implied. I implied it.”

“That’s a way to handle it, I guess,” Bobby said with a shake of his head. “Maybe not the way I would have gone about it, but you’re the captain.”

“Oh!” Buck snapped his fingers as he remembered. “When Chief Williams came by the station-”

“When did she come by the station?” Bobby interrupted to ask. “Why?”

“Yesterday morning, asking for names of people that wanted to be the honor guard and pallbearers at Jones’ funeral.”

“Unless the funeral is two months from now, you’re not doing it,” Athena said, cutting off her husband before he could even start. “What date did they settle on? You thought later in the month, but it’s later in the month and…”

“Second Friday in June,” Buck told her. “Anyway, she was asking about everyone. I couldn’t tell her when you were supposed to be back since, y’know. You haven’t told me anything.”

“Oh, uh.” Bobby shared a look with Athena. Buck did not like that look. “Not really sure, yet. I have a doctor’s appointment, we’ll talk about it then.”

“Okay,” Buck said, drawing the word out. May seemed just as lost as he was, which was not comforting. “You- You are coming back, right?”

“That is the plan,” Bobby said with a firm nod.

There was a light rapping at the front door, the kind made by a small fist. May got up to answer it, and Buck turned back to the task of fixing dinner to the sound of an excited Jee-Yun letting out a squeal.

And he did his best to squash down the anxiety squirming in his gut.

Chapter End Notes

When I looked up possible treatments for chronic back pain, meditation was one of the things I got. And Bobby is suddenly leading meditation sessions at the end of the finale.

It wasn't intentional on my part, but I ain't complaining.

Yappin' Away

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie have their twice-monthly therapy appointment, and there's another house fire

“So.” Dr. Copeland leaned back in the armchair she was sitting in and eyed the both of them up. “Another day, another near death experience.”

Twice a month, Buck and Eddie marched into the good doctor’s office. There wasn’t much regularity to their appointment times, since they had to work around their shifting hours at the station. This one had actually been rescheduled, thanks to the aforementioned latest near death experience. Buck figured, and Copeland agreed, that there wasn’t much point to being there if Eddie was inevitably going to sleep through it.

But Eddie had stopped sleeping so much after changing to the weaker pain meds, and he was healing, and so here they were.

“We’re keeping you employed, Doc,” Buck joked, grabbing his favorite of the various throw pillows that populated Copeland’s gold-colored couch, the faux fur one that reminded him of cotton candy. “Or at the very least, we’re keeping your bank account padded.”

“Technically speaking, it’s the LAFD’s money,” Copeland reminded him, since their insurance (mostly) covered their visit. “Though it was nice that I could get a new porch and not worry about the cost.”

“I hope you put a plaque on it,” Eddie said, adjusting how he sat on the couch until his face wasn’t scrunched up in a poorly-hidden grimace of pain. “‘Paid for by the Diaz Trauma Fund,’ or something.”

“I’ll think about it.” She sounded amused, at least. “Now, should we talk about your latest brush with death? Or is there anything else you’d like to discuss today?”

“There’s so much to choose from,” Buck said, with a bit of a groan. “I mean, there’s our neighbor’s house burning down-”

“Which neighbors?” Copeland interrupted to ask.

“The homophobic ones,” Eddie answered, still bitter about the brats and their parents. “Edward and Shanna.”

“Was everyone okay?” she asked. Buck and Eddie shared a look, one that apparently told the doctor everything, because she let out a simple, “Ah. So there’s that…”

“Plus Jones’ funeral coming up,” Buck continued. “Pops is hurt. Chimney is hurt.”

“He lost a kid on Saturday and started looking up swimming lessons for Gavin,” Eddie said.

“And I’m playing captain until Pops is back,” Buck finished and then perked up, literally bouncing in his seat. “Oh! But Maddie and Chimney are finally getting married!”

“A lot of things to cover,” Copeland commented, tapping her pen on her notepad. “Most of it, I’m guessing, ties into the bridge collapse that was all over the news. Do you want to start there? Or with your neighbors? Or would you prefer to talk about something lighter, like your sister’s nuptials?”

“You say that as if you won’t make us talk about them eventually, anyways.”

The doctor merely shrugged at the accusation, because there was no point in denying the truth.

Buck glanced at Eddie, who looked back and heaved out a sigh.

“I spent most of the bridge collapse being crushed by a fridge in a camper van,” Eddie said. “So I can’t tell you much about that one. But the neighbor’s house burnt the night before. We were on the couch, I was about to fall asleep, when Buck nearly threw me to the floor to get to the door…”

Buck made a point of arriving about half an hour early for his shifts, these days. It was time that allowed him to talk to the out-going captain and hear about anything he needed to know. There wasn’t often much, just a brief overview of how the previous shift had gone and what kind of calls they went on, so he knew what - if anything - they were running low on. There was a book, tucked away with the station’s cookbooks of all things, that they wrote those kinds of things down in, but it was always good to hear it from the captain in person.

The others would start trailing in some time after he did, stopping by the locker room to change into their uniforms if they weren’t already wearing them. They generally came in with plenty of time to spare. Most of them, anyway.

“You’re cutting it rather close, probie!” Buck called out when he spotted Wolfe rushing into the station. The others were gathered around for the morning briefing, held on the bay floor today, so the probie’s tardiness had been very conspicuous. And he was tardy, their shift officially started a full minute before.

“Sorry, Cap!” the kid yelled back, ducking into the locker room. Through the glass, Buck could see him hurrying to get changed. Which brought his thoughts back to a question he’d been pondering ever since being given the role of substitute captain.

That question being: Did he have the power, at the moment, to get the locker room remodeled and those glass walls replaced with something that wasn’t quite so voyeuristic? Maybe if he had Toby or Ortiz “accidentally” back the trucks into it…

Anyways.

“Okay, guys and gals and Crawford,” Buck said, clapping his hands together to get everyone’s attention. “First thing’s first: We have two ambulances again!”

He threw out his arm, showcasing the second bus that had been waiting in the station bay when he arrived. The others oo’d and aw’d and clapped as appropriate.

“It has a new windshield, and it’s currently blood free!” Buck said. “Couldn’t convince them to put in heated seats, though. Sorry.”

Albert snapped his fingers and let out a mock-disappointed, “Darn.”

“Anyways, that’s… Pretty much all there was to say today,” Buck said, glancing down at his notes. Half the page was taken up by ‘AMBO BACK’ in big letters. “There’s a problem with some traffic lights on Washington heading into downtown, so we’ll try to avoid going that way. Uh, B-shift is supposed to clean out the fridge tomorrow, so you all have until C-shift does it for them on Thursday-”

“Hey!” Crawford complained.

“To rescue anything that you don’t want to be tossed into the trash,” he continued, ignoring the paramedic. “And that’s it. So, go away and-”

The shrill cry of the alarm interrupted him, dispatch spitting out details about a fire. The probie was already in his turn-out pants before the others even got to their equipment cages, and Buck was impressed by his eagerness. But before long, they were all piled into the trucks and peeling out of the station, sirens wailing.

It was another structure fire that they were being called to, near Los Angeles High School. With how early in the day it was, Toby had to maneuver the engine around school buses heading to drop kids off before they could pull up to the house that was burning. There wasn’t a gaggle of neighbors crowding the sidewalks to get a glimpse of things, Buck figured they were all off to work or taking kids to school, but there was a car pulled over with its driver hanging half out of her window, phone pressed to her ear.

It was a small house, squarish with a red roof that peaked up sharply and a stone and brick chimney attached to the north side. The fire, from what Buck could see, had started somewhere in the back corner. Smoke was billowing up in great plumes, and there was the orange flickering of fire visible around the edges and in the windows.

“Get started hooking those hoses up,” Buck ordered as he hopped out. He made his way to the caller, the only person around who could give them any more information. “Ma’am, do you know if anyone’s home?”

“N- No, I don’t live here,” she said. “I was on my way to the school, when I spotted the smoke.”

“Okay, thank you.” 

The driveway was empty, so maybe the house was empty, too. Maybe whoever lived here was currently going about their day, completely oblivious to what was happening to their home. It was a fair assumption to make. Except, Abuela had stopped driving herself around long before she moved in with Buck and Eddie. Her driveway had remained empty even when she was home, and he knew it was the same for countless other people in the city.

“Bosko, Wolfe!” Buck called out, getting their attention. “Gear up. You’re heading in to make sure there’s no one stuck inside.”

“Got it, Cap!” the probie said, already yanking open the compartment in the engine that had the oxygen tanks. The kid had a go-get-’em attitude today. Maybe Buck should be mean to people more often. But also, maybe Wolfe was too eager.

“When I say to go in!” Buck shouted at him. “Ortiz, Clarke, start venting that roof. Ravi, when they’re ready, I want those hoses hitting the fire from the side. Keep it from spreading to the neighbors.”

They didn’t know for sure if someone was trapped inside, so he was going to put his people's actual safety over the perceived safety of any hypothetical victims. Venting the roof allowed for the trapped smoke and gasses to escape upwards, along with the heat. It took some of the pressure off, and prevented things from literally blowing up in their face when they opened the door. And it also allowed for the water to reach more of the nooks and crannies that embers could be hiding in.

But then again, they didn’t know for sure if someone wasn’t trapped inside. Time might be at a premium. Decisions, decisions…

Buck eyed the fire, eating away at the back corner of the house. He could see the flames, so it was already feeding on oxygen from outside. And the front door seemed to be far enough away that it shouldn’t…

“Okay, go in,” he said, and then grabbed the probie’s shoulder when he started rushing off before Buck was even done speaking. “Be quick. When I tell you time’s up, you both get out.”

“We’ve got it,” Lena said, slapping him on the shoulder as she and Wolfe jogged to the house. She didn’t even bother checking if the front door was locked, insteading jamming a Halligan into the latch and viciously tearing at it.

The door swung open with a shower of splinters and a curtain of smoke, but there was no flashover. Buck let out the breath he hadn’t realized he was holding as he watched the pair duck inside, before turning his attention to the rest of the organized chaos around him.

Ortiz had the aerial up, and he and Clarke were sawing a hole through the roof just past the front door, while a couple of the others were poking at spots with the hooks. Ravi had one hose hitting the fire from the side, as Buck requested. Toby had another soaking the area around it, keeping it too wet for things to spread. Along the ground, at least.

“Toby,” Buck called, pointing at where the branches of a nearby tree were starting to catch. The driver-engineer nodded and adjusted the hose, spraying them before it could go farther.

He watched a bit longer, before deciding that they had it under control and that micromanaging didn’t help anyone. So he meandered over to the ambulance, where Albert and Crawford had gotten things prepped in the event that there was someone in the house that needed their attention. At the moment, though, it looked more like they were propping up the vehicle.

“I feel like this reflects poorly on my leadership,” he joked.

“That’s a you problem,” Crawford quipped, leaning against the ambulance door. They’d taken their turn-out coat off and left it folded up on the bumper.

Buck opened his mouth to say something snarky and sarcastic, but his radio crackling to life interrupted him.

“Cap, we’re comin’ out,” Lena said. “We’ve, uh, got patients. Hey!”

“How are they?” Buck radioed back, frowning at the way she cut off.

“Uh, not sure,” Wolfe answered, and in the background Buck was certain he could hear Lena snapping something that sounds like,

“Stop that, you little rat!”

  “Uh, okay, we’ll check them over when you get out here with ‘em,” Buck said, thumbing off his radio. “Did she say something about a rat?”

Albert and Crawford shrugged at him, even as they tugged on disposable gloves and got the oxygen masks ready. A few moments later, the pair of Lena and Wolfe made a hurried exit out the front door. And in their arms, wiggling and yapping, were a pair of fluffy pomeranians.

“Oh, hell no,” Crawford said, shaking their head when Lena and Wolfe got to the ambulance. “I do not do yap dogs.”

“That sounds like a you problem,” Buck shot at them, smiling brightly at their scowl.

“Well someone’s taking it,” Lena snapped. “It’s been trying to gnaw through my arm the entire time I’ve had it.”

True to her words, the little dog that she was holding had the sleeve of Lena’s coat in its little knife filled mouth, growling and snarling the entire time. The one Wolfe held wasn’t doing much better.

“Uh, Buck?” Albert asked, cringing away from the pair of tiny terrors. “What do we do…?”

“I think,” Buck said, nodding to himself, “that you four have got this handled.”

There was a chorus of “What?” and “No!” and “Get back here!” as he walked off, delegating that particular problem to them. Now that he was certain there was no one in the house itself, he could tell Ravi and Toby to spray it from the top with the intent of drowning the fire out. Ortiz and the others had progressed while he wasn’t looking, and now the roof was pockmarked with square holes.

“Dispatch, Captain One Eighteen,” he said, thumbing at his radio. “Any word on if anyone’s reached the owners of this house? And uh. Send animal control…”

It was mid evening, now. They’d eaten dinner, Toby and Albert had cleaned the dishes, and now they were all settling in for either a quick nap before the bell rang again or to just relax. Buck himself was sitting at one of the tables, a mostly blank notebook open in front of him and pen in hand. His phone was laying beside it and he had earbuds in.

Before the lightning strike, when he’d been taking the Leadership Academy classes that the department offered, he’d had the damnest of times keeping his attention on the lectures in order to take proper notes. Whether it was in the classroom, or at home with the recordings of them on his phone, his attention just always wanted to wander off.

That had been the biggest of his problems, growing up. If something wasn’t interesting enough to snag his attention and drag him through fifty rabbit holes in a single night, he had trouble keeping his focus on it. He’d learned to cope with it, and thankfully firefighting was a job that required movement and action, and the classroom portions of the fire academy were interesting enough that it kept his attention hostage.

But the leadership courses had been a different beast entirely, far too similar to college and grade school before it. He sat, he listened to that week’s speaker drone on and on, and then they broke up into groups for a quick little project and discussion that was only tolerable because Buck could socialize a little. It had been bad enough that Eddie took on a second full-time job of helping him focus, much to Buck’s embarrassment.

It was also one of the things that finally pushed him - or rather, pushed Copeland into pushing him - into actually making the diagnosis of ADHD official, rather than just presumptive. The bupropion he was on was an antidepressant, but it was also used off-label for ADHD, and so Dr. Weaver, whom Copeland had referred him to after his confessions of darker thoughts, had prescribed it as a two birds, one stone kinda strategy.

It was helping, on both fronts.

That voice at the back of Buck’s head, the one that was always certain that his place in his family was flimsy and precarious, that he was always a single moment away from being pushed out and replaced, had gone silent. Mostly. It whispered still, on occasion, but it was easier than ever to ignore.
