And today it was helping with the other thing. He’d pulled up the recorded lectures for the first time since he started his medication and sat down to take notes. Maybe it wasn’t the most fascinating of listening, and he quickly grew bored, but he still managed to sit there and to listen and to take better notes that he had three, four months ago. Plus, his attention wasn’t wandering like those pomeranians they’d rescued earlier in the day.

In fact, he managed to keep his attention on note-taking to the point that, when he looked up to find Dalton Wolfe sitting across from him, he was a bit startled.

“‘Sup, probie?” he asked, pausing the audio and taking out his earbuds.

“What are you doing?” the kid asked, nodding at the notebook.

“Oh, uh. At the beginning of the year, before I…”

“Died?”

“Yeah, before that. I was going through that leadership academy the department offers. I recorded the lectures, and now I’m redoing the notes.” Buck watched Wolfe’s face scrunch up and chuckled. “Not a fan?”

“I never really liked school,” the probie said with a self-conscious shrug. “I’m not stupid, it was just… Hard, sometimes.”

“I get that,” Buck said, disconnecting his earbuds and putting them away. “I flunked out of college. Twice. Well, the second time I got kicked out because of stupidity.”

“I took the easiest degree LACC offered. But only because mom and Uncle Randy wouldn’t let me apply for the fire academy until I did.”

“Smart of ‘em,” Buck said, hand unconsciously grabbing at his leg. “You never know what might happen.”

His phone rang, Eddie’s name flashing across the screen along with a picture of his sleeping face. Buck grabbed it and ran his thumb across the screen, answering it with a cheerful,

“Hey, babe.”

“Okay,” Eddie said, and there was a lot of noise in the background. “First off, I don’t want you to freak out.”

“Why would I-” He cut himself off as a new noise made itself known in the background. One that he recognized right away. “Is that Gavin? Why is he crying!?”

“I said don’t freak out.”

“Too late, Eddie,” Buck snapped at him, flipping his notebook closed and getting to his feet. “What’s going on?”

His husband sighed.

“Well…”

The Kernel

Chapter Summary

In which a cliffhanger is resolved a week late, woops.

Chapter Notes

...I think this actually counts for Whumptober?

Eddie had not intended on spending Tuesday evening at the ER with a wailing toddler. Then again, he doubted anyone really planned for these sorts of things to happen.

The day had been going well up until then. Or, as well as such a day usually went, when Buck was at work without Eddie. Ever since the lightning strike and the revelations regarding Buck’s mental health that followed it, Eddie worried about his husband even more whenever the man was out of his sight. Not because he didn’t trust Buck’s ability to do the job safely, but more because he wasn’t there to help ensure it.  He wasn’t there to have his husband’s back, and had to sit at home hoping that the others could fill the void.

Anyways, the day had been going well. After Buck went to work and Chris was taken to school, Eddie watched bad daytime television with Abuela and Carla. Then he sat at the dining table to do the breathing exercises, a process that he wouldn’t admit was as agonizing as it was. But the alternative was to risk pneumonia, so the pain would have to be worth it.

Carla didn’t stay long after picking Chris up from school, heading home to her husband. Because it was the second-to-last week of school, the kid didn’t have any homework, so at his request they sat down with a bowl of popcorn and watched a Marvel movie. Eddie wasn’t sure which one specifically, because they’d all started blurring together in his head.

The movie was loud and bright and managed to keep Gavin’s attention for approximately forty-five minutes. Then he apparently got bored and decided to make a mess, smacking the bowl of popcorn off Chris’ lap to send it toppling to the floor. Hoover, living up his name sake, was quick to pounce on the spilled kernels as Eddie went to get the broom and dustpan. There wasn’t much left to sweep up by the time he shooed the dog away, and he needed Chris’ help since bending over was still not an action that his ribs took kindly.

Chris’ interest in family bonding time didn’t last very long past the movie’s end, the pre-teen disappearing into his bedroom once his tolerance for toddler shenanigans ran dry. Eddie couldn’t really blame him, since he remembered barricading his room to keep Adriana and Sophia out. He figured it was a thing that (most) older siblings did, especially with the age difference.

Sometimes he wondered if Maddie had done the same with Buck, retreating behind her bedroom door to escape an ever curious toddler. And then when he remembered that she was practically forced into the role of parent and probably didn’t have the choice of hiding in her room, he found himself wishing curses were real just so he could send a nasty one to Hershey.

Anyways, the movie was over and Chris was in his room. Abuela was in the kitchen, working on getting supper started, and Eddie joined her, because he was actually capable of assisting in the kitchen, thanks. Gavin was in the living room, left alone except for the dog, but Eddie didn’t worry about it because the kid had his toys and there was a cartoon playing on the television, and they could see him and hear him from the kitchen.

In hindsight, that was Eddie’s mistake.

It started with a change in the noises Gavin was making. Before, it had been a constant stream of babbling as he played with the toy firetruck Bobby got him and his stuffed bunny on the floor, interspersed with occasional squeals and giggling. There wasn’t a moment of silence, which was why Eddie wasn’t too worried. Silence meant that Gavin was getting into something, while the constant noise meant he knew where his kid was.

And then, instead of the babble and the squeals and the giggles, Gavin let out an unhappy whine, which was followed by another and then a whimper. By the time the tot was out-and-out crying, and Hoover was howling in sympathy, Eddie had already abandoned the vegetables he was cutting up to see what was wrong. Gavin looked up when he heard him coming, rubbing at his nose with fat tears starting to make their way down his cheeks.

“What’s wrong?” Eddie asked him, kneeling down. He would’ve simply swept the kid up into his arms, but, well. His ribs.

“Dada, ow!” Gavin complained, getting up to toddle over to him. One hand was still rubbing at his nose, and he reached out with the other to grab at Eddie. “Ow! Ow! Dada, ow!”

“You hurt your nose?” Eddie guessed. As gently as he could, he took Gavin’s hand and moved it out of the way so he could see what was going on. There was a small trickle of blood coming out of one of Gavin’s nostrils, a sight that had his heart breaking no matter how minor. “Oh, mijo, okay. Come on.”

It took careful maneuvering to lift Gavin up without his ribs protesting, especially with the unhappy kiddo’s legs flailing. Eddie managed it, though, and carried him into the kitchen with Abuela. It was also the nearest place they kept tissues, and he grabbed one to wipe at his kid’s nosebleed, before pecking a quick kiss on the tip of Gavin’s nose.

Normally, that would have been the end of it. Normally, after some attention and cuddling, Gavin would be bouncing right back and wanting to be free again. Except that wasn’t the case this time, because the kid kept crying and kept rubbing at his nose. Occasionally, there’d be another trickle of blood that needed wiping away.

Eddie was not an idiot and he was a trained medical professional. A trained medical professional that did not panic. Whatever was hurting Gavin, it had something to do with his nose, and it wasn’t a simple whack that caused it. 

Unless it was more than a simple whack. Though Gavin’s nose didn’t look red, except for where he was rubbing at it, and it didn’t look swollen. There was no bruising, nothing to indicate that he hit it hard enough to cause actual injury. But that just meant Eddie was back where he began, with a kid that was hurting and no idea why. And when he poked and prodded, Gavin cried even more. There was noth-

Wait, there was a lump. Eddie could feel a lump, a hard something. Ay dios, what if it was a tumor? How was Eddie going to tell Buck that he let their kid get cancer while his back was turned? Buck would never-

“Maybe he stuck something up there.” Abuela’s voice drew him out from his (definitely not a panic) spiral of thoughts. “Ramon used to do it all the time, el chico tonto.”

“Is that what you did?” Eddie wiped away Gavin's tears with a new tissue, and then tapped at his nose. “Did you put something up here?”

 Gavin nodded, and then let out another wobbly, “Dada, ow.”

“Está bien, okay.”

They kept their fully supplied first aid kit in the hallway bathroom. It was filled with more than a few things that may or may not have come from the station, because that was the benefit of volunteering to do inventory on slow days. Any guilt about that went up in a puff of smoke the first time he saw what was inside Bobby’s first aid kit, which was pretty much the same as theirs.

It was a bit of a juggle, getting the kit out from the cabinet without hitting his head or dropping Gavin, though Eddie managed it. The truly difficult part came when he got the penlight out and tried to get a look at whatever the kid tucked away for safekeeping. It was hard enough, getting Gavin to let go of him to sit on the toilet lid. It was impossible to get the tot to stay still long enough to shine the light where it needed to go.

Eddie attempted, briefly, to go in blind with the forceps they had. But that was an attempt that resulted in abject failure, along with more tears and a fresh trickle of blood that had to be wiped away.

“Perdon, perdon,” Eddie repeated, picking Gavin back up as he made a mental note to pack the kit back up later. “It’ll be okay. Estará bien.”

Abuela was frowning at him from the kitchen, when he returned from the bathroom. The frown deepened farther at Gavin’s continued teary sniffling and Eddie’s shake of his head.

“Whatever it is, I couldn’t get it,” he told her.

“Would you like for me to hold him while you try again?” she asked, but he shook his head again.

“We don’t have the right tools.” Some of Eddie’s frustration leaked into his words, however much he tried not to let it show. The forceps they had were too short, too snub-nosed, to reach and grab hold of whatever Gavin stuffed up there. “I think we’ll have to take him in. I mean, it’s not an emergency, but I don’t like the idea of leaving whatever it is until his doctor’s appointment.”

“Can you drive him?” Abuela asked. “With your ribs?”

“I’m not supposed to,” he admitted. They would make steering and everything else a literal pain, and any sudden movements (such as having to suddenly jerk the wheel because of idiots trying to merge) could make things worse. “But I might have to…”

This wasn’t exactly something worth an ambulance ride, not when that ambulance and the associated paramedics might be needed somewhere far more severe. He could call an uber, but then he’d need to transfer the car seat, base and all, and then take it all out at the hospital so the driver didn’t just take off with it after dropping them off. Pepa and Carla were options, but they were both probably settling down for the night with their own dinners, Carla especially, and with them came the same problem of the car seat.

“Bobby, hola.” Once again, Abuela’s voice tore Eddie out of his thoughts. She had the cordless phone to her ear, having apparently called the fire captain without Eddie noticing. “Is Athena working tonight?... Ay, o-... The little one has something up his nose that Eddito can’t get out… Si, si, mucho gracias.” She set the phone back down on the counter and turned to face him. “Bobby and May will be right over.”

Eddie just blinked at her.

The Grant-Nash residence was a five minute drive away, something Buck had been quite pleased about when they bought their house. This meant that when Abuela said that Bobby and May would be right over, they really were right over. Eddie was just slipping into his sandals, not bothering with his shoes, when one of them started knocking at his door.

“Okay, that’s- I’m guessing that’s May,” he said, grabbing his phone and keys. It could have been Bobby, but he’d been shuffling around like an old man just a couple nights ago, so Eddie doubted it.

“I wanna go with,” Chris insisted. He’d been very concerned when he was told that Gavin was being taken to the ER, regardless of the reason.

“Y’know what, sure.” Letting Chris go with them was easier than arguing. Eddie grabbed the diaper bag, with its ironed on LAFD patches, and slipped it onto the shoulder that Gavin wasn’t clinging to. “Go get- When did you put your shoes on?”

“When you told me what was going on,” Chris said, like it was obvious, before getting the door. “Hi, May.”

“Hey, Chris.” The younger woman was dressed in sweats with her hair pulled up in a messy ponytail. “Ready to go?”

“We need to get the carseat, but yeah,” Eddie said, only to pause when May waved his words away.

“We took Bobby’s truck,” she explained.

Bobby, shortly after the announcement that Buck and Eddie were going through the process of adopting Gavin, had gone out and bought a car seat of his own, just in case. He’d been very proud of it, too, showing it off to everyone afterwards. It had been residing in the backseat of Bobby’s truck ever since.

Gavin did not much care for it now, not when it meant separating from Eddie.

“So what did he jam up there?” Bobby asked from the front passenger seat. “Isabel didn’t say.”

“I have no clue.” Eddie sat the diaper bag on the floor with Chris’ crutches and wished he thought to grab Gavin’s bunny. As it was, he and Chris were doing their best to keep him settled. “I couldn’t get him to sit still enough to see it.”

“Brooke stuck a pea up her nose, once.” Bobby, when Eddie glanced up to look, had a soft smile on his face as he watched them through the rearview mirror. “She was more upset about Marcy and I holding her down to get it out, than she was about having it in there.”

They actually lived fairly close to an ER, all things considered. Unfortunately, it required getting onto the freeway, which was never a pleasant experience. Especially with an upset toddler in the car. Gavin only settled after they parked and Eddie was lifting him out of the carseat. Thankfully the ER lobby wasn’t as crowded as it could have been. It was still noisy, however, and Gavin clung a bit tighter at the sound of other people crying.

There was a grouping of empty seats, not far from the entrance. Bobby spotted it first and pointed at them, saying,

“We’ll take Gavin and sit over there, while you go up and get everything sorted at the desk.”

Gavin, it turned out, was a bit more willing to be separated from Eddie again if it meant he was in his grandpops’ lap. He was still upset, as he had been ever since the whole ordeal started, but it was mostly just sad little sniffles.

There was a lone lady running the in-take desk. She gave Eddie a tired smile when he stepped up.

“Hey, what are you here for?”

“Uh, my son decided to play hide and seek with something up his nose.”

The lady laughed.

“Okay, well, fill this out,” she said, handing him a clipboard with a form stuck to it, along with a pen. “Bring it back and we’ll get to you as soon as we can. Unfortunately, that… Might not be as quick as you’d like.”

Her eyes darted off to the side. When he followed them, he spotted a young man whose arm looked as if it had two elbows. He grimaced. Something jammed up a child’s nose really wasn’t very high on the emergency totem pole.

“Yeah, okay.”

Eddie went back to the others. May, without prompting, scooted over a seat so he could sit next to Bobby, who still had Gavin in his lap. He took out his wallet and phone, so he could find all the insurance info, and started filling everything in.

“Does Buck know?” Bobby asked. Eddie looked up at him.

“Huh?’ he asked, dumbly. And then, “Ah, shi- Shoot, no he doesn’t.”

Eddie had been too busy worrying over Gavin, and then worrying how to get them here, to inform his husband about what was going on. He felt kinda guilty about that, but he figured it could be forgiven.

Buck answered after only a couple rings, so they weren’t on a call.

“Hey, babe,” was his cheerful reply.

“Okay,” Eddie said, pinning his phone between head and shoulder as he went back to filling out the form. “First off, I don’t want you to freak out.”

He caught Bobby’s unimpressed look from the corner of his eye, but he didn’t pay attention to it.

“Why would I-” Buck started to ask, but at just that moment, Gavin had reached up to rub at his nose again, which irritated whatever he had stuck up there and set him off again. “Is that Gavin? Why is he crying!?”

“I said don’t freak out.”

“Too late, Eddie,” Buck snapped at him. “What’s going on?”

“Well…” How to put it. “We’re at the ER be-”

“What!? Okay, hold on, I’ll be right there.”

“What? What do you-? Buck?” Eddie looked at the phone screen, which told him his husband had hung up. He tried calling back, but to no avail. “Mierda.”

“Don’t freak out?” The fact that it was Chris, his baby boy, his oldest son, his pride and joy, passing judgment on his decisions somehow made it worse. “Dad…”

“I know, I know.” Eddie sighed and brought their insurance info back up. “Your pops’ on the way.”

“So should I expect my engine to end up parking itself through those doors?” Bobby sounded amused. He was bouncing Gavin a little, doing his best to settle him back down.

“Does he even know which ER we’re at?” May asked, which Eddie thought was a fair question. Buck had, after all, hung up before he could actually tell him.

“We’ll find out, won’t we?”

They didn’t have to wait long to find out, either. The filled-in form had been turned in and the man with the broken arm had just been called back when the ER doors burst open. Buck came rushing in, radio strapped to his chest, shoes squealing on the waxed floor, head turning this way and that until he spotted them.

“Eddie!” He sprinted over, sliding to a stop when he reached them, wasting no time at all in yanking Gavin out of Bobby’s lap and frantically looking him over. “What’s wrong? What happened? Have they seen him yet, did they say anything? Do you-”

“He stuck something up his nose,” Eddie said, interrupting his husband’s questions. Buck paused, mouth hanging open, before bursting out a laugh.

“Why didn’t you say that!?”

“You hung up before I could, and then you didn’t answer when I tried calling you back.”

“Yeah, I uh, I forgot my phone.” Buck adjusted his hold on Gavin, letting the kid lay his head on his shoulder, and then gave Chris a quick side hug that the preteen allowed without much fuss. “Hi, Pops, May.”

“Did you drive the engine over?” Bobby asked. 

“Nah, took the captain’s truck. It has sirens too. I left Ortiz in charge until I get back or they get sent out.” He patted the radio with his free hand, which would let him know if the 118 was needed on a call. “Your nose hurting you, little guy?”

Gavin nodded and intoned, “Ow.”

“We’ll get it taken care of, don’t worry,” Buck assured him. Eddie watched as Buck ran his fingers through Gavin’s hair, indescribable fondness welling up inside him. “How long have you guys been waiting?”

“Not long,” Bobby told him. “Eddie called you as he was filling out the paperwork.”

“We’ll probably be waiting for a while,” Eddie said. “It’s not exactly a medical emergency.”

“It’s an emergency to me,” Buck declared, as if that hadn’t been obvious.

However much they would prefer otherwise, they were not called back immediately. Two people that were there when Eddie arrived were seen first, and then someone who came in almost as dramatically as Buck, with blood pouring down their face. The last one was a bit comical, with both Buck and Eddie getting to the bleeding man before any of the nurses.

Occasionally, Buck’s radio would crackle to life and the firefighter would tense up, eyes darting between it and Gavin. He would go if he had to, Eddie didn’t doubt that, because Gavin wasn’t actually hurt. But that didn’t mean Buck wanted to leave their kid, upset as he was.

But then, finally,

“Diaz?”

They practically leaped to their feet. Chris went with them, while Bobby and May stayed in the lobby. A nurse led them to a curtained off bed, looking over something on her clipboard as she did.

“So little Gavin has something in his nose?” she asked, not looking at them. “We can take care of that, I’m sure.”

“I couldn’t get it out at home, and I didn’t want to just leave it in there,” Eddie said.

“No, no, of course not, better safe than sorry,” she agreed, pawing through a drawer. She pulled out an otoscope and stuck a new head on it. “Any idea what it is?”

“Couldn’t get a good look.”

“Any bleeding?”

“A little at first,” Eddie said, and saw Buck’s face twist up in concern.

“Okay dads, one of you hold him still while I take a look-see.”

Buck, who had been holding Gavin since he arrived, sat down on the bed with his arm around him. The nurse grabbed a rolling stool and pulled it up so she could sit on it.

“Ah, yeah, I see it.” She turned and dug through the drawer again, this time pulling out a pair of long, thin forceps that bent at a near 90 degree angle. “He isn’t going to like this.”

She was right, he did not. Gavin squirmed and cried and smacked at her, did his general best to get away from what she was doing. Eddie ended up holding his head still so the nurse could stick the forceps in, which seemed much better for the job at hand than the ones they had at home. In a matter of moments, she let out a pleased sound and was pulling out the offending little object, holding it up for them to see.

“Gross,” Chris declared, scrunching his face up.

“An unpopped popcorn kernel?” Buck squinted at the snot-covered thing. Eddie slapped his palm over his face.

“We watched a movie when Chris got home,” he said. “With popcorn, until Gavin knocked it to the floor. I thought I got it all swept up.”

“It’s a good thing you brought him in tonight rather than waiting,” the nurse said, dropping the kernel into the trash, along with the otoscope head. She grabbed a tissue and dabbed at the fresh trickle of blood that removing the kernel caused. “It would have swelled up and really gotten stuck.”

“Is that better, mijo?” Eddie asked, leaning over to press a kiss into Gavin’s hair. The little guy nodded.

“I’ll go get the paperwork so you can get out of here,” the nurse said, getting up. “I’m sure little Gavin wants a nap after such an exciting evening.”

“I should probably head out as well,” Buck said. The reluctance was obvious in both his voice and the way he made no move to get up nor let go of Gavin. “I’ve already pushed my luck as it is. And I might have parked in the fire lane.”

“Ay, dios, mi sol.”

“Hey, I’m a firefighter, I can do that,” Buck protested. “And my husband called, told me not to freak out, and then said he was at the ER with my kid.”

“Dad’s an idiot.”

“Chris!” 

Buck’s attempt at scolding him lost its effect when he was laughing as he did it. At least in Eddie’s opinion.

It's Definitely Nothing

Chapter Summary

In which there are dinner guests.

Chapter Notes

This is late. My update schedule has slipped, despite my best intentions. Oh well.

In the days following the call with the drowned child, Buck had been determined to get Gavin into swimming classes. Sure, at his age he probably wouldn’t actually be swimming, but it would hopefully be better than nothing. Because one kid lying lifeless by a pool had been one too many. Because Buck didn’t want to be the parent breaking down. Because Buck knew, far too intimately, how much the water could take from you, and how quickly.

Eddie’s idea had been to check what the YMCA offered. It had been promising at first, because they did offer lessons specifically for little kiddies just getting their feet wet. But as with so much in their lives, they just didn’t have room in their schedules for it. The lessons offered were only available Saturday mornings, the same time as Chris’ horse therapy and Eddie’s veterans’ group.

Buck had spent some time on Wednesday morning complaining about it as he served up breakfast. Mostly about how he knew all the other options were going to be expensive as hell, and that he’d have to fight with Eddie because the man would immediately volunteer to give up his group time to take their kid to the Y on Saturdays. Something Buck was not about to let him do, especially since he’d have people from the group - Nik and Gerty, at the very least - blowing up his phone to ask if something had happened.

“Why don’t you two just teach him yourselves?” Lena had asked, happily scooping some eggs and turkey bacon onto her plate. “I know that both of you can swim.”

“We don’t have a pool at home.” And they probably never would, now, no matter how much Chris begged. “The public pools are gonna be crowded as shit, and the beach will be a hundred percent worse.”

“Trying to teach him in a crowded pool is just a recipe for disaster,” Crawford agreed. 

“He’s never been in the water before?” Wolfe asked.

“Just bathtime,” Buck answered as he took a seat and waited for the food to make its way back to him. “He does okay with that, until it’s time to wash his hair. The kid does not like water running down his face.”

That had been a real issue at first, with the kid screaming at the top of his lungs while they rinsed his hair and leaving both Buck and Eddie feeling absolutely horrible by the end of it. It was Karen who offered them the solution, coming over one day with an adjustable visor to stick on Gavin’s head during bathtime.

“You could use the one at our place,” Ravi said.

“Yeah, guests are allowed to use it as long as we’re with them,” Albert chimed in.

“That… That could work.” Buck had completely blanked on the fact that the apartment building Albert and Ravi lived in came with a pool. “You guys wouldn’t mind?”

“Oh no, we’ll have to spend a day at the pool.” Ravi’s voice was flat and toneless. “The horror, the humanity. How will Al and I ever manage to survive?”

“Alright, alright, point made.” Buck rolled his eyes. “Oh! Remember guys, B-shift is supposed to clean out the fridge today, so if you have any tupperware or whatever in there, don’t forget to grab it.”

Buck made sure to catch Toby’s eyes as he said it. Toby was still bringing in his own tupperwares of food, though since Buck was making a point of planning their meals with the man in mind, he rarely actually had to eat it. 

Toby nodded, too busy eating to actually say anything.

“Hen’s coming back on Friday, right?” Albert asked. “Are we doing anything?”

“I was just thinking cupcakes,” Buck answered with a shrug. “I mean, it was just a concussion, it wasn’t like she got struck down by God or anything.”

Wolfe was the only one that laughed; the others just shook their heads or rolled their eyes, because he was given zero respect as their captain. Buck was still sometimes hit with the impression that everyone else got offended whenever he made jokes about the lightning strike. It helped, a bit, that Eddie had started making the jokes, too. 

(Copeland suggested that he actually ask them about it, like an adult, rather than run with any assumptions. Buck had not done that.)

Reynolds arrived as they were finishing up breakfast, and Buck gave him the abridged version of how the shift had gone. There wasn’t really much to report, beyond the second ambulance being back in service. Afterwards, he left him with Crawford to catch up and went to gather his things in preparation for leaving. The very end of the shift, where one shift was getting ready to go home and the next was trickling in, was always an awkward limbo. 

Technically speaking, if the alarm went off right now, it was supposed to be A-shift that answered. Since they were still on the clock and all. But in practice, if the shift was almost over, it was usually the guys from the next that went.

Buck was very much eager to get home today. The night before, knowing that something was wrong with Gavin but not knowing what, had been horrific. The fact that it turned out to simply be something - an unpopped popcorn kernel - stuck up his nose had been comedic in hindsight, considering how Buck had flown out of the station, grabbing the keys to the captain’s truck and taking off with sirens blaring.

It had only been when he was already on the road that he realized he hadn’t given Eddie the chance to tell him which ER they were at. And then he realized he had left his phone behind, probably on the table beside his notebook, so he couldn’t even call his husband back and ask. He’d ended up going to the one nearest their house, under the assumption that it was the most likely.

The drive from the station that morning was not at all the same as the night before. For one, it was in the jeep instead of the captain’s truck, and for another, he actually obeyed traffic laws. Mostly.

As always, Buck had a welcoming committee the moment he stepped through his front door. It was perhaps one of the best things about coming home, having Hoover and Gavin both running for him and demanding his attention. He knelt down so he could wrap an arm around the toddler and pet the dog at the same time.

“You’re looking better, little guy,” he noted, unable to help himself as he gave Gavin a quick once over.

“He’s feeling better. He was out like a light before we even got home,” Eddie commented, stepping into the living room. “I, meanwhile, spent the night vacuuming every inch of the house.”

“Taking no chances, huh?” Buck stood, picking Gavin up as he did.

“None at all,” his husband confirmed, sidling up to him for a kiss. “How did the rest of your night go?”

“Nothing major. I managed to make it back to the station before we got called out for a traffic accident. But hey, I think I’ve got a solution for the swimming lessons.”

“Oh?”

“There’s a pool where Albert and Ravi live.”

Eddie nodded and let out a hum.

“I forgot about that.”

“So did I,” Buck confessed. “But on the way home, I was thinking. Our weekends are pretty much filled, whenever we aren’t working.”

“That is the problem we were having,” Eddie agreed.

“And Chris only has like, less than a week and a half of school left.”

“That is also true,” the other man nodded. “So, the week after school lets out, we go over there and invade their space?”

“Exactly!” Buck grinned. Eddie totally got him. “Gives us time to buy our little guy some proper swim wear. And we can, y’know, look up videos for how to teach our kid some super not-drowning skills.”

“Who taught you?” Eddie asked, only to immediately roll his eyes and answer himself with, “Maddie, right.”

“Actually,” Buck said, setting Gavin down when the toddler started getting wiggly. “Dad paid for swimming lessons.”

He watched his husband’s eyebrows shoot up.

“Yeah, I know.” Buck shrugged. “If they were always horrible, Maddie and I wouldn’t have spent so long making up excuses for them.”

Growing up, he always did get the impression that Phillip loved them. He just loved Margaret more.

“So what do we get for a kid who hasn’t figured out the toilet?” Eddie asked, changing the subject a bit. “They make diapers for that, right?”

“They do,” Buck confirmed, pulling out his phone. “I may or may not have already ordered some.”

“Of course you did.”

The evening before Hen’s first day back on shift, Buck and Eddie had the Wilsons over for dinner. This wasn’t anything new or unusual, because they’d had them over multiple times after gaining custody of Gavin. It had been a while, though, and Buck always did like being able to play host. This time he had the excuse of celebrating the paramedic’s return to work.

Unfortunately, just before it was time for them to arrive, Gavin had decided that he didn’t actually like clothes, thanks.

“Gavin!” Buck yelped, chasing after the naked toddler, nearly tripping over Hoover. “Gavin, buddy, come back here!”

“No!” the toddler shouted back, taking a turn faster than Buck could and making a break for the living room.

“Why is he naked?” Chris asked, sitting on the couch. He’d abandoned whatever was playing on the television in favor of watching his stepfather nearly slide face-first into the wall.

Because he was - usually - a perfect saint of a child, Chris reached out and snagged Gavin as he sprinted by, gripping him under the armpits and tugging him up onto the couch beside him. Gavin wiggled and complained the entire time.

“It’s the terrible twos come early,” Buck groused, stomping towards the pair. “C’mon buddy. Diaper, pants. Aunties Hen and Karen are coming, you can’t be naked in front of the guests.”

“How many times did Maddie have to tell you that?” Eddie quipped from where he’d been following the chase at a more sedate pace. Buck turned to glare at him.

“Not a word from the peanut gallery, thank you.”

“So lots of times.” Eddie tossed a clean diaper his way, which Buck caught before kneeling down in front of the couch.

“Alright, little guy, how about this?” Buck held the diaper up. “You let me put a diaper on you, and I won’t make you wear anything else.”

Gavin seemed to consider it for a moment, but then shook his head with a smug sounding,

“No.”

Buck glowered at Chris when his perfect saint of a child had the audacity to laugh.

“This wasn’t actually a negotiation,” he said, manhandling the squirming little rebel so he could get the diaper on. “The only reason I’m not forcing you into clothes, young man, is because I know it’ll just be extra laundry later.”

“Truly, mi sol, you have learned the secret to parenthood.”

Buck’s retort was cut off before he could even say it by knocking at the door. Hoover was immediately there, tail wagging, before Buck could reach it. It was barely open before someone was snagging the toddler from him with a screech of,

“Gavin!”

“And hello to you, too, Karen.” Buck shared a look with Hen that was utterly missed by Karen, as she was busy doting on the happy child she was now holding. He stepped out of the way, letting them in. “Hey Hen, De- er, D.”

He still thought it was a stupid nickname. Did that make him a hypocrite?

“Hello, Diazes,” Hen said, the smile she was giving her wife a mixture of fond and amused. Denny was busy giving Hoover the pets that the dog demanded as he chatted with Chris. “How’re your ribs, Eddie?”

“Still broken,” Eddie answered. “But they’re healing.”

He was due for a check-up with the doctor, to see how well things were healing. They’d managed to get it scheduled for the same day as Gavin’s, at least.

“You’ve gotten so big,” Karen said, ignoring them all in favor of the baby. “Buck, stop feeding him, I don’t want him to grow up.”

“Pretty sure that’s considered child abuse,” Buck pointed out, closing the door. “And then Deidra will swoop in and take him away.”

“Bah, she’d understand.”

“What did you make?” Hen asked, sniffing the air. “It smells good, whatever it is.”

“Pops’ lasagna,” Buck stated proudly, chest puffing out. “Plus some garlic bread. It should be ready to take out of the oven soon.”

“It has been so long since I’ve had any of Bobby’s cooking,” Hen complained, heading for the dining room and taking a seat. “I guess yours will do in the meantime.”

“Oh, gee, how nice of you.” Buck rolled his eyes at Eddie as the man went to tell Abuela that everyone was here. “Chris, come help set the table!”

“You too, Denny!” Hen added.

There was much huffing and puffing, but the two soon-to-be teenagers dutifully trooped into the kitchen to grab the plates and silverware. Buck barely had to do anything, it was great.

“We’ve got an unopened bottle of red wine,” he said, heading into the kitchen to grab it along with glasses for everyone. There was also a pitcher of lemonade that he brought out as well, since the kids obviously weren’t getting any of the wine. “Would you like some Karen? Hen?”

It was a stupid question, really, one that he knew the answer to before he even asked. Buck and Karen had gotten wine drunk together more than once, especially in the days following Gavin’s first placement, after the initial two weeks with the Wilsons were up.

“Y’know, Karen, he has a high chair,” Eddie remarked as he came back into the room, Abuela following behind him.

“I know.” Karen was sitting at the table next to her wife, Gavin happily babbling away in her lap. “Does it look like I care?”

“Fair enough,” Eddie said, pulling out the chair at the head of the table for Abuela before taking his own seat. “More power to you.”

“So, Hen, Karen, how’s life been?” Buck asked, leaning against the back of Eddie’s chair. “No new foster kids?”

“Not yet,” Karen said. “We put a pause on it while she was, y’know.”

“Suffering?”

“Yeah, that.” Hen nodded. “How’s the station been?”

“Chaotic,” Buck said with a shrug. “Okay, maybe not that bad. Pretty much the same things as ever. Ravi got to do the Maneuver for a jumper.”

“Athena said we have a new guy?”

“Yeah, Toby. Not sure if he’s staying forever, or just until they find someone permanent. He’s cool though.”

“Who’s been covering for me and Chimney?”

“Crawford, from B-shift.”

There was a moment of silence, where Hen was clearly expecting something more. Then she said,

“Crawford? For both of us? Just Crawford?”

“We literally just got the second ambulance back,” Buck pointed out. “It’s all fixed up and shiny, and our blood’s been cleaned off.”

“Buckito,” Abuela tutted, shaking her head at him. He just shrugged.

“That makes sense, I guess,” Hen said, reaching over to tickle Gavin. “Who’ve they got in the captain’s seat?”

Eddie glanced up at him, frowning, before answering,

“Buck.”

The duh went unsaid, but Eddie’s tone made it very loud indeed.

“Oh! Of course.” Hen shook her head. “How’s that been?”

“Weird as hell.” Buck laughed. “Like I said, Ravi got to do the Maneuver for a call, and I had to tell the Eiffel Tower story. I got to send the probie to sensitivity training, though.”

“Sensitivity training?” Karen echoed.

“He has… Problems.”

“Oh, I’ve heard all about him.”

Buck had no doubt about that. Though he did wonder what colorful words Hen used to describe Dalton Wolfe to her wife.

“I bet you’ll be relieved tomorrow, then,” Hen said.

Buck tilted his head in confusion, and then shared a look with an equally bewildered Eddie.

“Well,” he started, feeling unsure. “It’ll be nice to have another paramedic on shift again.”

“No, I meant-”

Whatever Hen meant was cut off by the buzzer on the timer Buck had set for the lasagna. Sharing another look with Eddie, he went to pull it out of the oven. By the time he got back with the food, setting it on the table, the conversation had moved on. Chris was doing a lively retelling of the excitement from Tuesday evening.

Oh well. It was probably nothing.

An Assumption Has Been Made

Chapter Summary

In which Buck is a bit lost about someone else's view of things.

Chapter Notes

*glances at the date* Well. That's embarassing. I have absolutely no excuse, so let's move on.

(I just want to remind everyone as we go into this chapter that I like Hen. We like Hen.)

The trucks were pulling out just as Buck arrived at the station on Friday morning. It was shit luck for C-shift to be called out so close to the end, because they would inevitably be late getting home that day. Not unless it was something dumb and simple enough to have them returning within the next thirty minutes. Considering he just watched both ambulances follow after them, he kinda doubted that was the case.

The man behind, James Porter, looked up from cleaning the remnants of breakfast to greet Buck when he reached the top of the stairs to the loft. The guy would be getting a lot of shit next shift, just for being the one who got to go home on time.

“Want a cupcake?” Buck offered, setting the container of them down on the kitchen island. “Red velvet, home made.”

“Pass,” the man said. He carried a stack of plates to the sink and sat them down. “Which of your guys is on dish duty? Because I’m tempted to leave all of this for them.”

“Eh, I’ll just make the probie wash ‘em. It’s what he’s there for, right?”

The comment had the other firefighter laughing.

The sound of footsteps echoing through the empty bay caught the attention of both of them, Porter’s laugh trailing off into a frown. It was weird, because there shouldn’t have been anyone else in the station yet except for the two of them. So who the hell else was here?

Hen. Hen was apparently the answer to that question, as he saw when he went to the railing and checked. The paramedic was heading into the locker room with her work bag. Much earlier than he figured she would be.

“I guess she’s excited to be back,” he said, shrugging at Porter. “So what did they get called out for?”

“Something about a bus crash.”

Ugh. So they were definitely going to be out for a while, then. The poor bastards.

“Oh, they are gonna make you suffer your next shift,” Buck said. “You sure you don’t want a cupcake?”

“I think I’m gonna go ahead and change into my civvies,” Porter told him, wiping his hands on a towel now that he was done with clearing the table. 

“I feel like that’s the sort of thing I should be discouraging.”

Buck didn’t actually care what Porter did, though. Not his shift, not his problem, and it wasn’t like the man would be doing much of anything anyway. They kept a person behind when they went out on calls in case anyone came into the station needing help, like Amy with baby Gavin. With Buck and Hen both there, that was covered.

While Buck poked through the fridge, mentally going through the choices of what to feed the masses later, the rest of A-shift started to trickle in. Most of them made appreciative noise when they were directed towards the cupcakes.

“For Hen?” Albert asked, snatching one out of the container. “I hope you’re saving one for when she gets up here.”

“There should be enough, if you don’t all gorge yourselves,” Buck said, closing the fridge door. “Wait, where is Hen? She got here before all of you.”

Albert glanced at Ravi beside him, and then they both shrugged.

“She wasn’t in the locker room,” Lena said.

“I think I saw her go into Cap’s office,” was Clarke’s contribution as she waltzed up to the island. She reached into the container of cupcakes, only to freeze when Buck asked her,

“How many of these have you had?” 

Because he knew that she’d already snagged at least one.

“...They’re really good?”

He rolled his eyes and grabbed a couple out of the container, before heading for the stairs. A glance at his watch, and then the clock on the wall to compare, had Buck frowning around the still empty bay in consternation. Wolfe wasn’t late yet, but unless he pulled into the parking lot right that moment, he was going to be. And two shifts in a row, too.

Suddenly, making him do the dishes that C-shift left seemed a bit more well-deserved.

The door to the captain’s office was open when Buck got to it. Hen was inside, just as Clarke had said, sitting behind the desk and frowning at something on the computer. Which was… What was going on here?

“Brought you cupcakes,” he said, stepping into the office. “What, uh. What are you up to in here?”

“Trying to get onto FireStat,” was Hen’s answer as she turned away from the screen. “What kind of cupcake?”

“Red velvet.”

“Of course,” she laughed, making grabby motions. Buck rolled his eyes and handed her both of them.

“There’s a bunch upstairs specifically for you, but it didn’t seem like you’d be making it up there before the masses ate them all,” he said, walking around to the other side of the desk. “You were trying to get onto FireStat?”

“Trying being the keyword,” she groused. “Bobby’s password isn’t working, for some reason.”

“IT changed it so I could get on,” Buck explained, his frown matched by hers. “I didn’t really want to bother Pops by asking for it.”

“Didn’t he have it written down on a Post-It?”

“I didn’t find it until after I’d already bugged them.” He kept the new password on his phone, in the Notes app. Safer than on a Post-It that could be thrown away or misplaced. And anyways, that was neither here nor there because he could not for the life of him think of why Hen needed to get on FireStat at that moment. But, instead of pressing her on that, he just said, “You should come say hey to everyone, let them see you’re alive. Oh! You haven’t met Toby yet.”

“You’re right, I should go ahead and greet him,” Hen said, standing up with cupcakes in hand. “I noticed Jones’ name tag is still on his gear cage.”

“Yeah, I haven’t… I’m not sure what to do with it, y’know?” Simply removing Jones’ name didn’t feel right to Buck. They might not have been close, definitely not as close as their little core group, but Buck still thought of the man as a friend. Though that reminded him; he tapped his badge, specifically the loop of black cloth that he slipped over it on that first shift after the bridge collapse. “You need one of these. Those are upstairs, too.”

They walked into the bay together, but Buck came to a stop when he spotted Wolfe slinking in. He was being far from stealthy about it, too, looking about at the space the trucks should be with something like apprehension on his face. 

“And what time do you call this, young man?” Buck called out, hands on his hips. The probie froze like a rabbit, slowly turning to face him. It looked for a moment like he was about to simply bolt for the locker room, but clearly thought better of it and stayed in place while Buck and Hen made their way to him. “You’re late.”

“Sorry, Cap,” the kid said, ducking his head.

“Don’t be sorry,” Hen said, before Buck could even open his mouth again. “Just don’t be late. You need to be here on time, every day, to do your job.”

The probie’s face twisted, clearly unappreciative of the senior paramedic’s point. Buck hurried to cut him off when he opened his mouth, before he did or said anything that would result in more paperwork to do.

“Get changed and head upstairs,” Buck said. “There’s a pile of dirty dishes waiting for you at the sink.”

“But-”

“Nope.” Buck cut the kid off with a shake of his head. “Get changed, then dishes.”

“Where are the trucks?” Wolfe asked instead of hurrying off to do as he was told.

“C-shift got called to a bus crash, dunno when they’ll get back.” The probie was clearly not very pleased about that, though who knew why that was. The trucks being gone just meant that they had some guaranteed time at the start of their shift where they didn’t have to worry about the alarm blaring. “Get movin’, probie. The dishes are waiting for you.”

Wolfe, despite how much it looked like it hurt him, managed to resist the huffing and puffing he was no doubt dying to do. He barely even stomped on the way to the locker room like the petulant douchebag that Buck knew him to be at heart, however much they were working on that. It was the worst that Wolfe had acted out since their little chat in the captain’s office, and Buck was all too aware of what probably set him off.

That being the fact that Hen had lectured and told him off for his tardiness.

“You know,” Hen said, in a tone of voice that told Buck he was not going to like whatever words came next, “he reminds me a bit of you.”

“Shut your mouth,” he snapped, genuinely insulted. He knew he’d been unlikable as a probie, but surely he hadn’t been that bad. “C’mon, let’s introduce you to Toby. If he hasn’t started on his chores yet.”

“Shift just started, why would he already be doing them?”

“To get them out of the way before tonight,” Buck explained. “Though I’m pretty sure I saddled him with inventory, so I think he’d be okay.”

“Uh-huh…”

Hen was being… Weird, that was a good word for it. Like, after she chatted with Toby for a bit, she went and pulled out the captains’ logbook from where it was tucked with the cookbooks. Just started leafing through the past couple of weeks worth of notes that Reynolds and Mayfair and now Buck himself had all put in there. Not that she wasn’t allowed to do it, because there was nothing stopping any of them from picking it up and flipping through, it was just that no one but the (acting) captains really cared about what was in the logbook. Hen certainly hadn’t used to, except for her brief time in charge.

She’d been weird, too, when Captain Mayfair came rolling in with the rest of C-shift an hour and a half later. They stumbled out of the trucks and went straight for the locker rooms to change and head home, except for their captain who waited for Buck as he came down to meet her.

Well, Buck and Hen, because the paramedic also headed for her. There hadn’t been much for Mayfair to pass on before she herself went home, as she’d updated the logbook before they got called out for the bus crash, except for how they’d probably want to hurry up about making sure the buses and trucks were all stocked. Honestly, she mostly just complained, but that had been par for the course as Buck got more comfortable with their shift-change updates.

What wasn’t par for the course was the way Hen involved herself in this one. She’d given Buck a look when he started talking to Mayfair, and then Mayfair gave him a confused one when Hen pretty much took over. Not that there’d been much of a conversation to take over, because it literally had pretty much just been the other captain complaining about the timing of it.

Hen wasn’t being weird in a way that made Buck concerned, at least. She chatted with the others, avoided Wolfe as much as she could, caught up with Lena, and pretty much acted as she did before the concussion that had her stuck at home.

So, okay, she wasn’t being too weird. She was just doing the occasional weird thing.

The shift that Friday was a slow one. It started slow, it was continuing slow. Slow enough that the probie had to be threatened with Consequences when he joked about saying the Q-word just to have something to do. Slow enough that the alarm didn’t go off until just before Buck was about to get lunch started for the soon-to-be hungry masses.

Buck slipped into the captain’s seat, as he had done for every call for the past nearly three weeks. He grabbed the tablet off the dash as he slammed the door close and caught sight of Hen in the side view mirror. She looked annoyed about something, lips drawn into a tight line. But then she shook her head and went for the ambulance instead.

The call took them to MacArthur Park, where someone collapsed on one of the walking paths. Buck could feel the stares from both Albert and Ravi the moment he told Toby where to go, but he ignored them and kept ignoring them, right up until they were coming to a stop near the entry arches and they were all hopping out.

And then Hen was staring at him, too, when she and Crawford joined them. Not the frown she’d been giving him all day, but a look of concern.

“Yes, that’s where I died,” Buck snapped out. He caught Toby, Lena, and Wolfe all turning their heads to follow his finger to the apartment building that had been on fire that night. “I got better, it’s fine.”

Hen’s expression went from concerned to unimpressed.

“I’m fine!” he insisted. “And also maybe debating taking a selfie to send to Eddie.”

He could tag it with “#YOLO” and everything. Maybe add something about it being a shock to be there again. It’d be funny.

“Right.” Hen definitely did not believe him. Which was completely unfair, because it had been Eddie who’d almost had a panic attack the first time they drove past here following the lightning strike. “Lena, Wolfe, get the gurney.”

Lena raised an eyebrow and looked at Buck. Wolfe puffed him, clearly ready to argue back against his superior, and that just would not do.

“You heard her,” Buck said, cutting the kid off before he made him look bad. He’d been bragging about getting the probie under control, damn it. “C’mon, the caller said they’re near the picnic tables.”

It didn’t take long at all to locate them. There was a small crowd of concerned onlookers in the middle of one of the paved paths. They parted for the first responders, revealing a woman in a sundress kneeling beside an unconscious man laying on his back.

“Can you tell us what happened?” Hen asked, before Buck even opened his mouth. Crawford and Albert were already at the man’s side, pawing through their medical bags.

“We were enjoying his day off,” the woman said. “We’d been at the picnic tables, eating lunch, and then decided to walk around together. And then he complained about feeling sick, and then…”

And then her date collapsed.

“Rapid pulse,” Albert announced, fingers on the man’s wrist and his eyes on his watch. “Breath, too.”

“Feels like he’s running a fever,” Crawford added. “His skin’s red and dry.”

“Sounds like heat stroke,” Hen said. Which was dangerous and not ideal, but something easily treated if caught in time.

“Running fluids.” Albert had the IV set up before he said it, holding up the bag of saline so it started dripping.

“Heat stroke?” the man’s girlfriend repeated, sounding confused. “But we were just sitting at the tables over there. James wasn’t even sweating!”

The tables were in the middle of the grass, directly in the sun. And though their patient, James, hadn’t been sweating, his girlfriend had been.

“That probably didn’t help,” Buck said, nodding to Lena and Wolfe when they arrived with the gurney. “Is he on any medication?”

Heat intolerance was a side-effect of several medications, including bupropion. Thankfully, it wasn’t one he’d been hit with, considering what his job entailed.

“No.” The woman sounded unsure. “Does Benadryl count? He was complaining about allergies.”

“Benadryl counts,” Hen said, customer service voice in full force. “And it’s probably why he wasn’t sweating. Let’s get him on the gurney and loaded up.”

The girlfriend ended up riding with him, climbing into the back of the ambulance with Crawford and the patient while Albert got into the driver’s seat. Buck paused on the sidewalk and took the selfie he’d joked about, before heading for the engine.

And then he stumbled to a stop when he realized Hen was already in the captain’s seat. He hadn’t really paid attention to the fact that she hadn’t gone with the other paramedics, not that a third one had really been necessary. Even still, he would assume that she’d be sitting in the back with the others.

Whatever. It was weird, but it was whatever. Buck just climbed into the cab, ignoring the confused looks that the others were giving both him and Hen, and told Toby to drive as he focused on typing out the wittiest message he could think of to go along with the selfie.

(Eddie left him on read. The jerk.)

Worth It

Chapter Summary

In which Buck uses his words.

Chapter Notes

content warning for frank talk about suicide/suicide attempts and death

“You left me on read, you jerk,” was the first thing Buck said when Eddie answered the phone. They’d been back from the call at the park for a short while now, long enough that Buck could get lunch together and then bother his husband during his moment of free time while he waited for the timer to go off. 

Albert and Crawford had yet to return from taking the heat stroke victim to the hospital. Which either meant that they were stuck in traffic, stuck waiting for a bed to open up, stuck filling out forms, or had decided to get lunch somewhere else before coming back. If it was the latter, Buck was going to give them so much shit.

The others had all scattered to the four corners of the station, disappearing wherever they all went between calls. Toby was down in the bay, going through the compartments of the engine and checking everything off. Wolfe was in the gym, having managed to run everyone else from it through sheer obnoxiousness. Ravi and Lena were sitting on one of the couches while they, Buck was pretty sure, competed for who could run over the most bystanders in the firetruck simulator.

He had absolutely no idea where Hen was.

“I couldn’t find a good enough emoji to get my eye roll across,” Eddie retorted.

“Eye roll? You mean laughter, right?”

“Yeah, sure. A picture of you there with the caption of, ‘hope it doesn’t strike twice’ was oh so funny. I nearly broke my ribs again laugh- What do you have in your mouth?”

“Are you talking to the kid or the dog?”

“I wish it was the dog, he listens better,” his husband complained. “Give it here, mijo. Give i- Gracias. We don’t eat Abuela’s flowers.”

“There are worse things in that yard that he could try eating,” Buck pointed out. “It could have been a worm.”

“Ew,” was Eddie’s succinct reply. “How’s the day been so far? Can’t be too bad, if you’ve got time to harass me.”

“Oh, y’know. It’s been one of those days.”

“You mean it’s been qu-”

“I will divorce you right now,” Buck warned, cutting Eddie off and making him laugh. He could hear Gavin joining in as well, even if the kid didn’t know why his dad was laughing. “But yeah, it’s been… The opposite of loud. Our one call so far was for heat stroke.”

“Sounds exciting.”

“Oh, it was,” he drawled. “The probie’s acting up again, too, so I’ve had to deal with that. And Hen’s acting weird.”

“Acting weird?” Eddie repeated.

“Yeah, she-” Buck paused, glanced around, lowered his voice a little. “She was wanting to get onto FireStat this morning, and she went through the log book, and she got into the captain’s seat on the way back to the station. And- Okay, it sounds stupid when I’ve put everything out like that, but it’s weird.”

“Have you talked to her about it?”

Buck blinked and pulled the phone away so he could check the screen.

“I’m sorry,” he said, putting the phone back to his ear. “I was under the impression I was on the phone with my husband, not my therapist.”

“I remember, once, that you were the one talking about how we had to talk to each other. I know you made a big to-do of it with Bobby. There was hugging, there was even some crying…”

“Isn’t it Chris’ turn to hold the family brain cell?” Buck snarked. At the bay doors, the ambulance had returned and was backing into the station. “I hope he doesn’t have any big tests today, you might be causing him to fail.”

“Mi sol.”

“I don’t want to.” He was whining, and he knew it. “It’s probably nothing. It’s definitely nothing. It’s me, I’m the one being weird.”

“Must be a day ending in Y.”

“Oh, yeah, I get it. Because I’m always weird. You’re hilarious, babe.”

“Gavin thinks I’m funny. Don’t you, mijo?”

“Gavin was just eating a flower, I don’t think you can trust his judgment,” Buck pointed out. “Also, he thinks I’m funnier.”

Eddie’s snort was audible over the phone. It was kinda disgusting. Why did he love the man so much?

“Okay, but if you think-”

“Damn it, Eds.”

“If you think Hen’s being weird about something, talk to her. You’re the captain, after all.”

“I am the captain,” Buck agreed. “And I’m gonna take a lesson in being captain from Pops and just pretend I didn’t notice anything. I mean, he saw us terrorizing the young’uns with an ax and walked away.”

“I feel like you’re just making excuses to not talk to her.”

“Listen, if I wanted a therapy appointment, I’d call Copeland,” Buck pointed out. “I called my husband so that he can listen to me complain and then take my side on everything.”

“I did take your side-”

“No, no, my side is that Hen is being weird but I don’t need to go talk to her about it. You’re taking the side that she’s being weird and that I should go talk to her. See the difference?”

“...why do I love you so much?”

“Well, there’s that thing I do with my- Hey! You fuckers!” Albert and Crawford froze on the stairs for a moment, before continuing on up. They were both carrying bags of food. “Gotta go, my paramedics are about to die.”

He slid his phone back into his pants and glared at the pair of them, crossing his arms across his chest.

“We were hungry,” Crawford said with a shrug.

“I made everyone lunch! It is literally-” Buck glanced at the timer, gestured at it violently. “It’s ten minutes away from being done!”

“Hey, Rav! I got your cheeseburger and tots from Monty’s!” Albert called over, setting the bags he was carrying on the kitchen island.

“Sweet!” Ravi jumped off the couch and bounded over. On the television screen, Lena had the firetruck ragdolling a group of pixelated bystanders.

“No, not sweet,” Buck insisted, glaring at the three of them. “I was making you food. You knew I was- If you didn’t want to eat my cooking, you could have just said that!”

“Hey, Cap?” Crawford looked up from where they were digging through their own bag of food. “I don’t want to eat your cooking.”

Buck glared at Crawford and then reached into their bag to steal a fry, ignoring their squawks of protest.

“I don’t think I’ve ever been so insulted,” he groused. “This better keep you filled until you go home tomorrow, ‘cause I ain’t gonna cook for you if this is how I’m treated.”

“Yeah, yeah, whatever, you’ll get over it,” Crawford said, dragging their food closer to themselves and out of Buck’s grabbing range. “Have you talked to Hen today?”

Oh, God.

“Why?” he hesitantly asked in return.

The look that Crawford and Albert shared at that had even Ravi frowning.

“Well, we’re thinking that there might have been a miscommunication somewhere,” Albert said. “Maybe?”

“Did anyone tell you how long you were filling in for Nash?” Crawford asked him.

“For at least another month, or whenever Pops gets back.” Which would hopefully be soon. “What does that have to do with Hen?”

Crawford and Albert shared another look.

“I was not there for that conversation,” Albert said, throwing the other under the bus. “So you’ll have to ask them.”

Crawford rolled their eyes and smacked at the finger that Albert was using to point at them.

“Apparently,” they said, “she thinks she’s the captain now. Or that’s what I gathered from how she was talking on the way to that call.”

Buck blinked. And then he blinked again.

“Oh,” he breathed out. “That’s what she’s been doing.” And then, “oh, god, I’m gonna have to talk to her, aren’t I?”

Eddie was never gonna let him live this down. His husband was going to be a smug little shit the moment he heard about this.

Buck found Hen in Bobby’s office, sitting at the desk as she filled in a 902m. She looked up when he stepped inside. After a moment of internal debate, he pulled the door closed behind him. He wasn’t entirely certain what kind of talk they were about to be having, but he did know that neither of them would appreciate it if it was overheard by any of the others.

“Lunch will be ready soon,” he said, for lack of any other opening line.

“Thanks for telling me,” the paramedic said, eyeing the door curiously. “I’m guessing that wasn’t the only reason you’re here?”

“No, uh. I was wanting to talk to you about something.”

“That’s fine, I have time.” Hen set the form down on the desk and leaned back in the seat. “I’ve been meaning to speak to you, too. To thank you for backing me up this morning with the probie, for one. I know he has… Issues, with the idea of women in positions of authority. Though it would help more if you stepped back some. I know you’ve gotten used to being in charge-”

“Hen,” Buck said, cutting her off. “Did Bobby talk to you? Or Chief Williams?”

“No. Why?”

“Because I’m trying to figure out why you think you’re in charge.”

“Why I think?” Hen’s eyebrows rose sky-high. “Buck, out of everyone in this station right now, I’ve been here the longest.”

“Yeah, I get that you have seniority,” he said. “And I get that you were in charge when Bobby was in Florida last year, but that doesn’t mean you’re suddenly in charge now.”

“Buck.”

It was almost patronizing, the way she said his name. He had to breathe through his nose to keep from snapping at her.

“Hen,” he said in return, trying to keep his own tone even. “Williams put me in charge.”

“While I was out with the concussion. I’m back now. So...”

“So, no one’s told me otherwise. Until Bobby gets back, or Chief Williams herself says something, I’m running with the assumption that I’m still in charge.”

“Fine.” Hen shoved the chair away from the desk and stood, snatching up the 902m along with the pen she’d been using to fill it out. “Fine.”

Buck stepped out of the way of the door, allowing her to stomp her way out. He rubbed at his mouth anxiously, a headache forming between his eyes.

“Shit,” he sighed, before trailing back out as well.

The rest of the day was not any livelier than the morning. They had another call right after lunch, a pedestrian struck by a car. And then one for an afternoon delight gone horribly, embarrassingly wrong, involving something breaking that had Buck cringing in empathy and made worse by the fact that the other party was too tied up to help them.

At the moment, with the sun well below the horizon, Buck had sat himself at the table with that day’s paperwork. The wonders of proper medication, it was easier to get through than it would have been a year ago. The television was on and showing the news, thankfully not Channel 8. They were currently covering a structure fire from early that morning that one of the neighboring stations had responded to, though Buck wasn’t really paying attention to it.

“Okay.” Ravi sat down across from him, hands flat on the table top. “I really meant to ask you this earlier, but when were you and Eddie planning on coming over for the pool?”

“Um, the week after next,” Buck answered, scratching at his ear with his pen. “After school lets out, but before Chris took off for camp.”

He assumed Chris was still interested in doing the summer camp thing again. The kid had signed up for it the month before, but he should probably still ask, just in case.

“Okay, that can work,” the younger man said, fingers tapping. “S’long as it’s at the beginning of the week.”

“We aren’t exactly attached to a specific day, we’re just playing it by ear. Why?”

“Uh…” Ravi gave a furtive look around them before leaning in and lowering his voice. “I’m surprising Al with a trip to Disneyland. After we get off shift that Thursday, it’ll be straight from here to there. I even booked us a room at that hotel for the night, so we can do two days without needing to deal with traffic.”

“Aw, that’s sweet,” Buck teased. “That’s why you were taking all those extra shifts, isn’t it?”

“I had to gird my bank account.”

His answering laughter was interrupted by the sound of the alarm, sending them sprinting for the trucks.

There were calls that came in that firefighters dreaded. Anything to do with children, for example. Or calls to certain areas of town that everyone knew was going to be a pain in the ass, just for the types of people that were bound to be involved. And then there were the calls that were, unfortunate to say, as routine as they were tedious, thanks to Los Angeles’ very nature as a city.

When they arrived at the scene of this particular one, a tall apartment building in the middle of downtown, Buck jumped out to find Athena waiting for them on the curb. Her gaze was fixed on the building’s roof while other officers were keeping onlookers at bay. When he followed her gaze, Buck spotted the reason they were called.

“Do we know their name?” he asked her, staring up at the woman standing on the literal edge.

“No,” Athena answered, finally looking at him. “She’s refusing to talk to anyone. We’ve been hesitant to try and grab her, because that risks spooking her right off the side. So it’s a waiting game until the negotiator gets here and gives it a try, all while we stand around and twiddle our thumbs and pray she doesn’t…”

“Too high up for our ladder,” Buck said, glancing back at the trucks. “Definitely too high up for an airbag.”

After a certain height, terminal velocity earned its name no matter what a person landed on. A pool of water or a cushion of air, both would get the same result as hitting pavement. Maybe not as messy, but certainly as, well. Terminal.

“No telling what she’d do if she saw us inflating it, anyways,” Lena pointed out. She and some of the others had meandered over to them, waiting for their instructions.

“She hasn’t said anything to anyone?” Hen asked. It was the most that Buck had heard her say since the last call. She’d been stand-offish all day while they were at the station, ever since their not-quite-an-argument in Bobby’s office. “Not a word?”

“Not a word,” Athena confirmed with a shake of her head.

“So we don’t know why she’s up there.”

“I think we can all guess why she’s up there,” Lena said. “The bigger question is why she’s still up there.”

“I wanna try talking to her,” Buck blurted out, looking back up.

“Cap,” Crawford broke in, sounding dubious. Buck studiously did not look at Hen. “She’s not talking to anyone.”

“I know,” Buck said with a nod. “But I want to give it a try.”

He turned to Athena, watched her expression turn into understanding tinged with pain for the briefest of moments. Even if he had never outright told her, even if she was unaware of the full breadth of it, she was the only one there who knew. Maddie, he had no doubt, would be chomping at the bit to talk to her for the exact same reasons as he was now.

“Alright,” the sergeant said. “It’s not like we have anything else to try at the moment.”

After talking to someone over her radio, Athena escorted him inside. They took the elevator as high as it would go, and then took the stairs onto the roof. The door opened with a clang and the woman on the edge turned her head to look at them, the motion causing her to sway briefly in place.

She looked about his age, maybe younger, wearing an oversized sweater with her curly hair in a ponytail. And she looked… Tired, was the only word Buck could think of to describe her. She looked tired.

Buck stepped out onto the roof while Athena stayed in the stairwell, letting the door close after a moment’s hesitation. He walked to the ledge that the nameless woman was standing on, making sure all of his actions were exaggerated and telegraphed so she knew what he was doing. There were a couple feet between her and him, when he came to a stop. After a moment, she moved her gaze away, back to the city street below.

Looking down gave him a brief look of vertigo, all the people looking very small from up here.

“It is very high up,” Buck said, breaking the silence that had fallen over them. “But it’s got a pretty view.”

She was ignoring him, and he briefly wondered how many others had come up here to give it a try. The view they had gave him an idea, and he pointed a bit to the north-west of them.

“I died right over there,” Buck said, and that was enough to get her attention again. “You can’t really see it from here, the view’s blocked. But I died right over there, struck by lightning. We were responding to an apartment fire during a storm. I went up the ladder with a hose, and then there was light and heat. And then I was dead.”

The woman’s expression was very dubious as she took him in, and he could easily guess what she was thinking.

“I got better, obviously,” he told her. “But it was touch and go there for a bit, and I had a really messed up dream. The thing is though, I wasn’t even trying to die that time. I was just doing my job, minding my business, and God was just like, get smote, idiot.”

“That time?” the woman asked. Buck did a mental jig in victory.

“Have you ever… Have you ever gotten to a point where everything is a mess, and you look back and realize that it’s entirely your fault?” he asked her. “Like, like… Like you could see every bad choice, a whole line of them, that led you to that point. You’re in a hole that you dug yourself, and there’s no way out that you can see.”

“Yeah,” she said, glancing down below again. “I’ve been there.”

Buck himself had been there several times, because he never really seemed to learn.

“I like being liked,” he told her. “And sometimes, a lot of times, I don’t have the best impulse control. Which led to me being kicked out of community college because I spent all my tuition money on modifications for my bike and bankrolling keg parties, because I wanted people to like me and to think I was cool. I was so angry afterwards. At myself. I’d already been kicked out of school once, and I knew that I was just proving what my parents and everyone else already knew. That I was a loser, that I was always going to be a loser.

“So I was on my bike, speeding down the road, dreading going back home and telling my parents how I screwed up again. Knowing how they were going to look at me, how they were going to look through me again after they were done shouting and lecturing. And there was a car, pulling out right in front of me. They didn’t see me, I saw them. And I sped up.”

He had been lucky, so very lucky, though he hadn’t felt it at the time. At the time, when he blinked his eyes open and realized he was going to walk away from the remains of his bike, he just felt disappointed and quietly desperate.

“I’m just tired,” the woman told him. “I was in bed, looking at the clock and just wishing I could fall asleep and not wake up.”

“That’s why you came up here,” Buck guessed. “But why are you still up here?”

“You want me to jump?”

“No. God, no. Do you see the uniform I’m wearing?” He pointed at the LAFD patches on his shirt. “I was just wondering, y’know? ‘Cause you’ve been standing there for a while now. You could have done it at any point before I got up here.”

“I’m scared.” The words were quiet, and followed by a chuckle that lacked any amusement. “Too scared to take the coward’s way out. That’s ironic, right?”

“I don’t really blame you. There’s a lot of time to fall. And besides, what if it doesn’t work? I think that broke me more than anything else that day, the fact that I walked away.”

“You got better,” she pointed out, but there was more to that statement that went unsaid, if still so very loud. You got better. I don’t think I will.

“I chose to get better,” Buck countered. “I choose to get better. Every day.”

“That sounds exhausting.”

“It’s worth it,” he insisted. “And it’s easy, it really is. You just need something, anything, to look forward to. Something that has you taking one more step forward, that has you deciding to get up in the morning.”

“I don’t have anything.”

“Are you sure about that? Because it doesn’t have to be anything big. Tomorrow morning, when I get off shift, I'll go home and kiss my husband and make my kids laugh. On Sunday, most of the rest of my family is coming over for dinner, and I’ll spend the day cooking with my pops and my husband’s abuela. Or- Or, next month, the Barbie movie comes out-”

“Barbie?” the woman blurted out, sounding one part amused and one part horrified.

“It looks fun!” Buck said, defending himself as he took a step towards her. She didn’t notice. “I’ve already convinced my sister to see it with me, and my husband’s not getting a choice.”

“It’s a movie,” she said. “That’s your reason for living? A movie? About Barbie?”

“One of my reasons,” he corrected, taking another step. “If I die today, I’ll never get to see it. I’m telling you, it doesn’t have to be something big or life changing. It’s not on the same level as my sister getting married, but it’s still something. Something that has me choosing to get better.”

She did not look very convinced, but she was still listening to him, and so he decided to keep pressing.

“C’mon,” Buck said. “There has to be something that you’re secretly looking forward to. Something you’ll miss if you take the plunge now. I told you, it doesn’t have to be something big. It can be stupid, just s’long as it’s something that has you choosing to get up in the morning. Something that you look forward to.”

There was a moment, a long moment, before she finally said,

“Doctor Who.”

Yes!

“What about it?” he asked, tapping down on his excitement as he stepped a bit closer to her.

“There’s a character coming back,” she told him, eyes locked on the ground below again. “She was always one of my favorites.”

“See! There you go, that’s something.”

“And there’s a cat,” she said. “It comes to my window every afternoon. Lets me pet on it before running off again.”

“That sounds like two perfectly good reasons to get up tomorrow.”

“Really?” She didn’t make any effort to keep the dubiousness out of her voice. “A TV show and a cat?”

“I told you, it doesn’t have to be something big. And- And tomorrow, you might find a third reason. The day after that, another. Until you have a whole list of reasons, big and small, for why you’re choosing to get up in the morning. And then one day, you might find yourself getting up in the morning just because you want to, no other reason needed.”

“That sounds exhausting,” she said again.

“But it’s worth it,” Buck once again insisted. He held out his hand to her, palm up. She frowned at it, probably realizing how close to her he’d gotten while they were talking. There was a moment, and then another, where all she did was frown at it.

And then she reached out her hand and gripped his.

La Gata

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie finds a kitten.

Her name was Hannah. She gave Buck and Athena her name while they escorted her downstairs to be looked over. Hannah wasn’t quite eager to be taken on a ride to the hospital, but they had that policy for everyone whom they had to talk down from the literal edge, and Buck wasn’t about to break it now.

Athena didn’t say much during the trip back down, which had him nervous because he wasn’t certain how much she overheard, or if she listened at all. Who was he kidding, of course she listened, she wasn’t gonna let him go up there and not keep an ear out for if something went wrong. Which was proven true just before they split up - him for the engine and her for her cruiser - and she patted him on the cheek with a simple,

“May’s been talking about seeing that movie, too.”

“Y- Yeah, okay,” he stammered out. “The uh, the more the merrier.”

The night that followed was about as (un)eventful as the day that preceded it. They managed the miracle of eating uninterrupted, and then actually got consecutive hours of sleep. The alarm rang once, just after two in the morning, but not for anything serious enough to keep them out for too long.

That night, for whatever reason, Buck had trouble falling back asleep after the call-out. It was probably because he was too busy fretting over Hen, who’d avoided him all day whenever they weren’t going to or at a scene. Or maybe it was the bupropion deciding to mess with him a bit, or the looming full moon that was thankfully going to be next shift’s problem.

Whatever the reason, Buck was up in the loft awake while everyone else was in the bunks. He had a cup of tea that he wasn’t really drinking and a fresh book from the library a couple blocks away from home that he got for occasions just like this one. He’d been working through it at a steady pace, the silence broken only by the constant city traffic just outside.

Well, the traffic and the sound of someone moving about downstairs.

Buck perked up at the noise, marking his place and setting down the book as he got to his feet. From the railing, he spotted Wolfe doing his best to sneak out of the bunks without waking anyone up. He was almost doing a good job of it, too, except for how he was utterly failing at it.
