Not even bothering to try and sneak up on the kid, knowing damn well that he would just end up tripping and knocking something over if he tried, Buck jogged down the stairs. The probie startled when he heard him coming down the steps, jumping in place and spinning around.

“Couldn’t sleep either?” Buck asked him when his feet hit the bay floor.

“What? Oh, uh.” Wolfe blinked rapidly. “Yeah. I mean, n- no.”

“Uh-huh.” Buck eyed him for a moment, trying to figure out what exactly had the kid so rattled. “Well. At least I’m not alone, I guess. You can join me upstairs, if you want.”

“Sure, Cap.”

The kid didn’t sound sure, but whatever. If Wolfe decided to stay down here, or go back into the bunks, then it was no skin off Buck’s back. He turned and headed back up the stairs, hearing the younger man on the steps behind him a moment or two later.

“There’s tea if you want any,” Buck said, flapping a hand at the tea pot. He glanced over his shoulder in time to see Wolfe’s nose scrunch up. “Yeah, I don’t think I like it either. But it’s chamomile, so it’s supposed to help you, y’know, sleep or whatever.”

Bobby kept a stock of it tucked away in the back of one of the cabinets. It appeared there after several shifts in a row, following the captain’s return from being shot but before Eddie’s own, that he found Buck wide awake in the middle of the night. Buck never quite gained a liking for the taste, and he wasn’t sure if it actually helped, but it had become a staple of the station’s pantry in the years since.

At this point, he was pretty sure the smell alone achieved far more than the tea itself did, just by association.

Buck flopped back down into the armchair he’d been lounging in, picking up his book but not yet opening it. The probie lingered uncertainly by a couch until Buck prompted him with a nod to just go ahead and sit.

“So.” Buck broke the silence before it had the chance to become uncomfortable. “What’s keeping you awake?”

“Uh, I-” Wolfe fumbled for words and then shrugged. “Just… Just couldn’t sleep, I guess.”

“Right.” Part of Buck wanted to push, just to sate his curiosity, but he let it go. “The remote’s over there, if you can find something worth watching at this time of night.”

The kid grabbed the remote off the coffee table and started flipping through channels. Much as Buck already knew, there wasn’t really anything on this late at night. His fingers tapped against the spine of his book as he debated going back to reading. But he clearly needed to check back in with Wolfe, if his behavior towards Hen was anything to go by. So he sighed and set the book down in his lap before he even opened it.

“So,” Buck said, breaking the silence between them. “Do you want to talk about how you acted with Hen?”

Wolfe’s shoulders went up nearly to his ears.

“No.”

“Too bad, we’re gonna anyway.”

“Why?” Wolfe demanded petulantly. “I didn’t do anything.”

“Because I didn’t give you the chance,” Buck pointed out. “I bet if I had, you’d have said or done something you shouldn’t’ve.”

“Well, she shouldn’t have been trying to tell me what to do,” the probie complained. “She ain’t in charge.”

“You had no problem with me telling you what to do before I was wearing the captain’s hat. So that excuse isn’t working.” Should he just straight up ask? Screw it. “Is it because Hen’s a woman or because she’s black?”

Props to the probie, but he managed to catch whatever he was about to say before it came out of his mouth. Buck was pretty sure Wolfe was literally biting his tongue as he threw the remote onto the table, before getting to his feet and storming off. He didn’t bother turning the TV off, of course, so Buck had to reach over and do it himself.

“Every day,” he thought aloud as he settled into his chair and opened his book again. “Every day, I’m reminded how lucky we are with Chris…”

It was not unusual for Eddie to not come home right away on Saturdays, when his veterans group let out. This was especially true for the last couple of weeks with Stanley giving him rides there and back. Not that Buck minded; he would always, always encourage his husband to go out and make new friends. Just like Eddie did with him, however much he occasionally regretted those end results.

No, what was unusual was how on this Saturday, Eddie stayed out past lunch and well into the afternoon. He was alive, judging from the texts, but he was being… Cagey. His answer, when Buck asked him what he was up to, was a simple Out with Stanley. Which, yeah. Obviously.

Gavin was down for his post-lunch nap and Buck was contemplating calling Eddie, when the man called him. Buck hurried to answer before it woke their kid.

“I was wondering if I was gonna have to ask Ma to hunt you down,” he joked.

“Ah, no, don’t need that.”

“And that’s good to-” He cut himself off at what he heard in the background. “Is that Sage barking?”

Sage was Stanley’s service dog. Buck hadn’t ever spent much time with her, but he couldn’t remember if he’d ever heard her actually bark before. Actually, he was pretty sure there were several dogs barking now.

“Uh, no. That’s- Don’t worry about that,” Eddie said, which was always a worrying phrase. “Could you come and pick me up?”

“I thought Stanley was bringing you back.”

“He was going to, but there’s uh… A thing?”

“A thing,” Buck repeated. “Alright, where am I going?”

“The animal shelter just off Western Avenue.”

“The- What?” Buck glanced down to where Hoover was curled up beside Gavin’s bed. “Why are you at an animal shelter?”

“I promise to explain when you get here.”

“You better,” Buck told him, following it up with a quick goodbye before hanging up.

After checking in with Abuela - she and Pepa were entertaining a group of their church friends on the back patio, thankfully sans Mrs. Salcedo and her pointy nails - he was out the door and on the road. The drive took less than twenty minutes, and Buck was soon pulling into the parking lot of the shelter.

He spotted Eddie soon enough, his husband standing awkwardly outside the doors, holding-

“Is that a cat?” Buck blurted out after he slammed the button to roll the passenger window down. “Did you come here and get a cat?”

It was a kitten, specifically. A little ball of black fur that seemed so very tiny with how it was cupped in Eddie’s hands. The little thing was crying up a storm, too.

“No, I didn’t come here and get a cat,” Eddie said, sounding almost offended as he climbed in. “She was in front of the library this morning, and then she was still there when we came out. The lady at the shelter said she was about seven weeks.”

“Uh-huh. And I’m guessing the reason you’re getting into my jeep with her is because you want to bring her home with us. Without even asking if I’m okay with it.”

His husband gave him his best attempt at Christopher’s puppy dog eyes, the ones Buck taught the kid to do. They were accompanied by Eddie holding the kitten up right next to his face and if Buck was actually against the idea, he would’ve crumpled then and there.

“Obviously I’m not saying no. Have you met me? Put those cow eyes away.” Buck shook his head. “Y’know, between the two of us, I bet people would expect it to be me coming up with new furry friends.”

“You adopted Gavin the moment you laid eyes on him,” Eddie pointed out.

“Yeah, but I didn’t bring him home with us until like, a year later. And that was only after his first forever home fell through. You? You’re two for two.”

“Shut up and drive, mi sol,” the other man ordered, finger scratching beneath the kitten’s chin. “The little lady is hungry. Aren’t you, chica?”

The kitten gave a screeching little cry in agreement.

“The little lady needs a name,” Buck commented, pulling out of the parking lot and heading for the pet store they frequented for Hoover. “What about… La Gata.”

The pause for a red light allowed him to look over and see the flat expression his husband was staring at him with.

“We are not naming the cat, The Cat,” Eddie said. “You’re not naming her anything. You got to name our kid, and the dog came with one. This is my turn.”

“Absolutely. You get full naming privileges, babe. And I’m sure it’ll be a beautiful name,” Buck said, and then, “Wait, didn’t you name Christopher?”

“Shannon and I named him. And it doesn’t change the fact that it’s my turn now.”

As if in agreement, the kitten screeched again. She was nuzzled up to Eddie’s chest and Buck really needed to be careful about keeping his eyes on the road. It would not do to crash and have to explain that he was distracted by his hot husband holding an adorable kitten in the passenger seat.

Between Buck and Eddie, their friends would probably assume that Buck would be the one to try and buy out an entire store to spoil their new family member. They’d probably be right, he had gone a bit over-the-top with toys to keep at the house for Jee-Yun, after all. Except they’d also be wrong, because Eddie was just as bad. One would just have needed to see all the things he splurged on Hoover, the day he brought him home, to figure that out.

“Eddie, please don’t make me be the reasonable one,” Buck was practically begging. Thank God he left Chris at home, or else he would be doomed.  “We can’t get this cat tree.”

“Why not?” Eddie asked in return. The cart that Buck was pushing around had a dozen different cat toys, wet food that was a bit on the pricier side, a pair of bowls, and various other necessities.

“Because it’s as tall as we are,” Buck pointed out. “If Gavin is anything like either of us - don’t think I forgot about your Spiderman routine, babe - then he will be climbing on it.”

He watched his husband’s mouth twist in displeasure.

“Fine.”

“How about this one?” Buck pointed at a cat tree next to the one Eddie was looking at. “It’s shorter, and it’s on sale.

“What do you think?” Eddie held the kitten up so she could sniff and paw at the one they had on display. “This one good enough for you?”

Buck could readily admit that the sigh he let out was far too besotted.

“So I saw that there’s another Diaz now.” TK sounded amused.

“Everyone’s seen it, I’m sure.”

Buck was leaning against the dining room doorway as he talked on the phone, watching Eddie and Chris both play with the kitten in the living room. Gavin had taken an interest in her, too, though they made sure he didn’t grab or pull or do anything that would result in claws and tears. Hoover, meanwhile, was staying far, far away from the tiny little thing for the moment. Which was probably a wise course of action.

“What’s her name?”

“Senorita Dolores Jeronima Diaz. Pretty sure we’re just gonna be calling her Senorita, though.”

“Well,” TK said after a minute. “That is a name, Sunshine.”

“I didn’t come up with it, that was all him,” Buck said. “Now, what about you? Excited to be heading back home on Monday?”

“Oh, sure. Absolutely dying to leave my little slice of tropical paradise.” His friend’s sarcasm was palpable. “I tried to convince Carlos to stick around for another week, but he wouldn’t go for it.”

  “Y’know, a trip to the beach sounds good right about now.”

“Yeah, yeah, yap it up. Anyways, he’s eager to get back and actually check on his mom in person, so no side-trips to surprise you.”

“Darn, how disappointing.”

“I’m sure it is. Dad’s being squirrely about something, so I kinda want to see what’s up, too,” TK said. “What about you? What’re your plans this week?”

“Well, there’s Gavin’s and Eddie’s doctors appointments.”

“Uh-huh.”

“I’m taking Eddie to a Dodger’s game on Wednesday.”

“That’ll be fun. For him, at least.”

“And then there’s Jones’ funeral on Friday.”

“...Ah.”

Old Wound

Chapter Summary

In which the author realizes just how much of this fic is just dialogue.

Early on Sunday morning, while watching his husband fix up breakfast, Buck blurted out,

“I don’t know what to do about Hen.”

He had spent Saturday mostly trying not to think about it. It was rather easy, chasing a toddler around the house was something that required almost all of his attention. And then there was the surprise addition of a new family member - Senorita was currently perched on Eddie’s shoulder, like a black and furry parrot - that managed to distract him completely. But that didn’t mean that he’d forgotten about it entirely, nor that he wasn’t worrying about it.

“I told you to talk to her about it,” Eddie reminded him as he kept a careful eye on the eggs he was scrambling. He’d had a field day with it when Buck finally got around to explaining what happened, lording it over him just as expected.

“I did! It just didn’t fix anything.”

“Why did she assume she’d suddenly be in charge, anyways?”

“I don’t know.” Buck pushed himself up so he was sitting on the edge of the counter and started thumping his heels against the bottom cabinets. “Probably because she would have been if she hadn’t been out, too.”

“Sure,” Eddie said, though it did not at all sound sincere. Buck kicked at his leg, raising an eyebrow in a silent question when his husband looked his way. “Look, all I’m saying is that you don’t know that for a fact and neither does she. It’s not like the role of substitute captain was just handed to you.”

“Wasn’t it?”

Eddie scowled at him for a moment before reaching up, careful to avoid displacing Senorita from her perch, and flicking him between the eyes.

“If it was ‘just handed’ to you, then it was ‘just handed’ to her last time,” Eddie said. “I don’t think that’s the argument you want to make if you’re trying to be self-pitying.”

“I’m not trying to be self-pitying,” Buck complained, rubbing at the spot he was flicked. “I’m just- What makes me a better fit for the job than her?”

“What makes her a better fit for the job than you?”

“Seriously? Do you mean something other than the several years of experience she has over me?”

“And Chimney has more,” Eddie pointed out. “We all saw how well that translated.”

Buck sighed and kicked his heels on the cabinet doors again in frustration, harder than before. He fell still when Eddie placed a hand on his knee and gave it a soft squeeze.

“You’re still worried that Bobby picked you out of nepotism, aren’t you?”

“Maybe,” Buck admitted. “I mean, you can’t tell me that other people don’t think it.”

“Fuck ‘em,” was Eddie’s simple response to that. “Listen, mi sol, there’s a reason that leadership is one of the things we’re scored on in our annual reviews. Bobby’s supposed to look for those qualities in a person and encourage them, for situations exactly like the one that we found ourselves in last month. Trust me, I should know.”

Sometimes, Buck forgot that his husband hadn’t just been some simple army medic. Eddie had been a Staff Sergeant, and that came with responsibilities that were remarkably similar to what Bobby and, currently, Buck were saddled with. Actually, he probably would’ve been the equivalent of Tommy, TK’s captain as a paramedic back in Austin.

“Maybe you should be the captain,” he joked.

“I’m perfectly fine with not being in a leadership role again,” Eddie said. He plucked Senorita off his shoulder, struggling for a moment to get her tiny little claws to let go of his shirt, and knelt down to set her on the floor. “What I’m saying is that Bobby wouldn’t have chosen you if he hadn’t seen those qualities, regardless of how he feels about you. Chief Williams definitely wouldn’t have.”

“I guess…”

“You guess.” Eddie rolled his eyes, before turning off the burner and moving the skillet over. “See if either of our kids are up, and tell Abuela that breakfast is ready.”

“Sir, yes sir,” Buck quipped, sliding down and dashing off before Eddie could smack him for it.

It didn’t dawn on him until he was picking up a babbling Gavin from the crib that the conversation had gone off on a tangent, leaving him without any answer for what to do about Hen. Oh well, there were going to be plenty of people coming over for dinner that evening that he could bother for advice.

Sunday Dinners were almost always a chaotic affair. They were loud and lively, with several conversations going on at once and often overlapping. That’s the kind of thing that happened when eleven or more people sat down at the same table, especially when two of them were toddlers and half the adults acted like children.

Things tended to get more sedate after everyone had eaten, though. They sat around the table, even with their plates empty and licked clean, and kept talking. Even after they started clearing everything away - a task never given to one of those who helped cook - they’d still hang around and enjoy each other’s company. Typically, groups would split off: a couple going into the kitchen to get the dishes cleaned; some heading into the living room with the toddlers, still rambunctious and not yet down for a nap; others stepping out into the backyard with the dog.

Chimney was part of the latter this time. Still healing from the injury he suffered during the bridge collapse, he limped his way out and then sat himself down in one of the patio chairs. Which was really very convenient for Buck, because this way he didn’t even have to come up with an excuse to get him alone.

“So, uh, how’s it all healing?” he asked, holding the door open for Hoover to pad his way out. The dog started doing a circuit around the yard, pausing every few paces to sniff at something. 

“Slowly,” Chimney grumbled. “I’m pretty sure I got better faster, the last time I was stabbed.”

“Last time you got stabbed by a surgeon,” Buck pointed out. “With an actual knife. This time it was impalement by an inanimate object and gravity.”

“That’s true. The other day, Jee wanted to show me something and she threw herself into my lap.”

“Oh, man.” Buck winced in sympathy.

“Yeah, many tears were shed. Very few of them by her.” Chimney turned his head, glancing at the closed back door, before angling his head up to look at Buck, who was still standing. “How… How are you doing?”

“How am I doing?” Buck echoed back, a bit thrown.

“Yeah. How are you doing with…?” Chimney made a vague motion in the air with his hand. “I heard about the call on Friday. With- Y’know, with the jumper that you talked down. Are you…?”

“Oh. Oh.” Buck ducked his head with a smile. Chimney wasn’t an idiot, and much like Athena, he’d been given big enough clues to draw an accurate enough conclusion. “Yeah, no. I- I’m fine. Everything’s… I’m fine.”

“Good. That’s good.”

“Yeah,” he said with a nod. “So Hen talked to you about Friday, then?”

Chimney’s eyes went wide.

“Look at the time,” he said, not even glancing at his watch. “I should probably get Maddie and Jee and go on- Gah!”
He’d pushed himself up too fast and now he fell back into his seat, clutching at the still healing wound in his stomach. Buck gave him a sympathetic pat on the shoulder before throwing himself into the neighboring chair.

“I’m not gonna ask what she said.” Damn his curiosity, though.

“Good,” was Chimney’s blunt, if relieved, reply.

“I did want to ask you for advice, though. ‘Cause Hen seemed pretty upset. Which I can understand, if she thought she was gonna be the one in charge when she got back. I just… I dunno. I didn’t expect her to give me the cold shoulder the entire rest of the shift.”

“Yeah, she… Yeah.” The other man let out a sigh. “Listen, I don’t think we’ve complained to you about Gerrard enough. Or about how awful the atmosphere at the station was under him. There is a reason that Hen and I are the only ones still around from before Bobby came in, and why we don’t really talk to any of the others.”

“I know that he was, um…” What was a polite way of phrasing it? “A prick.”

“If you weren’t a straight white man, you were at the bottom of the totem pole until you could prove that you were ‘worth it.’” Chimney eyed Buck for a moment. “He and the others would have had no problem with you. Not until they realized you liked guys, and then the comments would come. Telling each other not to drop the soap in the showers, accusing you of looking in the locker room.”

“Sounds like the kind of things I heard back in high school.”

“Probably would have been exactly the same things,” Chimney said with a nod. “From the moment, the very moment, I started at the station, Gerrard treated me like a janitor. I didn’t get to go on any calls until one of the paramedics, Eli, took me under his wing. The others had no interest in getting to know me at first. Eli said they were just ‘protecting’ themselves, that you didn’t name a puppy until you know it’s gonna pull through. I’m sure the fact that I was the only person of color in the entire building had nothing to do with it.”

Buck grimaced at the layer of bitterness that coated his friend’s words. It was old and tired bitterness, the kind that you didn’t really get over, just moved past.

“Hen, though.” Chimney shook his head. “Hen had it so much worse than me. I mean, whatever they thought about me, they at least didn’t say it where I could hear. Her first day, Gerrard introduced her to us all as the new diversity hire. Said she was, uh. Prettier than most.”

“Jesus Christ.”

“And then! Then he made sure she knew exactly what he thought of her. Told her, to her face, that she was gonna get one of us killed because there was no way a woman could do the job.”

That sounded familiar.

“Quick question: Why hasn’t Hen killed the probie yet?” Buck asked.

“‘Cause you were doing a good job keeping the kid on a leash and away from her,” Chimney answered. “But that’s off-topic. Or maybe not off-topic, but not what we’re talking about. The point I was making is that Hen started this job with everything pitted against her. She was a black lesbian, and Gerrard made sure she knew that meant she would never be good enough. That she was an albatross around all of our necks.”

“Okay…”

“Gerrard didn’t even see her as a firefighter,” Chimney stressed. “And then Bobby tells her that he wants her to be captain in his absence. That she could be a great captain.”

“Oh.” Buck was getting the picture. “She wouldn’t have been as upset if you’d been the one assigned to the job, would she? Or- Or Clarke.”

Chimney didn’t say anything. He simply grimaced and shrugged, which was answer enough.

“Okay,” Buck said, the word coming out in a sigh. “That explains why. Doesn’t really help me figure out what to do, though.”

“She’ll get over it,” the other man assured him. “She isn’t actually upset with you. It just hit her in an old wound. Just talk to her and clear the air.”

“Yeah, okay.”

It was Hen. Hen was his friend.

They’d be fine.

Later, while she and May and Bobby were leaving, Athena paused on the porch. She glanced at her husband, making a slow and stubborn progress to the car, before facing Buck.

“Eddie mentioned that there was an issue at the station,” she said. “With Hen?”

“Ma,” Buck groaned, resisting the urge to throw back his head in the exact manner as an exasperated Christopher.

“She hasn’t said anything to me, so I just know what Eddie told us,” Athena continued, ignoring his whining. She raised a finger when he started to say something. “And no, before you open your fool mouth, you being my kid is not affecting my friendship with her. No more than my being married to her boss.”

“I mean, she- If she felt more comfortable going to Chimney to vent and not you…”

“True, if it was anyone else, she probably would have had me over for a wine night,” the sergeant admitted. “But that’s not your fault, so do not start feeling guilty about it. And don’t try to lie to me that you weren’t about to apologize.”

Buck ducked his head with a, “yes, ma’am.”

“You were out back with Chimney, you said Hen vented to him,” she continued on. “I assume the two of you talked about this?”

“Yeah, he said it hit her in an old wound.”

“And I’m sure you can sympathize with her. You’re no stranger to old hurts making us see the worst of a situation.”

“Yeah.” It was why Buck acted like such an ass when he first met Eddie, and it was half the driving force behind the lawsuit debacle. “I plan on talking to her at the start of shift tomorrow.”

“Good,” Athena declared, before patting him on the cheek and stepping off the porch to catch up with Bobby and May.

The next morning, Buck did his best to tamp down his nerves as he waited for Hen to arrive for her shift. He figured he wasn’t doing too good of a job at it, judging by the amount of side-eying he was getting from the others. At one point, he was pretty certain Toby was about to come over to where he was peering over the loft railing and ask what was wrong, before the driver-engineer was intercepted by Albert. The paramedic shook his head and practically forced Toby to do an about face, dragging him to where he’d been lounging on the couches with the others.

(Not Wolfe. Wolfe had yet to arrive, but there was plenty of time for the probie to break his recent habit and show up before he was pushing tardiness.)

Buck spotted Hen coming in and made his way - sedately - down the stairs to wait for her to get changed out of her civvies and into her uniform. He caught her as she stepped out, and watched her face go flat at the sight of him. So, still upset.

“Hey, could we talk?” Buck asked her. “In Cap’s office”

“Sure,” she said, waving a hand for him to lead the way.

Neither of them talked as they crossed the bay, falling into a silence that was just this side of uncomfortable. It was only broken after they stepped into the office and Buck closed the door behind them. Hen was the one who spoke up, with a terse,

“So what did you need to talk about, captain?”

The way she said it had Buck letting out a tired sigh. He rubbed at the spot between his eyes and practically flopped into one of the chairs that sat in front of the desk.

“Really, Hen?”

His expression must have been even more hangdog than usual, because Hen’s shoulders slumped and she let out a sigh of her own.

“I’m sorry,” she said, taking the other chair. “I’m being an ass.”

“Little bit,” Buck agreed, curling away from her when she smacked at him. “I just… You were obviously upset on Friday. I just wanted to make sure that, y’know, we were okay.”

“Of course we’re okay,” Hen said, as if any other option was inconceivable to her. “I’ve just been an ass. I made an assumption and it made an ass out of me and- Well, just me. I don’t know what I was thinking, just assuming things like that.”

“To be fair, if you hadn’t gotten hurt, it probably would have been a fair assumption.”

“Yeah, but I was hurt and I wasn’t here. It makes no sense bouncing between captains until Bobby gets back. I knew that.” Hen shook her head. “I don’t know what I was thinking. Maybe I wasn’t.”

“Hey, I’ve done my fair share of not thinking.”

“Oh, I know,” she laughed. “I was just going through my morning, in this world where I was the one wearing the captain’s hat.”

“And then,” Buck said, finger tapping on the desk. “A white boy comes in and takes it away from you.”

Hen grimaced.

“You talked to Chim.”

“Well, my friend was upset with me, and I didn’t know what to do,” he told her. “But yeah, we spoke a bit last night. He drew me a nice picture of why. Gerrard sounded like a dick.”

“He was,” she agreed with a sage-like nod. Then she eyed Buck. “I’m afraid to think how you would have done under him.”

“Chim said I’d probably be fine, at least until they realized that I wasn’t as straight as they thought.”

“You started flirting with people on scenes within your first week, it wouldn’t have taken long,” Hen said, laughing again. It petered out with a sigh. “The way Gerrard acted, you would have thought I was sent here just to spite him personally. He thought I was a blight on the department, and made sure I knew it. And then last year, Bobby offers me the role as temporary captain, tells me how great at the job I would be. It was like everything I worked for, all the years of sweat and tears and literal blood, finally paying off.  And then on Friday, with you, I just felt…”

“Like it was stolen from you?” Buck guessed. “Like the rug was pulled out from under you?”

“Yeah.” She tilted her head at him consideringly. “I guess you would know. You never really did say why Bobby didn’t leave you in charge while he was gone those weeks.”

Buck’s finger tapped a quick pattern on the desk as he thought about it.

“Taylor,” he finally said.

“Taylor? Taylor Kelly?” Hen’s face was scrunched up in confusion. “What did she have to do with you being skipped over then?”

“The uh, the story she did about Jonah, and all the headaches that followed. The brass wasn’t very happy about it. Alonzo definitely wasn’t.”

“But what does that have to do with-” She cut herself off, looking stricken. “They blamed you for it!?”

Buck shrugged awkwardly.

“They needed someone to blame, and I never exactly kept our friendship a secret.”

“But I’m the one that got her involved,” Hen said. “She wouldn’t have known about anything if Chimney and I hadn’t gone to her. You didn’t tell them that?”

“I wasn’t gonna throw my friends under the bus,” he said, simple as that.

“God, now I feel even worse.”

“Hey, no.” Buck reached over, gripped her shoulder. “It’s fine. Water under the bridge. Pops said to give them time to get over it, and they did. And then Taylor put out that stupid book of hers and told everyone anyway, so it doesn’t matter.”

“And here I am, being all miserable because my ego’s bruised, when I only got the spot after I practically torpedoed your chances.”

Buck let go of her, rolling his eyes as he did.

“No, that was Taylor,” he corrected her. “And that’s not the only reason. Pops only started looking at me because Chimney was on a different shift at the time, and you were busy mentoring Albert. Plus, medical school.”

“Good call, we all saw how well I managed to juggle captain duties with school.”

“You were always his first choice. I was just-”

The alarm blared, interrupting him as dispatch rattled off details about a structure fire. They both leaped to their feet and headed for the bay.

“That’s early,” Hen commented. “I think the shift just started.”

“Another early one,” Buck groused. “The probie better have gotten in on time today. If he’s still pulling up his pants, I’ll make him run drills all day.”

That had Hen laughing as she split off for the ambulance.

Wolfe, as it turned out, was already half-way into his turnouts by the time Buck got to his own. So there was that, at least.

Shots, Shots, Shots

Chapter Summary

In which a pair of regularly scheduled trips to the doctor prove to be both good and bad.

“Alright, so,” the doctor said, pulling Eddie’s x-rays up on his screen. The badge on his chest said his name was Burton. “I have good news and bad news. Which do you want first?”

Buck and Eddie glanced at each other, the former doing his best to wrangle a squirming Gavin in his lap. They’d mistakenly assumed that since the appointment for the toddler’s vaccines was (not quite) right after Eddie’s own, that it would be more convenient to just bring him with for both. It was an assumption that they had spent the morning suffering for, because keeping a toddler entertained in a waiting room was much, much more difficult than it was at home. And no one at home glared at them when they allowed their little guy to run around the place.

At the moment, Gavin was thankfully entranced with the rainbow slinky his Auntie Hen had gotten him. Buck didn’t know how much longer it was going to last, though.

“Good,” Eddie finally said, perched on the edge of the paper covered examination bed.

“The good news,” Burton said, gesturing between the pictures he had side-by-side, “is that everything looks good for how long it’s been. You’re healing well.”

“Yeah, babe. Your ribs look less like a jig-saw puzzle in that one,” Buck commented, leaning over Gavin’s head to peer at the most recent x-ray.

“They feel like less of a jig-saw puzzle,” Eddie said. “So, does this mean you’re going to let me go back to work?”

“They’re healed enough that I’ll allow it,” Burton told him. “But that’s where the bad news comes in.”

Buck sat up straighter in his seat.

“What does that mean?”

“It means that your husband’s ribs look well enough that he can go back on shift. I don’t feel comfortable with clearing him to do anything too strenuous, though.” Burton turned in his seat to stare Eddie in the eye. “Your ribs are healing, but they aren’t healed yet. Strain them enough and you might find yourself right back where you were. Or worse.”

“So I can go back to work, I just can’t work,” Eddie groused.

“Light duty,” the doctor told him. “No running into burning buildings, no carrying anyone out of them, no handling of the hoses.”

“What about getting cats out of trees?” Buck asked, shooting his husband a shit-eatting grin.

“Depends on how big it is. A well fed Maine Coon might be pushing it.”

Eddie rolled his eyes at the both of them.

“When can I get put back on rotation?” he asked.

“As early as next week,” Burton told him. This time it was Buck’s turn to roll his eyes as Eddie gave a fist pump at the words. “Light duties. If your captain can’t find you something to do that won’t go against my recommendations, then you’ll have to wait.”

“Don’t worry,” Buck assured him. “I have it on good authority that his captain can be pretty accommodating.”

The look that Eddie gave him at those words held every smart-ass comment the man so desperately wished he could say but couldn’t, thanks to their current setting and audience. Buck just grinned even wider at him.

“Hear that, mijo?” Eddie said instead, reaching across to run his fingers through Gavin’s hair. “Your dad’s going back to work.”

“I’ll go get the paperwork sorted out for you,” the doctor said, getting to his feet and heading for the door. “Including what you’ll need to send to the department. I’m sure you know the routine by now.”

“So,” Buck said, once it was just them and Gavin. “We’re gonna have a fight over what you’ll be doing when you start back, aren’t we?”

“Probably,” Eddie agreed. “You’re stubborn, I’m stubborn. It’s gonna be nasty.”

“And loud. Lots of yelling, I bet.” Buck said. “Where should we have it? At home or at the station?”

“If we fight at home, we can have angry make-up sex afterwards.”

“That is very true,” he agreed. Then he sighed, adjusting his hold on Gavin as he started wiggling more, slapping the slinky against Buck’s shoulder. “Can we do this? Me being your captain?”

“We’ve done it before, mi sol.” Eddie held out his arms; Buck, after a moment, passed Gavin over.

“For a day. And you pi-” He glanced at Gavin, with his little innocent ears. “Peeved me off so bad I went to yell at Bobby about it.”

“Hey.” He held his hand out again until Buck took it in his own. “I think we can do this. But if we can’t, then… I’ll switch over to B-shift, work under Reynolds until Bobby gets back.”

“No, no.” Buck shook his head. “We’d barely see each other if you did that. And I like working with you, knowing you have my back and I have yours. No, I’ll talk to Chief Williams about- Shoot, we should have talked about this last week. If we can’t make it work, then I’ll talk to her about making Hen the substitute captain, instead.”

“No.” Eddie squeezed his hand. “You’re not giving it up because of me. Not when Abuela said you were literally jumping for joy after Williams came to you.”

“I wasn’t jumping, I was just bouncing. A lot.”

“Whatever. Look, you and Bobby do okay-”

“He’s my pops, not my husband,” Buck pointed out. “And did you forget the time we didn’t do okay and I ended up suing him? I know you were there for that, you yelled at me.”

“You’re doing fine now. The two of us, we’ve been together and working together for years, and we’ve been fine.”

“As coworkers. Next week, I’ll be your boss. I’ll be giving you orders and telling you what to do.”

“Oh, because you never get bossy with me. You’ve never given me orders or told me what to do before.” Eddie rolled his eyes and ducked his head to whisper to Gavin. “Do you hear your papa? Isn’t he silly?”

“Papa silly,” Gavin solemnly agreed, and then threw the slinky onto the floor with a giggle.

“The silliest.” Buck bent down and picked the toy up. “I guess a small part of me is still afraid that the brass will decide we can’t work together. That they’ll swoop in and separate us and then we’ll have to, I dunno, go through some convoluted and highly convenient series of emergencies to prove we belong together or something.”

“I think you need to watch fewer telenovelas,” was Eddie’s response. 

The nurse coming in with the paperwork ended the discussion, and they were soon heading out the door.

Gavin’s own doctor’s appointment was scheduled for only a couple hours after Eddie’s. It was at the same hospital, if obviously not the same ward, which was the entire reason they’d assumed it would be more convenient to haul a toddler around all morning, rather than rush back and forth across Los Angeles. In hindsight, Buck wasn’t certain if that second option wouldn’t have been better, considering it was a full-time job to keep Gavin from having a meltdown while waiting for Eddie to be seen.

There was a park with a playground right next to the hospital, so that’s where they went after a quick lunch. Gavin got to run his little legs off, even if he was still dwarfed by most of the equipment. He spent most of his time in one of the toddler swings, demanding to be pushed over and over and over again.

He was not happy when it was time to go.

The waiting room for pediatrics was at least designed with children in mind. Gavin was currently focused on one of those bead mazes, with the brightly colored rigid wires that wound around each other. He was sharing it with a girl that looked a few months older than him. Buck kept an eye on the pair the entire time, because seeing them together made him realize something very, very important.

“We need to socialize him more,” he announced.

His husband looked up from his phone and blinked at him.

“Like a puppy?”

“Well- Yeah, I guess.” Buck shrugged at him. “I just realized that the only kid his age he spends any time with is Jee-Yun. We don’t want him to start school as the weird kid that can’t make friends.”

“You mean we want him to be more like Chris and less like you,” Eddie teased. “He’s a year old and change, we have time.”

“Time that’s gonna fly by.”

“We’ll ask your sister about Jee’s daycare, how ‘bout that?” Eddie bumped his fist against Buck’s thigh. “Now, stop worrying about the future and start worrying about the present. My baby boy’s-”

“I thought Chris was your baby boy?”

“They’re both my baby boys, shut up. My baby boy’s about to get needles jabbed into his tiny little arms. How are we going to handle this?”

“I’m going to cry,” Buck admitted. “He’s gonna cry and scream, I’m gonna cry and maybe scream with him.”

“Yeah, okay, that’s fair.”

It started okay, mostly, with Gavin merely being a bit uncertain about what was going on while the nurse measured his height and weight. He didn’t fuss, either, when the doctor, a friendly woman named Baird, came in and took his vitals before working the regular questions.

And then, they got to the reason they were there. Which was when the tears started flowing.

“Alright,” she said, checking her records. “We’ve got him scheduled for six shots this time.”

“Six?” Buck blurted out, sharing a look with Eddie.

“DTaP, Hib, PCV, MMR, Varicella, and Hep A,” Baird listed, tapping each one in turn where they sat in a little basket. “There’s no mention of anything at twelve months. The last round he had was for his six month vaccinations, back in September.”

“We got custody of him at the end of January,” Eddie told her. Gavin was in his lap, looking around the pastel colored doctor’s office with curiosity. “He was put in emergency placement in December. We don’t really know anything before that.”

“I’m not judging,” she said, as if anyone ever said that and wasn’t judging. “If you take off his shirt, we’ll try to get this done as quickly as possible.”

It might have been as quick as possible, but it was still not quick enough. Gavin started screaming the moment Baird stuck him with the first one, letting out a wail that tore at Buck’s heartstrings. He cried and kicked his legs and flailed his arms, did pretty much everything he could do to get away from it. And he kept crying, even long after they were done and they were leaving the doctor’s office for home.

Not even Eddie’s beautiful milk lullaby - “That is not what it’s called, you ass.” - seemed able to calm him down.

“Arrorró, mi niño. Arrorró, mi sol. Arrorró, pedazo de mi corazón,” Eddie was singing, bouncing Gavin in his arms. They were currently standing by the jeep in the parking lot, not yet able to head home because Gavin would just cry harder every time they put him into his car seat. “Este niño lindo ya quiere dormir.”

“I think I’m anti-vax now,” Buck tried to joke, wiping at his face. He had, indeed, started crying with the little guy half-way through the ordeal. “Holy shit, that was awful.”

“Háganle la cuna de rosa y jazmín,” Eddie sang, the words quieting as Gavin finally did as well. He didn’t stop bouncing him, though. “There we go, mijo. It was scary, and we were really mean for doing that to you, I know.”

“Ow,” Gavin sniffed, burying his face into Eddie’s shoulder.

“Big ow,” Buck agreed, rubbing at his tiny back. He paused to grab something out of the back floorboard. “But look what I found…”

He held up the vibrant orange rabbit plushie that their kid had gotten attached to. Gavin grabbed it from him, clinging to it tightly. 

“Think you can go into your seat now?” Buck asked him. “Then we can go home and you can play with Hoover.”

Gavin scowled at him for a moment before giving a tiny, imperious nod. Buck took him out of Eddie’s arms, giving the man’s healing ribs a break, and started strapping their kid into the car seat. Eddie, meanwhile, went around to the other side and slid into the front passenger seat.

“Hey, babe?” he asked, his phone out. “Carlos wants to know if you’ve talked to TK today.”

“Haven’t heard from him since their flight landed yesterday,” Buck said. He ran a finger down Gavin’s nose before pushing himself out of the seat and let the car door close with a soft thump. He slid into the driver’s seat and pulled out his phone, just to be sure. “Yeah, nope. Nothing since then. Why?”

“Uh…” Eddie scowled down at his phone as he typed and then waited for a response. “He doesn’t want to say?”

“Well that’s not good.”

“Nope. He also said TK’s not working, so.”

“I can take a hint.”

Buck stuck his phone to the jeep’s dash and pulled up his friend’s contact, swiping his finger across his name. He set it to speaker and started pulling the jeep out of the parking lot while they listened to it ring. When TK finally answered, it was with an odd sounding,

“Hey, Sunshine.”

Buck frowned at his friend’s tone. Eddie was too, when he glanced over.

“Hey, Teek. How’s life back in the real world?”

“Oh, it’s, y’know. Already wishing I was back at the resort. I don’t go back to work until tomorrow, so I’m just in limbo.”

“Right, right. And the mom-in-law?”

TK let out an amused snort at that, at least.

“She’s… I won’t say she’s okay. She’s getting along. Carlos is over there, now.”

Meaning that Carlos probably started texting Eddie the moment the door was closed behind him. Buck tried to rack his brain to come up with whatever could be bothering TK enough for Carlos to urge him to reach out, but also something he didn’t feel comfortable with telling them himself.

“So, hey, did you ever find out what your dad was being squirrely about?”

“Oh.” TK’s tone changed, enough for Buck’s eyebrows to raise. “Yeah, I- I talked to him. Uh, Uncle Robert’s dead.”

“What?” Buck blurted out. “When?”

“Right after the wedding, apparently. Dad said it had something to do with his Huntington’s.”

They stopped at a red light, long enough for Buck to share a wide-eyed look with Eddie and to mouth, ‘what the fuck?’ Buck might not have spent much time with the man at the wedding reception, but he hadn’t seemed at all like he was about to keel over at any moment. In fact, if Buck hadn’t already known about the Huntington’s, he wouldn’t have even guessed that anything was wrong with him.

“Wow, that’s… I’m sorry?” Buck winced at his own words. “Are you okay?”

“I don’t know. I don’t know if I’m sad. I’m supposed to be sad, right? I mean, I am, but he was my uncle. I should be sadder. Right?”

“I’m not going to tell you how to feel.”

“I wish someone would,” TK grumbled.

“You- You only knew him for like, a couple weeks, right?” Buck said. “He was practically a stranger to you.”

“He was still my uncle. Dad’s pretty torn up about it.”

“You’re not your dad, don’t base your feelings and reactions on his,” he said, channeling his inner Copeland. “Uh, have you talked to Cooper?”

Cooper was TK’s sponsor at AA. Probably a good person for TK to talk to if he was feeling guilty for not being upset enough.

“Wow, I can’t believe the spirit of my still-living husband has managed to possess you from over a thousand miles away.” TK was such a smartass. “I was about to call him when you called me.”

Buck rolled his eyes at that.

“Liar.”

“Oh, shut up. Wasn’t your kid getting his shots today? How did that go?”

That was a very obvious and unsubtle attempt at changing the topic. But Buck was going to be a good bro and not call him out on it.

“Awful. There was screaming, there were tears. It was a brand new trauma.”

“Right, right, and how did Gavin handle it?”

“Not much better…”

Marginal Danger

Chapter Summary

In which Buck experiences a hardship or two of being captain.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Gavin continued to be fussy for the rest of the day, and then the next one. His tiny arms were sore and tender, and he woke up Wednesday morning with a fever. It made him grumpy and clingy, demanding to be held by one of his fathers constantly, which led to a delay of plans in regards to Operation Fight-and-Make-Up. They almost didn’t go to the Dodgers’ game, either, except Abuela put her foot down and threatened to start smacking people with a spoon. The little old lady practically chased Buck and Eddie out of the house, locking the door behind them.

Thursday morning, the day before Jones’ funeral, started off on a higher note. Gavin was clearly feeling better, his small fever breaking the evening before, though he was still demanding affection and attention. Eddie ended up taking care of that, since he didn’t have anywhere to go; Carla was taking Chris to school.

The atmosphere at the station, when Buck arrived for his shift, was heavy. Darkly anticipatory, like they were pregaming for the funeral to come. He stowed his work bag in the locker he shared with Eddie and talked with Captain Mayfair, when she caught him coming out of the locker room. But afterwards, he didn’t go upstairs to work on breakfast, he didn’t go to Bobby’s office for any outstanding paperwork from his previous shift, he didn’t bother any of the others as they came in or got ready to leave.

He was distracted by a problem he’d been putting off for a month now.

“You’re staring at that pretty hard,” Hen remarked, her voice startling him a bit as she stepped up beside him. When Buck looked over, he saw she was already in uniform.

“I still don’t know what to do with this,” Buck told her, reaching up to tap Jones’ name plate on his turnout cage. The cage was empty, as it had been since they rushed out the morning of the bridge collapse. “I know it can’t stay there, that we’ll need to let someone else use the cage eventually. But it feels like… I dunno.”

“Like we’re desecrating something,” Hen suggested. Buck nodded and snapped his fingers.

“Yeah, exactly!”

“Maybe we could ask the others, they might have a thought or two about it.”

“That’s a good idea,” Buck said. He chewed on his lip for a moment, before pivoting on his foot and heading for the station’s loft, where everyone tended to gather before the shift officially started. “I’ve thought about asking Pops, but it didn’t seem like good dinner conversation.”

“How is Bobby doing?” Hen asked him as she followed along. “I only know what Athena’s told me, which has mostly been complaining about how stubborn he’s being.”

“He’s…” Buck paused on the first step, glanced around, and lowered his voice. “I don’t think he’s doing okay. Ma told you that the fall hurt his back again?”

“Yeah.”

“He’s moving like an old man, Hen. Actually, no, I’ve seen dying old men that could walk across a room easier than he can, right now.”

“Well,” she said, after a moment. “It was a bad injury the first time, right? And Bobby ain’t no spring chicken, anymore.”

“Yeah, so everyone keeps reminding me.” Buck did his best to keep from snapping at her. It wasn’t Hen's fault that other people had hammered that point before her. “But that doesn’t exactly make me less worried, y’know? And he and Ma aren’t telling me anything except, ‘don’t go borrowing trouble, Buckaroo.’ What if there’s something more and they just aren’t telling anyone?”

“It hasn’t even been a full month since the accident,” she pointed out. “Maybe give it a bit longer before you go full doom-and-gloom.”

“How long?” he asked. “What’s the amount of time I should let pass before I get to be worried?”

Hen scrunched up her lips in thought.

“Two months,” she told him, holding up her fingers. “If he’s not any better by the end of July, and they still aren’t telling you anything, then you get to be worried.”

“Fine,” Buck huffed out. “Gavin had a doctor’s appointment Tuesday, to get his shots.”

“Fun. How did that go?”

“We both cried.”

Hen’s cackling trailed him up the stairs.

“Good news!” Buck belted out as he crested the top step, clapping his hands together. “Eddie’s cleared to come back.”

“Does this mean I can go back to my station and leave all you weirdos behind?” Lena asked. She was leaning back against the loft railing, arm’s spread wide. “No offense to all you weirdos. Or you, Toby. You’re the only other normal person here.”

“Uh. Thanks?” Toby broke off from plaintively staring at the coffee machine as it did its magic to frown at Lena.

“Some offense taken,” Crawford said. They were perched on the back of the couch that Albert and Ravi were lounging on. “I’m B-shift. It’s A-shift that’s full of weirdos.” They glanced over in time to catch Clarke and Ortiz’s glare from across the loft. “What!? It’s true!”

“They’re not wrong,” Ravi turned around to add. “B-shift has far less drama than we do.”

“Awesome Shift is not weird, we’re entertaining and full of personality. Boring Shift is just boring. And lame,” Buck said. “And the doc was very clear that Eddie was to do light duties only, so he’ll be helping the paramedics a lot instead of anything physical. But Lena, if you just really want to abandon us in our time of need…”

“I do,” she bluntly stated. “But I also want to see if this leads to another blow-up, so I guess I’ll stick around.”

“Thanks,” he drawled, as dry as the Mojave.  “Your sacrifice is appreciated.”

“It better be.”

“How is this going to work?” Toby piped up to ask. The coffee was done brewing and he was hurriedly pouring himself a mug. “He’s your husband, right? Won’t it be weird for him to have you giving him orders and telling him what to do?”

Buck smirked and opened his mouth, only for Hen to stick her finger in his face with a barked out,

“Do. Not.”

“Rude! I wasn’t gonna say anything like that,” Buck protested, playing up the offense and batting at her finger. She rolled her eyes at him. “It’ll be fine. Eddie and I are adults, and we’ve worked together this long without any problems.” Albert cleared his throat. “Okay, one time. And that was me being stupid and totally deserved.”

“You say that like it doesn’t happen often,” Hen said, heading over to get coffee for herself, now that Toby wasn’t hovering possessively over it. “Eddie’s actually okay with being stuck on light duties for however long?”

“We’re gonna send Abuela and the boys to Pops’ on Saturday, so we can yell at each other about it in peace,” Buck said. The others laughed. “He can either be okay with light duties, or he can keep being a stay-at-home dad until Doctor Burton says otherwise. Them’s his choices.”

“Twenty on them having a blowout before the end of Eddie’s first shift back,” Lena said. Buck turned his head to scowl at her, only to redirect it at Hen when the paramedic chimed in with,

“I’ll get the box out of my locker.”

“Or,” Buck cut in, rolling his eyes at them, “we could not make bets on me and my husband having an argument at work. That’s an option, too.”

“But that isn’t as fun,” Ravi said, and grinned at the glare the comment got him.

“I miss that brief period when you were afraid of me,” Buck groused, shaking his head at them all. He looked over the bay, spotting Wolfe lingering downstairs just outside the locker room. “The probie’s here and in uniform on time for the second shift in a row. If he keeps his mouth shut today, he might just be my new favorite.”

“That is a bet no one’s going to take,” Hen told him, patting him on the shoulder as she passed him to go back downstairs. Presumably to fetch the aforementioned box.

“Gambling in the station is against regulations!” he hollered after her, like a hypocrite, but she just laughed at him again. 

It had become a minor habit for Buck to keep track of the probie. At least enough to know if the kid was on the verge of saying something that would have Buck doing more paperwork. The fact that the kid wasn’t very popular on their shift, and so the others avoided him as best they could, helped with the endeavor. But the fact that Buck was keeping an eye on him is how he noticed that Wolfe was acting a bit high-strung.

In the not-quite thirty minutes since their shift started, the kid hadn’t spent any time upstairs. Instead, he’d stuck to the truck bay, hovering near their turn-outs. Seriously, the kid hadn’t moved more than twenty feet from them. And the entire time, he kept glancing up at the station alarm, like he was expecting it to go off at any moment.

It was weird. Buck couldn’t remember him acting like this previously, but then again, the kid hadn’t the chance the last few shifts. The one shift they hadn’t been called out within minutes of being on the clock, was the shift he’d sent the kid upstairs to clean dishes before he could make an ass of himself with Hen.

It was weird. And Buck could either find out what was going on, or he could leave Wolfe to it. What would Bobby do?

From Buck’s own experience, he could reliably state that Bobby would probably walk the other way while pretending he didn’t see anything. Even if what he was pretending not to see was his subordinates threatening each other with an ax. Bobby was a wise man, and his example was one to be followed.

Except, the moment that Buck turned on his heel and headed for literally anywhere else, the alarm finally went off. Dispatch rattled off details about a house fire while they rushed for their gear, Wolfe already half-way into his just by virtue of being the closest of any of them.

“We’re heading for West Twenty-Ninth Place,” Buck relayed once they were all in and he’d snatched up the tablet. “Place, not Street. Take us down Western.”

“Got it, Cap,” Toby said with a nod, already pulling the engine out of the station.

The house they were responding to was just off of Western Ave. They could spot the smoke rising from it as they crossed the Freeway, which had Buck urging Toby to drive that much faster. It might have been a cute bungalow, once upon a time. But as they pulled to a screeching halt in front of it, Buck could already tell that it was a loss.

“Get those hoses hooked up!” he bellowed as they all jumped out, his gaze fixed on the house-shaped inferno in front of him. How did it get this far involved without someone calling it in earlier? It was already threatening to envelope one of its neighbors if they didn’t hurry. “I want water hitting Bravo side now. And I want those two homes emptied!”

“On it!” Ravi said, rushing for the hydrant down the street with a large wrench in hand and the supply line over his shoulder. A couple others, Perez and Hendrix, went for the neighbors.

“Dispatch, One Eighteen on-scene,” Buck relayed into the radio. “House is fully involved and threatening neighboring structures. We might need assistance.”

“Understood, One Eighteen.” It was Maddie’s voice on the other end. “We’ve lost contact with the caller.”

He shared a look with Hen, who’d come to stand beside him. Wolfe had joined as well, practically bouncing on his toes as he awaited orders. Buck spared him a quick scowl before returning his attention to his sister.

“Do we know his location?”

“...No.”

Every fiber of his being was telling him to put on oxygen and rush into that house. And mere months ago, he would have done it gladly and without pause. But that wasn’t his role today. His role today was to decide if any of the others were going to do it in his stead. His role today was to decide whether or not he was going to risk his people searching through that raging inferno for someone that may or may not already be a lost cause.

It made his stomach twist.

(“You used to be reckless,” Bobby had told him once, over a year ago now, while they waited for their flight back home to be called. “And you’re only ever reckless with yourself. Whenever you propose a dangerous idea, it’s always with yourself in mind. You’re actually very careful about not putting anyone else in danger.”)

“Get on the hoses,” Buck ordered, not even looking at the probie. He was busy staring at the burning house, flames claiming just about every inch of it that he could see.

“What!?” Wolfe sounded dismayed. “Cap-”

“Go,” Buck snapped. Wolfe puffed up, like he was actually going to try and argue about this. Not that Buck was not going to give him the chance. “Now, probie. Or you can go sit in the truck.”

Wolfe turned red, either with embarrassment or with rage, Buck didn’t know which. What he did know was that, after a moment of fuming silence, the kid finally stomped off to do as he was told.

“Are we going in?” Hen asked after he was gone, the words quiet.

“Jones’ funeral is tomorrow,” Buck stated. He chewed on his lip, fingers tapping away at his thigh as he tried to decide what to do. “That structure is not safe.”

“It’s not,” she agreed.

“There’s someone in there.”

“There is.”

Buck’s instincts were screaming at him to try and save whoever was inside. They were also screaming that it was too dangerous for him to send in any of the others. It was a problem that was so much simpler just a few weeks ago, when he would have been the one going in.

“Okay,” he breathed out. And then a bit louder, “Okay. Bosko! Toby! Strap on your gear, you’re going inside.”

The pair of them, in the middle of assisting with the hoses, gave a nod and rushed for the engine’s compartment that held the oxygen tanks. Hen hurried over to where Albert and Crawford were staging, getting ready for the person that she would (hopefully) be treating soon.

“You have five minutes,” Buck told Lena and Toby. “When I radio you to get out, you do it.”

They nodded at him again before jogging to the house’s front door. Standing off just to the side of it, Lena used a battering ram to open the way. A fireball didn’t immediately explode outward, just a wall of smoke, so they went on in.

And Buck waited and hoped he hadn’t made a mistake.

They didn’t make the full five minutes. It was barely three, before they were reporting that their way forward was cut off by the fire. They complained - or, well, Lena complained - when Buck ordered them to come on out empty handed.

He didn’t relax until he could lay eyes on them both.

Just before midnight, Chief Williams arrived at the station. She had Buck gather everyone in the bay, which meant waking up the ones who had decided to catch some sleep in the bunks. It ended up being a ruffled bunch that stood, attentive, before her.

“Tomorrow, we’re all saying goodbye to a brother,” she told them. “Some of you signed up as Honor Guards, others as pallbearers, the rest are simply paying respects. Either way, each of us has a long and uniquely exhausting day to look forward to. Because of that, and especially because of the use of your station’s apparatus in the proceedings as well as the station itself for the reception, the One Eighteen is being taken offline until the start of shift on Saturday.”

She gave them a once-over.

“Head home, get some proper sleep,” she ordered. “We are all going to look our best tomorrow.”

There was a smattering of “yes sir”s from the gathered firefighters before they all dispersed, heading for the locker room to change and go home. Buck lingered, though, hesitantly and unsure. The battalion chief raised an eyebrow at him once it was just him and her standing there.

“Yes, Diaz?”

“Eddie’s cleared to come back to work,” he blurted out.

“I know, I saw the paperwork.”

“And that’s… Okay?” He grimaced at himself. “I- I just mean- He’s my husband. I’m acting captain. So I just…”

Williams closed her eyes and let out a sigh, as if talking to him about this made her so very tired. It was a reaction he was more than used to.

“Diaz,” she said, crossing the distance to stand right in front of him. “I’m sure you’re well aware of how much of a boys’ club this department used to be. Right?”

“Yes, ma’am,” Buck answered with a frown. “But I don’t…”

“Not too long ago,” Williams continued, like he hadn’t said anything, “it was a sausage fest. A pit of testosterone. Hell, it still is. And because it was all men, no one bothered to come up with rules or regulations about relationships between fellow firefighters. Because clearly, since there weren’t any women around, it would never become an issue. Right?”

“W- Well…”

“We’re still playing catch up, honestly,” she said. “These days, aside from the waivers, we leave the rules about in-station fraternizing up to the discretion of the individual captains. And that is as far as we’ve gotten.”

“S- So you’re saying it’s perfectly okay for my husband to work under me,” Buck said, trying to understand, “because of homophobia and misogyny?”

“Yes, that is exactly what I’m saying. It’s simply never been considered important enough to address and so we haven’t gotten around to it.” Williams reached up then and grasped his shoulder. It was a very tight grip. “You will not give us any reason to get around to it. Understood?”

“Y- Yes, ma’am,” Buck quickly agreed, trying not to squirm beneath her glare.

“Perfect.” She let him go, gave him a pat, and turned around to leave. “I’ll see you tomorrow, Diaz!”

“Yes, ma’am,” he said again, rubbing his shoulder.

Several people were liable to die of laughter from the irony when they heard about this.

Chapter End Notes

I actually rewrote part of this chapter at the very last minute, because I was staring at it like, "Buck would not do that!"

Also, I managed to find a draft of the updated rules&regs from a few years back. Relationships between firefighters were not mentioned AT ALL. And it amused me to come up with a reason for why that would be.

And Darkness Comes Too Early

Chapter Summary

In which there is a funeral.

The porch light was off when Buck pulled into the driveway. There was a lamp on in the living room, visible through the window. He spotted the curtains moving while he was getting his bag out of the backseat, and so he wasn’t surprised when the front door opened before he even touched it.

“You’re home early,” was Eddie’s worried greeting. He was backlit, with Senorita a little black silhouette perched precariously on his shoulder. It was an adorable habit of hers that would inevitably lead to bloodshed when she got bigger. “By about eight hours.”

“Nothing happened. Williams just sent us all home so we didn’t embarrass ourselves by falling asleep at the service,” Buck hurried to explain, stepping inside and letting his husband close the door behind him. He scrunched up his nose at the smell that hit him, and he spotted both his and Eddie’s dress shoes sitting on the floor in front of the couch. There was an open tin of shoe polish on the coffee table, with an accompanying cloth.

“Couldn’t sleep,” Eddie told him, answering the unspoken question. “Figured they should be shiny for tomorrow.”

“Thanks, babe.”

Buck debated for a moment about carrying his bag down the hall, but he ended up just dropping it by the couch before settling into the cushions with a sigh. Hoover padded up to him, drawn out from whichever room he was camping in by the sound of his arrival, to demand his regular tribute of pettings. When Eddie joined him, picking up the cloth and one of the shoes and going back to polishing it, Buck let himself slide a bit so he could rest his head on Eddie’s shoulder.

They sat like that for Buck didn’t know how long. Long enough for Senorita to hop off Eddie’s shoulder and drape herself on Buck’s thigh, instead. Long enough for Hoover to spin a circle into the floor and curl up at their feet. Long enough for Buck’s thoughts, medicated as he was, to wander away from him so he blurted out,

“Were we even friends?”

Eddie twisted his head around to look at him. Buck was busy looking at the wall behind the (turned off) television and so he didn’t see his husband’s expression, but Eddie sounded offended and slightly confused when he said,

“You are my best friend.”

“What?” Buck raised his head to frown at him. “Oh! No, not us. I meant Jones. Were we even friends with him?”

“Of course we were,” Eddie said, going back to his task.

“Were we? I don’t even know his brother’s name.”

“Do you know the name of Bobby’s?”

Buck opened his mouth to say that of course he knew the name of Bobby’s brother. Except in that moment he realized with dawning horror that, no. No he did not.

“Shit,” he hissed, sitting up.

“Maybe we weren’t as close to Jones as we are with Hen,” Eddie said, ignoring the metaphorical grenade he’d just lobbed through Buck’s metaphorical window. “Doesn’t mean we weren’t friends. And don’t bother Bobby right now, he’s probably asleep.”

Buck set his phone down on the end table, having pulled it out to do exactly that. 

“It’s just…” He paused to lick his lips and find his words. “I feel like we took him for granted, y’know? He was so quiet all the time, it was easy to forget he was there. Even when he was driving us around places.”

Eddie let out a hum.

“C’mon,” he said, getting to his feet and nudging Buck to do the same. “Our shoes are as shiny as they’ll get, and we have a long day ahead of us in a few hours. Let’s get some sleep.”

“Yeah, okay.”

Buck gently peeled Senorita off his thigh before following Eddie to their bedroom, his freshly shined shoes in one hand and his work bag in the other. The garment bags holding their Class A dress uniforms were hanging from their closet door, ready and waiting for tomorrow. The shoes went beneath them and his bag was once again dropped onto the floor, this time beside their laundry hamper. They undressed and got ready for bed, and Eddie bullied Buck under the covers before wrapping around him like kudzu.

Morning came far too quick, their alarm dragging them back into the waking world with a piercing wail. Gavin was wide awake, judging from the noises coming from the monitor, and Buck pulled himself out of bed to take care of that and make sure Chris was moving, while Eddie went to take the first shower.

Carla let herself in, around the same time that Buck was coming back from letting the dog out to do his business. Gavin ran up to greet her, and she happily bent down to press a kiss to his cheek.

“You’re here early,” Buck remarked, frowning at the clock on the wall to make sure he wasn’t running later than he thought.

“I figured you and your hubby would be a bit busy this morning, getting ready,” Carla said, setting her purse down on the breakfast bar. “Good morning, Isabel.”

“Hola,” Abuela chirped from where she was fixing breakfast. She’d gotten up shortly after Buck had.

“We have a bit of time to get ready.” Buck glanced at the clock again as he dug through the cabinets for the pet food. “Not much, though. Maddie and Chimney are coming over to drop off Jee-Yun, since the Lees are both working, and then we’re driving with them.”

Senorita dove head first into her food bowl as soon as he started filling it, leaving her half-buried in kibble. Not that she seemed to notice.

“When does it start?” Carla asked.

“Nine.”

The aide’s eyebrows went up.

“Then you really don’t have much time,” she said. “Go, Isabel and I’ve got the boys.”

“Eddie’s in the shower still, so-”

“Buck!” his husband called from down the hall. “Your turn!”

“Nevermind.”

Carla laughed and shoved at his shoulder, urging him on. When he got to the bedroom, Eddie was still dripping, towel wrapped around his waist. Buck barely got a moment to appreciate the sight before he was being shoved again, this time not nearly as gently.

The shower was quick and perfunctory, a skill he’d been forced to learn because emergencies did not particularly wait for convenient times to happen. When he came out, his garment bag was waiting for him on the bed, unzipped with his dress uniform sitting on top of it. Eddie was already in his, except for his shoes.

Eddie glared at him when Buck offered to help with them, and glared harder when Buck snorted at his wince when he bent to put them on.

“Well don’t you both look spiffy,” Carla remarked when they came out, caps tucked under their arms. “C’mon Chris, we need to go.”

“Coming!” the preteen called out, hurrying from his room with his bag.

Eddie stopped him on his way by.

“We may not be back by the time you’re home,” he told him, wrapping arm around his son’s shoulders. Chris, in turn, immediately squirmed out of it. “Enjoy your last day.”

“We’re not gonna do anything,” Chris complained as he went. “Bye Dad, bye Pops!”

“Love you!” Buck shouted after him. Chris shouted something back that might have been “love you, too” but he closed the door behind him at the same time, so Buck couldn’t be sure. “He’s not wrong, the last day of school is always a waste. Woah, watch it little guy. Papa needs to look nice.”

Gavin, sitting in his booster seat, continued making grabbing motions for Buck with hands covered in the food he decided was better played with than eaten. Abuela tutted, grabbing a washcloth to clean him off.

“Are you sure you’re okay watching both him and Jee together by yourself, if Maddie gets here before Carla’s back?” Eddie asked her.

“Bah!” Abuela flapped a hand at him. “I am old, not incapable. And it wouldn’t be for very long.”

“Oh, por favor perdóname, worrying about my abuela breaking another hip while running after a pair of toddlers.”

“Why would I run after them? We have a dog for that.”

Buck just shrugged when Eddie turned to him for support. Because she had a good point.

The arrival of Maddie and Jee-Yun a short while later was announced by a series of tiny knocks that was followed by Maddie letting them both in. She was dressed in black, a dress that fell to just below her knees and a thin sweater over top of that.

“I didn’t see Carla’s car,” she said, setting Jee’s bag on the couch and her carseat on the floor. The little girl herself immediately ran for her Uncle Buck, who picked her up and swung her around. “Isabel, are you-?”

“Bah!” the old woman snapped, even as Buck and Eddie laughed.

“We’ve already had this argument today,” Eddie said. “The Diaz stubbornness comes from her.”

“Mi Edmundo was a mule,” Abuela countered with a sniff. “I was the only one who could be as stubborn as him. Ramon and Pepa had no chance.”

“Good thing these two decided to adopt, then,” Maddie remarked. Her brother squawked in offense. “Howie’s in the car. You ready to go?”

“Ready as we can be for this,” Buck said, setting Jee down, who pouted until she spotted Hoover. 

Chimney was sitting up front, and it seemed rather petty to make a man with a healing gut wound move to the back. Which meant Buck and Eddie ended up folding themselves into the back, a task that was unpleasant for the both of them. At least the other two seemed to get a kick out of it, judging from Chimney’s smirk and the picture of them that Maddie took

“The Cathedral of Our Lady of the Angels, right?” Maddie asked as they started off.

“Yeah,” Buck confirmed, leaning forward a bit. “Was Jones religious?”

“He’s feeling guilty about not knowing him better,” Eddie explained.

“I think it’s just one of the only places big enough to hold everyone that’ll be there,” Chimney said. “Line-of-Duties always draw a crowd.”

“We wouldn’t know,” Eddie said. “Which is a good thing.”

“The last one was just before I started, I think,” Buck said, trying to remember. Funerals for off-duty deaths and the retired tended to be smaller and less of an affair. “So I haven’t been to any.”

“I’ve been to too many,” Chimney said, looking out the window rather than at any of them. Maddie reached over to squeeze his hand.

He didn’t say much of anything else for the rest of the drive.

A funeral for a firefighter who fell in the line of duty came with much pomp and circumstance. Austin Jones’ was not any different, especially with his parents choosing to have one with full honors.

It started with a procession from City Hall to the cathedral, led by the honor guard along with pipes and drums, with the casket on the back of the 118’s engine. Bobby and Chimney, however much they grumbled and complained and insisted, were taken directly to the cathedral to wait with everyone there. The path there was lined with even more firefighters in their dress uniforms, standing at attention and saluting as they went by.

When they arrived at the cathedral, Buck and the others that had signed up to be pallbearers - Ravi and Albert and Ortiz, along with Jones’ brother and brother-in-law - carefully (so very carefully) pulled it off the truck and carried it inside, with Jones’ family following along and the pipes and drums leading the way once again. Once inside, and after the casket was set down at the front of the sanctuary, they went and stood in front of their seats while waiting for the others. They, too, didn’t sit down until everyone had filed in.

It took a very long time before they could sit.

After that, the funeral went as most funerals did, or so Buck assumed. It wasn’t like he’d been to very many, after all. People went up and talked, and they talked, and they talked. The mayor talked, the fire chief talked, the president of the union talked. A lot of people who had never once spoken to Jones stood up there and talked about him, saying a lot of pretty things about duty and privilege.

The only exceptions were Jones’ sister and his father. And Bobby.

“I am still not sure if we choose this life,” the captain said, clutching tight to the podium. He walked the entire way there from the pew with his back straight, though his face was lined with pain. “Or if this life chooses us. Some of us run into the fire, eyes wide open, while for others, the fire comes to them. Whichever way it happens, when we answer the call, it changes our lives forever. And in being changed, we, in turn, change the lives of others. For the better, I hope.

“I don’t know which one Austin Jones was, if he chose it or if it chose him. He never said, and I never thought to ask. That was the thing about him, the way he rarely told much of anything. He was always there, always dependable, a vital pillar of the station, the man who drove us to every emergency so we could change a person’s life for the better. But he was so very quiet.”

Bobby paused. Buck could see his grimace, however subtle he tried to be about it, and could just about make out the way his knuckles were turning white with how tight his grip on the podium was.

“He was so very quiet,” Bobby continued. “He would come in every shift and do his job and do it well. Dependable, reliable. But content to keep to himself, content with himself. When we, far too often, fell to foolishness in the tedium between emergencies, he was content to watch and to listen, rather than join. But he was there, always. One of us even when he didn’t join in. On the bad days, on the good days, on the days in between.

“He was there, and now he’s not. And however quiet he may have been, the silence he is leaving behind will be louder than anything.”

Buck wasn’t certain if that was the intended end, or if Bobby just reached his limit. Either way, the captain nodded and walked back to his seat. He trembled the entire way, and Buck saw Athena dig out a prescription bottle, shaking out whatever it contained into her hand to force onto her husband. Buck felt like he was spying on something he shouldn’t see, quickly turning back to face the front again.

After Bobby, after the speeches were all done, after Jones’ parents were given flags and certificates and medals, came the bell. Ten rings, to symbolize the end of Jones’ duty.

His mother, Tabitha, had been silently crying the entire time. His sister started in the middle of her eulogy and hadn’t stopped, and his brother while they carried the casket in. But his father had remained stone faced the entire time, as implacable as his son. 

Until the bell.

That was when Jones’ father let out a wailing sob that echoed through the entire building.

“Pretty sure I hate bagpipes now,” Buck announced, taking off his cap and throwing onto the table in front of him. They were at the station, the bay filled with tables and chairs, and catering provided by someone in the department or the union. The trucks and ambos were parked out front.

After the funeral, Buck and the other pallbearers carried the casket to an awaiting hearse. While the rest of them went to the station for the reception, the family followed the hearse to the cemetery. Buck wasn’t certain if they were going to bother with putting in an appearance again, or if they would go elsewhere to continue mourning in private.

“Does anyone like bagpipes?” Eddie asked, sitting down next to him.

“Statistically, someone does,” Hen said. She glanced over to where Bobby was sitting at another table, Athena having pushed him into the seat and was now looming over him like a threat. “And I’ll grant that you were right. There might be something to be worried about.”

“Thank you!” Buck was just relieved that someone agreed with him.

“I still say you give it time before catastrophizing.”

“Yeah, whatever.”

“Hey.” Eddie poked Buck in the side, digging in with a finger and making him squirm away. “Go get me a drink?”

“Seriously? Your ribs were broken, not your legs.” That, of course, got him a pair of the saddest puppy dog eyes aimed his way. Buck rolled his own and shoved himself to his feet. “Fine, fine.”

“Gracias, mi sol!”

 The man was lucky that Buck loved him.

The only other person at the table with the drinks was Ortiz, who nodded to him in greeting. But the man’s attention was mostly on the table Bobby was sitting at. When Buck turned, he saw that Wolfe had joined him, along with a man he vaguely recognized as Deputy Chief Torres, the probie’s uncle.

“How much do you think the probie complains about us?” Ortiz asked, idly taking a sip of his punch.

“Depends on the day, I think,” Buck answered, filling a cup for himself as well as Eddie.

“They’re smiling too much for them to be talking about that, at least,” the other man said. “If he was ragging on Cap for being too hard on his precious nephew, the sergeant would be at someone’s throat by now.”
