Athena, standing beside her husband, was being perfectly polite. But she was also prone to overprotectiveness, especially with people close to her being hurt, so Ortiz probably had a point.

“If the probie was going to complain to his uncle about anyone, it would probably be me,” Buck said. Ortiz barked out a laugh.

“Probably. You and Hen. And Jones, after that morning he snapped. It was the only time I have ever heard him raise his voice like that.”

“Same,” Buck agreed. “Hey, why did Jones yell that day? I meant to ask, but…”

“Ah, well.” Ortiz sighed. “The probie was talking about that fire. Or, what he’d have done. How, if he’d been there, no one would have died because he wouldn’t have just stood outside.”

“Oh.” Well. Fuck you very much, Wolfe.

“Don’t let him get to you, man,” Ortiz told him, clapping a hand on his shoulder. “He’s an idiot, you’re a good firefighter. If you thought it was too dangerous to go in, then it was too dangerous to go. Especially without your gear.”

“Th- Thanks.”

“Not a problem, man. Anyways, Toby is around here somewhere. I’m gonna drag him out from wherever he’s hiding away.”

Ortiz left with a nod, and Buck figured he should be getting back to Eddie with his drink before he got too impatient. Except, a thought struck him, and he stopped to frown across the bay at Wolfe.

The probie, according to Ortiz, had said he wouldn’t have stayed outside, that he would’ve gone into the house, would have saved Shanna and her baby. But Buck didn’t remember mentioning anything that morning about what he and Eddie had actually done in the face of the fire, not until the others had all dispersed and Bobby came to talk to the two of them. Wolfe was already down the stairs by the time Eddie mentioned it, wasn’t he?

Buck glanced at the station loft, his frown growing deeper, before going off to rejoin Edde and Hen.

Their voices must have carried, he decided. The voices must have carried, because he couldn’t think of any other explanation for it. After all, it wasn’t like Wolfe had been there to see it.

Another Side

Chapter Summary

In which we focus on Athena for a bit, and meet someone with half of a puzzle.

Saturday Afternoon

There was a minor cacophony of noise coming from behind the door, which got worse the moment Athena knocked. That dog her husband took into protective custody - read, stolen and then rehomed - started barking its fool head off. There was a thump from somewhere deeper in the house, the pitter-pattering of small feet followed by the thudding of ones much larger, and giggling that promised nothing good. She waited patiently, the noises getting louder and somewhat more worrisome, until someone finally answered the door.

“Ma!” was how Buck greeted her, shirt askew and panting a little. “Come in, come- Hoover, back up.”

“Having troubles today?” she asked, not even trying to hide her amusement. The dog was on her the moment she was inside, tail wagging while he nosed at her legs. These pants would need to be thrown into the wash as soon as she was home, or else she’d be sneezing when the allergy meds wore off.

“Abuela went out with Pepa, something about church friends or other grandchildren she liked more, I don’t know,” Buck said with a shrug. “And Eds’ not back yet.”

Athena glanced back at the door, in the direction of Eddie’s truck that she had seen sitting in the driveway, before turning back to her stepchild with a raised eyebrow.

“Stanley came by and took him to their meeting,” was the answer to her unasked question.

“Is he still not cleared to drive himself?” Athena had assumed that he was, since he was returning to work the next day.

“He is,” Buck said. “We just forgot to tell Stanley.”

“Nana!” came a small cry, and suddenly a tiny human was throwing himself into Athena’s leg. “Nana! Nana! Nana!”

“Hey, there, baby.” Athena bent down and plucked Gavin off the floor. “You ready to go?”

“Yeah!” Gavin nodded his little head.

“Getting him into his little shoes was a trip,” Buck said, and only now did Athena notice what the toddler was wearing on his feet. They looked more like particularly stiff socks than shoes, and she wondered how long it would take before one or both were lost. “Seriously, Ma. Thank you for doing this.”

“Because having our grandbabies over is something Bobby and I hate.” She rolled her eyes. “And if scheduling time for arguing means you won’t leave in the middle of dinner and then come back to serve him with papers, well…”

Buck’s expression was utterly flat.

“Ha. Ha.” He shook his head. “And actually, in this case, I think it would be him that- Whatever. Chris!”

Athena leaned back a little, because dear Lord, Buck had a pair of lungs.

“Yeah!?” the preteen shouted from down the hall, voice muffled by a closed door.

“Are you ready to go!?”

“Yeah!”

She heard a door being thrown open, and then the tapping of crutches and uneven footsteps. Chris came out of the hall and into the dining room looking very put upon and like the world was being asked of him, which Athena thought was rich. Within twenty minutes of them getting back to the house, Chris would be enthralled with whatever new dessert Bobby decided to show him how to make.

“I don’t know why I have to leave just so you and Dad can fight,” Chris complained, rolling his eyes. He put his entire head into the motion.

“Because your dad and I try not to scream at each other when you’re around,” Buck answered with a - much more subdued - eye roll of his own. “And since I’m gonna be telling him what he can and can’t do at work, there’s gonna be screaming.”

“Your grandpops has stuff for making brownies,” Athena said, and watched as Chris brightened considerably. “That’s what I thought. Where’s this one’s bag?”

“Oh, here!” Buck scooped up the bright red diaper bag off one of the comfy chairs. “Do- I should have asked before. Do you need his seat out of the jeep?”

“I drove Bobby’s truck over, we’re good. C’mon, Chris.”

Buck followed them out the door and to the truck, carrying along the diaper bag. He didn’t go back in once everyone was situated in the truck, opting instead to stand on the sidewalk and watch them leave. He was visible in the rearview mirror until Athena turned the corner for home.

The Diaz house was not very far from home, a five minute drive most of the time, which Athena was very grateful for. With half her family living on the other side of the country, she was glad that Buck was so insistent on staying close. 

She was not glad about the unfamiliar car sitting on the curb when she got there.

“Who’s that?” Chris asked as he clambered out.

“I don’t know,” Athena admitted, frowning at the car. She got Gavin out and set him on the ground before going back for his diaper bag. When she settled it on her shoulder and bent down to pick him back up, the toddler shook his head. “Oh, you want to walk inside? Okay.”

Gavin ran over to where Chris was waiting for them, his small hand immediately reaching up to grip one of Chris’ crutches. Athena lingered back for just long enough to pull out her phone and take a quick picture of the pair walking up the sidewalk together. She didn’t plan on sharing it with anyone else, because some things were just for her.

The tot’s independence came to an end the moment they got inside and Gavin caught sight of the stairs leading down. He had no problem, at that point, with being picked up again and carried. Athena gave her house a once-over and frowned when she spotted neither Bobby nor their unknown guest. It wasn’t until they reached the bottom steps that she spotted her husband outside on the back patio.

Something had him agitated, judging from the way he was gesturing wildly with the arm that wasn’t gripping the patio table.

“You boys stay here,” she said, setting Gavin down on the couch. “I’ll go see what Grandpops is up to.”

“Okay,” Chris said, frowning at her. He was a smart boy, he’d probably noticed the same thing she did. Athena smiled at him before heading outside.

“-still do the job!” Bobby was saying, quite loudly, when she slid the door open. She hurriedly closed it as she stepped out. Their unknown guest, a woman, let out a frustrated huff.

“Can you!?” she demanded. Athena was fairly certain she was one of the battalion chiefs. “You could barely walk up to the podium yesterday! You’ll be retiring in just a few short years anyway. There is absolutely no shame in-”

“No!” Bobby snapped. “I am going to keep doing my job for as long as I am able. And I am able.”

“Is everything alright out here?” Athena asked, cutting in before the volume could increase even further.

“Chief Williams here just came over to… See how I was doing.”

Williams gave her a curt nod, the woman’s attention focused entirely on Bobby.

“Bobby,” she said, “if you can’t do the job…”

“I can!” Bobby argued. “I’m not retiring, not a single second before I have to. Not until I’m forced to.”

Williams dragged a hand down her face.

“No one’s forcing anyone,” she said.

Athena let out a derisive snort.

“I should hope not,” she said, eyeing the battalion chief down. 

“I am not retiring,” Bobby repeated. His grip on the table, Athena couldn’t help but notice, was getting rather white-knuckled. “Not now.”

“Fine,” Williams sighed. “Fine, fine. We’ll let it go for now. But Bobby…”

“Yeah,” Bobby snapped again, before letting out a sigh of his own. “Yeah, I know.”

“Okay.” She nodded to him and then to Athena, and then went to open the patio door. She stopped, head tilted curiously. “The Diaz children are here.”

“Their fathers are planning to have a fight about what Eddie will be doing at work,” Athena explained.

Williams laughed.

“Better than them causing a scene when it’s my problem,” she said. “I’ll let myself out. Good day, Bobby. Sergeant.”

Bobby stayed standing until the battalion chief had gone through the house, up the stairs, and out the door. And then, the very moment they heard the door close, he collapsed into one of the patio chairs like a building being demolished. Athena looked at him, worried, but kept inside all the comments of concern she wanted to let out.

“Not now,” he said, almost begging. “Not… Later. Not now.”

“Later,” she conceded, because there was no point pressing the issue now. Not when they had the boys to keep them from seeing their parents argue. “Chris is looking forward to the brownies.”

“The kid loves me for my food.” It was a weak attempt at a joke, but it was better than nothing. “Tell him I’ll be right in.”

“Alright.”

Athena started inside, to pass on the message and make sure Gavin wasn’t getting into anything. Like Williams, she paused just before crossing the threshold. Unlike Williams, she turned on her heel and went back to Bobby, to press a kiss to the top of his head.

And then she went inside.

Sunday Morning

Athena’s shift literally just started, she hadn’t even made it to the station, when dispatch had her responding to a call. On the brightside, when she pulled up in front of the house in Lafayette Square, her coffee was still steaming. She was, however, somewhat curious as to why she was the one responding to this particular call.

“Over here!” The homeowner, a woman in a yellow sundress with her hair up in a bun, waved her arms. She was holding a garden hose that was still dribbling water. “Officer!”

“It’s Sergeant Grant,” Athena corrected reflexively as she made her way over. “Dispatch said that you reported a fire…?”

She was not the person to call for a fire. It wasn’t like she could shoot it or put it under arrest.

“Someone tried to set my house on fire,” the woman said, turning and leading the way to the side of the house. “I put it out already - the hose - but someone tried to set my house on fire!”

“Ma’am, are you sure that- Oh.”

The fire, whatever fire there had been, was indeed out already. What was left was a blackened hedges and what might have once been a styrofoam cup. Athena could make out what looked like the remnants of a bundle of matches, a cigarette butt, and the burnt scraps of paper. There was something else at the bottom of the cup, but it was too melted for her to recognize it.

“I still think they sent the wrong person out,” Athena said, kneeling to look closer at the mess before her.

“Then who should they have sent!?”

“Me,” came a new voice.

Athena stood and turned, spotting the owner of the voice coming towards them. He was short and balding, with a camera hanging from around his neck, and the windbreaker he wore declared him to be Arson Investigation.

“Jay Knight,” he introduced himself, and barely slowed down for Athena to do the same. He zeroed in, instead, on the burnt hedges and the melted cup. “Ah, yup. Just like the others.”

“The others?” Athena asked.

“Six fires in the past, oh, thirty days?” Knight knelt and took out a pen to prod at the mess, humming to himself as he did. “Each one started from the outside, at the side of the house. Each one started from an improvised incendiary device, like this one. We’ve been having trouble figuring out a pattern, however.”

“What do you mean?” Athena asked him, curious now. It wasn’t her beat, LAFD handled arson, but still.

“The first one was in the middle of the night,” Knight explained, before pausing to lift up the camera and take several photos in a row. “The second, a couple weeks later, in the middle of the day. The last four have been early in the morning, every third day.”

“That sounds like a pattern.”

“It does, if you take out the first two,” he said. “Ma’am?”

“Huh? Oh, me?” the homeowner perked up.

“Yes. Do you have a security camera? Or your neighbors?”

“I have one of those doorbell cameras.”

“Excellent! Do you think I could get a copy from this morning?”

The homeowner nodded like a bobble head before running off, dropping the hose as she went.

“The problem,” Knight said, continuing from before, “is that we have too many fires and not enough people to look at them all. Nineteen investigators are not enough to look into each and every fire in this city. This one gets a little more focus, since the arsonist has killed people, but-”

“How many people?” Athena asked, a little sharper than intended.

“Three,” he answered, the word coming out in a whisper. “Two in the first fire, one in the last.”

“Then you definitely…” She trailed off, a thought striking her. A brilliant thought. “You definitely need at least another set of eyes to look through the evidence, right?”

And she had a husband that needed something to keep himself busy, and who enjoyed mysteries a bit too much.

“I need several sets of eyes. And pairs of hands,” Knight said, standing up. “Why, do you have any?”

“My husband, Bobby, Captain Nash-”

“The One Eighteen,” he interrupted. “They’ve responded to four of the fires.”

Huh.

“Yes… He’s out with an injury-”

“I’m aware.”

“So he’s free for however long it takes for him to be cleared,” she continued. “And my husband is not someone who handles being idle well. If he wasn’t injured, he’d probably have gone dumpster diving for crimes. Again.”

“Everyone needs a hobby,” Knight joked, though it didn’t sound like he put much humor into it. “There’s nineteen of us. I’m not going to complain about help. Especially if it’s another firefighter who needs something to do.”

Athena smiled to herself as they quickly exchanged contact information before she was being called away for a disturbance elsewhere. Her husband would have something to keep his mind off the argument with Battalion Chief Williams, Jay Knight would have someone to help him investigate these arsons, and a criminal would be stopped.

But it was odd, she thought, how many of these fires her husband’s firehouse had responded to. Especially since she was fairly certain that this one would have been in their area, too.

The Nervous Giggles

Chapter Summary

In which an unfortunate couple gets stuck.

Chapter Notes

If it's not obvious, the second half of the last chapter happens concurrently with the start of this one.

It was nice, Buck thought, to be going through the motions of getting ready for work with Eddie again. It was always more fun with each other, than when one of them was stuck at home for one reason or another. There ended up being more stupid giggling than tired yawns, usually because one of them shoved the other out of the way of the bathroom sink a bit too hard and they smacked against the wall.

It was nice, Buck thought, to be going into work with Eddie again. Even if it was a Sunday. Even if his husband complained for most of the drive about being up so early after weeks of sleeping in. Even if it meant having to deal with an upset Gavin, who’d gotten used to having his daddy home all the time.

But now here they were, pulling into the station’s parking lot together, and Buck felt settled in a way that he hadn’t since the bridge collapsed all those weeks ago.

“Remember,” he said, shutting off the engine and unbuckling. “Light duties. You’re helping Al and Hen and Crawford. You’re not doing any heavy lifting, you’re not doing any rescues, you’re not-”

“Yeah, yeah, I know,” Eddie said, cutting him off. “I’m supposed to just stand there and look pretty while everyone else around me works. We spent yesterday arguing about this, mi sol, I remember.”

“We didn’t yell at each other for any longer than, I dunno. A half hour. The rest of the time was spent taking advantage of the house being empty and upsetting the dog with the noises.”

Poor Hoover. He’d been so distressed.

They clambered out of the jeep and grabbed their bags from the back, before making their way to the side entrance of the station. Buck came to a halt just outside and Eddie paused to look at him in question. And then jumped a little when his husband grabbed his ass and squeezed.

“Really?” he asked, amused.

“Hey, I won’t be able to do this again until tomorrow morning.” Buck squeezed again and let go. “Not without someone calling HR on me.”

“There’s nothing stopping me from calling HR on you now.” Mayfair’s voice had Buck startling. She was standing just inside, shaking her head at them. 

He gave her a sheepish wave.

“Morning, Maria.”

“Morning, Diazes.” She eyed Eddie. “Welcome back.”

“Thank you.” Eddie grabbed Buck’s bag and pulled it out of his hand. “I'll put these away.”

“Thanks, ba- er, Eds!” Buck called after him as he jogged towards the locker room. He turned back to Mayfair. “You, uh. You were joking about the HR thing, right?”

The other captain rolled her eyes before turning around and walking away.

After annoying Mayfair, and getting through the shift change briefing, there were plenty of things Buck could spend his time doing before the official start of his shift. There was paperwork from Thursday he could finish, there was the next round of chores he could assign, reports that needed uploading to FireStat.  He didn’t do any of those things, though.

Instead, after a moment of staring at the gear cages, Buck went and dug out a flathead screwdriver from one of the toolboxes. Some of the C-shifters were staring at him, he knew, but he ignored the feel of their gazes on his skin as he prised one of the name plates off. He did it slowly and carefully (so very carefully) so that he didn’t risk it snapping in half on him. Eddie came to stand beside him and watch while he worked, but the man remained silent.

“I still don’t know what I’m going to do with it,” Buck said, holding Jones’ name plate in his hand. “But if it stayed up there any longer, it risked being taped over for a floater. C’mon, I still need to assign everyone’s chores for the next few shifts.”

“Can we give Albert and Ravi bathroom duty?” Eddie asked as he followed behind him to the captain’s office, with a stop to put the screwdriver back where it belonged.

The rest of their coworkers trickled in while Eddie “helped” with the bureaucracy of being a captain, which mostly resulted in bickering and Buck getting distracted. By the time Buck had printed it out and brought it upstairs to be posted, their shift was just about to start and everyone had arrived. Even the probie, who they passed in the bay on their way to the stairs.

“About time,” Hen crowed from the kitchen island, where she was standing guard over a cake box. “I tried sending Albert down to see what was taking you so long, but he refused.”

“I wasn’t walking in on that,” Albert said, shaking his head.

“Walking in on what?” Eddie walked up to the island and, when Hen stepped aside, opened it to reveal the cake welcoming him back. “At least someone got me something, Buck.”

“The door was open, there was nothing to walk in on,” Buck said, rolling his eyes at his husband’s jab. “Except him being annoying.”

“Annoying? I was being helpful. I thought I had very good suggestions.”

“One of your suggestions was to make the probie be your butler.”

“Well it’s not like you’re going to actually let me do anything. I should get something out of my husband being the captain.”

“Maybe I should put some money down on that bet,” Crawford said, coming over with a saucer and fork, clearly intent on getting some cake. “There’s still time, right?”

Hen scooted the betting box, which Buck hadn’t even noticed sitting on the island, closer to the other paramedic and silently opened it to reveal the large pile of cash sitting within.

“Seriously?” Buck sputtered. He scowled as he watched Crawford, Albert, and Toby pull out their wallets to add to it. “Seriously!?”

“This is the bet about how long it’ll take for the two of us to get into a fight, right?” Eddie asked, peering at the betting box in curiosity. He took the knife that Ravi handed him and cut a large slice off the cake. “Nice, vanilla.”

“You are so boring.”

“Cállate, capitán. Can we get in on this?”

“Eds!”

“No,” Hen said succinctly, slamming the box closed. “You think I’m stupid?”

“I think a couple months ago, Buck and I made a lot of money off everyone else being stupid,” Eddie told her.

“Fuck you, Diaz!” Ortiz shouted from across the loft, not sounding too terribly upset.

“Is Dalton going to come up here?” Toby asked, getting himself some cake now, too. “Get himself some cake before it’s all gone?”

“Do we want him up here?” Lena Bosko muttered, just audible to Buck, as she waited for her turn. Some of the others laughed. It kinda made Buck feel bad for the kid, even if it was almost entirely his own fault.

Buck went to the railing, spotting Wolfe lingering in the bay even now. He frowned in concern. Was the kid feeling unwelcome with the rest of them? It hadn’t seemed to be a problem before, but Wolfe had stayed down in the bay at the start of their last shift, too.

He distinctly remembered thinking the kid was acting high-strung. But maybe that hadn’t been the case, at all.

Or maybe Buck was just looking into this too much.

“Probie!” he bellowed, getting Wolfe’s attention. “Come on up here and have some cake!”

The kid nodded up at him and started heading for the stairs. Reluctantly. And he kept glancing back the entire time, and then up at the alarm.

Weird.

It stayed weird. Whatever was going on with the probie, that is.

Unlike the last couple of shifts, they didn’t get called out to a fire first thing, which was a welcome change of pace. This meant that they could actually get through the start of shift checklists, making sure that everything was stocked and all the equipment ready for use. It was the kind of thing that was done at the start and end of every shift, and while Buck was sure that C-shift could be depended on, it was always best to be able to make sure of it themselves. Even if it was just to cover their own asses later.

Every time they were called out so early into their shift, every time they had to go out without making sure that everything was accounted for, they ran the risk of that day being the day that someone on C-shift decided to half-ass their job and one of them would suffer the price for it. That had yet to be the case, thankfully, because the men and women of C-shift were dependable. But still.

Anyways, back to the probie. They hadn’t been called out for a fire within their first hour, and that was typically the kind of thing to be thankful for. But for some reason, the more time passed, the more… Distressed? 

That wasn’t the word that Buck wanted, but it was perhaps the best description.

The more time passed, the more distressed Wolfe seemed to be. It was like their last shift, with the kid always coming back to hover around the turnout cages as if he was being magnetically drawn to them. He was very clearly anxious about something, to the point that even the others were frowning at him in concern as he would glance at the alarm constantly. At first in anticipation, then worry, then… Well, then in distress.

And maybe a bit of disappointment? Buck wasn’t sure.

The fact that the others had not only noticed the odd behavior of their unpopular probie, but were also showing concern, meant that Buck’s anxious mind wasn’t just seeing things that weren’t there. Which meant, in turn, that it needed to be addressed. Sooner, rather than later. Not that he was very eager to do so.

“I absolutely refuse to be this kid’s parent,” Buck told Eddie, as he put off talking to the probie. His husband was going through the inventory of one of the ambulances with Hen, and Buck wasn’t really helping so much as he was being an irritant. “I am no one’s dad here.”

Toby, who’d been doing maintenance on the nearby engine, frowned at him in confusion. But then, because he’d been there for a month, he simply shook his head and moved on with his life.

“You say that now,” Hen said, not even looking up from the list she was checking off. She was in the depths of the ambulance, going through the supplies. “But before you know it, you might be going to see Bruce Springsteen with him.”

“The more you compare me to him, the more I think you don’t like me, Henrietta,” Buck said, and then immediately ducked behind Eddie to avoid the unopened box of gloves she threw at his head. “That was uncalled for.”

Eddie went to pick the box off the floor. The fact that it meant he left his husband completely vulnerable to further impromptu projectiles was surely accidental on his part.

“Just go,” he said, tossing the box back to Hen. “Find out what’s wrong before you annoy us with your fretting.”

“Too late!” was Hen’s contribution.

“I will,” Buck said. “I’m going to. I just wanted to make it clear what wasn’t gonna happen.”

“And you’ve done that.” Eddie’s patronizing earned him a glare, not that he was paying attention enough to notice it. “Now go be paternal over the kid.”

“A big brother figure, at most!” Buck insisted, even as he headed for the captain’s office. “Dalton!”

The probie, busy looking dejected over on the other side of the bay, snapped to attention before jogging over. Neither of them said anything until they were in the office, Buck contemplating whether or not this was a Closed Door kind of talk. He ended up leaving it open, and went to perch on the edge of the desk.

“Is something wrong?” Wolfe asked, anxiety lacing his words. It was vastly different from the unfounded cockiness he usually exuded, whenever he wasn’t in trouble.

“That’s what I wanted to ask you,” Buck said. “Is everything, y’know. Okay?”

“Yeah? I- I mean, yeah,” the kid said. “Why?”

“That,” he said, gesturing at Wolfe. “You’re acting… I’m just concerned, is all.”

“Nothing’s wrong, Cap.”

“You’re sure?”

“Yup.”

Buck frowned at him, not believing that for an instant. It was very clear that something was going on, even to him. But it wasn’t like he could force the kid to confide in him. If he wasn’t willing to tell him what, or even if, something was wrong, then there was little Buck could do. Regardless of how much that bothered him.

“Alright then, if you’re sure,” Buck said, not feeling very sure himself. “You had that sensitivity training this past week, didn’t you? How did that go?”

“Fine.” And now they were back on familiar ground, with Wolfe sulking as he crossed his arms. “It was- It was fine.”

“Fine” was probably not the word he actually wanted to use.

“Do you think it helped? At least with recognizing the kind of things you can’t say or do around your coworkers?” Buck asked him. The probie made a motion that clearly would have been an eye roll before its premature abortion, and then nodded. “Each of us will have to trust you with our lives, at some point. Or the life of someone we care about.  And you’ll have to trust us with yours. It’s a trust that has to be earned, which won’t happen if you manage to drive everyone away by being an asshole. Okay?”

“Okay,” the kid said, sounding the epitome of sullen. “Sir.”

“And you’re sure there’s nothing…?”

“No. There’s nothing.”

Buck resisted the urge to huff out a sigh.

“Fine,” he said. “You can go back to doing what you were.”

Wolfe nodded and was halfway out the door before he was even done speaking. 

“Okay then,” Buck said to himself, dragging a hand down his face. “That went well.”

Most of the emergencies they responded to were entirely unrelated to fires. In fact, last year, the number of fires that the LAFD was called out for was less than thirty-nine thousand. On average, there was a structure fire every two hours and eleven minutes, which sounded like a lot until you took into account how those fires were spread out across the city and the various other stations. EMS? More than four-hundred thousand, one every minute and twelve seconds. 

All that to say, the fact that they’d had to respond to a structure fire just about every shift for the past couple of weeks was very weird. Their first call of the day being a medical emergency was very much the old normal.

They hopped into the engine and the ambulance, not bothering with turn-outs since it was an EMS call. Buck told Toby where to drive and then they were off with sirens blaring, the ambulance following behind. He frowned at the information on the tablet, however, because it was disconcertingly vague.

He grabbed the radio off the dash.

“Dispatch, Captain One Eighteen,” he relayed, still frowning at the tablet screen. “Stuck how?”

“They’re uh- They’re stuck together.” It was Linda, he was pretty sure, and it sounded like she was trying not to laugh. “They can’t pull apart.”

“Are they impaled?’

“S- Sure, we’ll go with that.” There was a brief burst of giggles before she cut herself off. When she came back, she sounded more than a bit breathless. “There’s a- There’s a piece of jewelry that got stuck. On him, in her.”

Horror raced up Buck’s spine as the full implication of her words dawned on him. In the driver’s seat, Toby blanched; Buck couldn’t see the others behind him, but heard someone let out their own strained giggles.

“Okay, let it out,” he told everyone. “Laugh it up now, because when we get there, we have to be professional.”

“Laugh?” Bosko asked. “I’m having sympathy pains. Stuck in her?”

“Stuck in her,” Buck repeated. He changed the channel on the radio. “Hen, it’s a sex injury.”

“Oh, God.”

The house they pulled up to looked quaint and innocent, completely belaying whatever deviant behavior was occurring within it on a Sunday morning. They tumbled out onto the sidewalk, unsure what they would need besides the gurney. The door was locked, but it was one of the fancy locks that had a code.

It was Sue that relayed the entry code to them, which meant that Linda would probably be getting a stern talking to about professionalism. In between the giggling.

“LAFD!” he called out as he, Hen, and Crawford went in. He wasn’t sure who all would be needed, and didn’t see the point in further humiliating the poor couple. Not that he honestly thought the humiliation would be at all avoidable.

“In here!” a woman called out, voice strained.

“In here” turned out to be a bedroom down the hall. The young couple were on the bed, half-covered by a sheet. The man’s face was hidden in the woman’s neck, and it sounded like he was muttering apologies over and over. Not that Buck could blame him, any.

“I’m just gonna stand right out here,” Buck said, stepping back to allow Hen and Crawford through.

“So how, exactly, are the two of you… Stuck?” Hen asked, as delicately as possible, as she pulled on gloves.

“I- I have a piercing,” the man said, voice muffled like he was talking into the woman’s neck. “And it- It got- It got stuck. On something.”

Oh, God.

“I felt something tear,” the woman said.

Oh, God.

“Okay. I’m going to have to take a look,” Hen said. There was the sound of fabric being moved, and then, “It looks like there’s some bleeding.”

Oh, God.

“Well, I’m not afraid to admit when something is outside my wheelhouse,” Crawford said, sounding far more professional than Buck himself felt. “Any ideas?”

“We can’t, uh. Separate the two of you here,” Hen said. “They’ll have to do it at the hospital, and then check how bad the tearing is. Cap?”

“Getting the gurney, and extra hands,” Buck said, hurrying back out.

“Oh, God,” he heard the woman say as he left, voice utterly empty of feeling.

The others were all grouped around the engine, and all conversation came to a halt the moment they spotted him coming.

“So?” Eddie asked, breaking the stilted silence. “How bad?”

“I want to laugh, but also scream at the same time,” Buck confessed. “We’re gonna take them in, obviously. Uh, Al, Eddie, bring the gurney. And then we’ll have to move them over without causing any more tearing.”

“Any more tearing,” Bosko repeated, sounding faint.

“What tore?” Toby asked.

“That is a question I am not gonna ask, because the answer horrifies me,” Buck said. “C’mon, let’s get moving. And absolutely no giggling until the ambulance doors are closed.”

How many people did it take to lift two full grown adults off a bed and deposit them onto a gurney? Almost all of them, it turned out, since they didn’t want to risk pulling them apart and causing further damage. It was a task made all the more difficult by how everyone involved, in silent agreement, refused to meet the eyes of anyone else as it was done.

After several stressful minutes, in which people tried very politely to grip places that would have been very impolite in a different context, in which the couple let out pained squeaks with every jostle, they managed to get them moved onto the gurney. Eddie came in with a blanket, because Buck hadn’t allowed him to assist in the lifting, and tucked it neatly around the two of them.

“There are, uh, people outside,” Eddie announced in a whisper. It didn’t matter, because no one was talking. “Neighbors. Come out to see what was going on.”

The pair on the gurney let out a despairing groan at that.

“Guess we’ll play interference then,” Buck said, clapping his hands together. “Hen?”

“We’ve got it handled.”

“Alright. Eds, Toby, c’mon.”

Between the three of them, they managed to keep the gawking neighbors from seeing too much of what was going on. There was no avoiding them noticing the gurney, but Buck thought they managed to keep the exact number of passengers hidden, and the onlookers were kept ignorant of why they were there. Beyond the unavoidable basics, at least.

Even better, they all managed to keep the giggles under lock and key. Past getting the couple onto the ambulance, with Hen climbing in with them and Crawford getting into the driver’s seat. They didn’t break until they were all in the engine. And then the moment Ravi started, none of them could stop.

Oh, God.

He Said Burn, Burn

Chapter Summary

In which a bet is almost won.

There was something peaceful about being up on the station roof in the middle of the night. Tranquil, in a way. The sounds of traffic all around, yet removed; the clear sky up above, a wide expanse of darkness surrounding the waning moon; the hints of ocean breeze that managed to reach, even with the ocean itself out of sight. An oasis away from the chaotic world.

At the moment, the only ones up there were Buck and Eddie. Both of them were sitting in one of the lawn chairs that had been carried up forever ago, gazing over the city as everyone else hunkered down in the bunks.

“Do you think,” Eddie started, breaking the peace, “that Abuela would mind if we called her right now to check on the boys?”

Buck rolled his head to look at him.

“I think she’ll try to smack us through the phone if we wake her up.”

“It’s not that late,” his husband said, pulling out his phone. He frowned at the screen. “Nevermind, yes it is.”

“Gotta get used to this again,” Buck teased. “Not being able to just creep into their rooms and check on them.”

“I didn’t creep. And don’t act like you don’t do the same.”

“Just wait until the day that Chris starts locking his door at night.”

“We have screwdrivers.”

Buck started laughing at the image that invoked, of Eddie breaking out the toolbox to take down Chris’ door just so he could check on him in the middle of the night. Not that Buck believed Eddie ever seriously would, but still.

“I feel like I’m crashing something.”

Bosko’s voice had Buck twisting in his seat. She was standing, hesitant, at the stairway entrance. Like she actually was concerned about interrupting them.

“Just parenting woes,” Buck told her. “C’mon, pull up a seat.”

“Thank God,” she said, hurrying over and claiming a chair on the other side of him. “The probie’s the only other one up and about right now. I don't want to be caught out by him and have to punch my way free, y’know?”

“I thought you said he was doing better?” Eddie asked. The question was directed at Buck, but Bosko was the one who answered.

“He’s still an annoying little shit,” she said. “And I have no interest in hanging out with him. He might, I dunno. Try and tell me how to do my job again.”

“Would you believe it if I told you he used to be worse?” Buck asked her. “His very first day here, after being transferred from the One Thirty-three for being… Well, himself, I guess. Eddie and I were giving him a tour, right? And we passed by Clarke checking some equipment. And he stopped and offered to show her the proper way of doing it.”

“Buck practically dragged the kid to the other side of the building to keep her from bashing his head in with an oxygen tank,” Eddie added.

“Pretty sure I spent most of that shift babysitting him.”

“Christ,” Bosko muttered, shaking her head.

Buck and Eddie nodded in agreement to the sentiment, the conversation seemingly reaching its natural end. The three of them fell into silence, but it wasn’t the peaceful, tranquil silence of before. There was something awkward about it, something strained. Bosko, when Buck looked, had her arms around her middle as she chewed on her lip. And Eddie, meanwhile, was playing with the fabric of his pants, his face set in a thoughtful scowl.

Neither of them were looking at each other. 

How much had the two of them actually interacted since the shift started? Buck wasn’t certain, but looking back at it, he wasn’t sure they really had. Not one-on-one. And the day Carla brought Eddie and the boys to the station, he and Bosko had seemed like they got along. But now, in hindsight, he thought there might have been something rather pointed in their comments to each other. A bit of vitriol in the humor.

This was really something he should have thought about ahead of time, Buck was realizing. He had assumed that since he was fine working with Bosko, his bitterness towards her for being his (perceived) replacement having been short lived once his anger found a new target in Bobby, that there wouldn’t be any problems. He’d utterly failed to take into consideration all the reasons that Bosko and Eddie might have strife.

“I was a shitty friend,” Eddie said, breaking the silence and interrupting Buck’s internal monologue. “I expected you to sit there and listen to me and care about my baggage, and I didn’t once return the favor. I was a selfish ass.”

“You were,” Bosko agreed. “You’re also pretty bad at apologies.”

Buck heard his husband sigh.

“I’m sorry,” Eddie said. “For being a selfish, shitty friend.”

“Not that we really were friends,” Bosko said, relaxing into her chair. “I’m sorry, too, for what it’s worth. I pushed you into the fighting because it was what helped me. I never stopped to consider that maybe, for someone with your issues, it would just make things worse.”

“With my issues?”

“You punched a handicapped guy.”

“He wasn’t handicapped. You know he wasn’t handicapped. I explained this to you when it happened.”

Bosko simply shrugged primly. Buck shook his head and leaned back in his chair, closing his eyes and letting the two of them talk it out. He breathed in, taking in the various smells of the city, some pleasant and some not so, brought to them by the wind. Car exhaust, the 24-hour taco place next door, the not-so-distant ocean, smoke-

Buck’s eyes snapped open and he eased to his feet.

“You still do it any?” Eddie was asking.

“Nah. Everything stopped during the pandemic, y’know?” Bosko answered. “And then, the first time I went back, there was a lucky hit to my side. Didn’t tell anyone, went into work like everything was fine. And it was, until I had to carry this guy out of a building and my ribs just gave out completely. I ended up dropping us both. Coop was so pissed.”

“Why did you tell that story?” Eddie asked. “Now Buck will definitely- Buck?”

“Do you smell that?” Buck asked, gaze fixed on the skyline as he tried to figure out the direction of the source. Somewhere to the west of them, he thought.

“Do I-? Oh.” Another gust of wind had brought the smell of smoke back, stronger than before. “Where is that coming from?”

“I’m not sure,” Buck started to say, but was interrupted by Bosko jumping to her feet.

“There,” she said, pointing. Buck followed her finger, barely spotting the pillar of smoke rising on the horizon. It was a dark shape moving against a slightly lighter sky. “How close do you think that is?”

“Close enough,” he said. There was the sound of sirens, but there was no telling if they were responding to that fire or heading somewhere else. “We should go make sure everyone else is up, get a headstart on-”

The wailing of the station alarms cut him off. The three of them ran back inside, where the others were stumbling out of the bunks. Wolfe was already tugging on his turnouts. Through the PA system, dispatch was rattling off information about a two-alarm apartment fire, though it was mostly drowned out in their rush to get moving.

The 133 was already on site when they pulled up with the trucks and ambulances, though they were still setting up with hoses. Buck leaped out and jogged to where Captain Shore was directing her people.

“Where do you need us?” he asked.

“Everywhere,” she answered, staring up at the apartment building. It was six stories tall and didn’t look as if it had been in the best shape to begin with. Now, with a fire quickly consuming the back and right sides of the building, he figured the other captain shared his worry that it might collapse at any moment. “There are residents that are unaccounted for, and both my buses are transporting victims from another call.”

They spent a few moments conferring, before Buck went back to his own people to give out marching orders. Hen and the other paramedics were already setting up a staging area for triage, and he sent an unhappy looking, but compliant Eddie to assist with that. He sent Toby and a couple of the men from the ladder truck to help with the hoses.

And the rest he divided up to go in.

“Ravi, you’re with Bosko. Probie, you’re with me,” Buck said, ignoring the glare that Eddie no doubt had fixed on him. Buck wouldn’t be happy about his husband going into this building without him at his back, he knew that Eddie would be feeling much the same.

Wolfe nearly tripped over himself in his excitement, as he ran for the compartment on the engine with the oxygen tanks. Buck simply rolled his eyes and followed at a more controlled pace.

The building was filled with smoke and baking heat, nearly impossible to see through. The main stairwell was just inside, and his pairings of firefighters split off to check through each floor. Occasionally, they’d have a coughing civilian literally stumble into them on their way out the building.

Once they reached the top floor, it was just Buck and Wolfe. While visibility in the hall was better than in the stairwell, there was still smoke gathering in a cloud by the ceiling, and the heat was just as oppressive. More so, as they got closer to the half of the building where the fire raged.

They went through, banging on doors and busting them open to ensure no one was inside. Buck wanted to go faster, except he knew they were going through as quickly as they safely could. But the floor beneath their feet was starting to make worrisome noises, and he wasn’t sure how much time they had before the situation took a turn for the worse.

One of the apartments, near the corner where the fire was stronger and starting to poke through the floors, was occupied. They heard a woman’s voice, weak and faint, calling for help as soon as they started banging on her door. When they forced their way inside, they found an older woman lying on the floor in her nightgown.

“Oh, thank you,” she said, trying her best to raise her head up. There was blood trickling from her temple. “Thank you. I’ve been calling for help for ages, it feels like.”

“We’re here now,” Buck assured her, kneeling by her side. “What’s your name? Do you remember what happened?”

“Carol. I fell on my way to the door,” she answered. “Hit my head.”

“Okay. Well, as much as I don’t want to move you just yet, we can’t stay here,” he said. “We’ll get you outside and our friends will look you over.”

Buck motioned for Wolfe to get to her other side, and together they lifted her to her feet as gently as they could. Not gently enough, it seemed, because she let out a cry as soon as she put weight on her legs.

“My hip,” she complained.

“We’ll carry you out, it’s fine,” he told her, getting her situated. “Wolfe, get her-”

“Wait!” she cried. “Timmy. Did Timmy make it out?”

Buck shared a look with the probie, who was just as clueless as he himself was.

“Who’s Timmy?” he asked her.

“He’s in the apartment, down the hall,” she said, gesturing. “His mother works nights.”

“How old is Timmy?”

“He just turned twelve last week.”

Buck closed his eyes and bit his tongue, to keep the words that he wanted to say from falling out. He took a breath before opening them.

“Alright, I’ll get him. What’s his door number?”

“Six Thirty-three.”

“Okay. Wolfe, get her out of here.”

Wolfe opened his mouth, like he thought he was going to argue the order. But Buck simply glared him into silence, pushed him towards the stairs, and headed for apartment 633. His radio crackled with the other teams reporting in, saying they were done with their sweeps and heading back out. 

“This is Buck, One Eighteen,” he relayed as he walked. The floor was creaking even more now, and there was smoking wafting through the air around him, and he didn’t trust what would happen if he lingered for too long. “Wolfe is carrying out a patient with a head wound and injured hip. I’m looking for another resident.”

When he reached 633, and banged on the door, no one answered. He wasn’t sure if that was because Timmy had made it out, because he didn’t hear, or because he couldn’t respond. It was a risk he wasn’t going to take.

The door was, much like all the doors, concerningly flimsy. It flew open beneath his boot with a crack.

“LAFD!” he called out, stepping into the apartment. “Timmy!? Carol sent me! Are you in here!?”

A door deeper into the small apartment slowly opened, and a terrified face poked out. The boy had clearly been crying, was still crying, and he started coughing as the smoke got to him.

“Hey, hey, it’s alright,” Buck said, moving to kneel beside him. He wiped at the kid’s face, though it didn’t do much to help its messy state. “I’m Buck. You’re Timmy?”

“Y- Yeah,” Timmy answered, before he started coughing again.

“Okay.” he said, frowning. He took off his oxygen mask and pressed it to the kid’s face, ignoring the way the smoke immediately started irritating his own throat. “That better?”

Timmy nodded.

“I’m gonna pick you up and get us both out of here, that okay with you?” Buck asked the kid, waiting for another nod before doing just that. Timmy immediately tucked his face into Buck’s shoulder, clinging tight to his coat. With the boy held securely, Buck stepped out into the hallway and started for the stairs.

Which was, of course, when the floor finally gave way in an explosion of smoke and embers.

Eddie would not describe himself, in any way, as “okay” about Buck going into that blaze without him. He knew the others could tell his displeasure, what with how the others kept glancing at him while they worked through the cases of smoke inhalation and minor burns that were being sent their way. Half his attention was focused on the patients in front of him, but the other half kept perking up every time his radio crackled to life.

The radio grabbed his full attention when he heard Buck’s voice.

“This is Buck, One Eighteen. Wolfe is carrying out a patient with a head wound and injured hip. I’m looking for another resident.”

Whatever expression Eddie was currently wearing, it had his current patient - a young woman covered in soot and with a burnt hand - cringing away from him. He gave her a smile that came across as more of a grimace, finished wrapping her hand in gauze, and sent her off to either be transported to the ER with any of the others or to find her own way there.

“I think,” he heard Crawford say, from somewhere behind him, “that Bosko is gonna be winning money tonight.”

Eddie rolled his eyes as he heard both Hen and Albert laugh at that. He turned around to check the entrance to the apartments, spotting Wolfe coming out with the old woman they’d been warned about. Crawford and Albert met him halfway, and they helped get her onto a gurney.

And then, from the apartment building, there was the sound of things crashing. A whoomph of air, a burst of sparks. Some windows on the fifth and fourth floors exploded outward, sending shards of glass flying outward that sparkled in the artificial light of the streetlamps. 

Eddie’s heart leaped to his throat even as he took an aborted step towards the fire. Shore was on the radio, demanding that people check in and confirm they were safely outside. Everyone did it immediately.

Almost everyone.

It took too long for Buck to respond, long enough that Eddie started marching for the nearest truck and the oxygen tanks stowed inside its compartments. He was digging one out when finally, finally, he heard from his stupid asshole of a husband.

“Buck, One Eighteen. Stuck on the sixth floor, we’re cut off from the stairs.”

“Where are you?” Eddie demanded, before Shore had the chance.

“Delta side, Charlie corner,” Buck replied. There was another sound of shattering glass, a window on the far end of the right wall. When Eddie looked, he spotted a hand - Buck’s hand - waving out from it. “Could really use the ladder. And someone to help this kid down. Not you, Eddie.”

Ortiz was already maneuvering the ladder into position and Eddie hurried over, glaring up at the window his husband was in. He watched, impatiently, as the aerial was extended and Ravi clambered up. A kid, about Chris’ age, looked to be clinging to Buck like a limpet. When Ravi reached them and held out his arms, the kid shook his head and clung tighter.

Buck bent his head down to say something to the boy, though Eddie was far too away to hear it. Whatever it was, it was enough for him to let Ravi to grab hold and start carrying him down, allowing Buck to gingerly transfer over to the ladder and follow. The moment his feet hit the ground, Eddie was on him.

“You-” he started, puffing up for a good lecture, only to freeze in befuddlement when Buck raised a hand to quiet him.

“They’ll win,” Buck said.

Eddie blinked, not understanding at first. And then he spotted the others, all watching them in anticipation. He swallowed down the words he wanted to say, took a breath, and gave a nod.

He had always been a petty man.

“You weren’t wearing your mask,” Eddie said instead, almost smirking at the sound of someone’s disappointed groan.

“Tim-” Buck paused to cough. “Timmy needed it.”

“Of course,” he said. He grabbed Buck by the wrist and started dragging him to the ambulance. “Let’s get you looked over.”

“I’m-” Buck started, only for his jaw to click shut when Eddie turned to glare at him. “Yeah, okay.”

Eddie could yell at him in the morning, after they got home.

The Ache

Chapter Summary

It's like a sandwich: some whump between two slices of fluff.

Early Monday afternoon, Eddie pulled out the picnic basket and together, he and Chris packed it for their visit to Shannon. It was a regular occurrence, though without a set schedule: every so often, for one reason or another, the pair would decide it was time to go to the cemetery. Buck wasn’t certain what the reason for it today was, or if there even was a specific reason beyond simply wanting to go.

He knew that Eddie had plans for one-on-one time with Chris, afterwards. Ever since bringing Gavin home, they made an effort to carve out time to focus on just their oldest. Or tried to, at least. It was difficult, with all the things they had crammed into their schedules. Easier, now that it was summer break.

With Eddie and Chris having their own plans for the day, it meant Buck would be left to his own devices. But that was fine, he already knew what he was going to do. He made sure Gavin’s diaper bag was properly packed, loaded him up in the jeep, and was on the road not very long after Eddie and Chris.

With Maddie and Chimney’s move, with them living just beneath Griffith Observatory, it wasn’t quite as convenient to just drop in on his sister as it once had been. Instead of driving over a few blocks, a trip to see her could end up with him sitting in traffic for an hour. It was still worth it to see her and his niece, it would always be worth it, but still. There was nothing fun about sitting in traffic in the LA heat with a toddler in the back.

It wasn’t until he was almost there that Buck had the thought that maybe, just maybe, he should have called ahead and checked in with his sister. To at least make sure she hadn’t any other plans. He was familiar enough with her schedule to know that she didn’t work that day, so that wasn’t a fear.

Oh well, if worse came to worse, he’d just take a turn and go bother Hen.

Luckily, it didn’t come to that. When Buck got to their house, both Maddie’s and Chimney’s cars were in the drive. He parked on the curb, got the diaper bag and his grumpy kid out of the back, and bounded up to the front door to knock.

When Maddie answered, she took one look at them and shook her head.

“Sorry, no, we’re not interested.”

Buck gave her the flattest expression he could muster.

“Hey Jee!” he called out. 

There was the sound of tiny feet running through the house, and then his niece shoved her way past her mother’s legs. She squealed and tap danced in joy. Gavin wiggled until Buck put him down, the two of them immediately sprinting off to cause mischief and mayhem together.

“That’s cheating,” Maddie complained without an ounce of heat. She stepped aside to let him in, and he made his way into the living room, putting the diaper bag down by the couch. Chimney was stationed on one of the comfy chairs, remote in hand as he flipped through channels.

There was no sign of Gavin or Jee-Yun, though Buck was fairly certain he could hear giggling coming from somewhere.

“Hey, Buckaroo,” Chimney said, giving him a wave. “I just sat down, so I am not getting up.”

“You would if you loved me.”

“Big if, there.”

“Ouch.”

“Is there any particular reason you’ve graced us with your presence today?” Maddie asked, heading into the kitchen. Buck must’ve arrived in the middle of her cleaning up after lunch, judging from the looks of things. 

“Do I need a reason to visit my big sister?” he asked, sitting down at the kitchen table instead of doing anything helpful. “We don’t do wine nights anymore, it’s been awhile since we’ve just hung out.”

“She does them with Karen, now,” Chimney said, raising his voice a little since he was farther away. “Sometimes Josh and Linda come over. It’s awful.”

“What’s awful about it?”

“When it was just you and her, we’d make fun of you,” the other man said. Buck made a face at him. “Now they gang up on me.”

“...yeah, sorry, I can’t find any sympathy.” Buck ignored the indignant squawk Chimney made in response. “Eddie took Chris to hang-out today.”

“Ah, so your sister is runner-up,” Maddie said with a nod. She dumped a plate into the sink, looked at the others piled up in it, and then blatantly turned her back on the entire thing. “I see how it is, now. What are they doing?”

“Visiting Shannon,” he answered. He pushed himself up and made his way to the sink, grabbing the pair of rubber gloves off the back. “And then, I dunno. Something that doesn’t involve a toddler tagging along.”

“What, Chris getting tired of being a big brother?” Maddie asked, glowering at him as he turned on the sink and started rinsing some of the dishes. “Stop that.”

“Make me.” Buck gave her his prettiest smile. “And no, he loves being a big brother, you know that. Hell, a couple nights ago we caught him singing Eddie’s beautiful milk lullaby to Gavin.”

“One, that’s adorable. Two-”

“Beautiful milk lullaby?” Chimney asked.

“That.”

“It’s Spanish. But I swear to you, one of the lines is something about, ‘esta leche linda,’” Buck said. “This beautiful milk.”

“Better than a song about a baby falling out of a tree,” Maddie said. She reached over and turned off the water, smacking his hands away. “Seriously, stop being helpful. I’m grateful, but stop it.”

“You don’t sound grateful,” Buck said, and received a smack to the arm for his lip. He rolled his eyes and took off the gloves, tossing them back where they were. “Hey, uh. Jee-Yun’s daycare, does it have any openings?”

“It might,” Maddie said, drying and putting away the dishes that Buck had gotten through. “Why? I thought you all were good on childcare for Gavin?”

“We are, it’s just-” Buck was interrupted by the giggling duo rushing into the living room. The tots paid them no mind as Jee-Yun grabbed one of the toys that she had left, before dashing off again with Gavin on her heels. “She’s the only one his age we know. Otherwise it’s just Chris and a bunch of adults.”

“None of your neighbors have any kids?”

“None his age, not anymore,” he told her, grimacing at the thought of Edward and Shanna. “And he won’t be old enough for preschool for a while, yet. I just don’t want him growing up lonely, I guess. Not like…”

“Like you did?” Maddie gently asked, picking up the thread he’d allowed to trail off.

Buck nodded. Growing up in Hershey was lonely, in a house clouded with a grief he never understood, a house that he always felt like an intruder in. He’d never felt like he could properly fit in with his classmates, always getting the impression - and being bluntly told more than once - that he was better tolerated in small doses. Their parents had shoved the burden of caring for him onto Maddie, until she fled for her own good. And then there was no one else that would give him the time of day, not unless they were getting something out of it.

“Buck,” she said, cupping his cheek and forcing him to meet her eye. “There is no way that Gavin is going to grow up in a house with you in it and ever feel lonely.”

Chimney let out a pained yelp, causing the both of them to turn around and see if he was okay. He waved them both off, settling back into his chair.

“Sorry, I was- I was gonna get up and go and let you have your moment,” he said, still grimacing. “Just- Just ignore me. Pretend I’m not here.”

“Too late, moment’s ruined,” Maddie said, rolling her eyes even as she smiled. “I’ll ask, see if there’s room for him. Even if there isn’t, or even if there is, I can talk to some of the other parents, see about setting up a playdate.”

“Thanks, Mads.” Buck pressed his forehead to the top of her head, and felt his sister patting him on the back.

“You’re my baby brother, and he’s my nephew,” she said. “What else would I do?”

There was a crash down the hall and they pulled apart. They both waited, breaths held, as they listened for-

Twin cries filled the air.

Buck and Maddie let out identical sighs, and then went to go find their children.

When Athena sat down across from him at their dining table, drink in hand, Bobby immediately understood that his grace period was over. It was her expression that told him as such, the no-nonsense set of her eyebrows that she so often directed at the kids. Buck and Eddie included, of course.

On Saturday, they’d had Chris and Gavin for most of the day, and it wasn’t the kind of talk to have with such an audience. On Sunday, Athena worked, so Bobby was spared simply by her absence and then her exhaustion. But it was Monday, now. Monday afternoon, actually: she’d given him the morning, which was kind of her.

But the grace period was over.

“So,” she said, a single syllable with far too much weight to it.

“So,” he echoed.

“I think it’s past time that we talk about this, Bobby.”

He scowled and looked away. Bobby absolutely did not want to talk about this. It was the last thing he wanted to do. But his wife was like a police dog when she was set on something: jaws locked on some perp’s leg, teeth digging in, refusing to let go. Occasionally she gave it a little shake, until the struggling stopped and everyone got with the program. Her program.

“I don’t want to retire,” he told her, before she could give that little shake. “I can do the job, I can- I don’t want to give it up.”

It was amazing what time and changing circumstances could bring about. Bobby still vividly remembered that awful night when Buck was over, begging him for help to get his job back, spitting fire when he realized it was Bobby blocking his way. The way they sat at this exact table as Buck protested that he was perfectly fine, how he claimed to have never felt so good.

And now here was Bobby, who was not perfectly fine. Here was Bobby, who could count the number of times he’d felt worse on a single hand. The irony was absolutely proof of a higher power. Not that he was in a position to appreciate it.

“I don’t want to retire,” he repeated.

“I know you don’t,” Athena said. “I’m not saying you should. But I’m not worried about your job right now, baby. The brass aren’t either, I’m sure. They’re worried about you. Everyone is. Me, Buck, Hen, everyone.”

“I’m-” Bobby started to say, but the lie died on his lips just as his back gave a twinge. He gripped the edge of the table to steady himself.

There was the sound of chair legs scraping across the floor, and then Athena was moving into the seat next to him. She placed her hand over his, squeezing it lightly.

“I’m not okay,” he confessed in a whisper.

“I know,” she said.

“It hurts.”

“I know,” she said again.

“I can’t- I don’t- Not again. I don’t want to do it again.”

The first time he’d injured his back, he lost himself to the pain, to the efforts he took to dull that pain. And then he lost everything else, too.

“You won’t,” Athena said, even as Bobby shook his head in denial. “You won’t. It hurts now, but of course it does. You fell off a bridge-”

“Rolled. I rolled off a bridge.”

Ass over teakettle he went, right into that metal box.

“But just because it hurts now, doesn’t mean you’re right back where you were. You know that,” Athena told him. “And we can work with your doctor, find better ways to handle it in the meantime. A massage therapist, maybe.”

“I don’t think I could handle someone touching my back,” Bobby said. “I’d probably try to punch them.”

And fail, because it would require moving his back too much.

“I think a good place to start is with a distraction. Get you out of your head and focused on something other than your back.”

Bobby did his best to summon a smirk.

“I’m not sure how active of a participant I can be right now. You might have to take the reins.”

Athena smiled and rolled her eyes at him.

“While I have no doubt in my abilities to keep you very distracted,” she said. “I had something else in mind.”

“Oh?”

“I met an arson investigator yesterday morning,” she explained. “I volunteered you to help him with his current case.”

“Why?”

“Because there’s been six arsons, and the One Eighteen has responded to four of them.”

Alright. She had his attention.

(Athena always had his attention.)

On Tuesday, they invaded Ravi and Albert’s space, to use their building’s pool so Buck and Eddie could teach their youngest how not to drown. Chris came along just to take advantage of the water.

They met Albert at the entrance, Eddie carrying the toddler and diaper bag while Buck was stuck carrying towels and everything else. 

“Hello Diazes, Ravi’s already at the pool,” Albert said, then pointed at Gavin. “If that one poops in the water, we’re all getting banned.”

“Don’t worry, he’s in special diapers,” Buck told him. “Aren’t they adorable?”

Technically speaking, Gavin was wearing two special diapers, a reusable one over top a disposable one. They were absolutely not gonna risk any surprise floaters today, no sir. And they were very much adorable, if Buck’s opinion mattered any: there were little cartoon fishes on them and everything.

Ravi was in the water when they got to the pool, and he waved at them without bothering to get out. Buck picked one of the lounge chairs to dump their things onto, and Chris practically threw his crutches onto the ground beside it before he was hurrying to get into the water, too. Buck and Eddie followed at a more sedate pace, the former slipping in while the latter sat on the edge, Gavin in his lap.

“Okay, little guy,” Buck said, grabbing hold of Gavin’s feet and wiggling them. Somewhere behind him, he heard Chris and Ravi get into a splash fight. “This is just like a big bath, okay? A really big bath.”

“You like baths, right, mijo?” Eddie reached in and splashed Buck in the face, which made Gavin giggle. “Or you don’t hate them, at least.”

“I think your other kid is drowning my boyfriend,” Albert said. Buck turned to look and saw that Chris did, indeed, seem to be doing his best to put Ravi into a watery grave.

“Eh, he’s fine,” Buck said. The younger man rolled his eyes and went to rescue Ravi. “Alright, kiddo, you ready?”

“You sure about this, mi sol?” Eddie asked as Buck reached out to grab Gavin.

“Absolutely. It’ll be fine.”

It was not fine. The moment Buck started bringing him into the water, Gavin started crying. Arms flailing, legs kicking.

“Hey, no, it’s fine, it’s fine, it’s- Eddie! Eds, how does that stupid milk song go!?”

Eddie rolled his eyes and slipped into the pool with them.

“Arrorró, mi niño. Arrorró, mi sol. Arrorró, pedazo de mi corazón,” he sang, and like magic, Gavin started calming down. He was still very unhappy with what was going on, but at least he wasn’t making it the entire city’s problem. “There we go, mijo.”

“Okay, let’s do this,” Buck said. He moved Gavin around so the tot was reclining against his chest, and used his arms to support the rest of him in the water. “Let’s go see what your big brother is doing, yeah?”

“Drowning Ravi, still,” Eddie said, following Buck as he walked backwards. “I think- No, wait, it’s Albert. He’s drowning Albert now.”

“Chris!” Buck called over his shoulder. “Stop trying to kill them! I don’t want to have to find coverage for them tomorrow.”

“Okay!” Chris shouted back. There was the sound of sputtering and splashing, presumably the pre-teen releasing his victims. Buck wasn’t looking, so he couldn’t tell.

“It’s like being glared at by Senorita after a bath,” Eddie said.

“They’ll get over it. Won’t they, little guy? Now let’s work on teaching you how not to drown…”

Puzzle Pieces

Chapter Summary

In which the characters finally realize what the readers figured out half a year ago.

Bobby was just finishing up his lunch - a simple sandwich, because he hadn’t felt like making anything more - when he heard the front door open. Footsteps, two pairs, started down the stairs, and Athena shouted out for him.

“In here,” he called back.

Athena was in uniform when she stepped through the den and into the kitchen, as she’d gone into work earlier that morning. The man with her was wearing an LAFD windbreaker over a department polo and slacks. He was short and balding, and carrying a folder fat with papers.

“Bobby, this is Jay Knight,” Athena said, gesturing at the man. “We met Sunday morning when I was sent to follow through on a call about an attempted arson.”

Bobby started to get up, only to be waved back down by the both of them. He was grateful for it - his back had given a painful twinge the moment tried.

“It’s nice to meet you, captain,” Knight said, offering his free hand for Bobby to shake.

“Likewise,” he said. “I just had lunch, did you-”

“We’re fine.” Athena’s interruption came with a fond roll of her eyes.

“My wife always packs me something, I’m good, “ Knight assured him, pulling out a chair and taking a seat. He set the folder down in front of him. “Now, the sergeant here seems to think that we can help each other.”

“I do feel like I’ve been set up on a blind date,” Bobby remarked, eying his wife. Athena, in the middle of pouring herself some lemonade, merely looked over her shoulder and raised a prim eyebrow. Like she was daring him to complain about it.

“But how much has she told you?”

“There’s been six structure fires that you’ve determined are connected,” he said. “And that my station has responded to four of them.”

“Seven fires,” Knight corrected. “That we’ve confirmed.”

“There was another?” Athena asked, sitting down at Bobby’s side with her glass of lemonade. She placed a second glass in front of him, along with a pair of his painkillers, and stared him down until he obediently swallowed the both.

“An apartment fire, Sunday night,” Knight said, tapping on the folder. “The One Eighteen responded to that one, too. All signs point to it being done by the same person, with a make-shift incendiary device placed at a spot where it would catch and spread.”

“Counting the attempt that morning, that’s twice in a single day,” Athena remarked. “Are they escalating?”

“Either that, or they knew their first attempt was foiled.”

“How?” Bobby asked, reaching over and pulling the folder towards him.

“Your guess is as good as mine.” Knight shrugged. “Maybe they drove past and saw it was still standing. Maybe they’ve been keeping an eye on the news, or maybe they have access to a scanner.”

“Wouldn’t be the first time someone obtained one of those for dubious purposes,” Athena said. Her tone was dark, and Bobby understood exactly why: after the ladder truck bombing, after Freddie Costas was arrested, a scanner had been found in his workshop of horrors. The thought of someone doing something similar was not pleasant.

“They do sell them on Amazon for-”

“Athena.” Bobby had opened the folder and had begun to flip through the information. Except he’d stopped nearly immediately, shock raising up his spine.

“What is it?” his wife asked. In response, he tapped on the photos that accompanied the first fire.

There was a sharp intake of breath, and then she dragged the folder closer to herself.

“What?” Knight asked, leaning over the table slightly. His eyes were looking between Bobby, Athena, and the folder.

“The first fire,” Athena said. “It was the Diazes’ neighbors. They called it in, they were the first ones there.”

“Diazes?” Knight repeated. “As in Edmundo and Evan Diaz?”

“Eddie and Buck, yes,” Bobby confirmed. Athena flipped the page, and they both flinched at the pictures that were revealed. He’d known that there’d be pictures of the victims, but he still hadn’t been prepared to see them.

Athena shut the file, more firmly than she really needed to.

“The same Diazes that work at your station, one of whom is currently acting captain?” Knight continued. “The same station that responded to most of the fires?”

Bobby shared a look with Athena. He could tell that she didn’t like where this was heading, either.

“Yes,” he admitted, drawing the word out.

“Most,” Athena emphasized. “Not all of them.”

“I think,” Knight said, reaching over and pulling the folder back to him, “that it would be beneficial to talk with the Firefighters Diaz.”

“I’ll go with you,” Bobby blurted out. He - slowly, carefully - pushed himself out of his seat. “They’re both on shift today, it would be nice to visit the station.”

“We’ll both go,” Athena declared.

The arson investigator regarded them silently for several moments. Finally, he gave a shrug and stood up as well.

“Fine,” Knight said. “I’ll see you both at the station, I guess.”

It was hard to hurry into his shoes and up the stairs in his current state, though Bobby certainly tried. Athena kindly, patiently, trailed behind him the entire way up and to her police cruiser. Knight hadn’t waited and was already gone, a fact that had Bobby’s stomach twisting in worry.

He didn’t like the idea of this stranger storming into his station and accusing his son of anything. Not without him there to stand between them and clobber the man for it, his back be damned.

Thankfully, though, Knight was still in his car when they pulled into the station’s parking lot. He was just getting off the phone, and didn’t get out until he spotted the pair of them heading his way. The windbreaker he’d been wearing was laying in the backseat.

The station was busy when they entered. There were people rolling up hoses in the bay and going through inventory of the trucks. There was the sound of laughter coming from the upstairs area, and weights clanging in the gym.

It felt almost like going home again.

“Pops! Ma!” Buck shouted the moment he spotted them. He bounded towards them, exactly like an overexcited golden retriever. Eddie followed at a more sedate pace. “I didn’t expect to see you today!”

Buck went to hug them, only to freeze mid-motion when he realized Athena was in uniform.

“Is, uh.” He glanced over his shoulder at a frowning Eddie, and then warily eyed Knight, a complete stranger to him. “Is everything okay?”

“I’m actually here to talk to you and your husband,” Knight told him. “Is there somewhere we can do that without all the… Eyes?”

He gestured at everyone else in the station, who were all watching them curiously.

“Sure, um. We can use Cap’s office,” Buck said with an anxious glance to Bobby and Athena.

The office wasn’t very different from how it’d looked the morning of the bridge collapse. It was pure muscle memory to take the seat behind the desk, and Bobby didn’t think about it until his butt met the cushion. Buck didn’t seem to care: he was still standing, Eddie by his side, looking at the three of them in turn until his gaze caught on the folder in Knight’s hand.

“There’re less stacks of paperwork than I remember,” Bobby noted. Not to say that there was no paperwork waiting to be gone through.

“The meds have helped,” Buck said. “So uh, what’s going on? Who are you?”

“This is Jay Knight,” Bobby said. “He’s an arson investigator.”

“There’s been a series of arsons,” Knight explained, putting the folder down on the desk. “It’s been difficult to put a pattern to them, because we hadn’t been able to find a connection between them other than the method.”

“I met Knight on Sunday morning,” Athena said. “I was responding to a call, a homeowner put out an attempt to burn her house down by herself. We talked, he explained what was going on.”

“And then you drafted me to help,” Bobby added, giving his wife a mock glare.

“Idle hands…”

“That explains the who and the what,” Eddie said. “What about the why?”

“One of the common threads, besides the method and the timing of the last few fires,” Knight said. “Was that your station responded to most of them. Five of the last seven.”

“Are you talking about the fires we’ve been responding to first thing?” Buck asked. “There was one this morning. Wait, five?”

“Six of the last eight, then. And those fires are part of a later pattern. There were two to start with that didn’t fit the timing, but were started the same way.”

“Is that why you’re here?” Eddie asked. “Because the One Eighteen has responded to so many? Wait, then why did you want to talk to both of us? Why not just Buck? He’s acting captain.”

Bobby shared an uneasy look with Athena.

“Because,” he said, reaching over and opening the folder, “the moment I started looking through these fires, I spotted another connection. One that Knight didn’t know enough to see.”

“Okay.” Buck frowned, leaned down to look at what was in the folder. “I don’t-”

He stopped and stared for a long moment.

“Eds, that’s our street,” he said, the words coming out in a whisper. “This address is- Is this Edward and Shanna’s house? I didn’t… With everything that happened the next day, I didn’t even think…”

“It was arson?” Eddie asked, looking at the files over Buck’s shoulder.

“It was the first of these arsons,” Knight said. “That we know of. The only thing connecting it to the others was how it was started. Until Captain Nash and Sergeant Grant helped me realize that you, Captain Diaz, connected it to all but one of the others.”

Buck blinked, gave a wild glance to Bobby and Athena, and then frantically turned to Knight.

“I didn’t do it!” he just about shouted. “I- I mean, sure, they were homophobic assholes and their shitty brats egged our house, and we’re pretty sure they-”

“Buck!” Eddie snapped, making frantic motions to silence his husband.

Bobby let out a despairing sigh and covered his face. He felt Athena pat him on the shoulder.

“I know you didn’t,” Knight said. Everyone stared at him, even Bobby. “Your alibi checked out.”

“I have an alibi?” Buck blurted out. “I- I mean, of course it checked out. Because I didn’t do it.” Then he leaned in close to Eddie and, in what was probably meant to be a whisper, asked, “What was my alibi?”

“I called Captain Mayfair,” Knight told them. “She confirmed that you’ve been arriving about half an hour early ever since you were appointed acting captain. With our estimated times for when these fires were set, it just doesn’t work out. For either of you.”

“Oh. I told you I didn’t do it,” Buck mumbled, turning back to the folder. He started flipping through the files, stopping when Bobby slapped a hand down on them.

Bobby shook his head and hurriedly flipped through the papers, until he felt they were comfortably past the gruesome photos. Buck looked at him in confusion for a moment before he seemed to realize what Bobby was keeping him from seeing, because he grimaced and gave him a thankful nod.

“So what do you know?” Eddie asked, perching himself on the back of one of the chairs sitting in front of the desk..

“We know that the incendiary device they’ve been using has been consistent, based on what we’ve recovered,” Knight explained. “A lit cigarette with three matches wrapped in notebook paper and secured with a rubber band, stuck in-”

“The Pillow Pyro!” Buck shouted, looking up excitedly from the case files. “John Leonard Orr, he was one of the biggest arsonists ever! He was a fire captain in Glendale. That was how he started all of his. He’s currently doing time in Centinela.” He twisted around to look at Knight. “Are you gonna go talk to him? Ask him what he thinks?”

“This isn’t Backdraft,” Knight dryly remarked. Buck, Bobby noticed, started bouncing in place. “You can tell, because none of us have to wear those stupid thigh-highs.”

“Before the Nineties-”
