“Mi sol,” Eddie said, gently gripping his shoulder and derailing what was no doubt going to be an informative rant.

“Right, right.” Buck turned back to the folder and tapped the page he was on. “Uh, this other fire we didn’t respond to. It was the day Hen came back to work.”

“What about her?” Athena prompted.

“Not her specifically,” he continued. “But uh, C-shift got called out for a bus crash almost as soon as I got to the station. They didn’t get back until, I dunno. A couple hours later. The address for this fire is near Oxford Square, I’m pretty sure we would’ve responded to it if they hadn’t been called out.”

“The one the homeowner put out on Sunday morning was in Lafayette Square,” Athena said, words tense. “That would have also been in your cover area, right?”

“It would,” Bobby confirmed with a nod.

“So that would be every fire then,” Knight declared. “Except for the first one, we can assume every fire was set with the intent of the One Eighteen responding to them. And the first one was your neighbors.”

It was not a comforting thing for any of them to hear. Bobby knew it wasn’t for him.

“So what do we think that was about yesterday?” Albert asked the moment they were outside the station, and away from anyone else.

“I have no idea,” Ravi answered. He’d been just as curious as anyone else, when Bobby and Athena both came in with whoever that stranger had been. The fact that they’d taken Eddie and Buck to the captain’s office, for a discussion that either man refused to talk about afterwards?

It was suspicious as shit.

“Maybe we should start checking the trucks for bombs,” Albert said. “Y’know, just in case.”

“Get in the car,” Ravi said, shoving at him. His boyfriend just laughed and did as he was told, sliding into the passenger seat as Ravi got behind the wheel.

“I’m just saying, we’ve all heard the horror stories,” Albert was saying as they pulled out. “Everything was fine, mostly, when I got here. But if something like that is about to happen again, I- Rav, this isn’t the way to the apartment.”

“I know,” Ravi said, having turned left instead of right at the intersection.

“So… Where are we going?”

“It’s a surprise.”

Ravi threw his boyfriend a bright smile before turning back to the road.

“I’m having a bit of deja vu here,” Albert complained. He was somehow managing to sprawl in his seat, despite the lack of space. “I think I liked it better when the positions were reversed.”

“You’ll like it when we get there.” Or so Ravi hoped. “Shut up and look pretty.”

“We just got off a twenty-four, why-”

“I said shut up and look pretty!”

“Sorry, sorry.”

It took most of an hour to get through Los Angeles traffic. Albert, fortunately, didn’t complain for more than ten minutes about how they could have been in bed. Unfortunately, he spent most of the rest of the time belting along with the radio.

Albert could not sing; Ravi loved him anyway.

“That’s a sign for Disney,” Albert said, stopping mid-song to point it out. “Why are we getting off the freeway in Anaheim?”

“Because this is where the surprise is,” Ravi answered simply.

He could see hope and excitement begin to light up in his boyfriend’s eyes, and he was almost tempted to just spill it all right then. Except the singing had been very bad, and Ravi was not actually as good of a person as he pretended to be. So he kept quiet and watched as Albert got more and more visibly excited as they passed more signs directed them towards the Disney park.

And then he watched it dim as they passed up the turn into the resort’s parking, where they’d go if they were just doing a single day trip. Albert slumped in his seat, letting out the saddest of sighs.

“That was just pathetic,” Ravi said.

“Shut up,” Albert grumbled. “I got excited, okay?”

“Obviously.”

“Shut up,” he whined. “Where’s this surprise?”

“Here,” Ravi said, taking the turn into the parking lot for one of the park’s hotels.

Albert made a squealing noise that he would absolutely be getting made fun of for later, and then reached over to smack Ravi’s arm. Hard.

“Hey! I’m driving!” Ravi complained.

“You’re an ass!”

“I am taking you to Disney.”

“You could have told me that earlier . You liked watching me get my hopes up only to have them die,” Albert accused. Ravi could only shrug. “Whatever, hurry up and find a parking space.”

“You’re being extremely bossy, Alley Cat.”

“Hurry up and park!”

Ravi rolled his eyes, but did as he was bid. As soon as the car was in park and Ravi was yanking the keys out of the ignition, Albert was scrambling out the door. Ravi figured he was running full sprint for the nearest park entrance, except then his own door was thrown open and he was being manhandled. He found himself pressed against the side of the car and kissed senseless.

When Albert stepped back, after an indecently long make-out session for such a public area, Ravi was left to catch his breath.

“Um, uh,” Ravi stuttered, all language having abandoned him. “Huh.”

“C’mon, let’s go!” Albert grabbed his hand and started dragging him. Ravi wasn’t sure which direction they were headed, much less if it was the right direction, because he was still trying to gather his wits.

“W- Wait, our bag!”

“Hurry up!”

“So in the nineties, they released a profile for firefighter arsonists,” Buck said, scrolling through his phone.

It was Thursday night, and he was sprawled out on their bed with his laptop open in front of him and surrounded by copies of the papers from the arson cases. He’d offered to help, and Knight - apparently pressed for assistance - had happily accepted. The arson investigator even emailed him doorbell camera footage, from the homeowner who managed to prevent herself from becoming a statistic on Sunday.

It was currently playing on his laptop, though he wasn’t paying much attention to it.

“And you’re about to tell me what it says,” Eddie guessed. He was folding clothes on the side of the bed that wasn’t covered like a murder board. A task made difficult by the kitten swiping her claws at everything.

“You know me so well, babe,” Buck gushed, leaning over to peck a kiss on his cheek. “Okay, so, a firefighter arsonist is typically a white male-”

“Of course.”

“Between seventeen and their mid-twenties. Typically, one or both of their parents are missing from the picture, but if they’re both present, then their relationship with their parents isn’t good. Uh, probably living with their parents. Lack of stable interpersonal relationships, a history of poor occupational adjustment.”

“What does that mean?” Eddie asked. He held up a sock; Senorita immediately started batting at it. “This is more hole than fabric.”

“Yeah, I definitely need new ones,” Buck admitted. “But uh, I’m not sure what it means, either. It also says they did low-paying jobs, or did work that required menial labor.”

“Vague.”

“This is all vague. Uh, firefighter arsonists typically become firefighters for the excitement, not to help people,” Buck continued, going down the list. “Average to above average intelligence, but poor performance in school. And- Oh, of course.”

“What?”

“A history of: alcoholism, childhood hyperactivity, depression, borderline personality, suicidal tendencies, and! Homosexuality.”

“Be gay, do crimes,” Eddie said, and Buck burst out laughing. “What does that even mean? That a firefighter is more likely to be an arsonist if they’re gay? What about you? Are you only fifty percent as likely? What about me?”

“Well, I- Huh.” Buck blinked down at his phone as a thought struck him.

“Gonna share with the class?” Eddie asked, poking him in the side when he was silent for too long.

“White male,” Buck said, reading them off like a checklist. “Poor relationship with parents. Lack of stable interpersonal relationships. History of low-paying, menial labor jobs. Did poorly in school. Childhood hyperactivity, depression, suicidal tendencies… Babe, I think this list is describing me.”

“Probably not the best thing to joke about,” Eddie said. He’d given up on the laundry and was now playing tug of war with Senorita, using Buck’s poor, abused sock. “Not when you came close to being accused of it already.”

“Yeah, you’re-”

There was movement on the laptop screen, a flash of yellow that had Buck reaching out to slap the keyboard. The video paused, stuck on what he had seen: a bright yellow mini cooper going down the street. A very familiar yellow mini cooper.

One that had no reason to be there at that time of day.

His hand shaking, Buck glanced down at the list on his phone, and then back to the screen.

White male. Early twenties. Missing dad. Living with his mom. Did poorly in school. Definitely lacked stable interpersonal relationships.

“He never answered the question,” Buck said, more to himself than to Eddie.

“Mi sol?”

“He never answered the question.” He couldn’t stop looking at the car on the screen. The driver wasn’t visible, but he’d seen that car before. “I asked him why he wanted to be a firefighter, and he never actually told me. And- And that night, when we had everyone over for the engagement party, he was staring at their house…”

Eddie reached over, laid his hand on top of Buck’s knee.

“Who?” he asked. “Who are you talking about?”

“Wolfe, the probie.” The words came out breathless. “He- He was acting weird on Sunday, remember? High strung, anxious. What if it was because he was expecting the alarm to go off, but the longer it didn’t…”

“Buck,” Eddie asked, slowly and carefully. “What are you saying?”

“I- I never saw his car,” he said, instead of answering. The fire they responded to with the college girls, the one that they found Wolfe already there. The probie claimed to have been driving by when he saw the fire, but… “His car wasn’t there.”

He pulled up Athena’s contact info and pressed call. It rang, and it rang.

“Do you know what time it is?” she asked, sounding annoyed.

“Ma.” He stopped, cleared his throat. “Sergeant Grant. I think I have information about the arson case.”

The bright yellow mini cooper stayed unmoving on the screen.

Circumstantial Frustration

Chapter Summary

In which Buck runs into a roadblock.

Chapter Notes

Today's the day, guys!

“Alright,” Jay Knight said, rubbing his brow. “Run this by me. Sergeant Grant said you think you know who the arsonist is?”

They were gathered around the Grant-Nash dining table. When Buck called Athena the night before, she’d listened to what he said and took it seriously, telling him to come over the next day with the evidence. So here he and Eddie were, while Abuela watched the boys.

Buck had brought his laptop with him, and it had already played through the doorbell camera footage once while they waited for the arson investigator to arrive. Currently, the screen was paused to show the arsonist himself crossing the yard: tall, in jeans and a purple Lakers hoodie, with his face half-covered by a black cloth mask and shadowed by his hood and a red-billed cap, and his hands covered by blue nitrile gloves.

“We have this probie at the station,” Buck said. “He’s… Problematic.”

“Understatement,” Eddie muttered.

“His name’s Dalton Wolfe,” Bobby added. He was sitting at the head of the table. Athena, not in uniform this time, was standing at his side with a hand on his shoulder. “He transferred to us the month before the fires started.”

“Transferred?” Knight’s eyebrows rose.

“Problematic,” Buck reiterated.

“Okay. What does he have to do with the fires?”

“The day before the first fire,” Buck said, “before Edward and Shanna’s house burnt, we had an engagement party for my sister at our house. Wolfe was the last person to arrive. And he spent a long time, I mean a long time, kinda… Staring? At their house. It was the only house near us with a Trump flag, so he knew it was theirs.”

“You did mention having trouble with them. But the fires…?”

“He drives a yellow mini cooper. Which, I don’t know how he fits into it, because he’s taller than me. But I was looking through everything last night, talking with Eddie, and I had the camera footage playing. And that’s when I saw it.”

Buck reached over and unpaused the video. As they watched, the arsonist - Wolfe, Buck knew it was Wolfe - ran off screen. A few minutes later, the yellow mini cooper was flying by, in the direction of what was undoubtedly the station.

Knight frowned, rewound the video and let it play again. Then again. After the third time, he paused it with the yellow car on the screen.

“So it’s a yellow car,” he finally said.

“It’s the same car,” Buck insisted.

Knight glanced at the video again. The quality wasn’t good enough to make out the driver.

“The morning of the bridge collapse, Jones got really angry at him, remember?” Buck asked; Bobby nodded. “I’d never heard Jones shout at someone before, but that day he did. And I finally asked Ortiz what the probie said to tick Jones off, and according to him, Wolfe was talking about what he would’ve done. How if he’d been there at the fire, he wouldn’t have stayed outside, he would’ve run in and rescued them. But there was no way he could’ve known what we did.”

“There was a yellow car the night of the fire,” Eddie added. “I didn’t pay attention to it enough to remember what kind of car, but it almost hit Buck when we ran across the street.”

“So he might,” Knight said, “might have been there for the first fire. He might have been driving past the attempted fire.”

Buck huffed out a breath, feeling his irritation rise. Only Eddie’s hand on his thigh kept him from snapping at anyone.

“He was the one who called in the second fire,” he said. “He wasn’t working, he was suspended for the day. And he did exactly what he said we should have done: run in and be heroic. And for these other fires, the ones early in the morning? That was when he started running late for his shifts.”

“Tardiness doesn’t mean he’s an arsonist,” Athena remarked. Her tone was level, but she was watching him in interest. She believed him, Buck could tell.

“My first day back, the morning of that failed attempt,” Eddie said. “The probie was acting weird. Buck was concerned about it, enough to pull him into the office and ask if something was wrong.”

“Weird how?” Bobby asked. He started to lean forward, winced, and settled back into his chair.

“He was anxious,” Buck explained. “It got worse the more time passed without the alarm going off. And he stuck around by the turnout gear. He did the same the shift before, the Thursday before Jones’ funeral. He stayed in the truck bay, hovering around his gear, and he kept glancing up like he was waiting for the alarm to go off.”

“Sunday night,” Eddie said. “Everyone was in the bunks except for the two of us, we were on the roof. But then Bosko came up, saying she didn’t want to stay downstairs because she was avoiding the probie. Everyone was in the bunks or up on the roof. No one would have seen or noticed if he left and came back.”

“The night after the bus crash,” Buck said, remembering. “The one we didn’t respond to? The same night as the jumper, remember, Ma?”

“I do.” Athena nodded.

“That night, we got enough down time to sleep until we got called for something minor, sometime after midnight,” he continued. “We came back, everyone went back to the bunks. But I couldn’t fall back asleep, for whatever reason. So I hung around the loft with a book. And I caught Wolfe sneaking out of the bunks.”

Knight sat there quietly, eyes boring into the laptop screen and the frozen image of the yellow car. His finger was tapping on the tabletop, an even metronome in the silence of the house. Buck waited, impatient, for him to say something, anything.

“Okay,” the investigator finally said. “Say that I believe you.” Yes! “We don’t have proof.”

“We don’t have-?” Buck grabbed the laptop and jabbed his finger into the screen. “That’s his car!”

“I don’t know that,” Knight countered. “We can’t make out the driver clearly, we can’t see the license plate, we can’t even see if there’s any bumper stickers. This is just a random yellow mini cooper. Do you know how many of them there are in this city? I don’t.”

“But it’s obviously him!” Buck argued, his frustration getting the better of him. “We just listed off all of that evidence!”

“Circumstantial evidence, Buckaroo,” Athena said. She didn’t even tell him off about his tone. Probably because she shared his frustration. “It might be his car. He might have been acting weird because he was setting the fires. Or, it’s someone else’s car, and he was acting weird for an entirely different, entirely innocent, reason.”

“But that second fire, the day he was suspended. He was definitely there, he called it in. He got to act heroic and- And I told him he did a good job.”

That might be the most galling part of it. Wolfe set a fire, got to act heroic by saving the people he endangered, and then Buck showed up like an idiot and gave him praise. God, had that come across as encouragement? Did Wolfe hear those words and take them to mean he should go out and set more houses on fire? Put more people in danger?

Kill more people?

“We don’t have proof,” Knight repeated. “It’s suspicious, but it’s not proof.”

“So how do we get proof?” Eddie asked. He hadn’t removed his hand from Buck’s thigh, and he squeezed it in comfort.

“They stuck a tracking device on John Leonard Orr’s car,” Buck said. “Two, actually. He found the first one and thought it was a bomb.”

“That got approved because they had a matching fingerprint,” Knight pointed out. “And it took time.”

“We don’t have time!”

“We could put a tail on him,” Athena offered, and Buck got his hopes up. “But it’d have to be approved, too. And again, without proof, it wouldn’t be a priority.”

“So, what?” Buck asked, being very careful not to snap at her. “We just wait? We know he’s going to start another fire, and we just let him?”

“We don’t know that,” Athena said, even though she didn’t sound like she believed it either. “We suspect it.”

“Arson is one of the hardest crimes to prove someone guilty of,” Knight said, and Buck knew that. Knew it, and hated it. “The crime itself can erase all the evidence they leave behind. Orr got indicted because the tracking device had him showing up at another suspicious fire. He’s suspected to have set how many?”

“Two-thousand,” Buck dutifully answered.

“And he got convicted of how many counts of arson?”

“Twenty-three.”

“We need proof,” Knight said, leaning back in his chair. “Hard evidence that links him directly to the fires. An eyewitness, a video that shows him better.”

“He’ll make a mistake,” Bobby said. It was the voice he used all the other times he tried to defuse a situation. His peacekeeping tone, reasonable and even. “They always make mistakes. If he’s the one setting these fires, he’ll make a mistake somewhere. And we’ll get him then.”

Buck so desperately wanted to rage. He wanted to scream himself hoarse until they listened to him, until they did more than just give him empty platitudes and promises. Until they did more than insist that all the evidence he gave them wasn’t enough.

But that wouldn’t actually get him anywhere, would it? All that would achieve was pissing off everyone in front of him.

“Fine,” Buck bit out. He closed and grabbed his laptop, and then stood. “Then I’ll… I’ll wait, then.”

Wait until Wolfe messed up.

Wait until they were putting out another fire that Wolfe set.

Wait until Wolfe killed someone else.

Buck stormed off, Eddie following right behind.

Bobby sighed when he heard the front door slam shut. The headache forming was almost worse than his back.

“Your son is absolutely going to do something stupid,” Athena remarked, eyes fixed on where Buck and Eddie had gone.

“My son?” Bobby scowled up at her, not that she noticed. “You mean our son.”

“He’s your son whenever he’s about to be an idiot and play amateur detective.”

“Does he do that a lot?” Knight asked, sounding curious. Bobby had almost forgotten he was there.

“Too often,” Athena said with a sigh. “It once got him put in hospital jail.”

Bobby watched Knight mouth the words ‘hospital jail’ to himself.

“I’m not sure how relevant this will be,” Bobby said. That was a lie: he was pretty certain what he was about to say was very, very relevant. “But Dalton Wolfe’s uncle is Randolf Torres.”

“Randolf Torres?” Knight repeated. “As in Deputy Chief Randolf Torres?”

“The one and only.”

“Shit.” The investigator rubbed at his brow again. Maybe he was getting a headache, too. “Then we definitely need something concrete.”

“He couldn’t interfere with the case any, could he?”

“I… I don’t think so?” Knight didn’t sound very sure. “He doesn’t oversee arson investigations at all. And besides, criminal investigation - investigating his nephew - would be entirely up to the police. There’s no reason he should even know about it before his nephew does.”

“But?”

“But Torres has been in the department for decades,” he said. “He’s been a deputy chief for years. That’s the type of job that requires rubbing shoulders with all sorts of people. He could’ve made friends in a lot of places.”

“And if any of those friends hear we’re investigating his nephew for arson and murder,” Athena said, “they might let it slip.”

Knight nodded grimly.

At which point, Bobby knew, one of two things could happen. Either Torres stood aside and said nothing, allowing them to investigate his nephew. Or Torres protected his nephew. Wolfe could simply, one day, move out of state and out of their reach, free to do the same thing somewhere else.

“We need proof, and we need to do it without the wrong people finding out,” Bobby said. “So we-” Athena stared down at him, eyebrows raised. “So you need to investigate it delicately.”

“That’s better,” she said, patting his shoulder. “Unfortunately, your plan has a flaw.”

“Buck.”

“Buck,” she agreed. “And Eddie. Because whatever mess that boy gets into, Eddie will be right there with him.”

Bobby groaned and rubbed at his face.

“Maybe,” he said, “I should retire.”

His wife smacked him on the shoulder.

This Casa de Diaz came with a detached garage. Buck and Eddie didn’t use it as such though. Instead, they’d converted it into a makeshift home gym. Most of the equipment was what Buck had at the loft, once upon a time. Since moving in, they’d added a treadmill and a freestanding punching bag.

After they’d gotten home from Bobby and Athena’s, Buck greeted Abuela and the kiddos, while doing his best to keep his frustration from overflowing. It wasn’t his family’s fault, after all, and he absolutely did not want to take it out on them. But that didn’t mean those feelings just disappeared. So he’d changed into work-out clothes and headed into the garage. And he’d been taking out his aggression on the punching bag ever since.

His phone, sitting on top of some shelves, started to ring. For the briefest of moments, he was tempted to ignore it. He didn’t; he wiped his hands off on a towel and swiped his finger across the screen. TK’s face immediately filled it.

“Someone’s working up a sweat,” his friend said, practically leering at him through the screen. “Looks good on you.”

“Thanks,” Buck drawled, propping the phone up so they could converse without him holding it. “What’s up?”

His tone was more than a bit sharp, and his friend noticed immediately.

“Well I was calling to just kvetch. But I think you need it more than me.”

Buck looked away from the dizzying motion of TK moving with his phone. It looked like he was at his and Carlos’ place, judging from his lack of uniform and the background when his friend finally settled down on the couch.

“I don’t,” Buck insisted, going back to the punching bag. “Kvetch away.”

“Uh-huh. The last time I saw someone rage punching like that, Carlos was pissed off about that swatting prank. C’mon, tell me, what’s turned my precious Sunshine into Raging Bull?”

Buck leaned against the bag, let out a sigh, and said,

“The probie burnt my neighbors’ house down.”

There was silence from the other end of the call. When he looked over, TK was just staring at him from the screen, mouth hanging open slightly.

“I think,” TK said, “that I’m going to need some context, Bucky Boy.”

So Buck explained it to him, whaling on the punching bag as he did. He told TK about the other fires. He told him about the probie’s odd behavior, his habit of being late to work. He told him about the probie’s car, and how he could place it at two of the scenes, at least. And then he told him about the meeting earlier.

“But they kept saying that we need more proof,” Buck said, punching with his words. “Because everything I gave them wasn’t enough! But to get more proof, they need more proof.”

He kicked it, sending the bag toppling backwards onto the floor.

“They need more proof,” he said again, panting heavily, “to get more proof.”

“No wonder you’re trying to murder that thing.”

“I don’t know what I’m going to do,” Buck confessed. “He’s going to do it again tomorrow, I know he is. And I, what? I’m supposed to be able to look at him and not punch him in the face?”

“Wait, do I have to be the reasonable one now?” TK asked, sounding horrified. From off-screen, Buck heard Carlos let out a laugh. “Okay, uh. Punching him won’t get you proof of anything. Though, if you punch him hard enough, it might solve the problem anyway. And also cost you your job.”

At that moment, Buck thought that almost sounded like a fair trade.

“You guys had a serial arsonist, didn’t you?”

“You mean the guy who burnt down Carlos’ apartment and blew up the station?”

“Yeah, him. How did they catch the guy?”

TK fell quiet again. When Buck looked over at his phone, his friend’s gaze was fixed on something beyond it. He looked… Sad.

“Carlos’ dad,” TK finally said. “He uh, he lied about there being a witness in the hospital. To bait the guy into coming in to kill them.”

“I’m not sure how well that would work this time.” Not if Wolfe dressed for every fire the same way he did in the video Buck saw. “Any other ideas?”

“...Punching him?”

Buck grabbed the towel and threw it at his phone.

Later, much later, he dragged himself back into the house to shower and change. Afterwards, he tracked down his husband. Eddie was in the nursery, sprawled out on the floor while he played with Gavin, Senorita the cat curled up on the small of his back.

“Feeling better?” he asked, looking up.

“No,” Buck admitted readily. “What are we playing?”

“Oh, the usual.”

“Block,” Gavin said, handing him a bright red one. At the tot’s prompting, Buck dutifully placed it on top of the stack he was building. And then, of course, Gavin knocked the entire thing over with a delighted squeal.

“I have no idea what I’m going to do tomorrow,” Buck said.

“What we’re going to do tomorrow,” Eddie corrected.

Buck nodded, because yeah, of course it was the two of them. His husband, Buck had no doubt, was probably just as frustrated as he himself was. Eddie was simply waiting his turn to go vent.

“The punching bag’s free,” Buck said, sitting down on the floor. He scooped Senorita up and plopped her in his lap. “If you want to go a round.”

“Nah,” Eddie said. He took the block Gavin handed him and started stacking again. “I’m fine here.”

Boiling Point

Chapter Summary

In which Buck is a man of action.

Buck was not any less frustrated the next morning. In fact, his frustrations seemed to have festered overnight, causing him to wake up feeling more than a bit snappish. To the point that he started biting Athena’s head off, while on the phone with her on the way to the station.

“No, there wasn’t a tail put on him this morning,” was her answer to his queries. “Captain Maynard thought everything was too flimsy to justify it.”

“Flimsy!?” Buck snapped. “How was any of that flimsy? Did you tell her everything we told you?”

“Yes,” Athena said, her voice irritatingly calm. Buck knew she wasn’t talking down to him, but it really felt like she was. “But it’s like we told you yesterday, all of that is circumstantial. It’s not enough, we need more.”

“You’re never going to find more if you don’t do anything! What, you’re just going to sit back and let him set more fires? Kill more-”

“That’s enough.” Eddie reached up and tapped the screen of the phone, docked on the dash, and ended the call. It was probably a good thing, Buck would definitely feel embarrassed and ashamed for his outburst later, but he wasn’t feeling very appreciative of it at the moment. “You can apologize to her when you’re calmer.”

“Apologize!? Why do I-” Buck realized he was on the verge of shouting at his husband, and nearly bit his tongue off with how hard he shut his mouth. He flexed his fingers on the steering wheel, taking and releasing several breaths through his nose. “I’m sorry.”

“Are you going to be okay today?”

Probably not.

“Yes,” he ground out. “I’m not going to- I’m not gonna actually punch the piece of shit.”

“Good. Cause with the state you’re in, Lena might try to recruit you,” Eddie said. He smiled at the dirty look Buck threw at him. Then he sighed, the smile fading with it. “I’m sorry, mi sol.”

“I just-” Buck took another breath in an attempt to force down his temper. “I get Chimney’s phone unlocked, and I’m yelled at for not doing things the right way. I help solve that case of Sue’s hit-and-run, and I’m yelled at for not doing things the right way. I save Amy from her shitty step-father, and I’m yelled at for not doing things the right way. But this time? This time I did the right thing. I went to Ma, I didn’t try to do anything myself, and it. Wasn’t. Enough!”

He smacked the steering wheel again, hard enough that the heel of his palm stung with it.

“I did what everyone tells me I’m supposed to do,” he said, quieter now. Calmer, now, even with the fire still burning through his veins. “I didn’t rush off, I didn’t try to do it myself. I took everything to Ma, trusted the actual professionals to handle it. And it doesn’t matter, it’s not good enough.”

“It matters, mi sol,” Eddie insisted. He laid his hand, warm and comforting, on Buck’s thigh. “They know to keep an eye on him, now.”

“Fat lot of good knowing does if they don’t do it.”

“And,” his husband said, “you can throw this in our faces the next time we yell at you for rushing off half-cocked.”

Buck snorted out a laugh.

“I don’t think anyone would actually appreciate me doing that,” he said, and then smiled at Eddie. “Thank you.”

“How are we going to handle today?” Eddie asked him as they pulled into Buck’s normal parking spot at the station.

“I have no idea,” Buck admitted, shutting off the jeep and slumping in his seat. “I don’t know what to do, Eds. I know I’m right, I know it’s him. But I don’t know what to do to stop him. Athena and Knight, they keep saying they need better proof, and I don’t know how to get that proof.”

“How did they get that tracking device on Orr?” Eddie asked.

“There was a fingerprint,” Buck said. “One fingerprint, from the scene of one fire. That they couldn’t even match to him when they first found it.”

As firefighters, all their fingerprints were in the system. But Knight had been right, when he talked about the fire itself destroying evidence. And even if it didn’t, the arsonist - Wolfe - was wearing gloves in the video. The odds of getting a usable fingerprint, or any fingerprint at all, was dishearteningly low.

“So. How are we going to handle today?” Eddie asked again.

Buck heaved out a sigh and threw open his door.

“What I’ve been doing, I guess,” he said, grabbing his bag from the backseat. Eddie, on the other side of the jeep, got out and did the same. “Keep an eye on him, put out any fires he starts, and do our jobs.”

“And make sure he doesn’t know that we know,” his husband said as they headed into the station. “I do not want a repeat of Jonah.”

Buck paused mid-step, realizing the biggest problem with that idea: his poker face was awful. Eddie froze beside him, clearly having the same thoughts.

“I’ll just focus on not punching him,” Buck said, walking again. “It’ll be fine.”

Eddie, matching pace with him, did not look at all convinced. And for good reason, because it was very much not fine.

Buck would like to think that it at least started fine. He’d calmed down enough that he managed to get through the shift hand-over with Mayfair without his frustrations taking center stage. But the closer they got to the start of shift, the closer they got to the alarm going off and them having to put out another arson, the worse his mood became.

It was a special hell, knowing that something awful was happening and yet finding oneself utterly incapable of preventing it. Buck was a man of action, and it was killing him that he could not act. It was killing him that there was nothing he could do to stop any of it. Not any way he could see, at least. Not then, not in that moment.

Buck was simply stuck waiting.

Waiting for another fire, waiting for the probie to make a mistake, waiting for the others to find the vaunted evidence that they needed to prove what he already knew. Not that they were likely to find it, given that they kept insisting they needed the evidence before they could look for it.

“What’s wrong with the Cap?” he heard Toby ask somewhere behind him. Currently, Buck was standing at the railing of the loft, gripping it hard enough that his knuckles were white as he looked over the truck bay. And apparently his darkening mood was becoming more and more apparent.

Whoops.

“Don’t worry about it,” Eddie told him, leaning against the railing beside Buck. The man hadn’t left his side all morning. “Al, Rav, how was Disney?”

“It was so much fun,” Albert gushed. “I got to build a lightsaber!”

“He also ran over a little girl on the way to Mickey,” Ravi said.

“Oh my god.” Crawford sounded a mixture of amused and appalled.

“I didn’t run her over,” Albert grumbled. “And she should’ve gotten out of the way.”

Buck’s grip on the railing got even tighter as he spotted movement at the station doors. Wolfe was waltzing into the building, appearing for all the world like he hadn’t done a damn thing wrong. The sight of him had Buck’s temper flaring brighter than it had before, racing white-hot to burn through his veins.

“Probie!” he bellowed, voice echoing off the rafters. All the conversation around him ceased; the probie jumped like a startled rabbit. “You’re late!”

“Shift just started,” Hen commented.

“He’s not in uniform,” Buck ground out, not looking her way. “Get dressed, Wolfe! And get to work!”

“Okay.” Eddie’s voice was nearly a whisper as he pushed himself off the railing. “So we’re not handling it.”

Personally, Buck thought he was handling it quite well. He hadn’t immediately rushed downstairs to deck the probie, after all. Even if the temptation to do so was so very strong.

“C’mon,” his husband said, wrapping gentle fingers around one of his wrists. “Let’s go.”

Buck wasn’t sure where they were going, but he let Eddie lead him away nonetheless. Behind them, as they walked off, he could hear Lena Bosko asking the others,

“So what crawled up his ass today?”

The alarm rang fifteen minutes into their shift. Buck had spent that time, with Eddie’s help, trying to keep his temper reigned in and his frustrations from boiling over again. It was very difficult, since it all sparked back up every time he looked out into the station and spotted Wolfe going through life like he hadn’t done a damn thing wrong.

Pepa hadn’t been wrong, all those years ago, she might’ve even understated things a bit.

Eddie Diaz was a saint.

Like all the fires before it, this one was residential. Like all the fires before it, the side of the house was ablaze by the time they arrived. It wasn’t as bad as it could’ve been, it wasn’t as bad as the others had been in the past, but they could still see the fire spreading as they watched.

Sitting on the sidewalk, coughing his lungs out, was a teenaged boy. In his lap, clinging to his shirt and crying, was a little girl still in her pajamas. The sight of them had Buck’s temper flaring again.

It stayed burning, his anger, throughout the rest of the call. The orders he barked were just this side of too-harsh, he knew, and did his best to bite it back. But it was difficult, so very difficult. Especially when he heard the little girl ask for one of her toys, only to immediately start crying again.

He managed to appease himself by putting Wolfe on crowd control, instead of letting him anywhere near the fire he started. But it was only a minor and petty joy, one that did next to nothing to soothe his temper any. 

The atmosphere in the truck on the way back to the station was tense. It was Buck’s fault, he knew. The others talked and joked, but they were all clearly uneasy with his very apparent mood. Not helped by the way he didn’t join in, even when they did his best to drag him into it.

The moment the trucks were all back in the bay, the moment he was no longer needed to direct them, he stormed into the captain’s office. His helmet, which he hadn’t bothered to stow away, was thrown across the room to bounce off the wall. He raised a fist, ready to vent some of this anger out on the desk, only for a hand to grab him by the wrist.

“That won’t help.” Eddie was pressed against Buck’s back, free arm wrapping around him. He hadn’t changed out of his turnouts either. “It’ll feel good, but only for a moment. And it won’t help.”

“What if they’d been asleep?” Buck asked, letting Eddie take his weight. “It’s summer vacation, they should have been sleeping in. He set fire to a house with children in it, and he- Why? What is the point?”

“I don’t know.”

“I’m losing it, Eds,” he whispered. “I’m losing it, I am. I can’t just keep… I have to do something. Anything.”

“Okay, so.” Eddie guided him into one of the chairs, and then took the other next to it. “How do we get the proof they need?”

For the first time all day, since perhaps that conversation at Bobby and Athena’s, Buck felt his face break into a grin.

The first option was a long shot, Buck knew. It was also the easiest.

The thing is, Buck was currently acting captain. It was a role that carried responsibilities, but it also carried power. Bobby always liked to call the station his. His house, his rules, his trucks. And Buck figured that if he followed that logic, then technically? Technically, what he was about to do was entirely permissible and above-board.

A huge breach of privacy, one that would have Buck raging if it happened to him, but technically permissible.

They waited until the middle of the night, when everyone was catching as much sleep as they could in the bunks. The shift had been the usual chaotic affair, though it slowed down the later it got. It was easier for Buck to get through, now that he wasn’t just having to watch the trainwreck happen in front of him.

The others had clocked his change in mood, giving him odd looks at the way he did a complete 180. But that was fine, he was used to his coworkers thinking he was weird.

Buck and Eddie didn’t quite creep into the locker room, but they definitely did their best to avoid making any noise. Wolfe’s locker was along the right wall, in the back corner. When Buck went to open it, Eddie stopped him with a hand to the chest and held out a pair of nitrile gloves.

“Seriously?” The question was hissed out.

“We’re doing this right.”

Buck rolled his eyes and took the gloves, tugging them on. Eddie did the same with his own.

None of them had any padlocks on their lockers. They were allowed, if they wanted, but no one really saw the point. And honestly? If they couldn’t trust their coworkers with their things, then they definitely couldn’t trust them with their life.

Buck didn’t feel any ounce of guilt for violating that trust now.

Wolfe’s duffle bag wasn’t very exciting looking: black, with the Nike Swoosh on the side. It also wasn’t very heavy when they pulled it out and placed it on the bench behind them. The zipper was too loud for how quiet the station was, and both of them held their breath as they waited for someone to pop up from somewhere and ask what the hell they were doing.

Inside was just Wolfe’s civvies. Jeans, a t-shirt for a band that Buck was fairly certain that May was a fan of. But no purple hoodie, no red-billed cap, no stash of matches. But there was something that caught his attention.

“What are you doing?” Eddie asked as he watched Buck stick his face into the empty bag.

“It smells like…” Buck took a sniff, grimaced. “Like he keeps his dirty laundry in here.”

“Gross.”

“But I think I smell cigarettes.”

Buck held the bag out for Eddie to take a whiff, but his husband was quick to shake his head.

“I’ll take your word for it,” he said. “If it’s anything like yours, I don’t want my nose anywhere near it.”

“Like yours smells any better,” Buck said, rolling his eyes. “Where’s his keys?”

“Over here,” Eddie said, holding them up. Buck held out his hand expectantly, only for Eddie to draw his own away. “Uh, no. We are not pawing through his car in the middle of the night.”

“Babe-”

“No. That’s just asking for us to be caught and who knows what’ll happen then,” Eddie said, putting everything back into Wolfe’s duffle bag. “Go get us some flashlights, we’ll peek through his windows.”

“You are the best.”

Buck leapt to his feet and pressed a kiss onto Eddie, before hurrying off to do just that.

The inside of Wolfe’s yellow mini cooper was a mess. Food wrappers and empty bottles in the floorboards, a beach towel thrown carelessly in the backseat. It was also almost as much of a bust as the kid’s locker. Almost.

There was no purple Lakers hoodie, no container of gasoline, no open box of matches.

But there was a red-billed LA Angels cap sitting on the car dash.

“Eds,” he called out quietly, shining his light on it through the window. “Look!”

“He lives in Los Angeles and he’s a fan of the Angels,” Eddie said, words dry.

“Eddie.”

“Having shitty taste in sports teams doesn’t make him an arsonist.”

“Yellow car, cigarette smoke, red-billed cap…”

“It’s not me you have to convince,” Eddie pointed out. “I already believe you.”

Buck let out a huff. As nice as it was to hear, it also wasn’t very helpful.

“This isn’t enough, is it?” It wasn’t really a question, but Eddie shook his head anyway.

“I don’t think so. Not if the car itself wasn’t.”

“Okay,” Buck breathed. “Option B it is.”

On the other side of the car, Eddie shut off his light and let out a series of colorful swears in two different languages. He was not a fan of Option B.

He also didn’t let Buck paw through the car, so he didn’t have much room to complain.

Option B

Chapter Summary

In which Buck takes matters into his own hands, and drags Eddie along with him.

Athena expected Sunday dinner to be filled with tension. The way Buck stormed out on Friday, plus the way he’d talked to her on Saturday morning? Yeah, she definitely expected some tension.

The young man was frustrated, and she both understood and sympathized. It was the worst part of police work, sometimes, knowing who the culprit was but being unable to sufficiently prove it. Knowing and yet having to let them run free. That didn’t mean she appreciated being snapped at as he had, nor that she appreciated being accused of doing nothing. Not when she was doing everything she could.

So, yes, Athena walked into the Diaz household expecting a tense atmosphere and more snapping. She definitely expected for the apology - and she was getting an apology - to be begrudging and half-hearted. She did not expect to walk in to find Buck acting as cheerful and bouncy as he ever did.

“I’m sorry, Ma,” Buck said first thing, the moment she walked through the door. His words were heartfelt, not half-hearted. “I shouldn’t have talked to you like that. I know it’s not your fault and that you’re doing what you can.”

“It’s forgiven,” Athena said, eying him warily. Her attention was split by Gavin running up to her and demanding to be picked up. “But don’t do it again.”

“Where’s Chris?” Bobby asked as he made his slow way to the kitchen, where Isabel was already waiting. Athena followed, because her instincts were screaming at her. “This’ll be the last time I get both my grandkids in one place for the next couple weeks.”

“His room,” Buck said. “He’ll come out when he smells the food, he always does.”

“I miss when mi bisnieto was just as excited to help make the food,” Isabel tutted as she sliced up vegetables.

“Yeah, but talking to his friends is cooler than hanging out with us.”

“How was your shift yesterday?” Athena asked, absently patting Hoover on the head as she went past the dog. There was something not quite right about how Buck was acting. Something off, or… No, there was nothing different from how Buck normally acted. He was excited and cheerful, like always. That was what piqued her instincts.

His behavior was entirely unlike how he’d been on Friday, or the morning before.

“Fine.” There, Buck’s tone was short and sharp. That was closer to what she expected from him today. “There was another fire, we put it out. Nothing exciting past that.”

Athena narrowed her eyes, taking a seat at the breakfast bar and situating Gavin on her lap.

“You’re doing what you can,” Bobby said. “Just focus on that. He’ll make a mistake eventually, and that’s when they’ll get him.”

“Here’s hoping he’ll make a mistake soon,” Buck said as he pulled on an apron and stepped up beside Isabel. His words were all cheer. No anger, no frustration. Nothing at all like how he acted on Friday. Nothing at all like the way he snapped at her on the phone.

This was the same man that stole Chimney’s phone and snuck into his hospital room to get it unlocked. The same man that cut her off in traffic on the way to the dispatch center when it was being held hostage, who was on the verge of dumpster diving for evidence with that awful reporter before Athena stopped him. The same man that sped like a hooligan through Los Angeles and then assaulted an off-duty officer to save a teenage girl.

And here he was, just cheerfully hoping?

Athena raised an eyebrow and shared a look with her husband behind Buck’s back, before shaking her head. Her idiot boy was definitely planning to do something stupid. She just hoped Eddie kept him from going completely off the rails.

(Who was she kidding? She’ll end up having to bail the both of them out of whatever foolhardy idea was being cooked up.)

“Remember,” Buck was saying, “if you want to come home, just call us. We’ll drop everything and come get you.”

“Pops,” Chris groaned and rolled his eyes.

“Just let him get it out of his system, mijo,” Eddie said. He wanted to reach out and brush his hand through Chris’ hair, but resisted the urge. Chris would just huff and get upset about being embarrassed in front of his friends. “He worries.”

Once again, Chris was about to get loaded onto a bus and go away to camp for the next two weeks. And once again, Buck was fretting over it. He’d spent that morning with a clipboard, tormenting all of them while making sure that Chris had everything he needed. And Eddie spent that morning helping Chris unpack everything that he didn’t actually need behind his husband’s back, because sometimes Buck went a little overboard when it came to his loved ones.

(The only reason the upper cabinets in their kitchen weren’t baby proofed, like at Maddie and Chimney’s old place, was because of Abuela's colorful and creative threats.)

“Are you saying you wouldn’t steal the truck if he called, asking to come home right that moment?” Buck asked.

Eddie simply shrugged, because he couldn’t lie nor did he want to admit to anything. In his arms, Gavin let out some babble and reached for his brother.

“Make sure they don’t do anything stupid,” Chris told the tot. “I won’t be here to do it.”

“Okay, yeah, I think it’s time for you to get on the bus now,” Eddie said. “Gavin, tell your brother bye.”

“Bye-bye,” Gavin said, opening and closing his little fist in a wave.

“Bye, Gavin,” Chris said, leaning in to give him a hug and a kiss on his forehead before hurrying off. Over his shoulder, he tossed out a, “Bye Dad, bye Pops!”

“Wow,” Buck drawled as he watched him clamber into the bus. “I can feel the love.”

“I’m sure he’ll call us before he goes to bed tonight,” Eddie said, giving into the need to reassure his husband. And also himself. “If only to tell Gavin goodnight.”

Buck glanced down at Gavin and grimaced. Eddie didn’t need to be a genius to figure out why. Gavin would, without a doubt, be throwing a fit or three over the next couple of weeks as he figured out that his big brother was not at home to play with him. He’d be fine at first, probably, since he was used to Chris being at school.

But eventually…

“Make sure we don’t do anything stupid,” Buck muttered, holding out his hand for Gavin to grip and then swinging it a little. “He’s setting you up to fail, little guy.”

“So you admit it,” Eddie said, waving at Chris through the bus window. “The plan is stupid.”

“It was your idea.”

That wasn’t entirely accurate. But it definitely wasn’t inaccurate, either.

“At least Chris won’t be here to see it explode in our faces,” is what Eddie said, instead of trying to defend himself.

“I’m sure it’ll be fine. What’s the worst that could happen?”

Eddie didn’t say anything. Eddie simply stared at him.

“Yeah,” Buck admitted with a sigh. “I heard it the moment I said it…”

Hen was, up until this point, enjoying a relaxing evening with her wife. Denny was with his dad, who had managed to avoid pissing them off enough that they cut him off. Karen was curled up on the couch beside her, both of them enjoying a glass of wine to go with the stupid and corny movie that was playing on the television.

Actually, it probably wasn’t fair of Hen to call the movie stupid or corny. She actually didn’t know what was happening in it, because frankly? The more wine that her wife drank, the more affectionate she got, and Hen simply did not care about anything else at that moment. The movie was purely background noise.

And then her phone rang.

“Ignore it,” Karen told her, nosing at her neck.

It was tempting. It was more than tempting. But she could see her phone’s screen, could see Buck’s name, and Athena’s words from earlier in the day came back to her then.

“They’re about to do something stupid,” she had said. “I’m telling you now. They’re going to do something stupid and I’ll have to skin the hide off both of them.”

Athena hadn’t told her much, but Hen wasn’t stupid. Not as smart as her beautiful rocket scientist wife, but she wasn’t stupid. The sergeant shows up at the station with Bobby and a stranger, and then both Buck and Eddie start acting weird? Yeah, there was something going on, and if Athena felt the need to forewarn her, she’d do well to stay in the loop.

“I’ll regret it if I do,” Hen said. Karen pouted at her even as she pulled back enough for Hen to reach for her phone, and Hen was very tempted to let her phone ring out anyways simply to kiss it away. But alas. She grabbed her phone and answered it with a simple, “Yeah?”

“Hen!” was Buck’s overly cheerful greeting. “Is this a bad time?”

“Yes!” Karen answered for her, close enough to have heard Buck’s question.

“It’s a better time than an hour from now,” Hen told him. Because if he had called an hour later, she would have been far too busy to answer it. “What’s up? What do you need?”

“Oh, uh. Well… I’m going to be late tomorrow. Eddie and I both will be.”

“How late?” Hen asked, sitting up.

“Uh. I’m not sure yet? It’ll depend on- It doesn’t matter,” Buck said. “There’s- There’s just a thing. I’m not sure when I’ll be able to get to the station.”

“Okay.” She drew the word out. “Should I guess what this has to do with me?”

“Well you’re the other, y’know. Captain person. I figured you could be the person in charge while I’m not there?”

It was almost insulting, the way she was being handed it like a consolation prize. It would be an even worse insult, though, if Buck hadn’t called to ask her. If he was simply going to be late, it would have been a small matter to ask Captain Mayfair to stay over. Then again, if Athena’s warning was right, it probably wasn’t as simple as being late, like he claimed.

“Sure,” Hen said, agreeing easily enough. “Do I want to know what’s going on?”

“Uh. Nothing exciting,” Buck lied. “It’s- It’s nothing, really. Nothing to worry about.”

“Uh-huh. Right.” Nothing, her ass. “I’ll hold down the fort until you get in, then.”

“Awesome, thanks Hen!”

She stared at her phone for a moment after he hung up, before calling Athena.

“So you know what you told me about them doing something stupid?” she said when her friend answered. “It’s tomorrow morning.”

On the other end of the line, Athena let out a tired sigh.

“This was such a stupid idea,” Eddie grumbled. He was hunched down in the front passenger seat of the jeep, trying to hide from the world outside. “He’s going to see us.”

Eddie, much as Buck hated to admit it, had a point. The jeep was not at all inconspicuous. But it wasn’t like they had a wealth of options when it came to vehicles suitable for a stake-out.

“It’ll be fine,” Buck insisted, reaching over blindly to give him a reassuring pat. He had a pair of binoculars that he was using to keep an eye on Wolfe’s yellow mini cooper, parked in front of the house he lived in with his mother. 

This was Option B. Eddie was not a fan of Option B, despite the fact that he was technically the one to come up with it. Jokingly, but still. Buck, however? Buck was a big fan of it.

Saturday night, when it became apparent that Option A - find something incriminating in the probie’s belongings - was a bust, Buck went snooping through personnel files. For a good cause! And it was, once again, technically okay because he was the acting captain. From there, he got the probie’s home address.

That morning, a Tuesday, Buck and Eddie got up even earlier than they normally did, and didn’t bother to dress in their uniforms. They checked on Gavin, fed Hoover and Senorita, and then drove all the way to the neighborhood of Vermont-Slauson. There was a conveniently placed alley way, not far from Wolfe’s home, that Buck backed the jeep into.

They’d been there ever since, waiting for Wolfe to make his move.

“Do you even need those?” Eddie asked, and Buck knew he meant the binoculars. “He’s not even a block away. I can see his car from here just fine.”

“It’s a stake-out, babe,” Buck said. “We have to look the part.”

“We’re going to get the cops called on us before we even get the chance to stalk the guy,” Eddie complained.

“We’re not stalking him, we’re tailing him,” he corrected. “And no one’s even gonna pay attention to us.”

“We’re not cops, it’s stalking.”

“But we are peace offi- Shit!” 

There was movement at Wolfe’s house, and Buck reflexively ducked down in his seat as he watched the probie finally step outside. He was dressed in another hoodie, not the purple Lakers one, and had his Nike duffle hanging off his shoulder. The kid didn’t even look in their direction as he got to the mini cooper, throwing the bag into the passenger seat and then folding himself inside.

Buck tossed the binoculars onto the dash, started the jeep, and then held his breath. The mini cooper pulled off the curb and headed in the direction of the station. After a couple seconds, Buck pulled out and followed.

“This isn’t going to work,” Eddie said again, like the Debbie Downer that he was. “He’s going to see us following him and wonder what the hell we’re doing.”

“I’ll keep us back,” Buck said, keeping his eyes on the target. Wolfe’s car was far ahead of them, but they weren’t exactly at risk of losing the kid so long as they didn’t stay too far back. The mini cooper was, after all, a very bright yellow. “It’ll be fine.”

“Stop saying that.”

“I thought you didn’t believe in jinxes.”

His husband just grumbled a complaint.

It was safer, once they got into LA traffic proper, if difficult in a different way. There were plenty of other cars around them, and so Buck didn’t have to worry about sticking out like a sore thumb. But it was easier for them to be separated, too, and more than once Buck was afraid he’d actually lost the brightly colored vehicle. Every time, though, he managed to spot it again.

Eventually, the probie made a turn that took him away from the direction of the station. It took some maneuvering for Buck to follow him without looking like he was following, going past and then doing a loop, hoping he didn’t accidentally pull out in front of the kid.

“How do you think he chooses?” Eddie asked.

“Street names out of a hat,” Buck joked, though his heart wasn’t in it.

The probie had pulled onto a quiet street and parked in an alley, much as Buck himself had done while waiting for him. Once again, they had to take a roundabout route to avoid being noticed, parking somewhere they had a clear enough view of the kid.

“He’s got his trunk open,” Buck told Eddie, using the binoculars again. The kid had that LA Angels hat on, as well as the nitrile gloves. Buck couldn’t see what he was doing exactly, but knew it wasn’t good.

He blindly smacked Eddie on the arm.

“What was that for?” his husband complained.

“If you’d let me go through his car, we wouldn’t have had to do this,” Buck said. “He’s keeping everything in his trunk.”

“I’ll remember this for next time.”

“Hopefully there won’t be a next time,” he said. “Shit, he’s closed the trunk, he’s heading down the sidewalk and- And he’s rounded the corner. I can’t see him now, c’mon.”

Buck once again tossed the binoculars onto the dash, then threw his door open as he hurried out. He heard Eddie jump out on the other side, and he jogged down the street to catch up to the probie-turned-arsonist.

“Do you have it?” Buck asked, glancing back.

“I have it,” Eddie said, holding up the small extinguisher.

The two of them ducked behind a bush. Buck peered over it, spotting Wolfe a bit farther down. The kid wasn’t even keeping an eye out for anyone that might be watching, much less following. A shared look with Eddie had them both slipping out from behind cover and moving forward, keeping pace with Wolfe, pausing every once in a while to duck out of sight again.

Finally, Wolfe came to the house that he had decided was the one to torch. He jogged across the lawn, casually trampling over some begonias, and disappeared on the other side of the building. Buck sprinted forward, hearing Eddie right behind him. Turning the corner, he found the probie tucked behind some hedges, kneeling over a styrofoam cup with bundled up matches and cigarette sticking out of it.

As Buck watched, he lit the cigarette and stood.

And then froze at the sight of them.

“C- Cap!” The kid’s voice was muffled by the cloth mask he was wearing, but the fear was unmistakable. “I- What are you-”

“What am I doing here?” Buck finished for him, stalking forward, all the anger rushing to the fore. “What am I doing here?”

“Cap, I- I can explain…” Wolfe tried, glancing frantically at the incendiary device. “It’s not-”

“Don’t,” Buck snapped.

Eddie casually stepped around them both with the extinguisher and sprayed the entire thing down. Wolfe let out a noise, something wounded, something outraged. It pissed Buck off even more.

“Nine fires,” he snarled, stomping towards the kid, who scrambled backwards. Wolfe tripped over his own feet and fell on his ass. “Nine fires. Three deaths. One of them was a baby!”

He was just within grabbing distance, just within punching distance, when Eddie drooped the extinguisher and wrapped his arms around him.

“Don’t, mi sol…”

“I could hear Shanna begging for help!” Buck itched to beat the kid bloody, but he didn’t try to break out of his husband’s hold. “I heard Clara crying! And I had to stand there and- Why!? Why would you do this? Any of this!?”

“The- The first one was an accident. I didn’t mean to,” Wolfe tried, and both men let out twin scoffs.

“Accident?” Eddie repeated, almost mocking. “You didn’t mean to?”

“I was just- I was just trying to scare them,” the kid said. “For being assholes.”

“Oh God,” Buck breathed out. Something inside him broke at the words. “You did it because we came in complaining about them.”

“And the others?” Eddie asked, still holding Buck back. “The ones that had nothing to do with us?”

Wolfe simply shrugged and looked away, which made the rage flare even hotter.

“We’re firefighters!” Buck snapped at him. “We fight fires, we don’t-”

“Exactly!” Wolfe shouted back, scrambling to his feet. “We’re firefighters! We’re supposed to fight fires! I joined to fight fires! I didn’t go through everything at the academy to talk some whacko nutjob off the ledge or help people who get their dicks stuck in a worthless slut! I didn’t become a firefighter to do all that boring shit!”

All the rage drained out of Buck as he stared in disbelief at the probie. He felt Eddie’s hold on him loosen, no doubt for the same reason.

“That’s the reason?” Buck shook his head in disgust. “The job wasn’t exciting enough for you? You burned down people’s homes, you killed people, because the job was too boring? That’s- That’s pathetic. You’re pathetic.”

“Shut up!” Wolfe snapped at him, taking a step forward. “Shut up! No I’m not!”

“What you are is under arrest.” Athena’s voice came from behind them. Buck turned in Eddie’s arms, spotting her stepping around the corner with her gun out. “For murder and arson.”

“Ma?” Buck looked at her in confusion. “How? Why?”

“I texted her,” Eddie said with a shrug, sounding far too self-satisfied. He let his arms drop, trusting that Athena’s presence was enough of a deterrent.

“ When!?” Buck demanded, but then his attention was diverted back to the probie.

See, most people, when confronted with a cop pointing a gun at them, did the smart thing and froze. Dalton Wolfe, as Buck was rapidly figuring out, was not prone to doing the smart thing. Instead, he started moving, presumably for flight. Though, given that they were between him and his way out of there, he might’ve been going for the fight response, instead. Either way, he didn’t get far.

It had been many, many years since Buck played football in school. But he figured his old coach would be very impressed with the way he tackled Wolfe into the ground.

Winding Down

Chapter Summary

In which we spend some time in a police station

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

The last time Buck was dragged into Athena’s station, he’d been (gently) thrown into one of the interrogation rooms while Taylor was sent into another. This time, he’d simply been told to take a seat at Athena’s desk, and to not move, before being left to his own devices.

After tackling Wolfe to the ground before the kid could even try to make good on his escape attempt, Buck held him down while Athena put the cuffs on him. Then, after Athena dragged the (soon to be former) probationary firefighter to her cruiser, they’d waited until more people - officers and detectives and even Jay Knight, the arson investigator - arrived to handle the scene. Once they had, Athena followed Buck and Eddie in her cruiser as they walked back to the jeep, pausing to radio in the location of Wolfe’s vehicle, before ordering the two of them to follow her to the station. 

Neither of them tried to argue. Buck, because his survival instincts were not that bad and he knew better than to poke the bear. Eddie, because he was the one who told her where they were and what they were doing.

And now here Buck was, twiddling his thumbs while he waited for Athena to come back. She’d taken Wolfe deeper into the building, either to one of the interrogation rooms or to lock-up, and was no doubt making calls to everyone that needed calling. Eddie was on the other side of the room, giving his statement to another officer, which meant he wasn’t an option for distraction. 

So Buck took out his phone and wasted time texting people. He sent an apology to Bobby, to whom Athena had no doubt already ratted him out. He took a selfie and sent it to TK, which got him a series of confused and concerned emojis in return. Then he shot a text to Hen, informing her that she’d be down three men for the foreseeable future.

Buck wasn’t very surprised when his phone started ringing.

“Hey, Hen,” he said, wincing slightly as he answered it. “How’s it going?”

“Oh no, Buckaroo,” she said, voice stern. “What did you mean, three? What is going on?”

“I… Actually, I don’t think I’m allowed to tell you?”

“You’re not- Boy, Athena warned me yesterday that you and Eddie were about to do something stupid. She just didn’t tell me how stupid.”

Yesterday? God, why did he even bother keeping a secret from the sergeant?

“It- It wasn’t that stupid,” Buck protested, though his heart wasn’t in it. “If it was, do you think Eddie would have gone along with it?”

The silence on the other end of the line was very telling.

“The probie isn’t in yet, either,” Hen said. “So that’s you two, plus him, plus whoever-”

“No, no, he’s the third.”

“He’s the third. So it’s you two, plus him. What’s going on that you three aren’t coming in yet?”

“I seriously don’t think I can tell you.” Buck hadn’t actually been told that he couldn’t, but he wasn’t dumb. This was, after all, an on-going investigation, and he’d stuck his foot in it enough as it was. Though… “There’ll probably be people at the station later. To ask everyone questions.”

“What the hell did you do that we’re getting investigated!?”

“We’re not being investigated,” he protested. A flurry of movement at the entrance doors snagged his attention. He recognized Deputy Chief Randolf Torres as he rushed in, along with Battalion Chief Miranda Williams, though he didn’t recognize the man and woman - both in suits - that followed on their heels. As he watched, Torres and the two strangers were sent in the same direction Athena took Wolfe; Williams spotted Buck and headed his way. “Oh, the brass’re here, I better go.”

Hen’s loud reply of “The brass!? What the fuck did you-” was cut off as he ended the call and stuck his phone back in his pocket.

“Ma’am,” Buck greeted the chief, standing to shake her hand. She returned it in a perfunctory manner.

“Diaz,” she responded. “Don’t you have a station to be running?”

“Hen’s handling it.”

Williams, based on the eyebrow that popped up, was not impressed with that answer. Probably because it was supposed to be Buck handling it, instead of him being stuck at a police station. She let out a sigh, though, and snagged a seat from a near-by desk to sit in.

“Why,” she said, “is it always the One Eighteen? The Greenway mess, the reporter, the rehab clinic that burnt down earlier in the year. One of you is always involved, somehow. And don’t get me started on how often one of you tries to die. I don’t have to deal with this kind of thing from the other stations I oversee.”

“We’re special?” Buck tried, retaking the seat he’d vacated.

“Where’s the other Diaz?” the battalion chief asked.

“He’s over there, giving his statement to Officer Williams,” he said, gesturing in his husband’s direction. A thought struck him. “You two aren’t related, are you?”

“No,” was Williams’ succinct answer. “Have you given your statement yet?”

“Uh, no, not yet. I think Ma- Um, Sergeant Grant was going to take it? I’m not sure. She just told me to sit here and not go anywhere, and I wasn’t about to argue.”

“Smart.”

“Who were the two that arrived with you and the deputy chief?” Buck asked, curiosity getting the better of him. “Lawyers?”

He figured that the deputy chief would get lawyers for his nephew. That’s the kind of thing someone did, when they loved their family and had the means.

“One of them. The other is Eleanor Boone, from the union,” she said. And then her expression turned serious. “Listen to me. Do not, do not, talk to Randolf without me, Sergeant Grant, or Eleanor present.”

“He’s angry, isn’t he?”

“Astoundingly so. I haven’t gotten the full story, and I know he hasn’t either. But he’s already insisting that everyone’s wrong, that his nephew is innocent, that it’s someone else’s fault,” Williams told him. “And I do not want to give him the opportunity to make you and your husband’s lives difficult. Not that I’m saying he would, but he might.”

“Well.” Buck let out a sigh. “It wouldn’t be the first time I’ve had the brass pissed off at me. Might be for Eddie, though.”

The battalion chief grimaced at the words.

“It doesn’t matter,” she said with a shake of her head. “He’s already going to be under scrutiny simply by virtue of it being his nephew. He won’t have the opportunity to take it out on either of you.”

Buck would believe that when it actually happened. Or didn’t happen, as the case may be.

A door opened somewhere farther in the building, in the direction Torres and Athena and everyone else had gone. Or, Buck assumed a door opened, because suddenly he could hear a man’s loud and very angry shouting. It started mid-sentence and got cut-off the same way, presumably as the door closed again. It had both him and Williams looking that way, so they both spotted Athena when she came into a view a moment later.

She did not look very happy.

“I’ll leave you to your mother’s tender mercies,” Williams said, standing up. Buck flushed, pleased, at the direct and casual way she said it. “And I’ll go give Eddie the same warning I gave you.”

“What warning?” Athena asked as she came to her desk.

“He is not to talk to Torres alone,” the battalion chief explained, before leaving the pair alone.

“She isn’t wrong,” the sergeant said, taking her seat behind the desk. “I’m not sure if they sent the union rep down here for Wolfe or for him.”

“Was that him shouting?” Buck asked.

“It was. I left Rick to deal with it,” Athena said. And then she fixed Buck with a glare. The strange thing was, though, that it was not nearly as anger-filled as he figured it would be. “You idiot.”

“Sorry, Ma,” he said, ducking his head. “How much trouble am I in?”

She stared at him for a moment, before shaking her head in… Fondness? That wasn’t right. Buck was expecting disappointment and a scolding, not whatever this was.

“None,” she said.

Buck blinked and stared at her.

“None?” he repeated. “But… What?”

“You showed up and put out a fire,” Athena drawled. “That is literally your job description. Nothing you did was illegal.”

“Eddie called it stalking,” Buck said. He sat back in the chair, feeling the slightest bit petulant, even if he wasn’t sure why. Relief should have been his primary emotion at that moment.

“I know, it’s what he said you were doing when he texted me,” she said. “But unless you’ve been following the guy for longer than just this morning, it wasn’t.”

“I…” He wetted his lips. “I thought you’d be, y’know. Angrier. With me.”

She’d certainly been pissed off at him the last time he’d done something like this.

“Buck,” Athena said, “I told Bobby you were going to do something like this after you stormed out on Friday.”

“Oh.” So apparently he was just that predictable.

“But I am upset with you.” There it was. It was somehow comforting, because he’d been expecting to be yelled at since she showed up at the attempted arson scene. “What did I tell you last year? After you went flying through the streets like a fool to rescue that poor girl?”

“To- To, um. Let other people know? What I was doing?”

“And what did you not do today? Or yesterday? Or the day before that? Because I know you’d already planned this little excursion before I even showed up at your door for Sunday dinner.”

Buck looked down at his hands, laying in his lap, and felt shame creep up his neck. Athena reached over her desk to put her fingers under his chin and gently urged him to look at her.

“You had no idea if Wolfe had a knife, or a gun,” she said. “You had no idea how he was going to react when you confronted him. He could have been dangerous. He could have hurt you, or Eddie. It was lucky that this ended as cleanly as it did. As nonviolent as it did.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I know you are. And I know that your first instinct will always be to help people, to fix a problem the moment you see it. It’s something we all love about you,” Athena said. “But you at least have to tell us when you’re about to do something like this. We can’t help if you don’t.”

“I’m sorry,” he said again. “But…”

“But?” she prompted.

“But would you have even let me?” Buck asked. “If I’d told you what I was going to do, you’d have stopped me.”

Athena’s face twisted into a scowl. She took the hand that had been holding his chin, and used it to smack him on the side of the head.

“Ow!”

“I literally took you with me to go rescue your sister,” she said, rolling her eyes and shaking her head. “Did you forget about that? And he’d already proven himself dangerous.”

“Right. Sorry.” Buck rubbed at the spot where she’d smack him. It stung. “Was it enough? Everything today?”

“It damn well better be.” She sat back in her chair and started going through desk drawers, before pulling out a legal pad and a pen. “Rick said CSU’s going through his car.”

“Wolfe was keeping everything in his trunk,” Buck told her, a bit too eager. “I think in that luggage compartment they have in the bottom, because I didn’t see anything when I looked Saturday night.”

Athena’s expression went flat as she looked at him.

“When you-? No, no. That I do not want to know,” she said, shaking her head again. “Just give me your account of events this morning. And please try to keep it from being self-incriminating.”

“Er. Should I start with Friday or…?”

“Just this morning.”

“Alright.” Buck cleared his throat. “Eddie and I left the house about an hour earlier, and went to Wolfe’s address in Vermont-Slauson…”

At one point, while Buck was giving Athena his statement, Torres finally came back out. He stormed into the bullpen, all rage and bluster. His gaze zeroed in on Buck, and he started towards him. Buck wasn’t certain if Torres intended to yell at him, or to blame him, or to try and convince him to say that it was all a mistake or a lie. But he didn’t get to find out.

The moment the deputy chief took a step in his direction, both Chief Williams and Eleanor Boone, the union rep, were there. One stepping in front of him, the other on his heels. Buck couldn’t quite hear what was said, it was all in whispered shouting, but he didn’t really need to. Torres was angry about his nephew’s actions, and he didn’t want to accept that they were his nephew’s actions. And Buck had always been a good scapegoat.

“That’s going to be a problem,” Eddie said, coming to stand next to where Buck sat.

“Definitely,” Athena agreed.

“Not your problem,” Williams said, coming over as well. A still fuming Torres was being led away by Boone. “Are you both done?”

“Not yet,” Buck told her. “I just got to Wolfe’s pathetic motive rant.”

“You both have an appointment at headquarters as soon as you are,” Williams told him, glancing back in the direction of Torres and Boone. “And Sergeant, I was told to make the request that our department be kept in the loop of everything.”

“I’ll see what I can do,” Athena said.

“Thank you.”

“Why do we need to go to headquarters?” Buck asked, exchanging looks with Eddie.

“Because the department is covering its ass,” was Williams’ blunt answer. “And there’s a deputy chief trying to stick his foot in it.”

Buck couldn’t really argue with that.

It was hours later, close enough to almost be considered evening, by the time Buck and Eddie actually made it to the fire station. The trucks were all sitting in the bay and everyone was upstairs in the loft, from the sounds of it, so they weren’t accosted as they strode to the locker room to finally change into the uniforms.

“Is that pizza?” was the first thing out of Buck’s mouth when they made it upstairs. Everyone’s heads popped up and twisted around to look at them.

“When you said you two were going to be late today,” Hen said, shoving her chair back and standing, “I didn’t realize you meant you were going to miss half the day.”

“Yeah, yeah.” Buck waved her off as he crossed the distance to the table, reaching down to snag a couple slices of pizza. He handed one to Eddie, who let out a happy noise before chomping it in half, his cheeks bulging out like a chipmunk.

“Where’s the probie?” Lena asked, leaning back in her chair to look past them.

“Uh.” Buck shared a look with Eddie, who shrugged. “Probationary Firefighter Wolfe is no longer with the department.”

“Yes!” Ravi shouted, throwing his head back and his arms in the air. “Yes! Thank you! Thank you!”

Everyone else’s response to the news was similar, if not nearly as spirited. Wolfe really had made himself very unpopular with everyone. Once upon a time, Buck would have felt bad for him. But that time had definitely come and gone.

“We’ll also be going offline for a bit,” he continued, clapping his hands together. “The brass will be sending people over to ask us questions, and uh. So will the police.”

“What did you do!?” Hen demanded, hands on her hips.

“Put out a fire,” Buck said, shrugging a little. Eddie slapped him on the arm and glared, mouth too full of pizza to say anything. “Okay, fine, Eds put out the fire.”

“And how does that lead to us being questioned by the police?” Crawford asked, tipping their chair back a little. “Actually, wait, why are the police going to be asking us questions?”

“The probie started the fire,” Eddie said, through a disgusting mouthful of food. He paused to swallow, before adding, “The probie started a lot of fires.”

“He- Why?” Toby asked, leaning forward, arms on the table. It looked as if he had an entire pizza, plain cheese, to himself.

Because he’s a pathetic piece of shit, was how Buck wanted to answer the question. But what he actually said was,

“So that we’d respond to them.”

“What the fuck,” Albert said, sounding a little horrified.

Buck simply shrugged. The sentiment really summed the whole thing up better than he ever could. 

After, when he’d scarfed down another couple slices of pizza, he headed downstairs. Once again, he grabbed a flathead screwdriver from one of the toolboxes sitting out and went for the turnout cages. This time, however, he was not nearly as careful with his task.

Wolfe’s name plate cracked in half when he prised it off. The pieces of it clattered against the bottom of the trashcan when he threw them in.

Chapter End Notes

This show's writers reuse names far too often. We have Battalion Chief Williams and Officer Williams; Charlie, old man with the grenade in his leg, and Charlie, kid poisoned by his mother; Tommy, Abby's boyfriend, and Tommy, the firefighter; Dr. Copeland, Buck's therapist, and the other Copeland, the dude that shot Eddie. Oh! And when they decided to give Taylor Kelly a backstory, they made her "real" last name Martin. Like Ali Martin. And there're probably so many others.

Moments in Time

Chapter Summary

In which there are time skips to wrap up loose threads, before another is laid out.

July

“Bobby.”

His wife’s voice had Bobby turning away from the stairs. She was standing in the doorway to the dining room, hip cocked and arms crossed

“Yeah?” He tried to sound casual, but he knew he was failing at it.

“Watching the front door isn’t going to make them arrive any faster,” Athena said with a shake of her head. “Buck’s the one we joke about being a Golden Retriever, stop taking his role.”

“I know,” Bobby said, turning back nonetheless. “It’s just… I want to know how it went.”

“We know how it went.” Athena came to stand at his side, arm curling around him. “Today was just a formality.”

“I know,” he repeated.

Today was the day of Gavin’s official adoption. Everyone else was waiting in the backyard, which had been decorated with balloons and streamers.
