Chimney and Albert had not been the ones to go shopping for decorations this time, so they weren’t baby shower decorations. The grill was lit and Michael - he’d flown in with David and Harry earlier in the week to visit - was being trusted to keep an eye on everything.

There was no reason to be nervous, Bobby knew. Like Athena said, going before the judge was a simple formality at this point. Gavin wasn’t going anywhere. But still, Bobby worried.

The front door opening drew him out of his thoughts. He must have obviously perked up, because Athena snorted out a laugh and pulled away from him.

“Come on down, boys!” she called out.

Chris was the first to come down, slow and steady as he gripped the railing. Eddie was right behind him, and Buck behind him, with Gavin secured in his arms and the diaper bag hanging from one shoulder so it bounced off his hip. It was the blinding smiles that both of the men wore that had Bobby finally feeling settled, though.

“Grandpop!” Gavin squealed, leaning as far out of Buck’s hold as he could, his little arms reaching out. Bobby immediately reached for him, happily taking his weight.

“Well?” Athena said. “Let’s see it.”

“Uh, hold on.” Buck reached into the diaper bag, frowning in concentration as he dug around, before tugging out a plain manilla folder. From that he pulled a single piece of paper that he held out for Bobby to see. 

There, against the watermark of the California state seal: Gavin Robert Diaz.

“Are you going to cry?” Buck asked, looking worried. “You can’t cry. You know I’ll start crying if you do.”

Maybe.

“No,” Bobby said, bouncing Gavin a little. “No I’m not.”

Not a single one of them looked like they believed that. Which was fair, because Bobby absolutely did cry a little when he put Gavin down to pull first Buck and then Eddie into rib-crushing bear hugs. And then he did the same to Chris, because he was not going to leave his other grandson out. 

Even if Chris complained about it the entire time.

 

August

Bobby was scheduled to return to duty a few days after Gavin’s adoption, the first week of August. It had been a journey, and he still wasn’t at a hundred percent. He knew it, he had no hope of ignoring it. His back twinged if he moved the wrong way, and it ached if he slept on it wrong. The pain was still a constant companion, even if it wasn’t as debilitating as it had been in the immediate aftermath of the bridge collapse. But it was manageable. It was being managed

It was a good thing, though, that being a captain meant he didn’t often do anything truly strenuous. His physical therapist, the one Athena had bullied him into seeing with Buck and May’s help, had been insistent about him taking it easy. Bobby had every intention of doing exactly that. The brass were watching him, after all. If they thought he was unable to do the job because of his back, they would ramp up the pressure to force him into early retirement.

Bobby had no interest in leaving this job anytime soon.

“Long time no see, stranger!” Captain Mayfair shouted out when she spotted him coming. She was leaning against the outside wall, beside the bay doors, though she pushed herself off as he came up. “Nice to see you walking easy again.”

“Maria,” he greeted, shaking her hand. “What are you doing out here?”

“Delaying you,” was her answer.

Ah.

“What do they have planned?” Bobby asked. He watched as she took out her phone and sent someone a quick text.

“I have been sworn to secrecy,” Mayfair told him, stowing her phone away again. “But the usual shenanigans your lot gets up to. We got back from a call to find about five of them decorating the loft. Pretty sure someone was about to murder Buck with his own clipboard, at one point.”

Bobby nodded in understanding, because there was a reason that he tried to keep all of them locked up. The only person who liked how Buck acted whenever he got his hands on a clipboard was Eddie.

“How long do we need to stay out here for?”

“I think it’s been long enough,” Mayfair said, turning on her heel and leading him inside.

Already, Bobby could spot the decorations that had been set up in the station’s loft. Balloons and bunting, with a Welcome Back banner hanging from the railing. But that wasn’t what had him coming to a stop just inside the doors. Mayfair came to a stop as well and looked back, letting out a hum when she realized what caught his attention.

“Yeah, Buck put that up,” she said. “A few weeks back, just after that whole mess with the arsons.”

There was a framed picture of Austin Jones hanging on the wall, the red name plate from his turnout cage fastened just beneath it. The photo wasn’t the man’s official department portrait, though he was in uniform and smiling broadly at the camera with his arms spread wide. And he looked young, too, young enough that he couldn’t have been long out of the academy when the picture was taken.

After another moment spent gazing at it, Bobby moved on. There was a stop at the locker room to stow away his bag before he headed up the stairs. The moment his foot hit the top step, there was a very large man wrapping himself around him. Bobby had to grip the railing for fear of toppling over with the force of it. He could spot everyone else standing around the loft, laughing at the scene.

“You’re back!” Buck cried, squeezing him tightly before letting go.

“And he’s no longer in charge,” Albert heckled from where he stood beside Ravi at the kitchen island. There was a large cake sitting out, one that Hen must’ve brought in.

“You shut up,” Buck shot back, twisting around to glare at him.

“Or what? You can’t make me scrub the toilets anymore.”

“I can,” Bobby pointed out as he stepped around Buck, who was too busy sticking his tongue out at Albert to get out of the way. “It’s nice to see the station still standing in my absence.”

“It was a close thing at times,” Hen said. She cut a large slice of cake and placed it on a plate with a fork, and then handed it to him. “But we managed.”

“Maybe now I’ll get my extra half hour of sleep back in the mornings,” Eddie said, lining up to get his own slice of cake.

“You say that,” Buck said, draping himself over his back. “And yet, we both know Gavin won’t let that happen.”

Eddie grimaced but didn’t argue.

“Where did you get the picture of Jones?” Bobby asked.

“Oh, um.” Buck flushed and rubbed at his neck. “His mom. That was an awkward phone call. Pretty sure she about cried when I explained what I wanted it for.”

“She did cry when she brought it in and he hung it up,” Eddie added.

There was a whispered argument happening between Albert and Ravi. Bobby couldn’t quite make-out what they were saying, but it didn’t seem to be a very serious argument. Finally though, Ravi sighed and shook his head and called out,

“Hey, Cap!”

“Yeah?” Bobby replied, at the same time Buck said, “What?”

And then Buck covered his face in embarrassment while Albert and Ravi broke down laughing. Bobby just shook his head and ate his cake. He wasn’t officially back on the clock yet, after all.

 

September

Chimney wasn’t really expecting much when he walked into the station for his first day back since the bridge collapse. There’d been a celebration for his return the night before - at the Diaz residence, because Buck really enjoyed being able to play host - so he knew there wouldn’t be one waiting for him here. But he did expect, at least, for the station to be filled with his coworkers. Instead, it was concerningly quiet.

The trucks were all parked in the bay, which told him that they weren’t out on a call. But there didn’t seem to be anyone here. Voices carried in this building, and so did footsteps, so he knew they weren’t simply out of sight.

So where the hell was everyone?

He managed to locate one person, at least: Toby Klein, the red-haired driver-engineer that replaced Jones, jogging down the stairs. Chimney had only talked to him a couple times before, after Jones’ funeral and at the celebration for Gavin’s adoption.

“Hey! Welcome back.”

“Thanks,” Chimney said, glancing around. “Where is everybody?”

“Upstairs,” Toby answered, pointing. “On the roof. Cap’s got a new bonding exercise or something.”

“How’d you get out of it?”

“I lied and told him it was against my religion.”

That didn’t really help with Chimney’s confusion. But, he figured, the answers were waiting for him on the roof. In that endeavor, he headed for the lockers to change and stow away his bag, before heading upstairs.

Things did not get any clearer when he arrived, though it did get brighter. The morning sun shone down on (almost) all of his coworkers sitting in a neat little circle. He paused for a moment simply to stare.

“Nice of you to finally join us,” Bobby commented. “Take a seat.”

“What are we doing?” Chimney asked, heading for the lone empty chair between Hen and Eddie. Albert and Ravi waved at him from the other side of the circle.

“Meditation,” Hen answered as he sat down.

“He says it helps with his back,” Eddie said, leaning in like he was sharing the latest gossip. “I think he just likes making us sit down and shut up for a while.”

“Not that that’s happening right now,” Bobby said, shooting them a mock glare. 

Eddie sat back and mimed zipping his lips, which just made Buck have to smother laughter on his other side.

“Alright, everyone, let’s clear our minds-”

“Harder for some of us than others, Cap,” Buck said.

“Let’s try to clear our minds,” Bobby amended, shaking his head. “What we do here is as important as any call, because it keeps us from becoming the emergency. So let’s close our eyes and focus on our breathing.”

Chimney did as he was bid, settling into his folding chair and closing his eyes.

“We can let go of past traumas,” the captain said. “We can let go of future expectations and allow opportunity to find us. We can find what brings us peace.”

And in the darkness behind his eyelids, Chimney saw his girls.

 

January

“I have an announcement,” Bobby declared. Everyone looked up from their lunch to stare at him.

“Well?” Eddie urged after a moment, when that was all the captain said. “What is it?”

“No, no, babe, let him be dramatic,” Buck told him, even though he wanted to know, too.

“He didn’t bring an apology cake,” Ravi said from the other end of the table. “So it isn’t our annual reviews.”

“Too early for those, anyways,” Albert added. “Which reminds me. Cap! Whatever week those are, I’m not going to be here. I’m gonna come down with the flu.”

“If you’re all done,” Bobby said, shaking his head at them. “The announcement is that I won’t be here. Athena and I are trying for another belated honeymoon.”

“Another?” Toby asked. “Is this your second honeymoon?”

“No, it’ll be their first,” Eddie told him. “Their last one got postponed.”

“What’ll it be this time, Cap?” Chimney set down his silverware and tipped his seat back. “Another cruise?”

“Another cruise,” Bobby confirmed with a nod. “It’ll be a few days in March, which means…”

“Tag,” Buck said, reaching around Eddie to slap Hen on the shoulder. “You’re it.”

“Gee, thanks,” Hen drawled, though she said it with a smile. “How magnanimous of you.”

“Hey, it’s your turn.” Buck shrugged, and then turned back to Bobby. “Where’s the cruise headed?”

“Up and down the coast of Mexico,” Bobby told him. “We’re leaving from here.”

“Well, hopefully,” Buck said, tucking into his food again, “you and Ma will be able to enjoy it this time.”

A couple months later, Buck would be kicking himself for jinxing it.

 

Fin

Afterword
